Chapter 1+3: Introduction & Risk Management

p7-p31

ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p7 – p15

Chapter Learning Objectives

  • Understand the fundamental differences between biopharmaceuticals and small-molecule drugs, and how these differences affect manufacturing facility design
  • Grasp the scope of this Guide: which product types it covers and which regulatory requirements it aligns with (21 CFR Part 211, EudraLex Vol.4, etc.)
  • Gain a thorough understanding of the six key concept definitions: Aseptic Operation, Axenic State, Bioburden-Free, Closed System, Open System and Functionally Closed. These definitions are the foundation for understanding the entire Guide
  • Understand the chapter structure of the Guide so you can quickly locate the role of each chapter within the overall design decision framework
1.1 Background

Original Text

This second edition of the ISPE Baseline® Guide: Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities intends to further reinforce the concepts described in the first edition of the Guide, provide examples of how these concepts can be put into practice, and detail the value and benefits of the approach described.

The biopharmaceutical industry has adapted to reflect available technologies, products with more focused patient populations, regulatory conditions, and continued pressures on costs, while maintaining high quality standards. This edition of the Guide develops concepts to reflect how these changes affect biopharmaceutical manufacturing facilities without sacrificing product quality, by reducing risk and enhancing the manufacturing control strategy.

As the relevant technology has evolved, the implementation of closed systems has become more accepted and routine; the Guide emphasizes the use of closed systems as an approach to mitigating risks associated with the production of biopharmaceuticals.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept Analysis — Why Is a Dedicated Biopharmaceutical Facilities Guide Needed?

Biopharmaceuticals are therapeutic drugs produced from living cells or organisms. Compared with traditional chemically synthesized small-molecule drugs, biopharmaceuticals differ in the following fundamental ways:

  • Very large molecular weight: Small-molecule drugs typically have a molecular weight below 500 Da; a biopharmaceutical such as a monoclonal antibody (MAb) reaches about 150,000 Da, more than 300 times larger
  • Complex structure: They have primary, secondary, tertiary and quaternary protein structures that are highly sensitive to shear force, temperature and pH
  • Manufactured by living cells: The process involves cell culture and fermentation and cannot be reproduced by conventional organic chemical synthesis routes
  • Different types of contamination: In addition to chemical contamination, the process faces the distinct challenges of microbial contamination and viral contamination

These fundamental differences mean that the facility design principles for small-molecule drugs cannot be applied directly, which is why ISPE developed this dedicated Guide.

Analogy — Kitchen vs. Operating Room

If small-molecule manufacturing is like "cooking" (a clear recipe, reproducible chemical reactions), then biopharmaceutical manufacturing is more like "nurturing a rare living culture." You must control not only temperature and ingredients, but also make sure no foreign microbial "intruders" enter the environment, because once the culture is contaminated, the whole batch is lost. This is why facility design has to be fundamentally different.

Key Point — Closed Systems Are the Core Philosophy of This Guide

The last sentence of the Background is extremely important: "the Guide emphasizes the use of closed systems as an approach to mitigating risks". This is not merely a recommendation; it is the core philosophical premise behind every later design decision in the Guide. Only by understanding why closure matters can you understand the logic of each design choice in the following chapters.

Questions for Reflection

  1. Why is the production cost of biopharmaceuticals so much higher than that of traditional small-molecule drugs? What does this mean for facility design?
  2. What industry trend does the statement "the implementation of closed systems has become routine" indicate? What competitive pressure does this create for a CDMO that still operates a traditional open facility?
1.2 Scope

Original Text

The ISPE Baseline® Guide: Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities (Second Edition) applies to new facilities for the development and manufacture of biopharmaceutical drug substances (or Active Pharmaceutical Ingredients (APIs)). This Guide applies to clinical and commercial cGMP production facilities.

Closed systems and closed processing is the central concept throughout this Guide. Closing a bioprocess impacts area classifications, layouts, and how a facility is operated. This Guide is intended to support the development of decisions which allow compliant and cost effective design of biopharmaceutical manufacturing facilities.

Types of drug substances considered in this Guide include:

  • Protein therapeutics
  • Synthetic proteins
  • Polypeptides
  • Monoclonal antibodies
  • Vaccines1
  • Biopharmaceutical drug substances isolated from tissues or body fluids
  • Transgenic biopharmaceutical drug substances
  • Gene therapy biopharmaceutical drug substances
  • Stem cells

1 Note: additional design criteria could be required for specific classes of biopharmaceutical drug substances (e.g., live vaccines). Concepts in this Guide can apply to various scales of operation from single digit liters to tens of thousands of liters.

The facility designs and processes described in this Guide are intended to acknowledge guidelines, such as ICH Guidance for Industry Q7 GMP for APIs [1], PIC/S Guide to GMP for Medicinal Products Part II [2], and ICH Q11 [3], and meet requirements, such as Eudralex Volume 4 GMP Part II Basic Requirements for Active Substances used as Starting Materials [4], and to US GMPs, e.g., 21 CFR Part 211 [5].

The intended audience for this Guide includes:

  • Professionals involved in the design, construction, qualification, and operation of biopharmaceutical manufacturing facilities
  • Regulatory and quality personnel involved in evaluating technical decisions associated with biopharmaceutical manufacturing facility design

The concepts in this Guide can be applied to other facilities, such as those for blood fractionation, although additional information may be required (e.g., information found in EMA Annex 14 for Blood Fractionation [6] and/or CFDA Annex 4: Blood Products) [7].

This Guide is not intended to be used in the retrofit or evaluation of older facilities; however, the execution of these concepts and strategies can be evaluated and implemented as it benefits production in a major modification to an existing facility.

This Guide is not intended as a replacement for cGMP regulations. This Guide is intended to support options and practices to achieve cGMP compliance.

The processes and facility designs described in this Guide do not apply to filling and/or packaging of sterile drug product.

The appropriate regulatory agencies should be consulted before advancing into detailed design and before starting major construction activities.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept Analysis — Who the Guide Applies To and the Regulatory Framework

Drug Substance (API) vs. Drug Product: This distinction is critical. This Guide covers only Drug Substance manufacturing facilities and does not cover the downstream Fill & Finish operations. Fill & Finish is governed by a different set of requirements, such as EU GMP Annex 1 (Manufacture of Sterile Medicinal Products).

Hierarchy of applicable regulations:

  • US: 21 CFR Part 211 (cGMP for drug products); API manufacturing generally follows ICH Q7
  • EU: EudraLex Vol.4 GMP Part II (requirements for API manufacturing)
  • ICH: Q7 (GMP for APIs), Q11 (development of chemical and biotechnological/biological APIs)
  • PIC/S: Guide to GMP Part II (highly harmonized with EU GMP Part II)

Overview of Product Types — Why This List Matters

The product types covered by this Guide range widely, from proteins to stem cells. The manufacturing environment requirements may differ for each type:

  • Monoclonal antibodies (MAbs): Currently the largest category in the biopharmaceutical market, produced mainly by mammalian cell culture (CHO cells)
  • Vaccines: May require additional biosafety classification considerations (live vaccines in particular are subject to stricter requirements)
  • Gene therapy: An emerging field involving viral vectors (AAV, lentivirus) that require special containment and biosafety design
  • Stem cells: The most complex category, usually produced at small scale in highly controlled environments

Analogy — Process Closure Is Like a Sealed Vacuum-Packing Line

Imagine a food factory. A traditional "open kitchen" lets workers reach in and handle food at any time, but it also gives dust and bacteria in the air a chance to fall into the food. This is the concept of an "Open System."

By contrast, a modern "sealed vacuum-packing line" runs the whole process, from the arrival of ingredients to the shipment of the product, inside enclosed piping, so the outside environment can never get in. This is the concept of a "Closed System."

The core message of the Guide is this: when you can make the process "closed," you can lower the requirements for the surrounding environment (cleanroom classification), because the environment is no longer the main source of risk. This directly affects facility construction cost.

Key Point — Three Important "Not Applicable" Cases

The Guide clearly states three situations that fall outside its scope. These are easy to confuse in practice:

  1. Not applicable to the assessment of existing facility retrofits (but it may be partly adopted if there is major refurbishment)
  2. Does not replace cGMP regulations (regulations take precedence; the Guide is a tool to support compliance)
  3. Not applicable to the filling/packaging of sterile drug products (Fill & Finish falls under Annex 1 and other guidance)

If a CDMO undertakes both Drug Substance manufacturing and Fill & Finish, it must be clear about which regulation or guidance applies to which area of which building.

Practical Application

A CDMO is planning a new facility. The client's pipeline includes a MAb (for cancer treatment) and a novel AAV gene therapy vector. From the scope of this chapter, you need to note the following:

  • Both are within the scope of this Guide (both are biopharmaceutical APIs)
  • AAV falls into the gene therapy category and needs additional biosafety design considerations
  • If your facility will also perform filling, the filling area must be designed with reference to Annex 1 separately
  • Before starting detailed design, you must first consult the regulatory authorities (FDA/EMA)

Questions for Reflection

  1. This Guide expressly excludes Fill & Finish operations. Consider: where is the boundary between the last step of Drug Substance manufacturing (for example, formulation or sterile filtration) and Fill & Finish? What effect does this boundary have on facility design?
  2. This Guide is aligned with both the US GMPs (21 CFR 211) and EU GMP (EudraLex Vol.4). Consider: in actual design, how should a conflict between the two be handled?
1.3 Key Concepts and Terms

Original Text

Several key concepts and terms are defined in this section of the Guide. Definitions for other terms used in this Guide are provided in the Glossary (see Appendix 3). When these terms are used in the context of this Guide, these definitions should be used.

This Guide discusses the concepts of closed processing and the potential impact of closing a biopharmaceutical drug substance manufacturing process on facility design. Critical Process Parameters (CPPs) and Critical Quality Attributes (CQAs) should be understood before defining process closure and other strategies. Critical process conditions should be understood and facility design should include critical design features.

Aseptic Operation

Operations that are devoid of measurable (detectable) bioburden. Aseptic operations generally require sterilization of the environment, equipment and process solutions to achieve the sterile state prior to use. Use of Biosafety Cabinets (BSCs) and laminar flow hoods are useful when aseptic open operations are required.

Axenic State

A culture that includes the intended living organism, but is free of all other contaminating organisms.

Bioburden-Free

A condition where bioburden is not detectable. Bioreactors used in cell culture or fermentation operations should be sterile prior to use for consistent and reproducible performance. Biosafety Cabinets (BSCs), Restricted Access Barriers (RABs) and isolators should provide an environment free of bioburden for consistent and reproducible aseptic operations. Sterile product manufacturing testing and qualification guidelines are by necessity more stringent and prescriptive than those for active pharmaceutical ingredient manufacturing and therefore are not required.

Bioprocessing

Bioprocessing is the attainment of medicinal products from living organisms. Includes all operations used in the manufacture of biologics including upstream processes such as inoculum preparation, cell culture/fermentation processes, harvest and clarification processes such as sedimentation and filtration processes, downstream processing such as chromatography and filtration processes. Bioprocessing also may include other aqueous processes, such as plasma fractionation and chemically synthesized drug manufacturing performed in non-bacteriostatic solvents.

Fill finish operations are not included in this group; however, many of the concepts presented would still apply.

Briefly Exposed

Open processes containing process and/or product components that are rendered closed by means of an appropriate closing process. Examples of briefly exposed processes include open buffer or media preparations where the solution is "briefly" exposed to the environment prior to closing by sterile filtration and/or thermal sanitization. For solution preparation, it is important that the interval between formulation and sanitization be defined and validated.

Briefly exposed operations may be performed in Controlled Not Classified (CNC) (see Chapter 4) or low bioburden environments if measures used to close the process are appropriate to mitigate risk of contamination from the environment. Pre-closure processing and hold periods and conditions must be carefully monitored and validated. The premise is that in-process (in-line) sterile filtration or thermal sterilization is more effective (and more appropriate) than a classified environment in mitigating the risk of contamination from the environment.

Local protected environments may be used for open processes. Examples include BSCs, Unidirectional Down Flow Hoods (UDFHs) or Unidirectional Horizontal Flow Hoods, isolators and RABs. A formal risk assessment (see ICH Q9) is required to fully evaluate the appropriateness and quality of the environment used for bioprocess operations.

Carryover

Contaminants detected in process streams arising from insufficient removal of contaminating components from previous manufacturing steps or batches. Maximum allowable carryover residues should be estimated and meet defined criteria.

Closed System

A process system that is designed and operated such that the product is never exposed to the surrounding environment. Additions to and draws from closed systems must be performed in a completely closed fashion. Sterile filters may be used to provide effective barriers from contaminants in the environment. A system is closed (or isolated from the environment) when the risk of contamination to the product or process cannot be mitigated by housing the operation in a bioburden-free or particulate-free environment.

"In Quality Risk Management (QRM) verification, the environment does not represent a critical aspect of a closed process... In a closed system, the probability of detecting a contaminant from the environment within the processing period is less than the process acceptance criteria."

A closed process is one that has been validated to show that there are sufficient layers of protection to mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment. Examples of closed systems include sterile single use bags supplied with integrated aseptic connection devices.

Critical Quality Attribute (CQA)

Physical, chemical, biological or microbiological properties or characteristics that should be within an appropriate limit, range, or distribution to ensure the desired product quality (ICH Q8(R2)).

Critical Process Parameter (CPP)

A measurable input (input material attribute or operating parameter) or output (process state variable or output material attribute) of a process step that must be controlled to achieve the desired product quality and process consistency.

Crossover (a type of Cross Contamination)

Contamination of a system by components or contaminants found in a neighboring system. Crossover typically occurs with open processes sharing environments.

Functionally Closed

Functionally Closed systems may be opened but are "rendered closed" by a cleaning, sanitization and/or sterilization process that is appropriate or consistent with the process requirements, whether sterile, aseptic or low bioburden. These systems shall remain closed during production within the system. Examples include process vessels that may be cleaned in place (CIP) and steamed in place (SIP) between uses.

Low Bioburden (Operation)

Process where a limited and controlled level of measurable bioburden is acceptable under specific conditions. (e.g., WFI, chromatography operations).

Module

Modules in the biopharmaceutical design context may consist of:

  • Integrated process lines (individual continuous bioprocessing modules)
  • Total integrated facility sections, including building, process, services and utilities elements
  • Process or building services/HVAC/utilities skids or repetitive items
  • Building sectional modules
  • Modular process or building elements

Open Process

A process that is exposed to the environment and therefore requires environmental conditions to mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment. Under QRM verification, the environment within the RABs and/or BSCs represents a critical aspect of the open process. Open sterile and aseptic operations must be performed in an environment where the probability of contamination is acceptably low. Open bioburden-controlled processing may be performed in a Grade 7 or 8 environment as appropriate for the unit operation.

Process Contact Surface

Surfaces of piping, components, equipment or systems that may or may not be exposed to product, but may contain solutions that are potentially exposed to product or product intermediates.

Product Contact Surface

Surfaces of piping, components, equipment or systems that are exposed to product. When systems used in multiple products, batches or process steps are reused, their product contact surfaces require cleaning and sanitization to reduce bioburden and the potential for carryover and crossover.

Sterile

Devoid of life. Zero bioburden. Free of bioburden.

Sterilization

Form of sanitization that results in a bioburden-free (sterile) condition. The different sterilization methods used in bioprocessing generally include thermal sterilization, sterile filtration (0.2 µm or better), chemical sterilization (e.g., VHP, chlorine dioxide, ozone) or irradiation (e.g., gamma, UV) to achieve the sterile state.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept Analysis — Aseptic Operation vs. Axenic State

These two terms are often confused, but there is an important difference:

Aseptic Operation: Focuses on the absence of detectable microbial load in the operating environment and materials. The emphasis is on maintaining sterility throughout the operation, including the combined use of sterilized environments, sterilized equipment and sterilized liquids.

Axenic State: Used specifically in cell and microorganism culture, it means that the culture contains only the intended organism and no other contaminating microorganisms. For example, a CHO cell culture in an axenic state contains only CHO cells, with no bacteria, fungi or other foreign contaminants.

The upstream process of biopharmaceutical manufacturing (cell culture) must maintain an axenic state; this is the basis for product consistency and safety. Once the axenic state is lost (that is, contamination occurs), the entire cell culture batch must be discarded.

Key Concept Analysis — Bioburden-Free vs. Low Bioburden

These two conditions represent different levels of stringency and apply to different stages of the process:

  • Bioburden-Free: No microorganisms at all are detectable. A bioreactor must reach this state (sterilized) before use. It is equivalent to a "sterile" state.
  • Low Bioburden: A limited and controlled presence of microorganisms is allowed. For example, a chromatography purification step cannot inherently be fully sterilized, but administrative and procedural controls keep the bioburden within an acceptable range.

Distinguishing these two concepts correctly is essential for facility design: different areas require different levels of environmental control.

Key Concept Analysis — The Core Difference Between a Closed System and an Open System

This is the most important concept in the whole Guide and deserves a thorough understanding:

Criteria for a Closed System: "the product is never exposed to the surrounding environment" and "it has been validated to demonstrate that contamination risk is adequately controlled." The key point is that the environment is not a critical factor for a closed system, which means a closed system can be placed in a lower-grade cleanroom or even in a non-classified area.

Open Process: The product is directly exposed to the environment, so environmental quality (cleanroom classification, microbial control) becomes a critical control factor.

Functionally Closed: An important concept that sits between the two. The system can physically be opened (for example, equipment that is reused after CIP/SIP), but appropriate cleaning, sanitization or sterilization procedures make it "equivalent to closed."

Analogy — Open Kitchen vs. Sealed Vacuum-Packing Line

Imagine two different food-processing scenarios:

Open kitchen (Open System): Cooks cut and cook in an open space with the food exposed to the air. To keep the food safe, the kitchen must maintain a high level of tidiness, temperature and humidity control, and personnel hygiene. This is like an open process, which needs a strictly controlled cleanroom environment.

Sealed vacuum-packing line (Closed System): From the arrival of raw materials to the output of finished goods, the whole process takes place in enclosed piping and containers with no exposure points. The production hall does not need to be a cleanroom, because the product never touches the environment. This is the logic of a closed system.

Key to design decisions: A closed system lets you locate the facility in a space with a lower environmental grade, saving a large amount of cleanroom construction cost. This is the business logic behind the emphasis ISPE Vol.6 places on "process closure."

Conceptual Framework — Determinants of Contamination Risk

Contamination risk = f(degree of process openness, environmental quality, duration)

Closed system: degree of process openness → 0
  → environmental quality becomes less important
  → a lower-grade cleanroom can be used (CNC or Grade 7/8)
  → facility construction cost is reduced

Open system: degree of process openness > 0
  → environmental quality becomes a critical control point
  → a higher-grade cleanroom is required (Grade 5/6/7)
  → facility construction cost is higher

QRM validation principle:
  Closed system → environment is not a critical factor (no-impact)
  Open system → environment is a critical factor (direct/indirect impact)

Key Point — CPPs and CQAs Are the Prerequisite for a Process Closure Strategy

The Guide stresses that before defining a "process closure" strategy, you must first thoroughly understand the CPPs (critical process parameters) and CQAs (critical quality attributes). The sequence is very important:

  1. First define the CQAs: what are the critical quality attributes of this product?
  2. Then identify the CPPs: which process parameters directly affect the CQAs?
  3. Then assess contamination risk: how would environmental contamination affect the CPPs and CQAs?
  4. Finally decide the closure strategy: which steps need to be closed, and what level of closure is required?

This chain of logic embodies the essence of QbD (Quality by Design): design decisions must be based on scientific understanding.

Key Point — The Importance of "Briefly Exposed"

"Briefly Exposed" is an intermediate-state concept that is extremely important in practice. When buffers or media are prepared, the solution is unavoidably exposed to the environment for a short time, but the process is then "closed" by sterile filtration or heat sterilization.

Key design requirement: the time interval from preparation to closure must be defined and validated. The length of this time window directly affects what grade of preparation environment you need (for example, whether a cleanroom is required) and how this step is monitored and validated.

Important insight: the Guide states that in-line sterile filtration or heat sterilization controls contamination risk more effectively than the cleanliness grade of the facility. This is a counterintuitive but important design principle.

Practical Application — Crossover Risk in a Multi-Product CDMO Facility

For a CDMO that handles different products for multiple clients, Crossover (a form of cross-contamination) is a major risk. By definition, Crossover occurs when open processes share an environment, or when the closure integrity of a system is compromised.

Design strategies to prevent Crossover:

  • Best: use closed systems wherever possible, physically eliminating the possibility of cross-contamination
  • Next best: assign separate cleanrooms or facility zones to different products
  • Verification: even for closed systems, the integrity of the closure must be validated to confirm there is no leakage

Questions for Reflection

  1. After a production batch, a bioreactor undergoes CIP cleaning and SIP steam sterilization before it is used for the next batch. Is this system "Closed," "Functionally Closed" or "Open"? Why?
  2. If a chromatography step requires connecting lines and sampling during operation, does that make it an "Open Process"? How can it be brought to the "Functionally Closed" standard?
  3. What is the fundamental difference between "Low Bioburden" and "Sterile"? In process design, when is Low Bioburden acceptable and when must Sterile be achieved?
1.4 Guide Structure

Original Text

1.4.1 Synopsis of Guide Chapters

Chapter 2: Regulatory
Chapter 2 provides an overview of the regulatory requirements for facility design and summarizes the sources that influence both regulators and industry best practices.

Chapter 3: Risk Management
Chapter 3 provides an overview of several approaches to risk management that may be applied to biopharmaceutical facilities.

Chapter 4: Process Closure
Closing a bioprocess impacts area classifications, layouts, and how a facility is operated. This chapter helps to define what a closed process is, and through the use of risk assessment tools, indicate how closure can be demonstrated. Examples of typical closed processes are given, as well as how some open processes can be closed.

Chapter 5: Operations
This chapter describes trends in the operation of biopharmaceutical facilities. Facilities are operating under diverse requirements — often they are multi-product facilities requiring short product campaigns and efficient and thorough changeover between products. Higher titers and products focused on smaller markets allow for reduced scale and increased use of single-use technology.

Chapter 6: Facility Layouts
Chapter 6 provides guidelines and discussions for the design of integrated cGMP facilities. This is in the context of regulatory, process, production, and technological factors that significantly influence the layout and flow of a production facility.

Chapter 7: Architectural Design
As the biopharmaceutical industry matures, there is an emerging pattern to many of the processes, equipment, infrastructure, and facilities. The use of modular process skid and facility concepts allows for faster and less costly projects. Chapter 7 discusses master planning which continues to be important as organizations plan their future and allow for growth opportunities against changing product portfolios and market demands.

Chapter 8: Mechanical and Electrical Systems
This chapter reviews requirements with a focus on the unique balancing of risks required for biopharmaceutical and vaccine facilities. The chapter discusses how mechanical systems are focused on environmental control to manage the conflicting risks of general product contamination, cross-contamination, biosafety, and biosecurity. The chapter also discusses how electrical systems can further support the mitigation of risk through support of mechanical systems in emergency situations.

Chapter 9: Sustainability
Carbon footprint, green, environmentally friendly, LEED, and BREEAM are now familiar terms, but this is still a changing area with new requirements and legislation across the globe. This chapter provides background and links to some of the latest trends in this area and how they affect biopharmaceutical thinking.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept Analysis — The Logical Relationships Between Chapters

The chapter structure of the Guide is not arbitrary; it follows a logical progression from "basic principles" to "specific design":

  • Chapter 1 (Introduction): Establishes the conceptual framework — why are different facilities needed, and what are the key terms?
  • Chapter 2 (Regulatory): Establishes compliance obligations — which requirements must be met?
  • Chapter 3 (Risk Management): Provides decision tools — how are risks assessed and quantified?
  • Chapter 4 (Process Closure): The core design philosophy — how do you decide which processes need to be closed?
  • Chapter 5 (Operations): The application context — how do modern biopharmaceutical facilities operate?
  • Chapter 6 (Facility Layout): Physical design — how are all the factors integrated into the facility layout?
  • Chapters 7-8 (Architectural/MEP): Engineering detail — how are the specific building and equipment systems designed?
  • Chapter 9 (Sustainability): Forward-looking thinking — how are environmental requirements balanced?

Analogy — Building a Biopharmaceutical Plant Is Like Building a Hospital

You would not start designing a hospital floor plan without understanding patient needs (CQAs) and clinical workflows (the process). You first need to know what type of patients will be served, what infection control requirements apply, and how to separate clean and contaminated flows. The chapter order of this Guide follows the same "from requirements to design" logic.

Key Point — Chapters 3 and 4 Are the Core

For facility design engineers and quality professionals, Chapter 3 (Risk Management) and Chapter 4 (Process Closure) are the two most important chapters. They answer the most critical questions:

  • Chapter 3: How do you systematically assess contamination risk? (Tools: FMEA, HAZOP, HACCP, ICH Q9)
  • Chapter 4: Based on the risk assessment results, how do you decide which steps need to be closed, and what are the corresponding environmental classifications?

The conclusions of these two chapters directly determine how much ISO 5/6/7/8 cleanroom space your facility needs, and therefore directly affect facility construction cost.

Key Point — What the Trends in Chapter 5 Mean for a CDMO

Chapter 5 describes several important industry trends:

  • Multi-product facilities: To reduce cost, more and more plants need to manufacture different clients' products in the same facility, which places very high demands on cross-contamination prevention design
  • Higher titers: Process improvements allow more product to be made per batch, which means a smaller facility can serve more patients
  • Single-use technology: Helps reduce cross-contamination risk and the cleaning validation workload

Practical Application — How to Use This Guide as a Decision Tool

In an actual facility design project, we recommend using the Guide in the following sequence:

  1. Chapter 1: Confirm whether your product type is within scope
  2. Chapter 2: Identify the applicable regulatory requirements (which market? FDA/EMA?)
  3. Chapter 3: Select an appropriate risk assessment method (FMEA/HAZOP/HACCP)
  4. Chapter 4: Based on the risk assessment, decide which processes need to be closed and determine the environmental classifications
  5. Chapter 6: Design the facility layout and flows according to the process closure strategy
  6. Chapters 7-8: Detailed architectural and MEP design

Questions for Reflection

  1. If a CDMO has both early clinical (Phase 1/2) and commercial manufacturing business, what does the trend of "product-focused patient populations" mentioned in Chapter 5 of the Guide imply? What flexibility does it require of the facility design?
  2. Chapter 9 discusses sustainability (LEED, BREEAM). In biopharmaceutical facility design, cleanrooms consume a great deal of energy (large volumes of HVAC air changes). How can a process closure strategy help reduce energy consumption while meeting sustainability goals?

Chapter Summary

  • The fundamental differences of biopharmaceuticals mean that facility design must follow different principles: manufacture by living cells, large molecular size and high sensitivity to contamination require tighter process control than traditional small-molecule drugs
  • Process closure is the core philosophy of this Guide: Closed systems reduce reliance on the surrounding environment, lowering facility construction and operating costs without sacrificing product quality
  • Six key terms form the basis for understanding the whole Guide: Aseptic Operation, Axenic State, Bioburden-Free, Closed System, Functionally Closed and Open Process
  • This Guide applies to Drug Substance manufacturing facilities and not to Fill & Finish; it is aligned with the major regulations, including 21 CFR Part 211 (US) and EudraLex Vol.4 GMP Part II (EU)
  • Understanding CPPs and CQAs is the prerequisite for process closure decisions: you must first know what matters most to product quality before you can scientifically decide which process steps need closure protection
  • The chapters follow a logical hierarchy: from regulatory foundation → risk assessment tools → process closure strategy → facility layout → engineering detail, with each layer building on the one before
ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p25 – p31

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the central role of risk management in biopharmaceutical manufacturing, and why it is the foundation of facility design rather than an add-on tool
  • Master the definition of risk: risk = probability × severity × detectability, and apply it to facility design decisions
  • Understand the core philosophy of the FDA's 2004 report "Pharmaceutical CGMPs for the 21st Century: A Risk-Based Approach" and its influence on modern biopharmaceutical facility design
  • Gain an in-depth understanding of the five main risk assessment tools (PHA, FMEA, FTA, HAZOPS, HACCP) and judge when each is appropriate
  • Master the logic of applying the seven HACCP principles to biopharmaceutical processes, and link them to the design decisions in Chapter 4 (process containment) and Chapter 6 (cGMP layout)
3.1 Introduction

Original Text

The goal of this chapter is to provide a method for evaluating the risks of cross contamination and contamination from the environment during manufacturing of bulk drug substance, specifically during bioprocessing. For further information see the ISPE Guide: Biopharmaceutical Process Development and Manufacturing [8].

Process definition, management, and design of appropriate controls should be commensurate with risk and based on science and process understanding. According to Good Documentation Practice (GDP), versioning the level of understanding is recommended as additional knowledge is attained and incorporated via process optimization over time.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - The Two Goals of This Chapter

The introductory paragraph of Chapter 3 is short, but it makes two key points clear:

Goal 1: Focus on two sources of contamination

  • Cross contamination: contaminants from other products, batches or steps entering the current process
  • Contamination from environment: contamination from the surrounding facility environment (air, surfaces, personnel)

These two sources directly determine your facility design requirements: if you can close the process, the risk of environmental contamination drops sharply; if you work in a multi-product facility, cross contamination is the primary concern.

Goal 2: Science-based design decisions

Control measures should be "commensurate" with the risk - this is the core principle of ICH Q9 Quality Risk Management (QRM). Do not over-engineer (a waste of resources), and do not under-engineer (a threat to quality).

Key Point - "Versioning" of GDP and Process Understanding

The guide mentions the concept of "versioning" under Good Documentation Practice (GDP), which is deeper than it first appears:

Understanding of a biological process accumulates over time - understanding at Phase 1 clinical is far less than at the commercial stage. The guide recommends that risk assessment documents be updated and versioned as process understanding deepens. This principle corresponds directly to the "Continual Improvement" concept in the ICH Q10 pharmaceutical quality system.

For a CDMO, this means that when taking over a technology transfer from a new client, early risk assessments may need to be re-evaluated and updated once the process is fully understood.

Analogy - Risk Management Is Like a Preflight Safety Check

Pilots must carry out a systematic safety check (preflight checklist) before every flight, even on an aircraft that has flown a thousand times. It is not because they expect something to go wrong, but because systematic risk identification is the only reliable way to prevent accidents.

Biopharmaceutical risk management works the same way: you cannot rely on the rule of thumb "we have always done it this way without problems"; you need a systematic method to identify, assess and control every risk point. This is the core spirit of the FDA's 2004 report.

Discussion Questions

  1. How does the principle "control measures should be commensurate with the risk" prevent over-design? Give an example of possible "over-design" in biopharmaceutical facility design, and how a risk assessment tool can avoid it.
  2. This section mentions two types of contamination. In a closed-system process, which type still needs attention? Why can't closure eliminate all contamination risk?
3.2 Risk Management

Original Text

High risk is associated with changes to drug manufacturing. Any changes in drug composition or quality could result in changes in efficacy or safety. Good product and process understanding is required to fully evaluate the impact of any manufacturing deviation.

In 2004, the FDA expanded the use of risk management into the biopharmaceutical industry. In their report, "Pharmaceutical cGMPs in the 21st Century — A Risk Based Approach" [41], the FDA describes how it will use risk management for evaluating manufacturing quality. Process and facility audits and evaluations by the US FDA are based on science. Risk assessments should be incorporated into Corrective Action and Preventative Action (CAPA) programs. Procedures should be developed for assessing risk and to understand the actions that need to be taken for different levels of risk.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - The Historical Significance of the FDA's 2004 Report

The FDA's 2004 report "Pharmaceutical CGMPs for the 21st Century: A Risk-Based Approach" was an important turning point in pharmaceutical regulatory history. Before it, FDA inspections leaned toward the "prescriptive" - checking compliance clause by clause against the regulations.

The report opened a new era of "science and risk-based" inspection, with these core claims:

  • Pharmaceutical manufacturers should understand their own processes, not merely follow the rules
  • FDA investigators should focus on real quality risks, not just document format
  • New technologies (such as PAT - process analytical technology) should be encouraged, not blocked simply because they involve "change"
  • Continuous improvement should be a normal part of manufacturing culture

This report directly led to ICH Q8, Q9 and Q10 (the quality trilogy), and laid the foundation of modern QbD methodology.

Key Point - Why Changes in Drug Manufacturing Carry High Risk

The guide opens by stressing that "manufacturing changes carry high risk", which matters especially for biopharmaceuticals because:

  • The process is the product: for a small-molecule drug, analysis can confirm that the structure is unchanged; for a biopharmaceutical, protein folding and glycosylation patterns can change with minor process changes, and those changes may affect safety or efficacy
  • The "structure" is hard to characterize fully: a 150 kDa MAb molecule is far more complex than a 500 Da small molecule and cannot be fully characterized by simple chemical analysis
  • The importance of CAPA: whenever a deviation occurs, the CAPA (corrective and preventive action) must include a risk assessment of whether the deviation could affect product quality

Discussion Questions

  1. How does the FDA's "science-based inspection" differ in practice from traditional "prescriptive inspection"? Which approach is better for innovation?
  2. If a CDMO wants to change its bioreactor supplier (same specification, different brand), does this count as a "manufacturing change"? What level of risk assessment is needed?
3.2.1 Purpose of Risk Management

Original Text

Risk is a product of the probability (likelihood) and impact (severity) of a potential hazard. The ability to detect a hazard, failure or risk is also an aspect of risk management. Sound process understanding, including understanding of critical control and CQAs of products, processes, raw materials, equipment, facilities, personnel and services is required.

Defining these attributes includes identification of potential risks and an understanding of how to control and monitor these risks, thereby mitigating potential hazards or risk to product quality. The overall goal is to pro-actively mitigate the risk of product adulteration or ultimately risk to patient.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - The Three Dimensions of Risk

The risk concept defined in the guide is consistent with ICH Q9, but it implies three dimensions (the basis of FMEA):

Risk = a function of probability × severity × detectability

  • Probability/Likelihood: how likely is the hazard to occur? (frequency, historical data)
  • Severity/Impact: if it occurs, how serious is the impact on the patient or product quality?
  • Detectability: if it occurs, can we detect it before it causes harm?

Together these three dimensions form the Risk Priority Number (RPN) = S × O × D in FMEA (severity × occurrence × detectability).

Formula and Framework - Risk Priority Number (RPN)

FMEA risk assessment formula:
RPN = Severity (S) × Occurrence (O) × Detection (D)

Scoring example (each dimension scored 1-10):
S: Severity 1 = negligible, 10 = fatal
O: Occurrence 1 = very rare, 10 = common
D: Detectability 1 = easy to detect, 10 = almost undetectable

Example: cell culture contamination risk
S = 9 (contamination causes batch loss, seriously affecting patient supply)
O = 3 (protected by a closed system, low occurrence)
D = 4 (online monitoring allows rapid detection)
RPN = 9 × 3 × 4 = 108

High RPN → additional control measures needed
Low RPN → existing control measures are sufficient

How facility design affects RPN:
- Adopt a closed system → lowers O (occurrence)
- Install online pH/DO monitoring → lowers D (detection score)
- Use multiple barriers → lowers both O and D

Key Point - "Risk Ultimately Comes Down to the Patient" Is the Highest Design Principle

The guide stresses that the ultimate goal of risk management is to "pro-actively mitigate the risk... to patient". Every biopharmaceutical facility designer should keep this sentence in mind.

This corresponds to the design decision hierarchy we described in PROMPT.md:

1. Sterility assurance / patient safety
▼
2. Product CQAs (quality attributes)
▼
3. Commercial flexibility and cost-effectiveness

When a design choice conflicts with patient safety, safety always comes first. The role of risk management tools is to give this order of priority a scientific basis rather than leaving it to subjective judgment.

Discussion Questions

  1. If a cell culture step becomes contaminated, the RPN is very high. There are two improvement options: A) upgrade the environment to a higher cleanliness grade; B) convert the open process to a closed system. Analyze the difference between the two options in terms of which RPN dimension each reduces.
  2. "Detectability" is the most often overlooked dimension of the RPN. In facility design, what specific measures can improve the detectability of potential contamination? (Hint: monitoring systems, sampling point design)
3.2.2 Complying with the FDA's Commitment to Process Improvement and Science-Based Compliance

Original Text

In the FDA report, "Pharmaceutical cGMPs for the 21st Century — A Risk Based Approach," dated September 2004 [41], the FDA states its commitment to process improvement and science based compliance:

"The FDA has committed to the adoption of risk management principles that will enhance the Agency's inspection and enforcement program, which is focused on protecting the public health. FDA has identified efficient risk management as a key element. Efficient risk management requires using the best scientific data, developing quality standards, and using efficient systems and practices that provide clear and consistent decisions and communications. FDA has identified efficient risk management as the primary way to address these challenges. This approach incorporates rigorous analysis to consistently identify the most important risks, and the use of a quality systems approach to designing, conducting, and evaluating FDA core business processes. CDER is implementing a new risk-based pharmaceutical quality assessment system. The system should encourage manufacturers to implement new technologies, such as process analytical technology (PAT), and facilitate continuous manufacturing improvements. As pharmaceutical manufacturing evolves from an art to a science and engineering based activity, application of this enhanced science and engineering knowledge in regulatory decision-making, establishment of specifications, and evaluation of manufacturing processes should improve the efficiency and effectiveness of both manufacturing and regulatory decision-making. Using a scientific framework to find ways of mitigating risk while facilitating continuous improvement and innovation in pharmaceutical manufacturing is a key public health objective. This new system will encourage the implementation of new technologies. Quality and productivity improvement share a common element: reduction in variability through process understanding. Reducing variability provides a win-win opportunity from both public health and industry perspectives."

Based on this statement by the FDA, the key to successful implementation of closed processing is the demonstration, validation, and confirmation that risk of process contamination by environmental hazards has been adequately mitigated. A formal and systematic risk assessment should provide the data to confirm when an environment is appropriate for a process unit operation.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Four Key Messages in the FDA Statement

Reading the FDA statement carefully, we can extract four messages that are critical to biopharmaceutical facility design:

  1. "Encouraging new technology": the FDA explicitly supports PAT (process analytical technology) and continuous improvement. This means facility design should leave flexibility for future technology upgrades (for example, designing installation interfaces for PAT sensors)
  2. "Manufacturing is science, not art": pharmaceutical manufacturing was traditionally seen as an art of "following the recipe". The FDA requires a shift to an engineering activity based on scientific understanding. This calls for deeper process characterization and documentation
  3. "Reducing variability is a shared goal of quality and productivity": "quality" and "efficiency", often seen as opposed in the past, find common ground in the risk management framework - reducing process variability both improves quality and reduces waste and rework, lowering cost
  4. "A quality systems approach": not isolated SOPs, but an integrated quality management system (ICH Q10)

Analogy - Choosing a Kitchen Design Based on Ingredient Sensitivity

Imagine you are a Michelin-starred chef designing a kitchen that must handle both sashimi (highly sensitive, needing strict temperature control and separate cutting boards) and grilled meat (which can be handled in a high-heat environment).

You would not bring the whole kitchen up to the highest hygiene standard for sashimi - that would be too expensive. Nor would you let sashimi and grilled meat share a cutting board - that would be too dangerous.

The right approach is to design work areas and control measures according to the sensitivity (risk level) of each ingredient. This is exactly the logic of biopharmaceutical facility design: based on the risk assessment result of each process step, decide the corresponding environmental grade and control strategy.

Closed system = sealing the ingredient completely in vacuum packaging, so it is unaffected by the surrounding kitchen environment.

Key Point - Three Verification Requirements for Closed Processes

The concluding paragraph of the guide is very important; it indicates that successful implementation of a closed process depends on confirmation at three levels:

  1. Demonstration: at the design/development stage, show by experiment or engineering analysis that the system is closed in concept
  2. Validation: a formal validation program proving that the closed system consistently maintains closure under the specified operating conditions
  3. Confirmation: ongoing routine monitoring and periodic reconfirmation to ensure the system has not degraded

These three levels correspond to three stages of the facility lifecycle: design → construction/commissioning → routine operation. Each stage needs the support of risk management documentation.

Practical Application - What PAT Means in Biopharmaceutical Facility Design

The PAT (process analytical technology) mentioned in the FDA's 2004 report is now quite mature. For a biopharmaceutical CDMO, facility design should consider:

  • Online monitoring points: install online sensors for pH, dissolved oxygen (DO), carbon dioxide and biomass in cell culture bioreactors
  • Real-time release (RTRT): replace end-of-batch sampling and testing with continuous process data, which requires the facility design to reserve interfaces for data acquisition and analysis systems
  • Automation and digitalization: facility design should plan the cabling and network infrastructure for future system integration (such as MES manufacturing execution systems and DCS distributed control systems)

PAT is not just an analytical technology; it requires facility design to consider, from the start, the full chain of "how to monitor, how to transmit data, how to make decisions".

Discussion Questions

  1. The FDA says "reducing variability is a shared goal of quality and productivity". Give an example of a facility design decision that both reduces quality risk and improves production efficiency.
  2. A closed process must be "validated" to be considered closed. If your closed system includes a single-use bag, which tests should the validation include? (Hint: integrity testing, extractables/leachables)
3.2.3 Methods and Approaches to Assessing Risk

Original Text

Methods and approaches have been developed and used for assessing risks in a biopharmaceutical manufacturing program include:

  • Process/Preliminary Hazard Analysis (PHA)
  • Failure Mode and Effects Analysis (FMEA)
  • Fault Tree Analysis (FTA)
  • Hazard and Operability Studies (HAZOPS)
  • Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Points (HACCP)

Process/Preliminary Hazard Analysis (PHA)

The Process/Preliminary Hazard Analysis (PHA) tool is typically used early during the planning stages of a project. It is a relatively unstructured/improvisational program that can be adapted to other projects. It uses of a "what-if" list of questions looking at potential hazards and their impact. Brainstorming sessions with subject SMEs familiar with the process or system under review are normally used. Preliminary hazard analysis usually involves:

  • Listing of the hypothetical hazards
  • Identifying established safeguards
  • Estimating the severity of the hazard and the likelihood that it will occur
  • Prioritizing risks that need to be further mitigated

Failure Mode and Effects Analysis (FMEA)

FMEA is a structured program that can be adapted to risk evaluation of complex systems or scaled down to evaluate a small component. FMEA considers failure modes one at a time and is less effective for evaluation of interactions between systems. In addition, it does not consider personnel operational issues. The execution of an FMEA begins with listing of the hypothetical hazards or failure modes. The severity of the hazard and the likelihood that it will occur and the ability to detect the failure mode are then estimated. The risks that need to be further mitigated are prioritized.

Fault Tree Analysis (FTA)

Fault Tree Analysis (FTA) is a structured platform used to define a particular event and identify its causes. The output of the FTA is a fault tree that identifies the "gates" to a fault or potential hazard. Since FTA looks at only one failure at a time, FTA of a process or large system can be very large and complex. FTA can be most effective when it is used to identify the root cause(s) of a particular event (e.g., contamination of a bioreactor).

An FTA begins with identifying and defining the problem. A fault tree is then constructed with the event listed at the top of the tree. The "basic events" or assignable root causes that can lead to the top event are graphically linked with "intermediate events and gates" to show the connection between the top event and the root causes. The fault tree is then analyzed or "solved" by identifying all of the combinations of events that can cause the top event to occur.

Hazard and Operability Studies (HAZOPS)

HAZOPS is a highly structured platform used to perform a detailed risk assessment. It considers hazards that are related to: Materials, Equipment, Personnel, Operations, and Facilities.

It is a systematic process relying on SMEs from various disciplines to provide a variety of points of view. HAZOPS begin with system or scope definition. Typically, a PFD or P&ID will serve as the basis of the study. Specific study "nodes" are identified (e.g., process parameters or unit operations). Using pre-determined guidewords, the study nodes are evaluated. Hazards or potential deviations are identified and the severity and likelihood of occurrence are considered. Consequences of the deviation are listed. Safeguards that are already established are identified to see if these mitigate the risk adequately. Those risks that are unacceptable and need to be controlled are ranked and prioritized.

Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Points (HACCP)

Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Points (HACCP) is a platform that was optimized for the food industry. It is a preventative system of risk control. In 2003, the World Health Organization (WHO) included Annex 7 "Application of HACCP methodology to pharmaceuticals" in its "Specifications for Pharmaceuticals" report [43]. HACCP is also a risk management process that formalizes the functions of risk control and mitigation.

HACCP evaluates aspects of a process that are critical to quality, comply with regulatory requirements, or are important for product or patient safety. A substantial amount of information on the product, its intermediates, and the manufacturing process is required to perform a HACCP risk assessment. When used, the HACCP is intended to be an integral part of the GMP documentation package.

There are seven HACCP principles to follow during a formal HACCP process:

1 Hazard Analysis: A cross-functional team of SMEs is needed. Stakeholders representing manufacturing, engineering, R&D, validation, quality and corporate (financial) groups who understand the risks and implications of identified risks from all perspectives. The team first assembles all pertinent background information including: Process Flow Diagrams (PFDs), Piping and Instrument Diagrams (P&IDs), Batch records, Commissioning and qualification protocols, Process monitoring data, Technology transfer packages. The team defines the product and its distribution. Intermediate and final product specifications are defined, listing the known CQAs for manufacturing, storage, preparation. A PFD describing the process is required. Raw materials, equipment used, services required, intermediate specifications should be detailed. Using a documentation package, the SMEs identify potential hazards. The team determines whether the hazard needs to be controlled (based on consequence/severity and likelihood).
2 Determine the Critical Control Points (CCPs). A CCP is where a control must be applied to prevent a hazard or to control a hazard to an acceptable level.
3 Establish the critical limits. Critical limits are the maximum/minimum values to which a parameter must be controlled to mitigate the risk of a hazard to an acceptable level.
4 Establish monitoring procedures. Monitoring procedures define the entire monitoring program. The procedures need to define what is to be monitored, how it is to be monitored, and how frequently. The procedures also define who is responsible. Monitoring should help ensure that control is maintained.
5 Establish corrective actions. Corrective action plans define the steps that will be taken when a deviation from a critical control limit occurs. This includes documentation of the deviation, disposition of the non-compliant product, and a preventative action strategy to mitigate re-occurrence of the deviation.
6 Establish verification procedures. The verification procedures should confirm that the HACCP system is being implemented according to plan and is effective in controlling the hazards that were identified. Verification is also used to confirm that the HACCP was comprehensive and performed properly.
7 Establish record keeping and documentation procedures. HACCP requires that a HACCP plan is established. The plan includes documentation of the hazards analysis, identification of CCPs and control limits as well as documentation of pertinent procedures and rationale. The Plan is a living document that requires updating as the operation evolves.

The evaluation of potential hazards to a bioprocess may be performed using one or multiple platforms listed above. The development of a hybrid platform also may be used successfully. The expectation is that a systematic approach is used for risk assessment, including pre-established criteria for evaluating the likelihood and severity of the potential hazards. Risk assessments should help to improve the understanding of the bioprocesses and potential hazards in the manufacture of biopharmaceuticals.

Tutorial Commentary

Analogy - Risk Tools Are Like a Toolbox of Different Functions

If facility design is like building a house, the five risk tools are like the different tools in a toolbox:

  • PHA = site survey tool: before construction, quickly assess what problems the plot has (soil, sunlight, drainage) and decide which need deeper investigation
  • FMEA = building materials quality checklist: assess the possible failure modes of each material one by one (will the rebar rust? is the concrete strong enough?) and calculate the severity of each problem
  • FTA = root cause analysis after something goes wrong: if a crack appears in the wall, trace backwards through all the possible causes of the crack
  • HAZOPS = plumbing and electrical piping review: review each pipe section one by one, asking "what happens if the pressure is too high/too low/the flow is reversed?"
  • HACCP = kitchen food safety plan: find the critical control points (such as meat cooking temperature), set standards, and establish monitoring procedures

In-Depth Analysis - Applying HACCP to Biopharmaceutical Facility Design

HACCP comes from the food industry, but its logic fits biopharmaceutical processes very well. Let us illustrate the application of the 7 principles with a bioreactor culture step:

  • Principle 1 Hazard analysis: identify potential hazards in cell culture (microbial contamination, viral contamination, accumulation of metabolic by-products, etc.), and use the PFD to analyze each input and output step by step
  • Principle 2 Determine CCPs: for example, the bioreactor inoculation point is a CCP (inoculation must be carried out under sterile conditions, and any error could cause loss of the whole batch)
  • Principle 3 Establish critical limits: for example, inoculation must be performed in an ISO 5 environment or under closed-system conditions; the bioreactor pH before inoculation must be within 6.8-7.2
  • Principle 4 Monitoring procedures: establish online pH/DO/biomass monitoring, and take samples regularly for microbial limit testing
  • Principle 5 Corrective actions: if pH goes out of range, immediately trigger acid/base addition; if microbial contamination is found, terminate the batch and start a root cause investigation
  • Principle 6 Verification procedures: periodically verify the accuracy of the monitoring system and review the effectiveness of the HACCP plan
  • Principle 7 Documentation: all monitoring data, deviations and corrective actions must be fully recorded in the batch record

Key Point - Links Between This Chapter and Chapters 4 and 6

The risk assessment tools in Chapter 3 are the "driving engine" of the design decisions in later chapters:

  • Link to Chapter 4 (process containment): the results of FMEA/HACCP tell you which process steps carry a high risk of environmental contamination → these steps need closure or stricter environmental control. Conversely, steps that are already closed can have lower environmental grade requirements.
  • Link to Chapter 6 (cGMP facility layout): HAZOPS is usually based on the P&ID → to do a HAZOPS you first need the process design. The facility layout must support the monitoring requirements of the CCPs identified in HACCP (for example, sampling ports need to be designed near a CCP).

Understanding this chain explains why the chapters of ISPE Vol.6 run in the order: regulation → risk management → process containment → facility layout. It is a logical flow from analysis to design, not an arbitrary arrangement.

Comparison Framework - The Roles of FMEA vs. HACCP in Facility Design

FMEA - best suited to equipment and system design assessment
Input: equipment list + process steps
Output: RPN ranking table + high-risk points needing improvement

Application examples:
- Assess failure modes of a sterilizing filter membrane
- Assess failure risks of the bioreactor agitation system
- Assess the design adequacy of CIP/SIP systems

HACCP - best suited to process control strategy design
Input: PFD (process flow diagram) + hazard identification
Output: CCP list + critical limits + monitoring plan

Application examples:
- Determine the inoculation point as a CCP and set sterile condition requirements
- Determine the virus inactivation step as a CCP and set pH/temperature limits
- Determine the final filtration as a CCP and set a pre-filtration bioburden limit

Combined use: first scan quickly with PHA → then analyze equipment in detail with FMEA → then build the process control system with HACCP

Practical Application - Risk Assessment Workflow for a CDMO Taking On a New Product

When a CDMO takes on a technology transfer for monoclonal antibody (MAb) production from a new client, the recommended practical risk assessment workflow is:

  1. Step 1 - PHA (project initiation): hold a brainstorming session with the client's technical team to quickly identify the main risks of this specific molecule (for example: high tendency to form aggregates? particularly sensitive to shear? special viral clearance requirements?)
  2. Step 2 - FMEA (detailed design): for the planned equipment (bioreactors, chromatography equipment, filtration systems), assess the risk of equipment failure one by one, calculate the RPN, and decide whether equipment upgrades or redundant designs are needed
  3. Step 3 - HAZOPS (P&ID review): systematically review the designed piping systems to ensure that all possible operating deviations have been considered and have corresponding safety design
  4. Step 4 - HACCP (after the process is locked): establish a complete HACCP plan, identify all CCPs, set critical limits, and establish monitoring and corrective procedures as the basis for batch record design

Discussion Questions

  1. FMEA "does not consider interactions between systems". In a multi-product biopharmaceutical facility, what risks might this limitation cause to be underestimated? Which tool is needed to make up for this gap?
  2. Step 5 of HACCP is "establish corrective actions". If the pH of a bioreactor goes beyond the critical limit, what steps should the corrective action include? (Consider: immediate action, batch disposition, root cause investigation, preventive action)
  3. The guide encourages using a "hybrid platform" for risk assessment. In what situations do you think the combination of FMEA + HACCP is especially effective? Give a specific facility design application scenario.

Core Concept - Comparing the Characteristics of the Five Risk Assessment Tools

Each of these five tools has its own best-fit scenarios, and choosing the right tool is critical:

Chapter Summary

  • Risk management is the scientific basis of facility design: quality risk management (QRM) ensures control measures are commensurate with the risk, avoiding over-design or under-design; all design decisions need a scientific basis
  • The three-dimension risk framework: risk = probability × severity × detectability (corresponding to O × S × D = RPN in FMEA); facility design measures can reduce one or more of the three dimensions
  • The FDA's 2004 report laid the foundation of modern regulation: science-based inspection, encouragement of new technology (PAT), compliance driven by process understanding, and reducing variability as a shared goal of quality and productivity
  • Compliance requirements for closed processes have three levels: Demonstration → Validation → Confirmation, supported by documented risk assessment
  • Each of the five risk tools has its strengths: PHA (early scan) → FMEA (equipment design) → FTA (root cause analysis) → HAZOPS (P&ID review) → HACCP (process control strategy); they can be combined
  • The seven HACCP principles are fully applicable to biopharmaceuticals: identify hazards → determine CCPs → set limits → establish monitoring → define corrective actions → verify → document; this framework forms the backbone of batch records and process control SOPs
  • This chapter links to Chapters 4 and 6: risk assessment results → determine the process containment strategy (Chapter 4) → determine facility environmental classification and layout (Chapter 6); the three chapters form a complete logical chain of design decisions

Chapter 2: Regulatory

p15-p25

ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p15 - p25

Chapter Learning Objectives

  • Understand the requirements that the major global regulators (FDA, EMA, PIC/S, WHO, CFDA) place on biopharmaceutical manufacturing, and be able to compare how the frameworks are alike and different
  • Grasp the fundamental difference between biosafety "containment" and cleanroom environmental "classification", so that these two commonly misunderstood concepts are not confused
  • Understand how "closed processing" changes the way facilities are designed: from protecting the product with the facility to protecting the product with closed technology
  • Be able to use CPP/CQA analysis to decide which process steps need a classified cleanroom environment and which do not
  • Become familiar with modern trends in optimizing biopharmaceutical production, including the ballroom concept, multi-product operation in a shared room, and how receptive regulators are to such designs

Chapter Contents

2.1 Introduction 2.2 Regulatory Requirements 2.3 Containment vs Classification 2.4 CQAs and CPPs 2.5 Biopharmaceutical Production 2.6 Facility Design Discussions 2.7 Sources of Information
2.1 Introduction: Regulatory Overview

Original Text

This chapter references key regulatory and guidance documents published since 2004 and highlights key updates to these guidances as they pertain to this ISPE Baseline Guide. These documents indicate that regulatory agencies are moving towards a harmonized cGMP definition. It is also clear that the regulatory agencies are addressing new trends in biopharmaceutical manufacturing including:

  • Closed processing
  • Multiproduct manufacturing facilities
  • Consolidated multistep processing suites
  • Single use systems

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

cGMP (current Good Manufacturing Practice) is the foundation of pharmaceutical manufacturing regulation. The word "current" means that the regulations must keep pace with the latest technology and industry practice.

The key message of this chapter: global regulations are converging (harmonized) and have begun to accept new trends in biopharmaceutical manufacturing, including closed processing, multi-product operation in a shared room, and single-use systems.

Analogy

Think of traffic laws around the world. Every country requires drivers to wear a seat belt, but the specific rules differ slightly. The same is true of cGMP for biopharmaceuticals: FDA (US), EMA (EU) and PIC/S (international) share the same core principles and differ slightly in the details. The aim of this chapter is to help you read this "global traffic-law comparison table".

Key Notes

The wording of a regulation may not change (for example, the text of US 21 CFR Part 211 has been unchanged since 1976), but how it is interpreted and applied keeps evolving. This means that even when the regulation is not updated, regulators' expectations still change over time.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the trend toward "harmonized cGMP" especially important for a CDMO? What is the commercial significance for a company that serves customers in the US, the EU and Japan at the same time?
  2. How has the rise of "closed processing" changed the design thinking behind traditional pharmaceutical facilities?
2.2 Regulatory Requirements and Guidance: Major Requirements and Guidelines

Original Text

There are numerous examples of how regulatory agencies are responding to advances in bioprocess capabilities and manufacturing strategies. A preliminary survey of guidance documents yields the following key regulatory directives:

PIC/S GMP Part 1 (Update: 1 Jan 2013)

Chapter 5 Production, Section: Prevention of Cross-Contamination

  • Item 5.19: Cross-contamination should be avoided by appropriate technical or organizational measures, for example: f) using "closed system" of production.

PIC/S GMP Annex 2 — Biological Medicinal Products (Update: 1 Jan 2013)

Premises and Equipment

  • Item 11: Simultaneous production in the same area using closed systems of biofermenters may be acceptable for products such as monoclonal antibodies and products prepared by r-DNA techniques.
  • Item 12: Processing steps after harvesting may be carried out in the same production area provided adequate precautions are taken to prevent cross contamination.

FDA Guidance: cGMP for Phase 1 Investigational Drugs (July 2008)

  • Clinical Phase 1 biologic drugs are exempt from 21 CFR Part 211 under 21 CFR Part 210.2(c).
  • Technologies facilitating cGMP conformance include:
    • Use of disposable equipment to reduce cleaning burden
    • Use of closed process equipment to alleviate the need for stricter room classification
    • Use of contract or shared cGMP manufacturing facilities

CFDA GMP for Drugs (Effective: March 2011)

  • Article 197: Measures to avoid contamination and cross-contamination, including using "closed systems" of production.
  • Annex 2 (API and Fermentation), Article 7: Closed or contained equipment should be used whenever appropriate.
  • Annex 2, Article 30: Viral removal/inactivation — "Open processing should be performed in areas separate from other processing activities." (Implying closed processes do not need separation.)
  • Annex 2, Article 47: For cell culture/fermentation with open vessels, "manipulations should be performed in the proper controlled environment." (Implying closed processes do not need a proper controlled environment.)

Japan GMP Guideline for Drug Products (2005)

  • Item 5.15: Closed or contained equipment should be used whenever appropriate. Where open equipment is used, appropriate precautions should be taken to minimize the risk of contamination.
"It should be noted that regional requirements can vary and both the global regulatory view and the specific agency requirements that govern products and facilities should be understood."

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

A common theme across global regulations: closed processing

As you read this section you will notice a striking pattern: whether it is US FDA, European PIC/S, Chinese CFDA or Japanese GMP, all of the regulations say the same thing: if you use closed processing, the facility environmental classification requirements can be relaxed.

  • Closed Processing: the product is not exposed to the surrounding environment at any point during manufacture; it is handled through piping, sealed vessels, single-use bags and similar means
  • Open Processing: the product is exposed to the surrounding air during some steps and needs the protection of a cleanroom environment

Regulatory Framework Comparison

Document               Key clause     Relaxation for closed processing
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PIC/S Part 1 (2013)   5.19(f)       Simultaneous multi-product production in one room (closed) is acceptable
PIC/S Annex 2 (2013)  Item 11       Closed bioreactors may be operated in a shared room
                      Item 12       Post-harvest steps may share a room (with adequate protection)
FDA Phase 1 (2008)    Sec.IV        Closed equipment can reduce the need for environmental classification
CFDA GMP (2011)       Art.197       Use closed systems to reduce cross-contamination
                      Art.30        Open processing requires isolation; closed does not
                      Art.47        Open operations require a controlled environment; closed do not
Japan GMP (2005)      5.15          Use closed equipment wherever possible
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Core logic: closed → environment is not a CPP → classification requirements can be lowered

Analogy

Imagine preparing food in your kitchen. If you are chopping vegetables on an open cutting board, the kitchen must be clean, free of flies and properly ventilated (you need "environmental control"). But if you use a sealed food processor, the surroundings matter much less, because the food is protected inside the machine.

The same applies to biopharmaceuticals: closed processing = the sealed food processor, open processing = chopping on the cutting board. When the process is closed, the effect of the facility environment on product quality is minimal.

Key Notes

The Phase 1 exemption from 21 CFR Part 211 is an important regulatory flexibility. Phase 1 clinical drugs do not need to fully meet the GMP requirements for commercial production, which allows small research organizations and academic centers to conduct early clinical trials and greatly lowers the barrier to drug development.

Note, however, that from Phase 2 onward, full cGMP requirements must be strictly applied.

Practical Application

When a CDMO accepts a customer project, it must confirm the product's target markets. If the customer's product is to be marketed in the US, the EU and China at the same time, the facility design must meet the requirements of FDA, EMA/PIC/S and CFDA simultaneously.

The good news is that these three regulatory systems relax requirements for closed processing in the same direction, so designing a facility that satisfies all of them is not as hard as it sounds, provided you understand the specific clauses of each.

2.2.2 Global Regulatory Information

Original Text

Global regulatory information is usually available online. For a facility design, both the global regulatory view and the specific agency requirements that govern products and facilities should be understood.

The main global regulatory agencies include:

  • U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA)
  • European Medicines Agency (EMA)
  • European Commission (EC), Public Health
  • World Health Organization (WHO)
  • International Conference on Harmonization (ICH)
  • Pharmaceutical Inspection Convention and Pharmaceutical Inspection Co-operation Scheme (PIC/S)
  • International Federation of Biosafety Associations (IFBA)
  • U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and U.S. National Institutes of Health (NIH)
  • Japan Pharmaceuticals and Medical Devices Agency (PDMA)
  • China Food and Drug Administration (CFDA)
  • India Central Drugs Standard Control Organization (CDSCO)
  • Association of South-East Asian Nations (ASEAN)

A listing of global agency links is provided by ISPE at www.ispe.org/regulatory-affairs.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three-tier regulatory structure is the key framework for understanding global oversight:

  • International harmonization tier (ICH, PIC/S, WHO): sets cross-border technical standards and guidelines, such as ICH Q7 (GMP for APIs) and the ICH Q5 series (quality of biotechnological products)
  • Regional regulatory tier (FDA, EMA, CFDA): legally binding regulations in each region, which must be followed for a product to be marketed
