  • Biosafety tier (CDC/NIH, IFBA): safe-handling rules for infectious organisms (note: this is a different system from GMP!)

Key Notes

Pay particular attention to CDC/NIH and IFBA in the list. These bodies do not regulate drug quality; they regulate biosafety. They appear on this list precisely to lead into the important distinction in the next section, 2.3, "Containment versus Classification".

For a CDMO in Taiwan, you also need to pay special attention to the requirements of the TFDA (Taiwan Food and Drug Administration) and to the status of ICH guideline adoption in Taiwan.

2.2.3 Interpretations and Applications: The Evolution of Regulatory Interpretation

Original Text

Some interpretations and applications of guidance and regulatory requirements continue to change. The text of 21 CFR Part 211 has not changed since issued in 1976, but the interpretations and applications have.

Open Processing Era (historical context):

When the regulatory requirements were initially written, most processes were either open systems or systems that were not closed. The product was exposed and at risk from personnel and the room environment. Regulatory agencies responded by interpreting CFRs to include classified room environments and elaborate facility requirements, including:

  • Nested cleanrooms with associated transition zones
  • Gowning areas (Personnel Airlocks, PALs)
  • Material Airlocks (MALs)
  • Complex "facility flows"

These were designed to protect the processing cleanroom environment from the surrounding less clean environments.

Closed Processing Era (modern trend):

Proven improvements in technology can functionally close an open system, such that the product is no longer exposed or at risk from personnel and the room environment. The room environment can become a "no impact system" or "non critical aspect" to the product.

"The aim of cGMPs and the application of those cGMPs through industry good practice is to protect the product. Facility features that are beyond this can be considered discretionary."

The US FDA is a promoter for improvements and application of better technology. A key technology application is closed processing where the room environment and personnel are separated from the product.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Regulatory interpretation is one of the most easily overlooked, yet most important, concepts in biopharmaceutical facility design.

Key insight: regulatory text ≠ regulatory expectation. The text of 21 CFR Part 211 has never changed since 1976, but FDA's interpretation of that text has kept evolving with technology. This means that:

  • Facility requirements that were needed in the past may no longer be necessary today
  • Designers must understand current regulatory expectations, not just read the text of the regulations

Analogy

Picture the era of 1976, when only conventional surgery existed. Hospital operating rooms needed extremely strict air purification because the wound was exposed to the air for a long time. Today, laparoscopic surgery needs only a few small incisions and the wound is exposed very briefly. The principle of "protecting the patient" has not changed, but the specific facility requirements are very different.

The impact of closed processing on facility design is like the impact of laparoscopic surgery on operating room design: the protection goal is unchanged, but the way of achieving it has been completely transformed.

Key Notes

"Discretionary" is the most important keyword in this section. Any facility design requirement that goes beyond the core purpose of "protecting the product" can be regarded as discretionary (although it is sometimes still worth implementing).

This gives engineers and companies design freedom, but it also brings responsibility: you must be able to justify scientifically how your design choices protect the product.

Practice Questions

  1. If every process step in a new facility uses closed processing, is the traditional "nested cleanrooms" design still necessary? Use the logic of this section to argue your answer.
  2. Under what circumstances might a company choose to add "discretionary" facility requirements voluntarily? What commercial or quality reasons could there be?
2.3 Containment versus Classification: Biosafety Containment vs Environmental Classification (the Most Important Concept)

Original Text

The industry's view of biotechnology has matured and "containment" concepts have been clarified. In the early years of biotechnology, there was a concern that an accidental release of a Genetically Modified Organism (GMO) into the environment could have a serious impact on the world environment. Some organizations added a Biosafety Level (BSL) 1 or 2 to their facility design for GMO containment. This blurred the facility design requirement distinction between non-infectious biopharmaceutical facilities and BSL facilities. The result was that many biopharmaceutical facilities looked like vaccine facilities.

The distinction between "containment" and "classification" should be clarified, as misuse of these terms can cause confusion.

Containment is typically reserved for:

  • Infectious organisms
  • Highly toxic or highly potent substances

Classification refers to a room environment cleanliness level as related to product exposure. An assumption that higher BSL containment relates to higher room environment classification would be incorrect. BSL containment is related to the hazard of the organism. Classification is related to the degree of the organism's exposure to the room environment.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Notes: The Most Important Concept in This Chapter!

This is one of the most easily confused, and most important, concepts in all of biopharmaceutical facility design.

Remember this fundamental difference:

  • Biosafety containment = protects personnel and the environment by keeping hazardous organisms from "getting out"
  • Environmental classification = protects the product by keeping environmental contaminants from "getting in"

The two directions are exactly opposite!

Analogy

An analogy for two-way protection:

Imagine a virologist who studies a highly pathogenic influenza virus in the laboratory and also has to produce a virus vaccine:

  • Biosafety containment = negative-pressure rooms, HEPA exhaust, protective clothing → keeps the virus from "escaping" the laboratory and protects people outside
  • Environmental classification = positive-pressure cleanrooms, HEPA supply air, cleanroom garments → keeps environmental dust from "entering" the product and protects vaccine quality

For a facility making a highly pathogenic virus vaccine, both requirements must be met at the same time, and they often conflict technically (negative vs positive pressure). Facility engineers must design a solution that resolves both.

In plain terms: you must prevent the virus from escaping the laboratory (biosafety) and also prevent the environment from contaminating the product (GMP). This is one of the biggest challenges in biopharmaceutical facility design.

Key Concepts

Why did this confusion arise historically?

When the biotechnology industry was just starting, there was excessive concern about the environmental risk of GMOs, and many companies layered Biosafety Level 1/2 facility requirements on top of their GMP facilities. As a result, non-vaccine plants ended up looking like vaccine plants (with many negative-pressure rooms and containment equipment).

This was an overly cautious design choice with no scientific basis, and it caused unnecessary construction cost and operational complexity. One of the core missions of this chapter of ISPE Vol.6 is to clear up this historical misunderstanding.

2.3.1 Biosafety Containment: Biosafety Levels (BSL) in Detail

Original Text

Appendix K of the NIH Guidelines for Research Involving Recombinant or Synthetic Nucleic Acid Molecules defines four ascending levels of biosafety containment:

Key regulatory sorting:

  • GLSP GMOs used as therapeutic drugs → governed by biopharmaceutical cGMPs
  • Non-vaccine drugs → regulated by biopharmaceutical cGMPs only
  • Vaccine drugs → biopharmaceutical cGMPs + additional BSL requirement
"A BSL rating is different from a room environment (cleanroom) classification. Closed vaccine processing for a BSL-3 containment facility can be in a Controlled Not Classified (CNC) room environment. The room cleanliness class is related to product exposure and not to the biosafety level."

A BSL-3 closed processing CNC facility must comply with CDC/NIH containment requirements, including:

  • HEPA exhaust
  • Negative relative room pressure to surrounding rooms
  • Air supply does NOT need to be classified (due to closed processing)

Tutorial Commentary

Comparison Table: BSL vs Cleanroom Classification

Biosafety level (BSL)            Cleanroom classification (ISO/EU GMP)
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Protects: personnel/environment  Protects: product
Basis: hazard of the organism    Basis: degree of product exposure
Features: negative pressure,     Features: positive pressure,
  HEPA exhaust                     HEPA supply air
Regulatory basis: CDC/NIH/IFBA   Regulatory basis: FDA/EMA/PIC/S
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
CHO cell facility (mAb):
  BSL = GLSP (lowest)            Classification = depends on open/closed
  → No BSL facility needed       → If closed: CNC is sufficient

BSL-3 vaccine facility (closed process):
  BSL = BSL-3 (high)             Classification = CNC (unclassified)
  → Needs negative pressure,     → No classified supply air needed
    HEPA exhaust
  → Counterintuitive! High BSL ≠ high classification

Key Notes

The most counterintuitive conclusion: a BSL-3 plant can be a CNC (controlled not classified) room!

If the BSL-3 vaccine process is completely closed, then:

  • negative pressure and HEPA exhaust are needed to contain the virus
  • but because the product is not exposed to the environment, classified supply air for a cleanroom is not needed

This completely breaks the intuition that "the more dangerous it is, the more facility requirements are needed". High hazard (BSL) ≠ high environmental classification (GMP cleanroom).

Key Concepts

GLSP (Good Large Scale Practices): this is the operating environment most familiar to biopharmaceutical companies. The CHO cells and E. coli K-12 used to make monoclonal antibodies (mAb), recombinant proteins and genetically engineered drugs all fall under GLSP.

GLSP means: no BSL facility requirements; only cGMP needs to be followed. This is why a typical mAb plant looks like an ordinary pharmaceutical plant rather than an infectious disease laboratory.

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO accepts a project for a recombinant protein drug produced in CHO cells and another for a live attenuated virus vaccine. What are the fundamental differences in the facility design requirements for these two products? How do BSL and GMP classification each affect the design?
  2. Why does ISPE consider it a mistake that many non-vaccine biopharmaceutical plants in the past "looked like vaccine plants"? What was the cost of this mistake?
2.4 Critical Quality Attributes and Critical Process Parameters: CQA and CPP

Original Text

Where facilities are designed to produce multiple products, CPPs (Critical Process Parameters) and CQAs (Critical Quality Attributes) of both current and projected future products should be considered in the facility design.

  • CQAs need to be controlled to ensure acceptable product quality.
  • Variability in CPPs can impact CQAs and should be monitored and controlled to ensure that a process produces the desired product quality.

CPPs are the processing variables (e.g., temperature, pressure, and pH) that can influence CQAs. CPPs are design aspects of a system.

A facility can directly impact a CQA. If a process step is open and product is exposed to the room environment, some room environment parameters will be CPPs (e.g., a potential source of contamination), which can affect product CQAs.

If a process step is closed and the product is not exposed to the room environment, the room environment parameters become non-critical aspects rather than CPPs.

"This is a key concept and the basis for the product protection approach in facility design. The application of segregation concepts stem from this concept."

Open process steps that cause room environment parameters to be CPPs should be identified on Process Flow Diagrams (PFDs).

Open Processing Rooms require:

  • Room environment parameters can be CPPs
  • The area environment should be classified
  • A processing cleanroom is needed
  • Nesting concept: cleanrooms surrounded by cleanrooms of lower classification
  • Environmental cleanroom cascades, pressure cascades, and HVAC segregation zoning
  • Transition zones: Personnel Airlocks (PALs) for gowning and Material Air Locks (MALs)
  • Facility and production flows to reduce environmental challenge entering PALs and MALs
  • Personnel may remove street clothes for facility uniforms (reduces particulate)

Closed Processing Rooms:

  • Room environment parameters are NOT CPPs
  • Items listed for open processing rooms are NOT required for non-CPP environments

Organizations may choose to add facility requirements that do not directly protect product (discretionary). For example, requiring facility uniforms for closed processing — gowning does not assist in protecting the product, but being gowned may assist with the staff being in a production attitude.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

CQA (Critical Quality Attribute): a quality characteristic that directly affects product safety, efficacy or purity. For biologics, common CQAs include:

  • Glycosylation pattern: affects half-life and immunogenicity
  • Aggregation: affects safety
  • Host cell protein (HCP): affects the immune response
  • Biological activity

CPP (Critical Process Parameter): an operating parameter whose deviation from the acceptable range would significantly affect a CQA.

Facility Needs for Open vs Closed Processing

Process type    Environment a CPP?   Facility requirements
──────────────────────────────────────────────
Open            Yes (environment     Classified cleanroom needed
                is a CPP)            Nested cleanroom structure needed
                                     PAL/MAL airlocks needed
                                     Cascading pressure differentials (positive) needed
                                     Personnel gowning area needed
                                     HVAC needs strict zoning
──────────────────────────────────────────────
Closed          No (environment      None of the above is mandatory
                is not a CPP)        Facility design is greatly simplified
                                     CNC rooms can be used
                                     Basic hygiene controls still needed
──────────────────────────────────────────────
Design decision logic:
  Open step → identify on the PFD → design the CPP environment
  Closed step → facility is non-critical → design can be simplified

Analogy

Compare an outdoor picnic (open processing) with storing food in a sealed insulated box (closed processing):

  • Outdoor picnic: you need to choose a clean spot, avoid flies and watch the weather, so the surrounding environment is a "critical" factor (CPP)
  • Sealed insulated box: the food is completely unaffected by the outside, and even in a dirty warehouse it will not be contaminated, so the environment is a "non-critical" factor

When your process step is "closed", the facility environment is like the warehouse outside the insulated box: its importance drops sharply.

Key Notes

The importance of the PFD (Process Flow Diagram): before designing the facility, engineers must first identify which process steps are "open" (with product exposure) and which are "closed". These open steps must be specially marked on the PFD, because they determine which rooms need cleanroom classification.

This logic is the starting point of the whole facility design: process analysis comes first, then facility design.

Practical Application

CDMO design strategy: maximize closed process steps and minimize the proportion of "CPP environment".

If most process steps can be designed as closed, the cleanroom area can be greatly reduced, which directly lowers:

  • Construction cost (building a cleanroom costs 3 to 10 times as much as an ordinary facility)
  • HVAC system complexity and energy consumption
  • Routine cleaning validation workload
  • Personnel training and gowning time

This is the core benefit driver in modern biopharmaceutical facility design.

Practice Questions

  1. The upstream process of a monoclonal antibody (cell culture) is run in a closed single-use bioreactor, while one downstream purification step requires open operation. Describe how the facility design of these two areas should differ.
  2. If "gowning" is "discretionary" for closed processing, why does this section say it "helps put employees in a production mindset"? From a QMS perspective, is this reason sufficient?
2.5 Biopharmaceutical Production: The Current State of Production Optimization

Original Text

There is now a better understanding of biopharmaceutical production, particularly for biopharmaceutical bulk drug substance processes. Production is repeatable and predictable. The industry has shifted to a state of production optimization. The focus is on:

  • Speed to market
  • Lowering cost of goods
  • Reducing risk
  • Increasing yields by increasing titers
  • Reducing losses and failures
  • Improving recoveries

Many techniques and process steps (e.g., process hold steps) that were once considered essential have now been redefined as non-value added exercises. Facility layouts and designs are responding to and focusing on efficiency, Quality by Design (QbD), risk assessments, and lean manufacturing principals.

A major focus is on the broad based application of closed processing to the entire production line. Closed processing:

  • Decouples the process from the facility
  • Effectively isolates the process from neighboring activities
  • Enables consolidation of multiple closed operations into a single process suite
  • Enables concurrent multistep, multibatch, and multiproduct operations within a single manufacturing suite

Closed processing has expanded the application of "physical separation" / "spatial separation":

  • Open processing: separate suites for each open step (e.g., pre-viral and post-viral reduction require separate rooms)
  • Closed processing: a series of smaller rooms may be replaced by fewer larger rooms — "ballrooms" or "dance floors"

Examples of physical separation by closed processing:

  • Closed pre-viral and post-viral reduction steps in the same room (PIC/S Annex 2 Item 12)
  • Upstream multi-product, multi-host simultaneous production in separate closed bioreactors in the same room (PIC/S Annex 2 Item 11)
  • Processing steps after harvest in separate closed equipment in the same room (PIC/S Annex 2 Item 12)
  • A product line and a waste line occupying the same pipe rack
  • A closed used portable vessel (dirty on the inside) in the same elevator as a closed cleaned vessel

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Biopharmaceutical production has entered an "era of optimization". This statement has far-reaching implications:

  • 1980s-2000s: technology exploration period; processes were not necessarily reproducible and facility designs were conservative
  • 2000s-2010s: process knowledge accumulated and large-scale production began
  • 2010s to present: processes are highly predictable, and the focus has shifted to cost optimization, speed and flexibility

This shift directly affects facility design philosophy: from putting "ensuring safety" first to balancing "safety + efficiency + flexibility".

Analogy

An analogy for the ballroom concept:

A traditional open-processing plant is like a building with many small partitioned offices: each process step needs its own room, separated by corridors and doors.

A closed-processing ballroom plant is like a modern open-plan office: everyone (every process step) works in the same large space, but each person has their own "computer desktop (closed equipment)" and does not interfere with others.

Advantages of an open-plan office: saves space, easy communication, high flexibility. Disadvantage: it needs better personal equipment (closed technology) to maintain privacy (cross-contamination protection).

Key Notes

"Closed processing decouples the process from the facility" is the core design principle of this section.

In the open-processing era, process steps were tightly bound to the facility environment, so changing the process often meant modifying the facility. Closed processing breaks this binding:

  • Process change (new product, new scale) → change the equipment, with no facility modification needed
  • The facility can be multi-purpose (multi-product): different products are made in different closed equipment in the same space
  • The facility lifecycle is extended and the return on investment improves

Practical Application

The "Dirty Inside, Clean Outside" principle:

The last example best illustrates the design flexibility of closed processing: a just-used "dirty" mobile vessel (clean on the outside, but with residue inside) can share the same elevator with a "clean" vessel. In the open-processing era this was absolutely not allowed; but with closed processing, as long as the outer surfaces of both vessels are clean, the "dirty" inside will not contaminate the other.

For a CDMO, this design flexibility means a smaller facility footprint, simpler material and personnel flows, and lower construction cost.

Practice Questions

  1. What benefits does "closed processing decouples the process from the facility" bring to a CDMO's business model? If a customer later introduces a new biologic, how does a facility with a closed design reduce the cost of modification?
  2. In the ballroom concept, the closed process steps of several different products run at the same time in the same room. How do regulators verify that such a design meets cross-contamination prevention requirements (see PIC/S Annex 2)?
2.6 Facility Design Discussions: Regulators' Expectations and Discussion of Facility Design

Original Text

As the process closes, the process room environment and the facility can adapt to become a non-critical aspect system to the product as the environment has no impact on processes in closed systems.

There is a technical solution to the concepts below, but the current hesitance is an emotional item based on two major factors:

  1. The industry is so familiar with facilities designed for open processing that the dramatic shift to a closed processing facility feels too different.
  2. How will the industry react?

Advanced Design Concepts for Closed Facilities:

  • Downstream multi-product simultaneous production in separate closed equipment in the same processing room.
  • Closed upstream and closed downstream in the same single processing room (one product at a time, campaigned basis; line clearance on a "room" basis).
  • Closed multi-product simultaneous upstream AND downstream in the same single processing room (line clearance on an "equipment" basis). This could be many fully closed processing lines in a large CNC production "ballroom." Traditional room separations of cell-containing, cell-free, and viral-free are replaced by closed processing. With fully closed processing, there is just "manufacturing."
  • Closed clinical Phase 2, 3, and commercial processing multi-product simultaneous upstream production in the same room. (Note: Phase 1 is pre-cGMP; Phase 2 through commercial are all cGMP — combining Phase 2 with commercial is not a clinical/commercial separation issue.)
  • Closed clinical Phase 2, 3, and commercial multi-product simultaneous downstream production in the same room.
  • Closed clinical Phase 2, 3, and commercial multi-product simultaneous upstream AND downstream production in the same room.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Notes

This section represents the industry's "cutting-edge thinking". These concepts were still considered forward-looking when published in 2013, and some are still being debated in terms of regulatory acceptance today.

ISPE states directly: the resistance to these concepts is mainly "emotional" rather than technical. This is a very honest statement, showing that design conservatism in the industry often stems from habit rather than scientific evidence.

Key Concepts

Three levels of multi-product operation in a shared room:

  • Level 1 (more conservative): upstream and downstream are in the same room, but only one product at a time, produced in campaigns. Line clearance is room-based.
  • Level 2 (moderately bold): several products run upstream at the same time and downstream at the same time, in the same room. Line clearance is equipment-based.
  • Level 3 (most advanced): Phase 2 and 3 clinical production and commercial production in the same room. Because they are all cGMP, there is no regulatory issue of "clinical/commercial segregation"; only the production scale differs.

Analogy

Differences in the granularity of line clearance:

Imagine a coffee shop that serves lattes, cappuccinos and Americanos at the same time:

  • "Room" cleaning = cleaning the whole coffee machine only after all orders are done: the least efficient, but the most conservative
  • "Equipment" cleaning = each coffee machine handles its own drinks and the machines are independent of each other, so only the machine concerned is cleaned at changeover: efficient, but requires good equipment isolation design

A biopharmaceutical ballroom design is like the latter: each set of closed equipment cleans itself, and the room itself does not need to be cleaned each time.

Practical Application

The commercial significance of clinical and commercial production in the same room:

Many CDMOs worry that mixing clinical and commercial production will cause regulatory problems. This section states clearly: as long as everything is cGMP (Phase 2+), there is no regulatory prohibition on sharing a room. The difference is only in production scale, not in regulatory level.

This has a huge impact on a CDMO's business model: a well-designed closed ballroom can serve several customers at different clinical stages at the same time, greatly raising the asset utilization rate of the facility.

Practice Questions

  1. ISPE says the resistance to the closed ballroom concept is "emotional" rather than technical. In real facility inspections, what do you think is the attitude of regulators (such as FDA investigators) toward these concepts? How can a company prepare to persuade regulators to accept such a design?
  2. What documentation and validation work is needed for "line clearance on an equipment basis" to demonstrate to regulators that cross-contamination between equipment cannot occur?
2.7 Sources of Information

Original Text

Manufacturing organizations may be willing to publish their facility designs in key industry media, allowing for a global benchmarking of the applications of cGMP requirements. Other sources of industry good practice include:

  • ASME Bioprocessing Equipment (BPE): Creates industry standards for the design of biopharmaceutical production equipment.
  • ISPE: Publishes a technical magazine and associated guidance documents.
  • Industry forums or consortia: Organizations join together to solve common industry problems, such as facility design and room environment classifications for processing steps. Example: BioPhorum Operations Group (BPOG).
  • Direct cooperation between operating organizations: Joint industry benchmarks. Example: Bio-Process Systems Alliance (BPSA).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Four tiers of industry standard sources:

  • Regulatory tier (FDA, EMA, PIC/S): legally binding and must be followed
  • Technical standards tier (ASME BPE, ISO): engineering standards recognized by the industry; not legally binding but widely adopted
  • Industry organization tier (ISPE, PDA): best-practice guidance, such as this Vol.6
  • Industry consortium tier (BPOG, BPSA): consortia formed by companies themselves to solve common problems and publish white papers

Key Notes

BPOG (BioPhorum Operations Group) is an industry consortium that has become increasingly influential in recent years; its members include the world's major biopharmaceutical companies (Pfizer, Roche, Amgen and others). BPOG best-practice documents are cited more and more often in FDA inspections, so understanding BPOG's positions is important when designing a compliant facility.

ASME BPE (Bioprocessing Equipment) is the gold standard for biopharmaceutical equipment design. It covers design requirements for piping, valves and vessels to ensure cleanability and sterilizability.

Practice Questions

  1. When facility design documents cite ASME BPE standards and ISPE Baseline Guides, what is the legal status of these documents? What consequences would there be if FDA found during an inspection that a company had not followed these standards?
  2. What is the commercial value for a CDMO of joining or following BPOG activities?

Chapter Summary

  • Harmonized cGMP: FDA, EMA, PIC/S, CFDA and Japanese GMP all support relaxing environmental classification requirements for closed processing. This is the most important regulatory trend for facility design.
  • Biosafety containment ≠ environmental classification: containment prevents organisms from "getting out" (protecting personnel/environment); classification prevents contaminants from "getting in" (protecting the product). The two run in opposite directions and must not be mixed up.
  • Most biologics (non-vaccine) fall under GLSP: they use non-pathogenic cell lines such as CHO cells and E. coli, need no BSL facility requirements, and only need to follow cGMP.
  • The CPP is the starting point of facility design: identifying which process steps are open (environment is a CPP) and which are closed (environment is not a CPP) determines the classification requirements of the facility.
  • Closed processing decouples the process from the facility: a facility with a closed design can realize the ballroom concept, multi-product operation in a shared room and multi-step operation in a shared room, greatly reducing facility area and cost.
  • Regulatory text ≠ regulatory expectation: 21 CFR Part 211 has not changed since 1976, but FDA's interpretation has evolved considerably. You must understand current regulatory expectations, not just read the old regulatory text.
  • Resistance is often emotional: industry resistance to the ballroom design comes mainly from habit, not from technical or regulatory obstacles. Understanding this helps you make more persuasive design proposals in customer discussions.

The Chapter's Core Insight: A Design Philosophy of Two-Way Protection

The most distinctive challenge of biopharmaceutical facility design is that it must meet two protection requirements that run in opposite directions at the same time:

Biosafety Containment

Direction: inside to outside (prevent organisms from escaping)
Purpose: protect personnel and the environment
Technical means: negative pressure, HEPA exhaust, personal protection
Regulatory basis: CDC/NIH/IFBA
Applies to: BSL-1/2/3 facilities (mainly vaccine plants)

GMP Classification

Direction: outside to inside (prevent contamination from entering the product)
Purpose: protect product quality
Technical means: positive pressure, HEPA supply air, cleanroom garments
Regulatory basis: FDA/EMA/PIC/S/cGMP
Applies to: all biopharmaceutical plants (depending on the degree of openness)

For most biopharmaceutical plants (using GLSP cell lines, non-vaccine): only GMP classification needs to be considered; no BSL facility is needed.
For vaccine plants: both BSL containment requirements (negative pressure) and GMP classification requirements must be met, and facility engineers must design a solution that resolves this conflict.

4A

Chapter 4A: Process Closure — Sources & Layers

p31-p42

ISPE Chapter 4 (Part A): Process Closure — Introduction & Layers of Protection

Chapter 4 (Part 1): Process Closure - Introduction, Sources of Contamination and Layers of Protection
ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p31 – p42 | Sections 4.1–4.4

Learning Objectives

  • Understand why process closure is the single most pivotal decision in biopharmaceutical facility design, and how it decouples the risk relationship between the process and its environment.
  • Identify the four main sources of contamination in biopharmaceutical processes (environment, personnel, equipment and raw materials) and understand how each spreads.
  • Grasp the onion-style logic of the "Layers of Protection" model: each additional effective layer of protection allows the cleanroom grade requirement to be lowered by one level.
  • Understand how the Closure Analysis workflow, including the Phase 1-3 risk assessment steps, systematically verifies that a process unit is truly closed.
  • Distinguish the three system types (open, functionally closed and fully closed), how each is operated, and the environment grade each requires.

Table of Contents

4.1 Introduction 4.2 Contamination Sources 4.2.1 Aseptic/Axenic Bioburden 4.2.2 Mitigation Strategies 4.3 Layers of Protection 4.3.1 BSC Environments 4.3.2 Strategies for Closing 4.4 Demonstrating Closure
4.1 Introduction: The Fundamental Logic of Process Closure

Original Text

The goal of this chapter is to provide a means to evaluate bioprocess unit operations and to discuss opportunities for closing these operations; therefore, decoupling them from the environment.

Isolating a process from its environment mitigates a major risk of contamination by adventitious agents, thereby yielding a process that is safer, more robust, more consistent, and where the risk of product adulteration is reduced significantly. Isolating a process from its environment also can mitigate risk when the release of the process fluid or aerosols can pose a risk to the operator, the environment or other open operations.

Technology improvements have resulted in more robust systems that can isolate bioprocess unit operations from the immediate environment. This is important, as control of the physicochemical conditions within a process micro-environment is more achievable than control of the macro-environment surrounding that process. As an example, it is easier to maintain an aseptic environment within a bioreactor than it is to maintain a low bioburden Grade 5 condition within a processing suite housing bioreactors and people.

Performing aseptic bioprocessing operations in an open system represents high risk. Where feasible, aseptic operations should be performed in closed aseptic systems. Closed processing represents the lowest risk option in terms of mitigating the risk of contamination from the environment for both aseptic and low bioburden operations. Operations that cannot be practically closed should be processed in a monitored clean classified environment that is consistent with the bioburden and particulate requirements of the process unit operation, whether aseptic or low bioburden.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Process Closure: Fully isolating the product-contact surfaces of the process from the external environment, so that the environment becomes a non-critical parameter for process quality. This is the central tenet of ISPE Vol.6 and the fundamental logic behind modern biopharmaceutical facility design.

Decoupling: The greatest significance of a closed process is that it "decouples" the risk between the process and its environment: even if the surrounding environment is not a cleanroom, the product in a closed system remains protected.

Adventitious Agent: A microbial contaminant unintentionally introduced during the process, including bacteria, viruses and molds. Because biopharmaceutical processes run in aqueous media, they readily become a breeding ground for such contaminants.

Analogy

A closed system = a sealed thermos flask: Imagine you are hiking in the mountains with hot coffee in a sealed thermos. No matter how cold it is, how hard the wind blows or how dirty the air is, the coffee inside is unaffected. That is the core idea of a "closed process": the system holding the valuable biological product is like that thermos, completely isolated from a harsh external environment.

By contrast, if you use an open paper cup, you need an extremely clean space (a Grade 5 cleanroom) to keep the coffee from being contaminated. The price of an open system is that it permanently requires expensive environmental protection.

Key Note

A micro-environment is easier to control than a macro-environment: A key insight of Vol.6 is that controlling the sterile environment inside a bioreactor is far easier and more economical than controlling the cleanliness of an entire process building. This overturns the traditional aseptic manufacturing mindset of a high-grade cleanroom throughout the plant and leads to a precise design that only needs to protect the contact surfaces.

Practice Questions

  1. Why can a closed bioreactor operate in a Grade 8 (grade C) or even unclassified space, while a traditional aseptic filling line requires a Grade 5 (grade A) environment?
  2. Which is more cost-effective for a CDMO: process closure or upgrading the cleanroom grade? Analyze from two angles: facility construction and long-term operation.
4.2 Sources of Product and Process Contamination

Original Text

Particulates in the environment can provide vehicles for adventitious agents, representing a biological risk to a biopharmaceutical process and product. A risk management program should be established to evaluate and address any potential impact of particulate contamination to process unit operations.

Bioprocessing is generally performed in an aqueous media that can support the growth of bacteria, viruses, and other adventitious agents; therefore, bioprocesses are prone to contamination by propagation of these agents. Facility designs and designs of process closures should account for potential sources of biological contamination.

Bioburden contamination (such as bacteria, yeasts, or mold contamination) can disrupt the physicochemical properties of a bioprocess solution, yielding chemicals and conditions that can be potentially harmful to the patient.

Bioprocesses should be monitored for bioburden and particulates, which should be controlled. Most bioprocesses typically include multiple clarification steps that would effectively remove small levels of particulate contamination (in the form of suspended solids), mitigating the risk of physical damage to equipment, disruption of a bioprocess unit operation, or adulteration of the drug substance.

Figure 4.1🔍 depicts the sources of product and process contamination.

[Figure 4.1🔍: Sources of Product and Process Contamination - illustrates the four main sources: ingredients/consumables, environmental factors (equipment/air/personnel/utilities), cross-contamination and carryover]

A. The ingredients used in media, buffers, and other bioprocess solutions can represent a significant potential source of microbial contamination. Consumables such as chromatography resins, filter media, and tubing also represent potential sources of contamination.

B. Potential sources of contamination from the environment include:

  • Equipment
  • Airborne particulates and bioburden
  • Contamination on personnel
  • Contamination from utility supply services
  • Contaminating waste systems

C. Sources of crossover contamination include contamination from concurrent multi-product, multi-batch, and multi-unit operations (including concurrent CIP and SIP process operations).

D. Carryover is typically a result of inadequate system preparation, including cleaning and sanitization operations between manufacturing batches.

Centralized CIP operations can be a source of contamination from the environment, carryover contamination, crossover contamination, and contamination from cleaning solvents and chemicals.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The four categories of contamination source:

  • A - Ingredients & Consumables: Media components, buffer raw materials, chromatography resins, filter membranes, tubing and so on. These "things that go into the process" may themselves carry microorganisms.
  • B - Environment: Airborne particles, equipment surfaces, skin flakes and microorganisms shed by operators, and contamination of utilities (purified water, clean steam).
  • C - Cross-Contamination: When adjacent processes, multiple products or multiple batches run at the same time, contaminants from one system enter another. A poorly designed CIP/SIP piping network can become a vehicle for cross-contamination.
  • D - Carryover: Residues from the previous batch or step (including cleaning agent residues) that are not effectively removed and are carried into the next batch. This is the core issue of CIP validation.

Analogy

Cross-contamination = chopping board management in a restaurant: A good restaurant chef uses differently colored chopping boards for raw meat, vegetables and fish to prevent "cross-contamination". In a multi-product biopharmaceutical plant, without strict segregation (hardware separation or process closure), it is like using one chopping board for every ingredient: the risk is very high.

Carryover = a cup that was not washed clean: You pour orange juice into a cup, fail to wash it properly, then pour in milk, and the leftover acid curdles the milk. In a biopharmaceutical process, protein or cleaning agent left over from the previous batch can seriously interfere with the purification or potency of the next batch.

Key Note

The special vulnerability of aqueous media: Biopharmaceutical processes run almost entirely in aqueous solutions (culture media, buffers, chromatographic purification). Aqueous media are a natural breeding ground for bacteria and yeast. Once even a small amount of contamination is introduced, it spreads exponentially within a short time (at 37°C culture conditions, E. coli doubles every 20 minutes), and the loss from scrapping the whole batch is severe.

The dual risk of CIP: CIP is a cleaning tool, but a centralized CIP system can itself become a contamination vehicle: if the same CIP piping cleans several pieces of equipment, residues in the piping may cause cross-contamination. This is one reason CIP design has recently tended toward dedicated CIP.

Practice Questions

  1. Which category of contamination source (A/B/C/D) is hardest to control when a CDMO handles products for multiple clients, and why?
  2. If the culture medium in a bioreactor is contaminated with yeast, what are the possible entry points? Deduce them from the list in 4.2.1.
4.2.1 Sources of Bioburden in Aseptic/Axenic Operations

Original Text

Cell culture and fermentation operations are by necessity aseptic/axenic operations. Contamination of these processes can result in loss or rejection of batches. Prior to inoculation, the medium within a bioreactor should be free of measurable bioburden (effectively sterile). Single use bioreactors and systems for cell culture operations should be discarded and replaced. Stainless steel systems should be decontaminated, cleaned, and sterilized prior to subsequent use. The reason for contamination should be investigated and addressed to minimize risk of another contamination.

Potential sources of contamination in cell culture operations include:

  • Breach of integrity of the Grade 5 environment in the BSC used in open operations
  • Failure in the bioreactor sterilization
  • Failure in media sterilization
  • Failure in bioreactor cleaning, resulting in soil carryover
  • Compromise/breach of sterile air filter integrity
  • Contamination of the cell bank or inoculums
  • Compromise/breach of the vent filter integrity and system pressurization failure
  • Compromise of the sampling system integrity
  • Compromise of the addition port(s) integrity
  • Contamination of the clean steam system
  • Contamination of process water system
  • Presence of prions or adventitious agents in animal derived ingredients
  • Failure of mixer system or mixer mechanical seals
  • Failure of aseptic transfer system sterilization, breach of transfer system integrity
  • Failure/breach of vessel integrity
  • Contamination of feed solution
  • Compromise/breach of perfusion system integrity
  • Leachables/extractables from polymeric components, glass, etc.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Aseptic Operation vs Axenic Operation:

  • Aseptic: The entire operating environment and system exclude all microorganisms; applicable to pre-inoculation preparation of cell culture, media preparation and so on.
  • Axenic: Specifically, a culture that contains only the intended target organism (such as CHO cells) and no other microorganisms. A cell culture batch must be maintained under axenic conditions throughout.

Pre-inoculation Sterility: Before inoculation, the medium in the bioreactor must be effectively sterile. This is usually achieved by SIP (steam-in-place). If SIP fails, the cells subsequently inoculated are immediately exposed to contamination risk.

Key Note

A warning on the 18 potential contamination entry points of a closed system: This list looks daunting, but it is really a "closed-system design checklist". Each listed entry point represents an interface that needs to be protected. A good system designer evaluating the closure of a bioreactor must review every entry point to confirm it has adequate protective measures.

The special risk of animal-derived components: Animal-derived raw materials (serum, hydrolysates) may carry viruses or prions (such as those associated with BSE). Serum-free media have greatly reduced this risk today, but the historical use of animal-derived components remains a focus of FDA and EMA review.

Analogy

18 contamination entry points = a castle with 18 gates: Imagine a medieval castle (the bioreactor) whose value lies in the safety of its interior. If the castle has 18 gates (sampling ports, vent ports, feed ports, agitator seals and so on), each gate can become an entry point for the enemy (contamination). The castle is only as secure as its weakest gate, not its strongest. This is why closure verification must cover every interface comprehensively.

Practical Application

As a CDMO process engineer receiving a brand-new 1,000 L stainless steel bioreactor and performing the first SIP validation, you need to:

  1. Identify whether all probe ports (pH, DO, temperature) are fully installed and have been sterilized (flame sterilization)
  2. Confirm that the vent filter integrity test has passed
  3. Verify that the steam sterilization step of the sampling system is effective
  4. Confirm that all feed ports are within the scope of the SIP procedure

Missing any one of these can lead to a batch being scrapped a few days after inoculation because of contamination, costing hundreds of thousands to millions of US dollars in raw materials.

4.2.2 Mitigation of Risks from Sources of Potential Product and Process Contamination

Original Text

The risk of contamination from raw materials can be mitigated and monitored with established quality control strategies. Ingredient specifications including bioburden, impurities, properties, and recommended storage conditions, should be understood. Dry solids should be stored in environments with controlled humidity and temperature. Solutes used in media and buffer formulation are usually not sterile; the solutions using these ingredients should be:

  • Sterilized
  • Stored under bacteriostatic conditions
  • Used within specified hold periods (with limited shelf life — before microbial contaminants proliferate)

Media are typically at higher risk than buffer solutions because of their growth promoting properties. The composition of media may affect their susceptibility to contamination: defined chemical media may be considered lower risk. The use of sera can affect the susceptibility to contamination. Consumables also represent potential sources of contamination.

Contamination from the environment and crossover contamination from neighboring systems can occur for open processes when these systems are in close proximity. Protecting an open system from potential contamination by operators requires effective sanitary gowning and/or other layers of system protection, including a clean environment. Enhanced gowning may help reduce bioburden levels in a suite.

Closing a system is considered an effective approach to mitigating the risk of crossover contamination or contamination from the environment. Closing a system also effectively mitigates the risk of contamination by personnel. Contamination from the environment of a closed system would require a breach of that system, typically as a result of multiple failures of mechanical systems (e.g., valve and/or filter failures) representing the layers of protection to that system. Crossover contamination of two adjacent closed systems would require the breach of integrity of both systems.

Carryover is typically a result of inadequate system preparation including cleaning and sanitization operations between manufacturing batches. Validation of the cleaning and sanitization program should adequately mitigate the risk of carryover. An effective cleaning and sanitization program should include adequate monitoring to ensure critical parameters are maintained, including:

  • Temperature
  • Time
  • Action (impingement and/or turbulent flow; pressure)
  • Chemical concentration and components
  • Sequence of cleaning steps

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The scientific basis of the hold period: Medium is not used immediately after preparation, so a maximum hold period must be set. This limit is not arbitrary; it is based on microbial growth kinetics studies, i.e. how long it takes, at a given temperature and pH, to go from "no contamination" to "contamination beyond the acceptable level". When the hold period expires, the medium must be discarded or re-sterilized.

Different risks of media vs buffers: Media contain rich nutrients such as carbon sources, nitrogen sources and vitamins, making them a "five-star restaurant" for microorganisms, so their contamination risk is significantly higher than that of buffers. Defined chemical media, whose composition is known and free of undefined animal-derived components, carry lower risk than complex media.

Key Note

The "multiple failure" protection logic of closed systems: Vol.6 states explicitly that for environmental contamination to enter a truly closed system, "multiple failures" must occur at the same time, for example a valve and a filter failing simultaneously. This design, in which contamination requires multiple failures, is the fundamental reason closed systems are safer than open ones, and the regulatory basis for placing closed systems in lower-grade environments.

The TACT principle of CIP: Effective cleaning requires four elements at once: Temperature, Action (mechanical force/turbulence), Chemical (cleaning agent concentration) and Time (contact time). If any one element is insufficient, overall cleaning effectiveness is greatly reduced. This is also the core evaluation framework for CIP validation.

Framework

CIP effectiveness = f(temperature × action × chemical concentration × time × step sequence)

Key concepts:
- Raising the temperature → can reduce the required chemical concentration
- Increasing mechanical force → can shorten the cleaning time
- Wrong step sequence (e.g. acid wash after an alkaline wash without adequate rinsing)
  → neutralization reaction products may block the piping or damage equipment

Three elements of CIP validation:
1. Visually clean
2. Microbiological indicators (bioburden limits)
3. Residue limits (e.g., TOC, conductivity)

Practice Questions

  1. If the SIP of a 2,000 L bioreactor fails but the equipment remains closed (valves not opened), can microorganisms from the environment enter? Why or why not?
  2. When a CDMO runs two adjacent batches of different clients' products, what additional considerations does CIP validation need to cover?
4.3 Providing Appropriate and Effective Layers of Protection to a Bioprocess Unit Operation: The Knowledge Core of Vol.6

Original Text

Different levels of bioburden and contamination control are needed for different unit operations. Different approaches and layers of protection are required to mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment for these different operations. The impact (or severity) of bioburden or particulate contamination will differ in different situations. The mitigation of risk from contamination present in the environment requires the implementation of sufficient layers of appropriate protection to a process system. The purpose of this section is to discuss possible options for closing an operation by providing appropriate layers of protection to that operation.

The use of highly classified cleanrooms for open operations can reduce the level of potential environmental contaminants. For aseptic operations, classified cleanrooms provide an environment with lower bioburden. For other bioprocesses, classified cleanrooms can lower the particulate burden and bioburden within the environment.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts: The Onion-Style Layers of Protection Model

This is the most important concept in the whole of Vol.6. ISPE's central argument is:

Each layer of effective protection = one level of cleanroom requirement that can be lowered

Like an onion, the more layers (layers of protection) there are, the safer the core (the product) is and the less the outer environment matters.

Analogy

Onion-style protection (each onion layer = one layer of protection):

Imagine the product as the core of the onion. For each layer of onion skin (layer of protection), outside "contamination" must first breach that layer to reach the core. When the onion has enough layers (closed equipment + closed transfer piping + controlled room), the product remains safe even if the outermost environment (the building space) is not a strict cleanroom.

Onion-Style Layers of Protection Model (from the inside out)

Core: Product (drug solution)
Layer 1 — Primary Containment
The closed equipment itself: welded joints, SIP connections, closed bioreactor
Layer 2 — Secondary Containment
Closed room / RABS / isolator
Layer 3 — Building Segregation
Physical separation of different process areas, independent HVAC
Layer 4+ — Environmental Controls
Cleanroom grade, gowning procedures, environmental monitoring

More layers of protection → lower external environment grade requirement → lower facility construction cost

4.3.1 Providing Appropriate Environments within Biosafety Cabinets

Original Text

Open aseptic operations require environments of extremely low bioburden, where the probability of detecting bioburden within those environments during the open operation is essentially zero/probability less than 1. Grade 5 environments, such as that found within properly managed Biosafety Cabinets (BSCs), are typically considered acceptable for open aseptic operations, as they meet this criterion. Even within BSCs, proper cleaning, operations, technique, preparation, gowning, staging, etc., and surrounding environment are required to maintain the Grade 5 condition.

Independent of the strategy used, the overall BSC management plan should include:

  • A well designed cleanroom housing the BSC. This design will consider:
    • The BSC
    • The HVAC (location of HEPA and air return) that mitigates risk of "rogue" unfiltered air currents in environment immediately surrounding the open face of the BSC
    • Adequate air change per hour rate
    • Location and design of airlock accesses
  • Restriction of the number of personnel occupying the room
  • Restriction of the amount of equipment located in room
  • Appropriate gowning of personnel
  • Proper aseptic technique in the BSC
  • Effective cleaning and sanitization of the BSC

For further information, see the ISPE Good Practice Guide: HVAC [58].

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Biosafety Cabinet (BSC): The BSC is core equipment for cell culture laboratories and seed preparation. It uses vertical laminar airflow and HEPA filtration to create a Grade 5 (ISO 5, equivalent to EU GMP grade A) micro-environment at the work surface.

The danger of "rogue" air currents: If the HVAC is poorly designed, uncontrolled eddies or turbulence in the room can disrupt the laminar flow near the BSC work opening and carry particles/microorganisms from the surrounding environment into the BSC opening. This is the hazard of "rogue air currents".

Key Note

BSC ≠ unlimited Grade 5 protection: Even when working inside a BSC, a Grade 5 environment is not automatically achieved. It requires correct aseptic technique, appropriate gowning, regular cleaning and disinfection, and good surrounding environment design (HVAC, airtight doorways). The BSC only provides the potential; operator behavior determines the actual result.

The importance of personnel numbers: Every additional person in the room housing the BSC adds particle and microbial load. This is why the number of people entering a BSC room is usually strictly limited, and non-essential personnel are kept out during use.

Practice Questions

  1. What problems could arise if the HVAC return air grille in a BSC room is poorly positioned (for example, directly facing the BSC opening)?
  2. In a CDMO, the seed train is usually run in a BSC. If you want to move from a BSC to a closed shake flask culture system, what design changes need to be considered?
4.3.2 Strategies for Closing Bioprocess Operations

Original Text

The goal for closing systems is to isolate the system product contact surfaces from the surrounding environment. Available technology allows systems to be closed or isolated from the environment and by default, from each other. Effective isolation can be achieved without the need for walls or segregation of the HVAC controlling the environment housing these operations. This strategy has previously been supported by regulatory agencies (e.g., process and utility piping have traditionally and typically been housed in uncontrolled interstitial spaces).

"Transfers between unit operations are considered critical to a successful bioprocess. Figures 4.2 and 4.3 show mixed piping systems that are routinely routed through uncontrolled interstitial (or 'gray') space because these critical systems can be shown to be isolated from the environment and from each other. The environment in these uncontrolled spaces represents a non-critical aspect to the transfer operation, because the critical transfer piping is closed."

The process transfer piping systems, routed through the uncontrolled interstitial spaces, represent extensions of the process equipment to which they are attached.

[Figure 4.2🔍: Closed Piping through Non-classified Mechanical Spaces]
[Figure 4.3🔍: Closed Piping through Non-classified Mechanical Spaces (3D Diagram)]

This strategy has been extended to closed buffer hold vessels that may be housed in Controlled Not Classified (CNC) or uncontrolled spaces. Although housed in an uncontrolled environment, buffer hold vessels are rendered closed prior to use by effective cleaning and sanitization.

Organizations should demonstrate that their process equipment is appropriately closed (and validated as such) or the agencies will continue to expect it to be housed in classified cleanrooms.

Advances in bioprocessing include technologies that provide effective isolation of process from the environment. For closed operations, the environment becomes a property or parameter with no critical aspect according to Quality Risk Management (QRM). For a process or system to be considered closed, the product contact surfaces and process environment needs to be completely isolated from the external environment during all manipulations, including setup, transfers, sampling and other process operations.

Open systems can be rendered closed with appropriate preparation, including cleaning and sanitization operations. Risk assessments would show that isolation of the product and process from the environment represents lower risk to product quality by eliminating a significant source of hazards. The elimination of the environment as a parameter having a critical aspect can have significant impact on the process design, on the process equipment layout, and on the overall facility design.

[Figure 4.4🔍: Open versus Closed (Ease of Contamination Figure)]

This is independent of the nature of the process or classification of the environment. As an example, bioreactors and fermenters that have been properly steamed-in-place and are appropriately closed and controlled have been shown to be capable of maintaining an axenic culture for months.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Controlled Not Classified (CNC) space: An environment between a "full cleanroom" and an "uncontrolled space": it has basic environmental monitoring and maintenance requirements, but does not need to meet a specific ISO cleanroom class. A closed buffer storage tank can be placed in a CNC space precisely because its closure makes the environmental classification non-critical.

Regulatory acceptance of interstitial space: Routing biopharmaceutical plant piping through unclassified mechanical interstitial space (grey space) has long been accepted by regulators. The reasoning is that the piping is closed, so the environment has no effect on the product inside. This "grey space rationale" is one of the closure strategies of Vol.6.

Analogy

Closed piping through grey space = a water pipe through a wall: When the water pipe in your home runs through walls and ceilings, you do not demand that the whole wall meet food hygiene standards, because the water flows inside a closed pipe. Process piping in a biopharmaceutical plant passing through an unclassified mechanical interstitial space follows exactly the same logic: what matters is the closure of the pipe itself, not the cleanliness of the surrounding walls.

The "extension" concept for equipment: Each section of closed piping connected to a bioreactor is regarded, for regulatory purposes, as an extension of the bioreactor itself, and enjoys the same closure protection. As long as the piping itself is closed, the classification of the external environment is irrelevant.

Key Note

The revolutionary significance of "the environment becomes a non-critical parameter": The most important sentence in Vol.6 is that, for closed operations, "the environment becomes a non-critical parameter in quality risk management". The regulatory implications are:

  • Closed equipment does not need to be placed in a cleanroom → construction cost is greatly reduced
  • Reduced environmental monitoring requirements → lower operating cost
  • Greater facility design flexibility → more equipment can be housed in a smaller cleanroom

The precondition: Adequate documentation and validation must be in place to "prove" closure; otherwise regulators will still require a cleanroom environment.

Practical Application

The business significance of CDMO facility design: If a CDMO plans to build a 5,000 L stainless steel bioreactor suite, the options are:

  • Option A (traditional thinking): Design the entire bioreactor suite as a Grade 8 (grade C) cleanroom. Construction cost is very high, the HVAC system is complex, and operating cost is high.
  • Option B (Vol.6 strategy): The bioreactor system undergoes full closure verification (SIP, pressure hold test, filter integrity test) and is placed in a CNC space. Grade 8 cleanrooms are built only in areas that truly require open operations (such as the sampling station and seed preparation room).

Option B can save millions to tens of millions of US dollars in facility construction cost, which is precisely the core business value of Vol.6.

Practice Questions

  1. For a 2,000 L bioreactor that has passed SIP validation and is placed in a CNC space, what ongoing monitoring measures are needed to maintain regulatory compliance of its "closed" status?
  2. If you were a regulatory reviewer, what documentation would you require to confirm that a "closed system" truly meets the closure standard?
4.4 Demonstrating that Processes are Closed

Original Text

4.4.1 Performing a Closure Analysis

This section provides an example of how risk assessments can be used to demonstrate that a process has been successfully isolated from the surrounding environment and that the environment no longer represents a critical aspect of this process.

Changes to a process or processing strategy should be systematically evaluated, e.g., by using a risk management tool and pre-established criteria. The tool should mitigate bias influencing the risk ranking of a hazard. The primary goal of a closure analysis is to confirm that a process unit operation can and/or should be removed from a cleanroom environment of higher classification to a controlled non classified suite. Closure analysis is a type of risk assessment that should focus on the probability and severity of exposing a process system to the surrounding environment before, during, and after the process operation. The scope of the risk assessment should be restricted to potential impact by the environment and should omit other process improvement initiatives.

Closure analysis can provide an opportunity to:

  • Evaluate and improve manufacturing processes
  • Reduce risk
  • Evaluate new and improved technologies
  • Reduce failures
  • Increase productivity

These improvements can lower the cost of manufacturing, improve consistency, increase reliability and provide a more predictable level of product quality.

For a process unit operation, all penetrations and operations that can influence the integrity of the system should be considered. Figure 4.5🔍 shows penetrations and operations that should be considered for a stainless steel bioreactor used in a cell culture operation.

[Figure 4.5🔍: Stainless Steel Bioreactor Used in a Cell Culture Operation - shows all penetration points and operating points that may affect closure]

The steps listed in Figure 4.5🔍 represent opportunities for breach of the closed integrity of the bioreactor system. The closure analysis should consider these steps and determine whether the methodology used in the step or subsequent steps adequately protects the integrity of the closed system. Stainless steel bioreactor systems require significant manual intervention during setup. The insertion of instrumentation into lower probe belts and assembly of vent and sterile gas filters can result in particulate and bioburden contamination of the vessel and system. The risk may be adequately mitigated by performing SIP on the system prior to use.

"This risk cannot be mitigated by housing an aseptic system in a Grade 8 or Grade 7 environment. Only Grade 5 should be considered acceptable for an open aseptic system."

A hot WFI rinsing and sanitizing rinse after manual insertions, but prior to SIP, may be needed to mitigate the risk of particulate contamination within the vessel chamber. Bioburden present in the system after human intervention is usually removed using thermal sanitization and particulates should be rinsed away with condensate in a self-draining system. Following SIP, the system should be maintained under constant positive pressure by supplying sterile filtered gas. Dissolved Oxygen (DO) and pH monitoring prior to inoculation can be used to confirm success of the SIP process in rendering the bioreactor closed.

This evaluation should be repeated for all unit operations in a process.

[Figure 4.6🔍: Phases of Risk Assessment Analysis]
[Figure 4.7🔍: Closure Analysis Risk Assessment Workflow – Phase 1 (Process understanding, PFD/P&ID assembly, system boundary definition)]
[Figure 4.8🔍: Closure Analysis Risk Assessment Workflow – Phase 2 (Criteria listing, closure risk ranking vs. pre-established criteria)]
[Figure 4.9🔍: Closure Analysis Risk Assessment Workflow – Phase 3 (Mitigation options for unacceptable risk)]

The workflow should be used only for process contact operations. In the program depicted:

  • Open aseptic operations would represent the operations or steps of highest risk
  • Performing controlled bioburden operations using sterile gamma irradiated single use systems would represent the lowest risk of contamination from the environment; however, other higher value options are available that also represent acceptable low risk

Phase 3 of the closure analysis evaluates options for mitigation of unacceptable risk due to insufficient product protection or process closure.

[Figure 4.10🔍: Fault Tree Flow Diagram - the fault tree used for the final assessment of closure integrity]

At the end of this workflow, only those elements, steps, or operations representing a moderate or high risk of contamination from the environment should be further evaluated in the final assessment. Should contamination from the environment be a possibility, the fault tree approach shown in Figure 4.6🔍 could be used for final assessment of adequate closure.

The final assessment addresses the needs of the process in a qualitative manner. The purpose is to determine whether it is more appropriate to close a process by upgrading the process system or to maintain status quo by leaving the operation open and housing it in an appropriately classified environment. A closure analysis risk assessment should show that most aseptic processes used in the manufacture of drug substances are closed. Exceptions may include inoculum preparation processes and seed cell expansion when shaker flasks are used. When closing the process is not possible, the operation should be housed in an appropriate environment.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The essence of Closure Analysis: Closure Analysis is a dedicated risk assessment focused on the "interaction between the environment and the process system". What makes it unique is that:

  • Its scope is deliberately limited to "environmental impact", and does not cover other process improvements
  • Its goal is to "remove the criticality of the environment", lowering the cleanroom grade requirement
  • Its result is the basis for the decision on "whether the grade can be lowered from Grade X to CNC"

The three-phase Closure Analysis workflow (Phase 1-3):

  • Phase 1 - Process understanding: Collect the PFD (process flow diagram) and P&ID (piping and instrumentation diagram), define the system boundary and identify all penetration points.
  • Phase 2 - Risk ranking: Establish closure assessment criteria, rank the risk of each penetration point/operation, and compare against predefined acceptance criteria.
  • Phase 3 - Risk mitigation: For risk items judged "unacceptable", evaluate upgrade options (such as switching to a closed transfer system or adding SIP steps).

Analogy

SIP validation = steam-sterilizing the pipe connections, verifying closure on site without disassembly:

Think of the gas pipe connections in your home: you would not take a connection apart and send it for sterilization before every use. Instead you confirm in place (in-place) that the connection is sealed, using a special method (such as a pressure test or leak detection). SIP (steam-in-place) follows a similar logic: the whole piping system of the bioreactor, including valves, filters and sampling ports, is not disassembled; high-pressure steam (121°C or above, for at least 15 minutes) is introduced in place to achieve sterilization. At the same time it demonstrates that the system can withstand steam pressure without leaking, which in effect also confirms closure.

Key Note

The logic of maintaining positive pressure after SIP: Immediately after SIP, the system switches to "positive pressure maintained with sterile-filtered gas". The purpose is that even if the system has a tiny leak, gas leaks from the inside out (positive-pressure outflow) instead of particles from the external environment entering from the outside in. This is the last line of defense of a closed system and the mechanism by which closure is continuously maintained.

DO and pH monitoring as indicators of closure: The stability of DO (dissolved oxygen) and pH before inoculation can indirectly confirm the success of SIP: if the medium is contaminated by microorganisms, abnormal changes in DO and pH appear within a few hours. This is a clever "functional test of closure", using the chemical properties of the medium as a biological indicator of closure success.

Exceptions: shake flasks and seed preparation: Vol.6 candidly acknowledges that some operations cannot be fully closed by nature. Shaker flasks used for seed culture expansion are inherently semi-open systems and still require the protection of a Grade 5 (BSC) environment. This is an exception to the closure strategy, not the rule.

Risk Matrix Framework

Closure Analysis risk assessment logic:

Each penetration point / operating step → assessed on two dimensions:

1. Probability of Exposure
   - Operating frequency
   - Operating duration
   - Degree of personnel intervention

2. Severity of Exposure
   - Impact of contamination on the process
   - Is there a subsequent removal step?
   - Impact on patient safety

Risk ranking:
   High probability × high severity → must be upgraded (closed)
   Low probability × low severity → acceptable (keep as is)
   Medium risk → evaluate mitigation options in Phase 3

Final decision:
   Closure upgrade OR keep open + cleanroom environment

Practical Application

Practical steps of a CDMO Closure Analysis (using a 2,000 L SS bioreactor as an example):

  1. Phase 1: Obtain the P&ID from Engineering and mark all penetration points (probe ports × 5, sampling ports × 3, feed ports × 4, vent ports × 2, transfer connections × 6), 20 potential exposure points in total.
  2. Phase 2: For each penetration point, assess: is it covered by SIP? Is there an emergency shut-off valve? Are there integrity test records?
  3. Phase 3: The sampling port is found to be high risk during sampling (open sampling) → upgrade to a closed sampling system (such as SureSampler® or a steam-barrier sampling valve).
  4. Documentation output: Produce a Closure Analysis report to support the regulatory filing for moving the bioreactor into a CNC space (such as a CMC module update).

Practice Questions

  1. What is the logic of using Fault Tree Analysis for closure assessment? How does it differ from traditional FMEA?
  2. Vol.6 says "most aseptic drug substance processes should be demonstrably closed". Why is shake flask seed culture an exception? Is there a technical solution that could remove this exception?
  3. Is a bioreactor "Closure Analysis" equivalent to applying an ICH Q9 QRM tool? What are the similarities and differences?

4.3 Table: Degree of System Openness vs Environmental Requirements

Table Analysis

Key Pattern

The table clearly shows the core logic of Vol.6: the higher the degree of closure, the lower the environment grade required.

The highest-risk open aseptic operations require ISO 5 (grade A), while fully closed systems need only CNC or even an unclassified space. This "jump in environment grade" is the commercial return on closure.

Regulatory Consistency Note

The logic of this table is highly consistent with the CCS (contamination control strategy) framework of EU GMP Annex 1 (2022 revision), and echoes the principle emphasized in the FDA Process Validation Guidance that "engineering controls take priority over environmental controls". The "closure first" strategy of ISPE Vol.6 has become the mainstream international standard for modern biopharmaceutical facility design.

Section Summary - Chapter 4 Part A (4.1-4.4)

  • Process Closure is the core strategy of ISPE Vol.6: fully decouple the product-contact surfaces from the environment so that the environmental classification becomes a non-critical parameter, thereby reducing facility construction and operating costs.
  • The four contamination sources are: ingredients/consumables, environmental factors (equipment/air/personnel/utilities), cross-contamination (multi-product/multi-batch/CIP piping) and carryover (incomplete cleaning). Each category has corresponding risk mitigation strategies.
  • The onion-style layers of protection model is the knowledge core of this chapter: each additional layer of effective protection (primary containment → secondary containment → building segregation → environmental controls) allows the cleanroom requirement to be lowered by one level, ultimately enabling closed processes to be operated safely in a CNC or unclassified space.
  • Closure Analysis is a three-phase (process understanding → risk ranking → mitigation option evaluation) dedicated risk assessment tool. Its final output is the scientific basis for the decision on "whether the equipment can be moved to a lower-grade environment", and regulators will accept it only if it is supported by complete documentation and validation.
  • SIP + positive pressure maintenance + DO/pH monitoring is the standard combination of methods for confirming the closed state of a closed bioreactor; closure verification cannot be replaced merely by upgrading the environment grade.
  • Exceptions: Operations that are inherently difficult to close, such as shake flask seed culture, must still be performed in a Grade 5 (BSC) environment. "Closure first" is a strategy, not a slogan that lets one ignore scientific reality.
4B

Chapter 4B: Process Closure — Appropriate Environments

p42-p49

ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p42–p49

Learning Objectives

  • Understand why personnel are the highest-risk contamination source in open aseptic operations, and how process closure mitigates that risk
  • Distinguish typical open aseptic operations (e.g., cell bank preparation, inoculum preparation) from typical closed aseptic operations (e.g., cell culture, filtration), with definitions and examples
  • Master the risk assessment logic for sterile filtration system configurations (series, parallel), and understand the true meaning and limits of redundant filters
  • Understand the concept of "briefly exposed operations" (e.g., centrifugation, buffer preparation) and why such operations can be located in a controlled non-classified (CNC) area
  • Understand the principles for choosing the environmental grade for weigh dispense operations, and the business benefits of a centralized weigh dispense design
  • Be able to apply this section to environmental grade planning for a new biopharmaceutical facility, with a rationale you can present to FDA/EMA

Section Structure

4.5 Overview 4.5.1 Open Aseptic Operations 4.5.2 Closed Aseptic Operations 4.5.2 Filtration Systems 4.5.3 Low Bioburden Operations Centrifugation — Briefly Exposed Weigh Dispense Section Summary
4.5 Appropriate Environments Based on Risk — Overview

Original Text

Where equipment technology limits the ability to close operations, those operations will need to remain open. Personnel represent one of the highest risks as sources of bioburden and particulates. Open aseptic operations involving human intervention or manipulation, represent high risk conditions.
