Closed processing can help mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment and from the "human risk factor."

When open aseptic operations are used, an appropriately controlled and maintained environment with sufficient layers of protection (e.g., Grade A/5) should be used to house the operation.

"Closed processing can help mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment and from the 'human risk factor.'"

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Ultimate Purpose of Process Closure

Section 4.5 is the "payoff" section of Chapter 4. Having established the Layers of Protection framework in the earlier sections, it now answers a key question: what grade of environment does a process need at each level of closure?

The core logic is: the more closed the process, the lower the environmental requirement, and the lower the facility cost. This is not a shortcut; it is a risk management decision with a scientific basis.

Personnel are explicitly identified as the highest-risk contamination source. This also explains why isolator design for a grade A environment removes the operator completely from the product contact area.

Analogy

Setting the kitchen standard by the type of food:

You would not apply the same kitchen standard to vacuum-packed canned food and to raw fish. Canned food (fully sealed) can be processed in an ordinary environment; raw fish (fully open) must be handled in the strictest hygienic environment.

Biopharmaceutical processes follow the same logic: fully closed systems can sit in a CNC (controlled non-classified) area, and only open aseptic operations need the protection of a grade A/B environment. The environmental grade should match the exposure risk of the process, not a uniform highest standard.

Key Note

The importance of the human risk factor: even a grade A laminar flow hood under a grade B background introduces a potential bioburden source whenever people operate in it. Reducing personnel intervention is therefore itself an important element of the contamination control strategy (CCS).

This is also one of the fundamental drivers behind the trend toward automated, closed facility design.

Practice Questions

  1. If a process can technically be closed but at high cost, while open operation would be acceptable, how should you decide? What factors should the risk assessment consider?
  2. What specific facility design decisions follow from the statement that "personnel are the highest-risk contamination source"? (Hint: think about RABS vs. isolator.)
4.5.1 Example Open Aseptic Operations

Original Text

Examples of aseptic operations in biopharmaceutical manufacturing that are typically open include:

  • Cell bank preparation
  • Inoculum preparation

Cell Bank Preparation

The preparation of Master Cell Banks (MCBs) and Working Cell Banks (WCBs) are usually open operations. The process of selecting a clone and culturing it in preparation of the MCB, requires open manipulations that are normally performed in a Grade A/5 environment.

Inoculum Preparation

The initial stages in culture propagation may be performed in spinner flasks, shake flasks, roller bottles, or wave bags, etc. Depending on the cell bank configuration, the transfer of cryo-stored cells may be open or closed.

Following an incubation period, the contents of a seed culture container are transferred into a larger container for subsequent incubation and expansion of the culture. The opening of containers and transfer of materials are open operations and considered high risk.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts: Why MCB/WCB Preparation Must Be Open

The preparation of the Master Cell Bank (MCB) and Working Cell Bank (WCB) is currently difficult to close completely, for reasons including:

  • Clone selection requires visual inspection and picking from individual culture dishes, which is inherently an open operation
  • Filling and labeling cryovials requires manual handling
  • The initial inoculum volume is usually very small (µL to mL), which makes transfer through a tubing system impractical

These operations are therefore usually performed in a grade A/ISO 5 (Class 100) biological safety cabinet (BSC) or laminar flow hood.

Risk Analysis of Inoculum Preparation

Why is inoculum preparation a high-risk operation?

  • Wave bags (rocking bioreactor bags) are closed in themselves, but they must be opened for inoculation (unless sterile connectors are used)
  • Each passage step of the seed culture, transferring from a small vessel to a larger one, is a potential contamination window
  • The more passages (scale-up steps), the greater the cumulative risk from open operations

Industry trend: modern CDMOs tend to use pre-validated sterile connectors and single-use systems to make these operations "functionally closed" and so reduce the environmental grade requirement.

Analogy

Inoculum preparation is like a researcher culturing a precious microbial strain in the lab: you must carefully open the dish and transfer colonies, and every opening is an exposure opportunity. This is why such operations need the "cleanest kitchen" (grade A): the material is fragile and precious, and the process inevitably involves exposure.

Key Note: Regulatory Perspective

EU GMP Annex 1 (2022 edition) explicitly requires appropriate environmental control for cell bank preparation. When reviewing biologics BLA/MAA submissions, FDA and EMA pay particular attention to whether the environmental grade chosen for cell bank and inoculum preparation has adequate scientific justification.

Documentation point: your Site Master File (SMF) or facility design basis (URS/FDS) should clearly document the exposure risk assessment for these operations, as the documented basis for selecting grade A.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: a CDMO is establishing cell banks for a client's mAb (monoclonal antibody) product. Using a traditional BSC approach requires a grade B background plus a grade A cabinet, which means higher facility construction and maintenance costs.

Alternative: evaluate pre-sterilized single-use inoculum preparation systems (such as Cytiva ReadyToProcess or the Sartorius Biostat series) combined with validated sterile connectors. This may allow the background environment to drop from grade B to grade D, substantially reducing HVAC cost and environmental monitoring frequency.

4.5.2 Examples of Closed Aseptic Operations

Original Text

Set up and preparation of closed systems should be managed, validated, and monitored to confirm that system integrity is established and maintained throughout a bioprocess.

Examples of aseptic operations in biologics manufacturing that are typically closed include:

  • Cell culture and fermentation operations
  • Bulk fill
  • Harvest operations
  • Filtration steps
  • Sterile hold operations for media and buffer

Preferably bulk fill is closed, but may be open based on the final container closure needs (such as recapping).

Cell Culture and Fermentation Operations

Single use systems used in aseptic operations are received sterile (typically polyethylene based systems sterilized by gamma irradiation) at the manufacturing site. A sterilization certificate should accompany each system certifying the conditions used for sterilization. Setup and preparation of the system for use in cell culture operations should ensure that system integrity and continued isolation from the environment is maintained.

The closing of a traditional stainless steel bioreactor includes cleaning and thermal sterilization and maintenance of a positive pressure on the system post-SIP to mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment. CIP and SIP operations should be validated. Following pre-manufacturing validation, CIP and SIP operations should be monitored to ensure that the system is maintained in a validated state.

The connections of all transfer systems also should be demonstrated to be aseptic or closed. The introduction of particulates into an open bioreactor in any environment whether Grade 7, 8, or CNC is a probability; however, the risk to the manufacturing process is considered to be small following a properly executed SIP of the system.

The initial phase in an SIP sequence during heat up of the vessel includes the condensation of clean steam (equivalent to purified water) along the cool surfaces of the vessel, resulting in a rinsing of those walls. However, if this initial "rinse" of the vessel is insufficient, a WFI (or other purified water) burst prior to SIP could be performed to ensure removal of any particulates that could have settled on the product contact surfaces during the opening of the vessel. The SIP would subsequently sterilize the vessel rendering it functionally closed.

Bulk Fill

Fully integrated bulk fill – filtration systems are available. With the exception of materials that cannot be filter sterilized, it is recommended that all bulk fill operations are prepared using an integrated filter – container system. The product at this phase of the bulk drug substance manufacturing sequence normally has high value. Performing a bulk fill operation open in a Biosafety Cabinet (BSC) should be considered bad practice.

If sterile filtration is not an option for the bulk fill operation, the use of an isolator to protect the required zero bioburden (Grade A; Grade 5) processing environment is recommended.

Harvest Operations

In some processes, such as continuously harvested perfusion systems, the harvest process should be aseptic. Filtration and sedimentation technology is typically used as the cell barrier in a perfusion system. Filtration systems, centrifuges and cell settlers should be steamed in place (or single use) to functionally close the fermentation/cell culture – harvest integrated system. The integrated system should maintain its aseptic state throughout the perfusion process until final harvest at end of batch.

Sterile Buffer Hold Operations

Solutions that are not used within a short time period after formulation should be stored sterile, even if used in low bioburden non sterile operations. Sterile solutions (media and/or buffers) should be stored in sterile single use systems or in traditional stainless steel vessels that have been sterilized prior to use. Exposing solutions that are stored long term to the environment should be considered moderate to high risk, unless these solutions are bacteriostatic. Buffer hold systems should be closed and either single use or sterilizable to allow for long term storage of buffers. These systems should be closed to prevent contamination from the environment.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts: Three Ways to Achieve Closed Operation

The closed operations listed in Section 4.5.2 can be achieved in the following three ways:

  • Fully closed: single-use bags, gamma-sterilized before shipment, requiring only an integrity check on site before use
  • Functionally closed: conventional stainless steel bioreactors, where SIP (steam-in-place) plus positive pressure maintenance makes the system "functionally equivalent to closed" during use
  • Validated aseptic connections: validated sterile connectors (e.g., ReadyMate, Lynx) used to transfer materials between systems while keeping them closed

Analogy: Functionally Closed

Functionally closed is like "a port that is sterilized every time it is used":

Imagine a high-end aquarium system. It needs to be opened occasionally for maintenance (water changes, cleaning), but after each opening it goes through a strict disinfection procedure (SIP) that returns the system to a sterile state. This design, "it has ports, but the ports are protected," is the essence of functionally closed.

The SIP of a stainless steel bioreactor is exactly this mechanism of "sterilization protection at every connection." After SIP is complete, the system is effectively closed, so it can be housed in a non-controlled environment.

Key Note: The Importance of a WFI Rinse Before SIP

The source text raises a frequently overlooked detail: during the SIP heat-up phase, steam condensate does "rinse" the vessel walls, but this is not necessarily sufficient. If particulates have accumulated while the bioreactor was open (e.g., during inoculation or sampling), consider a WFI rinse before SIP.

This detail is often omitted from facility SOPs, but it can become an inspection observation during an FDA inspection, especially for bioreactors in grade 8 or CNC environments.

Key Note: Bulk Fill in a BSC = Bad Practice

This is unusually strong wording for the book. The background is that bulk drug substance (BDS) at this stage is usually already in its final purified state and has very high commercial value (a single batch may be worth millions of dollars).

Open operation in a BSC exposes the most valuable product directly to the operator's microbial contamination risk, and the protection of a BSC is far lower than that of a closed filtration-filling system. ISPE's clear position: using an integrated filter-container system is industry best practice, and there is no cutting corners.

Special Considerations for Perfusion Systems

Perfusion is a continuous culture technique in which fresh medium is supplied continuously while product (usually mAb) is harvested continuously. Unlike batch or fed-batch processes, harvesting in a perfusion system is continuous, so:

  • Harvest equipment (centrifuges, filters) must be fully closed and integrated with the culture system
  • A closure failure at any harvest step can jeopardize the entire long-running batch (sometimes lasting months)
  • SIP validation of the cell settler and ATF filter is a key element of perfusion systems

Practical Application

CDMO facility decision scenario: a CDMO is designing a new upstream bioprocessing facility containing a 2000 L stainless steel bioreactor.

If the SIP procedure can be fully validated and positive pressure maintained, this bioreactor can be placed in a grade 8 (ISO Class 8) or even CNC environment, rather than being built into a grade D (ISO Class 8) cleanroom. The difference between the two lies mainly in HVAC system specification and maintenance requirements.

The cost savings from this one decision: the HVAC system cost difference can reach 30-50%, and the environmental monitoring (EM) labor cost is also greatly reduced. For a facility with 5-10 bioreactors, the cumulative savings can reach millions of dollars.

Practice Questions

  1. A 2000 L stainless steel bioreactor has a validated SIP procedure and an installed positive pressure maintenance system, but environmental monitoring records show occasionally elevated bacterial counts. How does this observation affect your judgment of its environmental grade requirement?
  2. If bulk fill cannot use a closed system because of the final container design (a secondary capping step is needed), what alternative should you choose? What does ISPE recommend?
4.5.2 Filtration Steps — Closure Analysis of Filtration

Original Text

The assembly and setup of a filtration skid is normally an open operation. The system may be rendered closed by effective conditioning, such as flush and/or SIP as appropriate for the filtration step. The use of a 0.2 µm (absolute) or better filter may not in all instances result in the effective closing of a process.

The assembly, preparation, conditioning, and configuration of the filtration system can determine whether a filtration system is effectively isolated from the environment housing it. The aseptic integrity of a gamma irradiated single use filter can be compromised with improper setup. All process contact surfaces of a sterilizing grade filtration system should be sterile on the post filtration side of a filter element for effective closure. This also applies to steamable filtration systems. Upstream configuration of an absolute 0.2 µm filter can have little effect on the integrity of a sterile barrier (or system closure).

Example evaluation of a typical configuration of a solution sterile filtration system is shown in Figure 4.11.

Figure 4.11🔍: Typical Configuration of a Solution Sterile Filtration System
Upstream
(Non-sterile)
⚠️ Open Zone
→
0.2 µm
Absolute Filter
(Sterile Barrier)
→
Downstream
(Sterile)
✓ Closed Zone

Yellow zone (upstream of 0.2 µm): non-sterile — HEPA environment adds little benefit
Blue zone (downstream): must remain sterile — any breach = system is open

Figure 4.11🔍 (reconstructed): Typical Configuration of a Solution Sterile Filtration System — p44

The components of this sterile filtration system that are to the left (prior to, depicted in yellow) of the 0.2 µm absolute sterile filter are non-sterile. The activities within the yellow area are open or briefly exposed activities. Adding HEPA filtration to the yellow space to reduce the environmental bioburden and particulate levels (to Grade 7 or 8) will have little if any effect on the product quality or the ability to provide sterile media (depicted in blue).

The integrity of the closed system depicted in blue depends on the integrity of the 0.2 µm filter and all attached components to the right (downstream) of the filter element. Breach of integrity of any of these components will result in an open system that can be compromised by the environment. Validation of the integrity of the system is integral to successful closure.

The value of redundant absolute 0.2 µm filters in series in a sterile solution filtration process as depicted below should be evaluated from a risk mitigation standpoint. Manufacturers will use redundant filters in the event of failure or breach of integrity of a system. Possible reasons for failure in sterile filtration systems include:

  • Temperature or pressure excursions during SIP
  • Fouling of the filter element during the product filtration process
  • Improper installation of the filter

Post integrity testing should confirm that failure of the filter element has occurred. Re-filtration of the media or buffer or product solution may be possible. The solution may need to be discarded. Where fouling occurred in the upstream filter, removal or replacement of upstream filter may be allowable, dependent upon an organization's protocols. Where the error occurred during SIP, both absolute filter elements in the filtration system could be compromised; therefore, redundancy provides little benefit in terms of mitigation of risk.

If the filtration system fouls during operation, it is probable that the lead (upstream) filter fouled. Typically, protocol calls for replacement or removal of the lead filter. If these are positioned in series as depicted in Figure 4.12, the lead filter no longer represents the sterile barrier and is no more than a very expensive pre-filter. In addition, the SIP of the lead filter actually increases the risk of the overall operation. A more cost effective and lower risk option is to use a non-absolute 0.2 µm filter that is not steamed in place during the setup of the system.

Figure 4.12: Redundant (Series) Filter Configuration
0.2 µm
Filter 1
(Lead / SIP)
→
0.2 µm
Filter 2
(True Sterile Barrier)
→
Sterile
Product

⚠️ If SIP fails both filters → redundancy provides no benefit. Lead filter after fouling = expensive pre-filter only.

Figure 4.12 (reconstructed): Redundant Filter Configuration — Series (In-Line) — p45

An alternative is to position the filter in parallel in a lead lag configuration for true redundancy of the system as depicted in Figure 4.13.🔍

Figure 4.13🔍: Parallel Filters Configuration (True Redundancy)
Upstream Feed
↙        ↘
Filter A
(Active)
Filter B
(Standby)
↘        ↙
Sterile Product

True redundancy: if Filter A fails, switch to Filter B — SIP failure does not affect both simultaneously.

Figure 4.13🔍 (reconstructed): Parallel Filters Configuration — True Lead-Lag Redundancy — p46

The different options should be evaluated for each specific operation to determine the best configuration for the application.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The "Directionality" of the Sterile Barrier

Figure 4.11🔍 reveals a key way of thinking: a 0.2 µm filter is a "one-way barrier": it protects only the downstream side and does nothing for the sterility of the upstream side.

This concept translates directly into facility design decisions:

  • The connecting piping, valves, and sensors upstream of the filter do not need to be installed in a sterile environment
  • All components downstream of the filter must maintain sterile integrity, and a leak at any connection is equivalent to opening the system
  • Therefore, raising the environmental grade of the upstream area (e.g., from CNC to grade 8) brings almost no improvement in sterility assurance

Key Note: The Logic Trap of Redundant Filters

Many plants install two 0.2 µm filters in a series configuration, believing this is safer. ISPE points out the flaws in this logic:

  • SIP failure scenario: if the SIP temperature/pressure is exceeded, both filters are affected at the same time, so the redundancy is ineffective
  • Clogged pre-filter scenario: a clogged Filter 1 has lost its sterile barrier function and becomes an "expensive pre-filter," while Filter 2 is the real sterile barrier, and the problem is that you do not know whether Filter 2 is also damaged
  • Installation error scenario: both filters may be affected by an error in the same installation, so the redundancy is again ineffective

Conclusion: true redundancy requires a parallel configuration, so that the two filters experience stress under different operating conditions and provide independent protection.

Analogy

Series vs. parallel redundant filters = fire protection system design:

If you have two smoke detectors "connected together" (in series) but sharing the same power line, a power outage disables both at once, and the redundancy is false.

A truly redundant system needs two detectors on independent power supplies (in parallel), so that when one fails the other keeps working. A parallel filter configuration follows the same principle: two independent filtration paths, each undergoing its own SIP, without interfering with each other.

Filtration System Risk Evaluation Matrix

Filter failure cause           Series redundancy     Parallel redundancy
                                effective?            effective?
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
SIP temp/pressure exceeded     ✗ Ineffective         ✓ Effective
(both affected at once)        (shared SIP)          (independent SIP)

Pre-filter clogging            △ Partly effective    ✓ Effective
(downstream filter is          (pre-filter must      (switch to the
still the barrier)             be replaced)          standby path)

Installation error             ✗ May be ineffective  ✓ More effective
(same installation)            (simultaneous error)  (independent installation)

Filter defect                  △ Effective           ✓ Effective
(manufacturing flaw)           (unlikely that both   (switch to standby)
                               are defective)
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Conclusion: series redundancy is ineffective against the most common
            SIP failure scenario
            Parallel (lead-lag) is the true redundant design

Practice Questions

  1. A new-build facility engineer proposes upgrading the piping area upstream of the main filter to grade 7 (ISO Class 7), believing this increases sterility assurance. How would you rebut this proposal using the logic of Figure 4.11🔍? What are the cost implications?
  2. If your plant is evaluating adding a second 0.2 µm series filter to a buffer sterile filtration system, what questions would you ask to assess the actual risk mitigation benefit of this decision?
4.5.3 Low Bioburden Operations/Systems

Original Text

The risk of contamination from the environment of a bioprocess should be mitigated for systems qualified as low bioburden. Low bioburden systems that are closed do not need to be sterilized prior to use and it may not be feasible to sterilize some systems. For example, WFI distribution systems are not sterile, but are effectively closed. There is little concern of contamination from the environment of the WFI systems except at the Points Of Use (POUs) where WFI is collected or sampled. The risk of contamination from the environment at the POU can be mitigated by maintaining a constant flow and pressure on the distribution loop.

Examples of bioprocess unit operations that are usually open and typically are not (or cannot be) sterilized include:

  • Chromatography operations
  • Ultrafiltration TFF Operations
  • Virus nanofiltration operations
  • Buffer preparation
  • Media preparation
  • Centrifugation operations

Open Briefly Exposed Operations

Some open operations represent very low risk as these are transitional operations that are subsequently closed, e.g.:

  • Media preparation operations
  • Buffer preparation operations
  • Centrifugation operations

The exposure of a process solution to the environment should be brief and validated for each process. The concept of briefly exposed is depicted in Figure 4.14.

Figure 4.14🔍: Briefly Exposed Process (schematic)
Closed
System A
→
⚡ Brief
Open
Exposure
→
Closed
System B
(+ 0.2µm filter)

Brief exposure → closed again → downstream 0.2 µm filtration removes any acquired bioburden

Equipment in transition zone (e.g., centrifuge) can be housed in CNC environment

Figure 4.14🔍 (reconstructed): Briefly Exposed Process — p47

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Low Bioburden vs. Sterile

Low bioburden and sterile are entirely different concepts:

  • Sterile: theoretically free of any viable microorganisms (SAL ≤ 10⁻⁶), achieved through a sterilization process
  • Low bioburden: the microbial count is controlled (e.g., <10 CFU/mL) but complete sterility is not required, maintained through closure plus control

A WFI distribution system is the most typical example: under the pharmacopoeial specification WFI allows at most <10 CFU/100 mL, and the system maintains low bioburden through hot circulation (70°C+) or continuous flow rather than being in a sterilized state.

Criteria for "Briefly Exposed Operations"

Figure 4.14🔍 reveals a very practical conceptual framework: if an operation is "briefly open, then closed again, with a 0.2 µm filtration step downstream," it can be defined as a "briefly exposed operation," and its equipment can be located in a CNC (controlled non-classified) environment.

The three determining elements:

  • The exposure time is "brief and bounded"
  • The system is closed again after the operation
  • A sterilizing-grade filtration step (0.2 µm absolute) downstream serves as the final barrier

Analogy

Brief exposure = a short open stretch in a tunnel:

Imagine a pipe that is mostly closed (the tunnel) with only one short open section (a bridge), but at the end of the bridge is a "super filter gate" (the 0.2 µm filter). Any "passenger" (microorganism) entering at the open stretch is stopped at the gate.

So although the open bridge section carries some risk, the downstream filter protects it, and the bridge does not need to be built in the strictest environment; CNC is enough.

Key Note: Which Downstream Operations Cannot Use This Framework?

Limits of applicability of the "brief exposure" logic:

  • If the process stream cannot be 0.2 µm filtered after exposure (e.g., because it contains macromolecules or cells that cannot be filtered), this logic does not apply
  • Virus nanofiltration is a filtration step, but its purpose is virus removal, not bioburden reduction, so it needs to be assessed separately
  • Chromatography operations are not sterile in themselves; the resin is usually maintained in a low bioburden state and does not require sterile conditions

Practical Application — Downstream Process Facility Design

Recommended environmental grades for typical downstream process operations (based on ISPE Vol.6 logic):

4.5.3 Centrifugation — Briefly Exposed Operation in Practice

Original Text

Buffer Preparation — Risk Analysis

Most buffer salts used in bioprocessing are not sterile when processed during buffer preparation. The sources of bioburden and contaminants in a buffer preparation include contamination from the environment, including contamination from operators and the contaminants present in the buffer salt package.

Risk of contamination from personnel can be mitigated by protecting the buffer salts in protected containers, such as gamma irradiated sterile bag containers. An in-line 0.2 µm process filter for the removal of bioburden and suspended contaminants from the process stream is likely to be more effective than addition of terminal HEPA filtration and over gowning of personnel in the buffer preparation suite. Upgrading the buffer preparation suite to a Grade 7 or 8 may reduce a small portion of the bioburden load in a briefly exposed buffer solution; however, the overall impact may be insignificant when compared to that of adding an in line 0.2 µm filter.

Exposure time also should be considered in a buffer preparation risk mitigation strategy. If buffers are not processed and/or stored at temperatures below 10°C (50°F) or above 50°C (122°F), or processed under other bacteriostatic conditions, exposure time can be significant. Microbial contamination can double as quickly as every 20 minutes under ideal conditions; therefore, buffers that are not consumed soon after formulation should be 0.2 µm filtered. It may be necessary to 0.2 µm filter any buffer not fully consumed within 24 hours and store the buffer in sterile containers.

Centrifugation as Briefly Exposed

Centrifugation for cell culture harvest clarification represents another example of an open operation that can be closed or briefly exposed operation. It is possible to configure a centrifuge, whether disk stack or tubular bowl for full CIP and SIP; however, centrifuges are not regularly steamed in place. A preferred approach is to combine the best of available technologies to make centrifugation a briefly exposed operation.

In the briefly exposed option, an aseptic cell culture is clarified rapidly by centrifugation. The goal of the centrifugation is to remove 95% to 98% of the turbidity (i.e., cells, cell debris, and other suspended solids) in the harvested culture. The remaining turbidity is removed by depth filtration followed by 0.2 µm filtration. If properly executed, the filtration steps render the process cell-free (thereby closed) thereby effectively isolating the process stream from the environment and from any potential bioburden that may have been present in the transition equipment.

In this scenario, the product or process stream is very briefly exposed to the transition equipment. From a process risk standpoint, the centrifuge and depth filter are analogous to a transfer pump and representing extremely low risk of contamination from the environment to the process; therefore, this equipment can be housed in a non-classified environment.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Risk Mitigation Priorities for Buffer Preparation

ISPE offers a counterintuitive but extremely important conclusion:

Adding an in-line 0.2 µm filter > upgrading the room grade to grade 7/8

Why? Because bioburden in buffer has two main sources:

  1. Contamination brought in by the buffer salts themselves (the major share)
  2. Airborne contamination from the operating environment (exposure time is short, so the effect is relatively small)

Upgrading the room grade mitigates only the second source and is almost ineffective against the first. An in-line 0.2 µm filter, however, eliminates contamination from both sources.

Cost-benefit conclusion: the cost of upgrading a room from CNC to grade 8 is far greater than installing a 0.2 µm filter. This is the optimal allocation of resources.

Key Note: The Practical Significance of Microbial Doubling Time

The source text says that "under ideal conditions, microorganisms can double every 20 minutes," and this figure has important practical implications:

  • If buffer is filtered 2 hours after preparation, an initial 1 CFU/mL could grow to 64 CFU/mL (2⁶)
  • If it is filtered after 4 hours, it could already reach 4096 CFU/mL (2¹²)
  • Storage at 2-8°C significantly slows doubling but cannot stop growth completely

SOP design recommendation: set a "maximum hold time before use" for prepared buffer (e.g., 24 hours); beyond that limit the buffer must be re-filtered or discarded. This limit should be supported by a microbial challenge study.

Analogy: The "Brief Exposure" Status of a Centrifuge

A centrifuge is like an "elbow" in a water pipe:

Water flows from a closed pipe (the closed culture system) through an elbow (the centrifuge) into another closed pipe (the filtration system). The elbow is not closed, but the flow time through it is very short, and downstream there is a "terminal filter gate" (0.2 µm).

Therefore, which environment the elbow (centrifuge) sits in (CNC vs. grade D) has almost no effect on the sterility of the final product. This is why ISPE says this equipment can be placed in a non-controlled area.

Estimated Cost Impact of the Centrifuge "Brief Exposure" Logic

Scenario: design of a harvest facility containing 4 disk stack
          centrifuges

Option A: Grade D cleanroom (ISO Class 8)
  Construction cost: +30-50% compared with CNC (mainly HVAC)
  Annual environmental monitoring cost: ~$50,000-100,000 USD
  Annual validation maintenance: ~$20,000-50,000 USD

Option B: CNC (controlled non-classified) + validated filtration steps
  Construction cost: baseline
  Annual EM cost: ~$5,000-10,000 USD (basic monitoring only)
  Annual validation maintenance: ~$5,000-10,000 USD

Prerequisites (required basis for Option B):
  ✓ Depth filtration after centrifugation
  ✓ 0.2 µm absolute filtration after depth filtration
  ✓ Exposure time validated and brief
  ✓ Centrifuge cleaning procedure validated

Conclusion: savings in facility construction cost + $60,000-130,000 USD
            per year
            This is the direct commercial value of "process closure"

Practice Questions

  1. If a CDMO is evaluating moving the disk stack centrifuges in its harvest facility from grade D to a CNC environment, what scientific justification do they need to prepare to support this decision? (Hint: consider validation requirements, regulatory documentation, and filter integrity testing.)
  2. If buffer is not used within 24 hours of preparation (and has not been 0.2 µm filtered), how do you handle this deviation? What might be the root cause of the deviation?
4.5.3 Weigh Dispense Operations

Original Text

Weigh Dispense

When materials arrive from suppliers, the material outer packaging and transportation materials (e.g., pallets, cardboard boxes) should be removed prior to introduction of the material into the manufacturing area to mitigate risk of contamination of the process area environment. Materials received are usually not sterile, having been manufactured in controlled or uncontrolled and unclassified environments. Powders and other non-liquid ingredients should be kept out of the manufacturing areas. These materials should be introduced into the manufacturing process via closed transfer systems. Where closed transfers are not possible, minimizing open operations is preferred.

Weigh dispensing operations should be performed in clean and controlled environments. The use of a well-designed weigh booth will both protect the ingredient during open manipulations and can protect the environment from dust or other particulates that can spread into the facility. The weigh booth should provide a Unidirectional Airflow (UDAF) HEPA filtered air supply over the weighing area and can collect stray particulates in a dust collection system. These particulates could include viable organisms and could serve as carriers for viral contaminants. The risk of these manipulations increases with increased volume and number of manipulations.

The kitting of pre-weighed materials into closed transfer systems can further mitigate the risk of particulate contamination by removing the open manipulations from the primary process areas and by providing a vehicle for the closed transfer of materials into a process system.

Centralizing the weigh dispense area is typically more cost effective and can minimize the number of areas where dust formation can occur. Locating this function in a cGMP area near the warehouse area may provide better control of the raw material inventory as partially open containers can now be stored and controlled by the warehouse specialists. The centralized weigh dispense area can serve as the warehouse – manufacturing transfer point enhancing control of incoming materials.

Additional Considerations for Solids Handling Operations

Handling large volumes in a central location can be beneficial, as risks spills and dust generation are higher. Kitting of material in closed containers for delivery to the manufacturing area is usually preferred.

A central weigh dispense next to a warehouse can provide a better method for controlling partial lots. When a raw material container is opened, it typically requires special handling and storage to prevent adulteration of product.

If raw material additions are batch dependent, there is likely a minimum threshold volume that will be required for each batch. A secondary staging area may be used to address the smaller remaining volumes that may be required in the specific batch. Options include a two stage weigh dispense function or standardization of batch sizes (possibly with discard of product) to streamline all additions.

The classification of weigh dispense areas should be consistent with the quality of the materials weighed and the quality requirements of materials prepared. Buffer salts are typically manufactured in CNC or uncontrolled unclassified environments. Quality of the buffer can be dependent on the:

  • Quality of the water (e.g., WFI ≤10 cfu/100 mL)
  • Quality of the salt
  • Time of incubation, prior to sterile filtration or prior to use

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Three Purposes of Weigh Dispense Design

Designing a weigh dispense area must serve three purposes at the same time, which sometimes conflict with one another:

  1. Protect the material: prevent environmental contamination of the raw material during weighing
  2. Protect the environment: prevent dust (including biologically active or viral vector particles) from spreading into the production area
  3. Protect the operator: prevent raw material dust (especially from high-potency APIs) from affecting employee health

The design of a weigh booth is therefore not only a GMP issue but also an occupational safety (OEL) issue. HEPA filtration, unidirectional airflow (UDAF), and a dust collection system are the core design features that address all three purposes.

The Strategic Value of Kitting

Kitting means pre-weighing and dispensing all the raw materials needed for a batch into closed bags/containers, then transferring them into the production area in a closed manner. Its benefits include:

  • Concentrating all open weighing operations in one controlled weighing room, rather than weighing separately in multiple production areas
  • Weigh once and pack closed; in the production area the bag only needs to be opened and added to the system, minimizing open operations in production
  • The batch record is clearer, and each batch's kit corresponds to a batch number, giving stronger traceability

Analogy

A centralized weigh station = the supermarket's pre-packing area:

A supermarket does not let customers go to the warehouse and cut their own beef; the meat is pre-packed in a controlled back area, and customers just pick up the sealed package.

A centralized weigh station means the same for a CDMO: raw materials are "pre-packed" in a HEPA-protected weighing room, and production operators simply collect the sealed kit, which minimizes dust exposure and open operations in the production area.

Key Note: Weigh Area Grading Is Based on the End Use of the Material

The source text stresses that the environmental grade of the weigh area should be consistent with the quality requirements of the material being weighed, not a uniform highest standard.

Practical grading principles:

  • Buffer salts: usually weighed in a CNC or non-controlled environment, because 0.2 µm filtration follows downstream
  • Media components: consider whether a sterilization step follows downstream; if so, CNC is acceptable
  • Raw materials added directly to a sterile system: require a higher level of protection (grade D or above)

Key factor: water quality + salt quality + hold time after preparation together determine the quality of the final buffer, and the environmental grade is only one (and usually not the most important) of the factors.

Cost-Benefit Framework for a Centralized Weigh Station

Distributed weighing (a weigh booth in each production area):
  - Dust risk in several areas -> environmental monitoring needed in
    many places
  - Partial quantities of materials need to be stored at several
    points -> complex inventory management
  - Each weigh booth needs its own maintenance and validation
  - Production staff need training in weighing operations

Centralized weigh dispense:
  - Only one high-specification weighing room to build and maintain
  - Inventory is concentrated next to the warehouse -> partial
    quantities are easier to manage
  - Only a few dedicated weighing staff need training
  - Production areas receive only sealed kits -> reduced complexity
    in production areas

Recommended location: immediately adjacent to the warehouse
Reasons:
  1. Raw materials do not have to cross the production area to reach
     the weigh station
  2. Opened containers with unused material can return to warehouse
     control immediately
  3. Serves as a material transfer control point between warehouse
     and production

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO is designing a new DSP (downstream processing) facility. They need to set up a weigh area for buffer preparation. Based on ISPE Vol.6 logic, what should the environmental grade of the weigh area be? What documentation do you need to support this decision?
  2. If kits of buffer salts must be stored in the production area for 3 days after kitting before use, what is the impact on weigh booth design (sealing requirements) and on production area management?
Comprehensive Risk-Based Environment Matrix (Section 4.5 Summary)

The matrix below consolidates the environment grade recommendations from Section 4.5 for all operation categories and can serve as a quick reference for facility design and regulatory submissions.

* Every grade selection requires a documented risk assessment as its basis, recorded in the facility design documents (e.g., FDS, URS), ready for FDA/EMA review.

Section 4.5 Summary

  • Personnel are the highest risk: in open aseptic operations, personnel are the main source of bioburden and particulate contamination; process closure is the core strategy for mitigating the "human risk factor"
  • Typical open vs. closed examples: MCB/WCB preparation and inoculum preparation are necessarily open operations and require a grade A/B environment; cell culture, harvest, filtration, and buffer storage can be closed and can be placed in a lower grade or even a CNC environment
  • The 0.2 µm filter is a "directional barrier": it protects only the downstream side, so upgrading the upstream environmental grade contributes almost nothing to sterility assurance, a common source of wasted resources in facility design
  • Redundant filters need to be in parallel (not in series): series redundancy offers very limited protection in an SIP failure scenario; only a parallel (lead-lag) configuration provides true redundant protection
  • "Briefly exposed operations" can be placed in CNC: operations such as centrifugation and buffer preparation can be located in a non-controlled area as long as the exposure time is short and 0.2 µm filtration follows downstream, significantly saving facility cost
  • Buffer preparation: in-line filtration > upgrading the room grade: the benefit of installing an in-line 0.2 µm filter far exceeds that of upgrading the buffer preparation room to grade 7/8, which is the optimal allocation of resources
  • Centralized weigh station design: concentrating weighing near the warehouse, with UDAF + HEPA + dust collection and a kitting strategy, minimizes open operations and environmental control requirements in the production area
  • Documentation is key: every decision to lower an environmental grade needs a documented risk assessment and scientific justification, ready for FDA/EMA review; this is the necessary step in turning technical logic into regulatory acceptance
5

Chapter 5: Operations

p49-p61

ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p49 – p61

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the time-based and space-based segregation strategies in multi-product facilities, and explain the differences between campaign manufacturing and concurrent production, including their cleaning validation requirements.
  • Understand the design advantages of single-use systems (SUS) (no CIP/SIP, smaller facility footprint) and their risks (supply chain dependency, extractables and leachables, E&L).
  • Analyze how closed systems and open systems affect environmental classification, gowning requirements, cleaning programs, and staffing levels.
  • Understand how automation, manufacturing execution systems (MES), procedural standardization, and operator training improve consistency and compliance in biopharmaceutical facilities.
  • Evaluate how capacity, scheduling, maintainability, and supply chain strategy affect the overall performance of a biopharmaceutical facility.
5.1 Introduction

Original Text

This chapter addresses the operational aspects of a biopharmaceutical facility and the impact of facility and equipment design decisions on manufacturing operations. Conversely, this chapter also describes how operability and maintainability considerations should influence the design of the facility.

Newer or updated facilities may enable more closed processing and less physical segregation. In conjunction with higher titers and smaller markets, allowing for increased use of single use systems with less cleaning and SIP. This can result in lower production labor requirements.

For information on segregation and flows, see Chapters 5 and 6 of this Guide.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Operability: whether the equipment and facility allow operators to carry out routine manufacturing work safely and efficiently.

Maintainability: whether the equipment can readily undergo preventive maintenance, calibration, and parts replacement without long periods of downtime.

The central proposition of this chapter is "two-way influence": design affects operations, and operational needs should in turn be reflected at the design stage. This is a concrete expression of the early involvement that ISPE emphasizes.

Analogy

Designing a facility is like building a kitchen. If the chefs' workflow and the equipment maintenance needs are not considered during design, the chefs will be stuck every day they work. This chapter exists to ensure that "when the kitchen is built, the chefs take part in the design discussions."

Key Notes

Higher titers mean the same quantity of drug can be produced in a smaller bioreactor, which is a key driver for the wider adoption of single-use systems (SUS). Small-batch processes suit SUS better, while large-batch processes still rely mainly on conventional stainless steel equipment.

5.2 Multiple Product Operations

Original Text

Multiple product operations require product separation either on a time (campaigned operations) or space (concurrent operations) basis through engineering design and procedures. Facilities may be required to run several campaigned or concurrent products.

Campaigned Product Operation

A campaigned product operation separation is based on time (producing one product at a time in a single space). For campaigned products, prevention of cross-contamination through cleaning validation, changeover procedures, and clearance between product runs should be demonstrated.

Clearance should be demonstrated by removal of product, components, and waste residues from process equipment. Clearance procedures should be appropriately tested to ensure compliance with regulatory expectations. Cleaning endpoints should be established using scientific reasoning. Dedicated equipment may be used.

Concurrent Product Operations

Concurrent product operations can be performed using spatial segregation to prevent product cross-contamination. A single product (dedicated facility) is an example of spatial segregation. These operations are usually used in non-product specific activities, such as media preparation or buffer preparation. They also can be used for product specific steps when closed systems are demonstrated. Concurrent production focuses on physical segregation (i.e., using closed systems) and procedural control to ensure the quality and safety of the products produced.

Campaigned production requires the ability to perform quick changeovers. Concurrent production of different products requires the ability to demonstrate an acceptable risk of crossover of one product to another.

The use of closed systems can minimize the risk of release of product fluids into the room environment during operation. Closed systems also reduce the risk of contamination via exposure of product to the external environment. This concept allows for side by side concurrent production of multiple products, e.g., for cell culture bioreactor spaces.

Where open operations are necessary, such as in seed preparation, BSCs within seed preparation suites can be used. In general, these operations are limited to a single product in a single room at one time. A risk assessment may be performed to determine when multi-product operations within separate BSCs can occur in the same room at the same time.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Campaign Manufacturing: different products are made in the same space at different times. Before changeover, cleaning validation must be completed and residues confirmed to be within acceptable limits.

Concurrent Production: different products are made at the same time in different spaces (or within closed systems). This relies on physical segregation or procedural control.

BSC (Biological Safety Cabinet): a local containment device that protects both product and operator during open processing, commonly used for open steps such as seed preparation.

Analogy

Campaign manufacturing = a restaurant that takes one booking at a time: French cuisine in the morning, then the kitchen is cleaned and the tableware changed in the afternoon, then Japanese cuisine in the evening. Every switch requires thorough cleaning, otherwise residues from the previous batch (cross-contamination) will affect the next one.

Concurrent production = cooking several dishes at once in separate pots: different products run at the same time in physically separate closed vessels without interfering with each other, provided the sealing is good enough.

Key Notes

Cleaning validation is the core compliance requirement of campaign manufacturing: the cleaning endpoint must be established scientifically, and residues (previous product, cleaning agents, microorganisms) must be verified to be below acceptable limits. Elastomers (rubber gaskets, hoses, diaphragms) need particular attention because they can absorb and then release residues.

Practice Questions

  1. Your CDMO facility is taking on biopharmaceutical products from three clients at the same time. Would you recommend campaign mode or concurrent mode? Explain your reasoning and the technical conditions required.
  2. In campaign manufacturing, what specifically do the regulatory requirements for "clearance" cover? Is testing only stainless steel surface residues sufficient?

Original Text (continued)

Multiple product operations require a facility to be flexible to allow for easy and quick changeover to new product processes or changes in product demand. Strategic approaches or levels of decisions to deliver flexibility include:

  1. Expandability: allows for future growth to incorporate changes, e.g., saving space to allow for building expansion. In the short term, this may be the least expensive option, while allowing for plans to deliver changes in demand or process changes.
  2. Adaptability: provides some pre-investment for future needs, e.g., shell space, local building services in the general area possibly with a valved service tie-in point. This allows for future modifications while reducing the effect on current operations.
  3. Flexibility: can provide utility connections to the planned location of new or modified equipment. This is the most costly and requires clarification of future needs.

Organizations may use platform processes for similar types of products to minimize changes required for new product changeover. Downtime consumed during changeovers reduces the operational time available to generate product. When running many short campaigns the changeover downtime can significantly reduce total throughput of a facility.

Automation changes required to bring a new product into a facility can be time consuming. Changeover to new product processes can be aided by the use of batch programming standards and recipe management strategies. Hard coded changes should be documented and re-qualified. Simple recipe changes can be verified.

Product changeover requires documented confirmation that residual product inside process equipment is removed and does not contaminate the subsequent product beyond acceptable limits. Cleaning validation strategies should include a rationale for the change-out of elastomers (gaskets, diaphragms, hoses) as part of product changeover.

Single use systems can eliminate the issue of lot to lot carryover. There is still a potential for crossover contamination; however, the components should be handled properly during system breakdown to avoid spillage post operation.

5.2.1 Clinical Trial Manufacturing

Clinical trial manufacture may require an early facility design and investment decision before having complete knowledge of a product or process. A step-wise incremental approach to GMP compliance, based on the stage of product development, may be used.

The level of process validation at each processing stage should be considered, particularly for the steps that have the greatest impact on product quality and consistency.

Clinical trial manufacturing can be more flexible and manual with more extensive process instrumentation and data collection to allow for the increasing knowledge of the product process during product development. Extensive in-process testing should be used to collect additional information and to help to ensure acceptable product quality.

During product development, change management systems should be used to manage changes that affect the facility.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Three Levels of Flexibility

ISPE classifies facility flexibility into three levels, from low to high:

  • Expandability: saves money now by reserving space for future expansion. The cheapest option.
  • Adaptability: reserves shell space and utility connections, so later modifications have less impact.
  • Flexibility: utility lines are already routed to every anticipated location, ready for plug-and-play use. The most expensive option, but the fastest changeover.

Because CDMOs change products frequently, they generally favor a Flexibility design, while a start-up biotech with an early clinical facility may adopt Expandability first to save capital expenditure.

The Commercial Value of a Platform Process

Biopharmaceutical companies (especially mAb manufacturers) establish a standardized platform process so that new products can quickly reuse existing upstream culture conditions, downstream purification steps, and cleaning validation methods. This greatly shortens changeover time and validation cost, and is a key competitive advantage in the efficiency with which a CDMO takes on projects.

Analogy

Recipe management = a restaurant's menu system: the automation system only needs to "switch to a different recipe" to change products, without rewriting the whole program, which greatly reduces the software qualification workload at changeover.

Special Considerations for Clinical Trial Manufacturing

Clinical-stage facility design faces a chicken-and-egg dilemma: process knowledge is still incomplete, yet FDA and EMA require clinical supplies to be made under GMP conditions. The solution is step-wise GMP compliance: early clinical phases allow more manual operation and flexibility, and controls are progressively strengthened as process knowledge accumulates.

Practical Application

A CDMO is evaluating whether to accept a new phase 1 mAb project. The client's process data is not yet fully established, but they want production to start in 6 months. ISPE's recommendation is to adopt an Adaptability strategy for facility design and a step-wise approach for GMP compliance, while also putting in place a rigorous change management system to track the impact of process changes on product quality.

5.3 Single Use Systems

Original Text

New products produced at higher titers can be run at smaller scale to satisfy market demands. This has allowed for increased use of single use systems. This impacts on facility design by reducing or eliminating the need for CIP and SIP operations. It also can substantially reduce the demand for water and the volume of liquid waste.

Considerations for single use systems include:

  • Cost (purchase, transport, storage, setup, disposal)
  • Dependence on single sourced approved suppliers
  • Leachables and extractables

Facility design factors for single use systems include:

  • Compliant inventory management
  • Receipt, inspection, and release storage space
  • Availability of pre-use release testing

A bar coding system can be used to manage inventory.

When single use system components are contaminated with product solutions, they can be considered bio-hazardous waste. Proper post-use disposal should be considered and these systems may be incinerated to:

  • Address the biohazard concerns
  • Minimize risk of liability
  • Benefit from the fuel value of the plastic

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Single-Use System (SUS): a process system made up of disposable plastic materials (bags, tubing, connectors, bioreactor liners, and so on). It is discarded after each batch, so no cleaning (CIP) or sterilization (SIP) is needed.

Extractables & Leachables (E&L): chemicals in plastic materials may migrate into the drug product under certain conditions (high temperature, organic solvents), which are extractables, or gradually leach into it under normal process conditions, which are leachables, affecting safety. Testing should follow standards such as USP <661> and ISO 10993.

Analogy

SUS = disposable tableware vs. porcelain: conventional stainless steel equipment is like fine porcelain, which must be thoroughly washed and disinfected after every use, a time-consuming effort. A single-use system is like disposable tableware: use it and throw it away, with no washing step, but with concerns about the safety of plastic materials (E&L) and waste disposal.

Key Notes

SUS supply chain risk is one of the most underestimated risks: many critical SUS components (especially bioreactor bags of specific sizes) have only one or two suppliers, and a supply interruption (as occurred during COVID-19) can bring an entire production line to a halt. ISPE and FDA both recommend that manufacturers establish a supply chain risk assessment and a qualification program for backup suppliers.

The regulatory aspect of waste disposal: SUS waste that has contacted biological products (especially vaccine processes involving live virus) is biohazardous waste. It must be autoclaved or incinerated and must not enter the general waste stream.

Framework for Comparing the TCO of SUS vs. Conventional Equipment

Conventional stainless steel equipment cost =
  Capital expenditure (CapEx)
  + CIP/SIP system installation and validation
  + Purified water / WFI consumption
  + Cleaning validation (across batches)
  + Periodic replacement of seals

Single-use system cost =
  Purchase price of SUS consumables per batch
  + Storage space and inventory management
  + Biohazardous waste disposal fees
  + E&L testing costs
  + Supply chain risk contingency

Key point: the smaller the batch size, the greater the SUS TCO advantage
      At larger batch sizes (>2000 L), conventional equipment actually has the lower TCO

Practice Questions

  1. What are the key points of E&L (extractables and leachables) testing? At what development stage should a manufacturer begin assessing the E&L risk of SUS?
  2. If the sole supplier of your SUS bags suddenly announces it is discontinuing production, how should your CDMO respond? What preventive measures could be built in at the design stage?
5.4 Impact of Closed and Open Systems on Operations

Original Text

According to Good Engineering Practice (GEP), process systems should be kept closed, where possible, to avoid exposing products to the room environment. For some biopharmaceutical products (e.g., viral vaccines or live viruses), closed systems also protect operators from exposure to these products.

Where it is not possible to demonstrate that a process system is closed, environmental controls such as room classifications, local ventilation, and higher gowning practices should be used.

It is usually easier to design a well-understood, robust process system into a closed system:

  • Early stage development or clinical trial supply processes are usually less developed, less characterized and are more often open.
  • Early upstream processes (pre-bioreactor) are usually open and run under BSCs. Using single use systems and plastic weldable connections may allow these processes to be closed.
  • Upstream processes run in bioreactors usually require containment to avoid contamination and are usually closed.
  • Downstream processes have made progress toward closed designs (e.g., by using on-line analytical devices which allow for real time mainstream collection).

When closed systems are used, the arrangement and access to processing systems and areas can be simplified. Closed upstream systems may be located in the same room as closed downstream systems. Airlocks may not be required and can reduce the footprint needed to accommodate operations. Using single use systems can reduce the clean space footprint that requires stringent housekeeping procedures:

  • Closed processing systems allow use of less stringent area environments (CNC) which can reduce capital costs and operating costs associated with cleaning, environmental monitoring, and gowning. There is also a reduction in time required to gown in and out.
  • Where upstream and downstream systems are located in the same room, operators can manage both systems. Staffing levels may be reduced significantly (as high as 50% with proper cross training) when closed systems are combined with suitable automation.
  • Closed processing systems can reduce downtime required for product changeover, as room clearance is not required before the next product is introduced.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Closed System: the process fluid is completely isolated from the external environment, so protection does not depend on room classification. A closed system is its own primary containment.

CNC (Controlled but Not Classified): compared with ISO 5/7/8 cleanrooms, CNC areas need only basic environmental control (such as HEPA filtration and positive pressure), with no strict particle or microbial limits, so construction and operating costs are significantly lower.

Plastic Weldable Connections: SUS tubing is joined by thermal welding, avoiding open connection operations. This is the key technology for converting an originally open process step into a closed one.

Key Notes

The "50% staffing saving" from closed systems is a striking commercial impact: closed systems plus automation allow upstream and downstream operators to work in the same space, and with appropriate cross-training, staffing requirements can fall by up to 50%. For a CDMO this directly affects quotation competitiveness and operating costs.

Closing the downstream process is the current technology trend: conventional chromatography operations have many open connection points, but advances in on-line analytical devices and automated fraction collection are gradually moving downstream processing toward closed operation.

Analogy

Closed system = a fully sealed food processing line: milk travels from the farm to the carton through sealed piping all the way, so no "cleanroom" is needed because the product itself is protected by sealing. But if any step requires opening the system (such as sampling), that operation must immediately be carried out in a strictly controlled environment.

Practical Application

CDMO facility design evaluation: if a new mAb manufacturing facility adopts a fully closed system design, the cleanroom area can be reduced from 2000 m² to 800 m² (with the remainder as CNC), while environmental monitoring locations fall by about 60% and staffing by 40%. These savings can reach tens of millions of dollars over a 20-year TCO, which is the core business case for ISPE's closed-system design.

5.4.1 Closure

Original Text

Open processing requires physical segregation and appropriate control of the processing environment. For further information on classified spaces and physical segregation, see Chapter 6 of this Guide.

For closed systems, closure is provided by process equipment. Each closed system acts as the primary containment for its contents. Other closed systems then act as a second level of protection against contamination; two closed systems need to fail for the contamination to occur. Definition of what constitutes a breech, consequences of the breech, and the required actions to re-establish closure should be considered. Procedures to address breech of closure should be established.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Protection Against Dual Failure

The safety logic of closed systems: contamination requires two closures to fail at the same time. This is consistent with the philosophy of the contamination control strategy (CCS) in EU GMP Annex 1, where multiple barriers provide redundant protection.

Breach of Closure: any event that compromises the integrity of a closed system (such as a detached line or a leaking connector). A written SOP must define how to identify it, how to escalate it, how to assess the impact, and how to re-establish closure.

Key Notes

A "Breach of Closure SOP" is a common inspection item during FDA and EMA inspections. If a facility claims to use closed systems to lower its room classification requirements (for example by adopting CNC), it must be able to show that the response procedure for a breach of closure is adequate and has been practiced.

5.4.2 Cleaning and Housekeeping

Original Text

Housekeeping is considered an indicator of the quality of an operation. Processing spaces should be well organized. Specific housekeeping practices should match the risk to the product and the classification of the space. Classified spaces require frequent cleaning with potent cleaning agents to ensure compliance to classified space definitions. An environmental monitoring program should be established to assess the effectiveness of the area cleaning practices.

CNC can use reduced cleaning regimens. For further information, see the ISPE Baseline Guide on Risk-MaPP.

Suitable cleaning and housekeeping amenities should be considered, e.g.:

  • Refuse disposal points
  • Handling and storage space for cleaning supplies and equipment
  • Access to appropriate water for facility cleaning

In multi-product facilities, cleaning practices should help to prevent cross-contamination. Facility materials should be compatible with common cleaning agents.

Access to production areas should be suitably controlled.

Biopharmaceutical facilities generally do not need fumigation when processes can be demonstrated as contained. Decisions on fumigation should be based on an appropriate risk assessment.

Pest control programs are normally required by regulations. Raw materials used in biopharmaceutical processing, such as media components or sugars, can be subject to contamination by pests. Tight-sealing, self-closing doors, sticky mats, and UV lights at key facility entry points should be considered. SOPs should be established for the use of suitable rodenticides, insecticides, and fungicides.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Risk-MaPP (Risk-based Manufacture of Pharmaceutical Products): an ISPE guide that provides a risk-based framework for the design and operation of multi-product facilities, with particular attention to cleaning validation and personnel protection for highly potent APIs.

The intensity of the cleaning program should be commensurate with product risk and area classification. Cleaning requirements for CNC spaces are significantly lower than for ISO 7/8 cleanrooms, which is one important source of the cost savings from closed system design.

Key Notes

Pest control in biopharmaceutical facilities is often overlooked, but it is in fact a regulatory requirement. Culture media components (sugars, amino acids) are themselves pest attractants. In FDA 483 observations, facilities have received 483 citations for finding insects in production areas.

Material compatibility: facility surface materials (walls, floors, equipment housings) must be compatible with commonly used cleaning agents (such as sodium hypochlorite and hydrogen peroxide) and must not corrode or shed particles during cleaning.

Analogy

Housekeeping = a mirror of the facility's quality culture: before the formal inspection begins, FDA investigators often first observe the overall tidiness of the facility. A cluttered corridor or an unlabeled waste bin may catch the investigator's attention sooner than any documentation problem. "Housekeeping is considered an indicator of the quality of an operation" is the subtext of regulatory inspections.

5.4.3 Gowning

Original Text

Gowning practices should match the room classification and HVAC design capabilities. HVAC design criteria should match product and process requirements, and could be affected by the number of operators required in selected spaces.

For example, gowning practices generally have a greater impact on CNC spaces with monitoring or microbial limits than CNC spaces with no monitoring. The higher the room classification, the greater the impact gowning has on meeting environmental acceptance criteria.

In classified spaces, jumpsuits are typically used with hair nets, shoe covers or facility dedicated shoes, gloves, and face masks at the highest area classifications. Where engineering controls cannot be employed to reduce personal risk adequately, organizations should ensure that appropriate personal protective equipment (PPE) is used, e.g.:

  • Safety glasses
  • Safety shoes
  • Goggles and/or face shields
  • Protective gloves
  • Respirators

Closed systems allow for relaxed gowning practices, e.g., use of laboratory coats or scrubs as a worker uniform to enhance operator comfort. Gloves, shoe covers, or dedicated facility shoes and hair nets may be worn.

Organizations may require disrobing before gowning. This may contribute to improved comfort while gowned (less layers of clothing) and adds another level of disconnection between open production environments and the environment outside.

Costs associated with high levels of gowning include:

  • Investment in airlocks, changing spaces, and the gowning materials
  • Time spent performing the gown in and out procedures
  • Higher cooling demand for operator comfort
  • Performance issues working while gowned

Working in traditional production environments (heavily gowned in a white box) can result in fatigue and stress. Facilities should be designed to allow operators to spend the minimum time in processing areas, e.g.:

  • Processing terminals (HMIs) can be located in adjacent production administrative spaces, allowing operators to enter the processing areas only when needed for set up or sampling.
  • Full glass views can be provided into processing areas from adjacent production administrative spaces.
  • The proper use of controls can assist in minimizing the amount of time operators need to supervise production equipment.
  • Daylight and/or an outside view into the processing areas or adjacent non-classified support space can be provided.
"Figure 5.1: Non-Classified Production Support Space Adjacent to Production Operations — Used with permission from Novo Nordisk and NNE Pharmaplan."
"Figure 5.2: Maximum Use of Viewing Windows to Reduce Need to Enter Production Areas — Used with permission from Novo Nordisk and NNE Pharmaplan."

Tutorial Commentary

Analogy

Gowning = surgical gown vs. chef's uniform: in a fully closed process, operators can work safely in a chef's uniform (a lab coat), but in an open process (such as aseptic filling) they must wear a full "surgical gown". This is uncomfortable and tiring, and over the long term affects both work quality and personnel health.

Moving the HMI to the adjacent administrative area = a car with a parking sensor: the driver can understand what is happening behind without getting out, and operators can monitor process status without entering the cleanroom, greatly reducing the time cost of gowning and de-gowning.

Key Notes

The hidden cost of gowning requirements is often underestimated: an operator may spend 30-60 minutes a day gowning and de-gowning, and in a multi-shift biopharmaceutical facility the accumulated labor hours per year are considerable. In addition, fatigue in hot cleanroom environments is one of the important sources of human error that FDA has observed.

Special gowning culture in the Asia-Pacific region: some parts of China and Japan require a separate shoe removal room and an air shower. These facilities must be planned early in facility design, otherwise retrofitting later is extremely costly.

Figure Analysis

Figure 5.1🔍 & 5.2 (Novo Nordisk / NNE Pharmaplan case): these two figures show best-practice design for a modern biopharmaceutical facility. Unclassified administrative support space (equipped with HMI terminals) is placed outside the production area, and operators can monitor the process through large viewing windows, entering the production area only when necessary. This design minimizes cleanroom exposure time, and the ample daylight and views improve the quality of the operators' working environment.

Practice Questions

  1. If the facility design moves HMI terminals to an unclassified administrative area, which GMP systems need additional design consideration (such as 21 CFR Part 11 electronic records and cybersecurity)?
  2. How does the gowning level affect the environmental monitoring program? How do the sampling frequency and limit settings differ between CNC spaces and ISO 7 spaces?
5.5 Automation, Procedures, and Training

Original Text

Traditional facilities which needed to fully integrate and coordinate CIP and SIP operations used supervisory controls. The plug and play nature of recent facility designs focuses control on unit operations. These systems allow for process engineers and automation engineers to work with users to develop and implement appropriate process automation controls.

5.5.1 Impact of Automation

An increased use of automation should be driven by quality and safety improvements, e.g.:

  • Automation drives consistency of processing actions — things are done at the same pace and in the same order.
  • Automation allows for data input verification checks to ensure the entry is within expected ranges. Actions can be prevented when interlocks are not satisfied.
  • Control systems can collect and organize data into records to improve the ability to monitor processes and to evaluate performance with a goal to achieve incremental improvements.

Cost saving opportunities are provided through: faster operations, more predictable actions, less failed lots due to non-conformance to required procedures, lower production staffing levels.

Increased data collection and analysis provides an opportunity for demonstrating product quality through process controls. Statistical tools can help to monitor process performance and can aid in process decisions. Alert and action levels should be justified and established for key process indicators and critical process parameters.

Automation can be used to support the claim of closure and to maintain system closure. System specific interlocks may be used to ensure the isolation of closed systems and that a closed environment is effective until (and unless) it is intended to breach closure.

Integration controls can be used to ensure that two closed systems are not inappropriately connected to each other or to the same service. For example, two systems connected simultaneously to a CIP system; therefore, facilitating a cross connection between the systems.

Automation usually increases the need for detailed knowledge of process operations at an earlier stage in the design of a facility. Increased use of batch programming standards and recipe management tools can expand the flexibility of automation controls, and decrease the costs and time to execute changes to automation software.

Cleanability

Cleanability should be considered when locating computer screens and stations in classified spaces. Units should be sealed to allow for appropriate space cleaning practices. Sealed units may prevent the proper removal of heat generated by the enclosed electronics.

Wireless Connectivity

Wireless connectivity may help in the collection of processing data and in facilitating the addition of plug and play portable equipment into facilities. Tablet type hand-held devices may improve efficiencies and timeliness of operations, such as data entry or confirmation of actions, e.g., material additions or status of equipment using barcode labels.

Manufacturing Execution Systems

A Manufacturing Execution System (MES) can be used to oversee the local manufacturing operation. Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) / Process Analytical Technology (PAT) can be used to control and monitor the process at the skid level. The documentation of the process sequence via time date stamps can reduce the need for quality checks, allowing for faster release of completed batch records and lots of material by focusing on review by exception. MES can serve as the verification function, allowing quicker operations and reducing staffing requirements.

Scanning a material label before completing an addition operation can ensure that material is through its release process and is not expired. Scanning an operator badge could confirm that an operator's training to perform a task is current. Scanning an equipment label can confirm the equipment is within its cleaning window and any required maintenance has been performed. These functions can reduce the probability of a rejected lot.

Process Analytical Technology
