Process analytical technology, used to provide live operational data, is becoming more available and useful, e.g., improved understanding of bioreactor operations and tools to collect monitoring data. This information can be used to speed decisions and to increase the consistency of process streams. PAT provides continuous monitoring and control of processes which demonstrates the quality of the product produced. When PAT is used during product development stages, it can aid in earlier and greater process knowledge, improved regulatory submissions, and simplify technology transfer to clinical and commercial operations.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

DCS/SCADA (Distributed Control System / Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition): responsible for real-time process control at the equipment level (temperature, pressure, flow).

MES (Manufacturing Execution System): responsible for operations management at the batch level, including electronic batch records (EBR), material tracking, personnel training verification, and equipment status verification.

PAT (Process Analytical Technology): real-time monitoring of critical process attributes (such as cell density, dissolved oxygen, and pH), moving process decisions from "after-the-fact testing" to "real-time adjustment", in line with the QbD spirit of the FDA PAT Guidance (2004) and ICH Q8.

21 CFR Part 11: a US FDA regulation governing compliance requirements for electronic records and electronic signatures. MES and EBR systems must meet Part 11 requirements, including audit trails, version control, and access rights management.

Analogy

MES = a restaurant's POS system (order management system): the waiter scans ingredient barcodes to confirm stock, the kitchen serves dishes following a standard process, and the system automatically records the preparation time and ingredients for each dish. The electronic batch record (EBR) is the biopharmaceutical facility's "digital order ticket", replacing traditional paper records and turning QA review from line-by-line checking into review by exception.

Interlocks = a car's seat belt sensor: the car will not start without the seat belt fastened. In the process, if a precondition is not met (such as an expired cleaning hold time), the system automatically blocks the next step and prevents human error.

Key Notes

"Review by Exception" is the most important QA efficiency gain from MES/EBR: with traditional paper batch records, QA personnel must check every data entry line by line; an EBR system verifies data in real time during the process, and only out-of-range data needs manual review. This can shorten batch record review time by 50-70%, with a significant effect on a CDMO's lot release cycle time.

Interlock design for closed systems is the key to compliance: a facility that claims closed systems must ensure closure through automated interlocks. For example, a scenario in which a cleaning system is connected to two production systems at the same time must be prevented physically or logically, otherwise batch-to-batch cross-contamination could result.

Automation System Hierarchy

Level 4: ERP (Enterprise Resource Planning)
         ↕ Material/order integration
Level 3: MES (Manufacturing Execution System)
         Batch management, EBR, personnel/material/equipment verification
         ↕ Recipe download / batch record upload
Level 2: SCADA/DCS (Supervisory and Control)
         Process data acquisition, alarm management, trend recording
         ↕ Setpoints / control commands
Level 1: PLC / field devices (field controllers)
         Direct control of valves, pumps, sensors

PAT tools are usually integrated at Level 1-2,
with data flowing to Level 2-3 for decision support

Practice Questions

  1. What specific requirements does 21 CFR Part 11 place on electronic batch records (EBR)? What compliance risks arise if the audit trail function is not enabled?
  2. If data from a PAT tool (such as an on-line pH probe or a near-infrared spectrometer) shows the process is outside its normal range, how should the MES respond automatically? What is the operator's responsibility?
5.5.2 Procedures

Original Text

Standardized procedures across multiple products and facilities should help to drive consistency and cross training of operators. Where feasible, the facility design should use error-proofing features, using, e.g.:

  • Color coding
  • Physically matched connections
  • RFID
  • MES and an automation system — to replace human verifications such as proper setup of transfer panel connections

When claiming the benefits of closed systems, the definition and practices required to achieve closure should be documented, in addition to actions required to return an area to closed when a breech is encountered.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Error-Proofing (Poka-Yoke)

A quality tool from Japanese manufacturing (Poka-Yoke) whose aim is to prevent errors before they occur. In a biopharmaceutical facility this includes:

  • Color coding: different colors of tubing for different products or fluid paths, to reduce mix-ups
  • Physically keyed connectors: only the correct line can connect to the correct port (preventing misconnection of lines)
  • RFID: equipment, materials, and personnel all carry unique identification, and the system automatically confirms whether a combination is permitted

Key Notes

The transfer panel is one of the most common sources of human error in biopharmaceutical facilities: operators connecting the wrong valve at a complex piping panel is a deviation event that has actually occurred. A system that combines MES with RFID can verify the validity of each connection before the operator makes it, greatly reducing this risk.

5.5.3 Training

Original Text

Operators should be appropriately skilled with a broad knowledge base. Quality and technical support personnel can observe operations and be present in processing areas during operations. This allows for immediate consultation of these personnel when questions or deviations occur. Multi-tiered training can help to ensure safe operations.

Training methods used should include:

  • Review of written procedures
  • Classroom
  • Simulations of facility operations
  • On-the-job mentored/supervised training
  • Skills demonstration

The use of simulations can provide personnel with the opportunity to react to alarm conditions without an impact on product quality or safety. Operators should be trained to look for trends in data and other indicators that suggest a problem is developing before an upset occurs. Operators should be comfortable reporting observations or errors when encountered.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: A Multi-Level Training System

Operators in a biopharmaceutical facility are not like those in a small-molecule facility who only need to run fixed equipment. They need to understand cell culture biology, the concept of seal integrity in closed systems, the alarm logic of automation systems, and a culture of deviation reporting. Training is not a one-off event but an ongoing competence maintenance program.

Key Notes

"Comfortable reporting of errors" is the core of GMP culture: FDA and ICH Q10 both stress that manufacturers should build a "Just Culture" of non-punitive reporting, so that operators dare to report deviations and errors immediately instead of concealing them. Concealing deviations is a serious problem that recurs in FDA 483s and Warning Letters.

Simulation training: especially important for breach of closure response drills in closed systems, letting operators learn how to quickly recognize and respond to a loss of closure without affecting a real batch.

Practical Application

A CDMO has just built a new facility using closed systems, greatly reducing cleanroom area. However, this also means operators now have to manage both upstream (cell culture) and downstream (purification) processes. The recommended training program should include: (1) cross-disciplinary technical training; (2) hands-on drills in closed system integrity testing; (3) deviation scenario drills using simulated processes; (4) periodic skills qualification to ensure that cross-training remains effective.

5.6 Capacity, Scheduling, and Maintenance

Original Text

5.6.1 Capacity and Scheduling

In the past, production capacity was determined by the cycle time of the production bioreactors. Where existing facilities are not designed for the downstream challenge associated with processes with higher titers, bottlenecks can occur in downstream operations. The throughput of a facility can be dependent on the mix of products produced in downstream processes, as these are usually specific to each product.

Where existing facilities are designed for the higher titer processes, account can be taken of the associated higher downstream product quantities generated. This can be through the use of smaller bioreactors with a higher utilization rate. These require smaller chromatography columns and the associated smaller buffer volumes. This then allows for more flexible production planning, and managing of bulk drug substance inventories.

For multi-product facilities, the downtime for changeover can consume operational time. This can affect the annual output of a facility and emphasizes the need for an efficient, quick product changeover.

Facility capacity impacts also affect scheduling of production operations. Operational models should help to optimize a facility for specified needs and changes to the product mix or other constraints. Facility bottlenecks and capacity limitations should be identified and addressed. Support functions may be some of the first bottlenecks encountered and should be included in operational models. Support functions may include:

  • Buffer and media preparation and supply
  • CIP
  • SIP
  • Critical utilities, e.g., WFI

Modeling tools can help to determine the optimal routine pace of production. This can help in leveling of resources. A predictable routine can provide consistency to operations and allow an efficient routine to be established. Incremental improvements can be obtained, as well as quality stability which can help to minimize risk.

5.6.2 Operability and Maintainability

Simulation and data analysis can accelerate technology transfer from development to full scale manufacturing. Modeling also can be used to guide development in making processing choices.

Manufacturing, quality, maintenance, and other operations functions should be involved early in the facility and process design, and in the definition of the user requirements.

Adequate space should be provided for equipment and instrument access, removal, and maintenance. Any specific requirements for portable and non-dedicated equipment should be considered. Space should be provided for storage, and clean-out-of-place and steaming where required. Equipment status and usage history should be collected and readily available; bar coded labeling technology and electronic usage and maintenance records may be beneficial.

Using standardized and modular facility designs can help to reduce errors and improve cross training. Mirror imaged designs should be avoided, as the location of devices in this approach can lead to confusion and operator errors.

Operational upsets (excursions) should be anticipated and recovery procedures established. Recovery procedures should include:

  • A structured investigation to determine possible impact of the upset on product quality
  • Assignment of root cause and associated corrective actions
  • Documentation of the investigation and decisions
  • Any necessary follow up to ensure completion of agreed actions and effectiveness checks

Maintenance excellence programs have placed a focus on performance based maintenance activities over previous time-based practices, e.g.:

  • The use of vibration monitoring can predict equipment failures in advance, allowing for planned shutdowns and maintenance when parts are approaching failure.
  • Process elastomer (hoses, diaphragms, gaskets) replacement is an area for focus. Use off-line studies to determine life expectancy and sufficient history to replace these components when appropriate.
  • Production operator autonomous maintenance looks to place specific maintenance functions with the operations team.

Closed process design can allow maintenance to be performed side by side with production operations. Equipment should be decontaminated and cleaned prior to opening. Options to allow for phased operation shutdowns to allow for periodic maintenance should be considered during design. In general, closed systems can allow for increased maintenance without facility shutdowns.

Where shutdowns may be necessary, partial shutdowns rolling through the facility can be scheduled, as the last batch of product moves through the process. Months of lost production required to bring a facility back up from a cold start should be avoided.

Redundant generation systems for critical clean utilities, such as WFI and pure steam, may be provided; however, maintenance to facility distribution systems may require periodic shutdown.

Operational improvement techniques such as lean and six sigma can help to reduce waste and variability within processes.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Downstream Bottleneck: as upstream process titers rise sharply, downstream purification systems (chromatography columns, filtration membranes) become the limiting bottleneck on whole-facility capacity if they are not upgraded in step. This is the core challenge in capacity expansion at many older facilities today.

Performance-based Maintenance: compared with the traditional approach of replacing parts at fixed time intervals, deciding when to maintain based on the actual condition of the equipment (such as vibration frequency and differential pressure trends) avoids unnecessary downtime and wasted parts.

Autonomous Maintenance: derived from the TPM (Total Productive Maintenance) concept, it has operators take on basic daily maintenance work (such as cleaning, lubrication, and visual inspection) so that engineering and maintenance staff can concentrate on more complex preventive and predictive maintenance tasks.

Analogy

The scheduling challenge = making several dishes at once in a single kitchen: a cell culture batch is like soup simmering on the stove, which cannot be taken off the heat midway (the cells would die). Scheduling must arrange all other activities around these "uninterruptible batches", including equipment maintenance windows, cleaning validation, and changeovers.

Backup WFI system = a hospital's backup generator: WFI is the "lifeline" of a biopharmaceutical facility, and any interruption can cause several batches to be scrapped. Redundant design raises capital expenditure but is a necessary investment for a commercial facility with continuous production.

Key Notes

"Mirror image design should be avoided" is an explicit ISPE warning. When two adjacent rooms or facilities use mirror-symmetric equipment layouts, operators working in different spaces can easily become spatially disoriented, which can lead to valve operation errors. A modular standard design (with the same orientation in all spaces) is the safer choice.

The cost of a "cold start" is very high: if a biopharmaceutical facility is shut down completely (for example for a major refurbishment), re-establishing seed cell banks, validating cleanrooms, and requalifying equipment can take several months, with zero output in the meantime and losses of tens of millions of dollars. Rolling, staged shutdown for maintenance is therefore far better than shutting down the whole facility.

Key Concepts for Capacity Calculation

Annual output =
  Number of batches × output per batch

Number of batches =
  (365 days × proportion of effective working days)
  ÷ (batch cycle time + changeover downtime)

Changeover downtime ↓ → annual batches ↑

Example:
  Batch cycle = 14 days
  Changeover downtime = 3 days
  Annual batches = 365 ÷ 17 ≈ 21 batches

If changeover downtime is shortened to 1 day:
  Annual batches = 365 ÷ 15 ≈ 24 batches
  About 14% more capacity without adding any equipment

Practice Questions

  1. A biopharmaceutical facility plans to expand from 3 products to 5 products (campaign mode). If each changeover takes 4 days of downtime, each batch cycle is 12 days, and 340 working days are available per year, calculate the maximum number of batches per year, and discuss the capacity impact of shortening changeover time to 2 days.
  2. Vibration monitoring is used to predict equipment failure. Which maintenance strategy does it belong to (preventive, predictive, or corrective)? In a GMP environment, how can you ensure that equipment replacement decisions based on vibration data are documented?
5.7 Supply Chain

Original Text

5.7.1 Supply Chain

Drug substance bulk production and the drug product fill finish operations may be situated at separate facilities and different locations.

The limited stability of biopharmaceutical products can complicate the product supply chain and drug product distribution. Bulk drug substance may be frozen to extend its shelf life. Freeze thaw technologies can be established for disposable bags or freeze-thaw vessels for higher volumes. Single use systems may be preferred, as vessel management (shipping, cleaning, and steaming) for freeze-thaw vessels can be more complex. Shipments should be closely monitored and controlled to ensure required temperatures are not exceeded. For further information, see the ISPE Good Practice Guide: Cold Chain Management.

Single site production can simplify the supply chain; there is only one site to manage and only one facility to operate and maintain. This may provide decreased overhead costs.

However, business continuity risks are increased with a single site production facility, e.g.:

  • Natural and man-made disasters can disrupt ability to supply the market.
  • Contamination issues such as viral contaminants can result in significant downtime.
  • Regulatory issues can delay release of product to the market.
  • Labor issues have an increased potential to be disruptive.

Organizations should determine a strategy to ensure continuity of product supply. Modular design can facilitate quick duplication, replacement, or relocation of manufacturing required to address facility performance issues or changes in demand.

Sharing capacity across different organizations can allow for some sharing of capital investment risk. Facilities may be planned and require investment four to six years before commercial output is required. When a product is delayed, discontinued or exceeds planned demand, expensive idle capacity can occur or a market shortage may develop. Flexible design can allow facilities to be repurposed for another product, e.g., under a contracted arrangement to produce product from another organization.

The distribution of final drug product may be complicated for large volume products with diverse destinations. Location of distribution near shipping hubs or patient centers should be considered. Regional transportation can be used where reliable expertise around cold chain shipping has developed.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Cold Chain: biopharmaceutical products (drug substance, DS, and drug product, DP) generally need to be transported and stored at -20°C or -80°C, and any temperature excursion can lead to batch rejection. Cold chain management is a core challenge of the biopharmaceutical supply chain.

Business Continuity Risk: single-site production is more cost-effective, but any shutdown event (such as viral contamination or a natural disaster) can interrupt supply to the whole market, with extremely serious consequences. A well-known case is the 2009 viral contamination event for Aldurazyme at Genzyme.

DS (Drug Substance) vs. DP (Drug Product): biopharmaceutical manufacturing is usually divided into a drug substance facility (DSP) and a drug product fill-finish facility (DP fill-finish), which may be in different locations and need a complete cold chain logistics link.

Analogy

Single site vs. multiple sites = putting all your eggs in one basket vs. diversifying: a single site reduces management complexity and cost, but any accident means a total loss; multiple sites add cost but provide business continuity. For life-saving drugs (such as rare disease therapies), an interruption of market supply can endanger patients' lives, and FDA treats the absence of a business continuity plan with the utmost seriousness.

Investing in a facility 4-6 years ahead = ordering tomorrow's restaurant ingredients in advance: biopharmaceutical facilities take several years to build, and by the time one is finished, market demand may have changed. This is one of the hardest capital allocation challenges in the biopharmaceutical industry. The CDMO model spreads this risk by serving multiple clients.

Key Notes

Viral contamination is the most serious shutdown risk for a biopharmaceutical facility: biological processes use living cells, and once the production facility is contaminated with an adventitious virus (such as minute virus of mice, MVM), the entire site may be shut down for several months for thorough decontamination and validation, with losses often exceeding hundreds of millions of dollars. This makes the viral control strategy one of the highest design priorities for a biopharmaceutical facility.

The supply chain value of modular design: a modular facility can be quickly replicated at a new location, providing redundant capacity for drug supply. When the main facility has a problem, a backup site can be activated quickly. Regulators (such as FDA and EMA) increasingly weigh this when reviewing licensing applications for biopharmaceutical facilities.

Practical Application

A CDMO is evaluating whether to accept a large commercial mAb manufacturing contract, and the client requires a guarantee of 12 months of continuous supply. ISPE's recommended framework is: (1) build a cold chain inventory buffer (frozen drug substance stock); (2) ensure backup site capability (or identify a qualified backup CDMO); (3) establish a rapid-response SOP for viral contamination; (4) specify business continuity terms explicitly in the contract.

Practice Questions

  1. How do biopharmaceutical companies typically balance the cost-effectiveness of a single site against the supply security of multiple sites? How does the CDMO model change this equation?
  2. When a cold chain excursion occurs (such as a temperature excursion during transport), how should the manufacturer assess the acceptability of the batch? What scientific data can support the deviation assessment?

Chapter Summary

  • Multi-product operating strategies: campaign manufacturing (segregation in time) requires cleaning validation; concurrent production (segregation in space) requires closed systems or physical segregation. The ability to change over quickly is the core competitive strength of a multi-product facility.
  • The two-sided impact of single-use systems (SUS): the advantages are eliminating the need for CIP/SIP, reducing facility footprint, and lowering changeover risk; the challenges are supply chain dependency (single-supplier risk) and the safety assessment of E&L (extractables and leachables).
  • The operational revolution brought by closed systems: closed processing allows the use of CNC (unclassified areas), which can reduce cleanroom area by up to 60%, lessen gowning requirements, save up to 50% of staffing, and speed up changeover (no room clearance needed).
  • The quality value of automation and MES: automation ensures process consistency; MES + EBR enable electronic batch records and review by exception, shortening the lot release cycle; interlock systems ensure closure integrity.
  • Scheduling must be designed around "uninterruptible biological batches": unlike small-molecule chemical synthesis, a cell culture batch cannot be interrupted at will, so all maintenance windows and changeover schedules must be coordinated with the biological process cycle.
  • Supply chain resilience is a strategic asset of a biopharmaceutical facility: cold chain management, viral contamination risk, and the business continuity decision between a single site and multiple sites must all be considered at the facility planning stage.
6A

Chapter 6A: cGMP Layout — Protection & Segregation

p61-p74

ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p61–p74

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the core drivers of biopharmaceutical facility layout design, including CPPs, CQAs, process scale and technology selection.
  • Understand how bioreactor scale (large/medium/small) affects the facility floor plan, and distinguish vertical stack-up from horizontal layout designs.
  • Become familiar with the four segregation concepts (physical, environmental, temporal, procedural) and be able to judge where each applies.
  • Understand how the degree of process closure fundamentally changes facility design strategy, moving from multiple cleanrooms to a single ballroom concept.
  • Be able to interpret the six cases (Case 1-6) and analyze the design logic behind the evolution from open to closed processing.

Contents

6.1 Introduction 6.2 Production Capacity & Technologies 6.3 Product Protection & Segregation 6.3.1 Segregation Concepts 6.3.2 Application 6.3.3 Physical Segregation 6.3.4 Facility Layout Overview 6.3.5 Flexible Ballrooms
6.1 Introduction

Original Text

This chapter focuses on the facility aspects of product protection and specific requirements of CPPs and product CQAs. Facility layouts can be diverse while still protecting the product; this chapter discusses reasons behind layout options to help to facilitate the preferred project solution.

The result of the design phase should be a layout that focuses on protecting the product while also addressing key project drivers, such as:

  • CPPs and product CQAs
  • Production processes, scale, and technologies
  • Product protection and segregation approaches
  • Facility and process flows
  • Equipment and facility cleaning and finishes
  • cGMP requirements for Clinical Phase 2 and Phase 3 through commercial production
  • Vaccine facilities and biosafety requirements
  • Product and process changes over time, including:
    • Flexibility
    • Adaptability
    • Expandability
  • Regulatory requirements
  • Operational requirements
  • Code and life safety aspects
  • Business and economic factors

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — The Starting Point of Facility Design

CPPs (critical process parameters) and CQAs (critical quality attributes) are the highest guiding principles of facility design. Every layout decision (number of rooms, airflow direction, corridor arrangement) should be traceable to the question: "How does this design protect the product's critical quality attributes?"

The output of the design phase is not a set of drawings but a physical embodiment of the protection strategy. The walls, doors and pressure gradients of the facility are all tools for protecting CQAs.

Analogy — Kitchen Design Determines Food Quality

Think of the kitchen design of a high-end restaurant. The head chef (the CPP/CQA) determines what the kitchen needs: separate raw and cooked food areas (segregation), dedicated knife racks (prevention of cross-contamination), and cold-storage temperature monitoring (environmental control). The kitchen must serve the quality of the food, rather than forcing the chef to adapt to the kitchen layout.

Biopharmaceutical facility design works the same way: product characteristics determine the facility design, not the other way around.

Key Point — Flexibility, Adaptability, Expandability

ISPE explicitly lists Flexibility, Adaptability and Expandability as key design drivers. This is particularly important for a CDMO:

  • Flexibility: the same facility can manufacture products for different clients
  • Adaptability: the facility can be modified at low cost when the process changes
  • Expandability: scale-up from clinical batches to commercial production without building a separate facility

Practice Questions

  1. If a biopharmaceutical product has a CQA requiring low bioburden, how does this affect the airflow design of the facility layout?
  2. Why do vaccine facilities need to give special consideration to "biosafety requirements"? How does this differ from conventional small-molecule drugs?
6.2 Production Capacity, Process, Scale and Technologies

Original Text

Business and marketing factors can establish desired production capacities for each product produced in a facility. Capacities are converted into a production definition, beginning with:

  • Block Flow Diagrams (BFDs)
  • Process Flow Diagrams (PFDs)
  • A process description
  • An equipment list
  • A preliminary material balance

Capacities also define the process scale, the process technology, and the degree of process exposure or closure. This production definition forms the basis for a facility design. Process adjacency diagrams are created at this stage to begin to graphically understand the interrelation of the process steps.

Production capacities can be produced at different scales with scale defined as a bioreactor or fermenter size. An example of the same production capacity at different scales is a single 12,000 liter bioreactor or six 2,000 liter bioreactors. Each approach has pros and cons from a scale up, regulatory and a business perspective and each production scale has a very different impact on a facility layout. Recently, the industry has been trending toward increasingly larger production scales for Monoclonal Antibody (MAb) production. Processing improvements have increased the production yield from bioreactors, allowing for the same capacity to be produced from smaller bioreactors.

Scales for Monoclonal Antibody (MAb) production may be described as:

  • Large scale production typically ranges from 10,000 liters to 30,000 liters. Large scale processes can be stacked in a vertical configuration to assist with gravity fed processing, drains, CIP return lines, and utility distribution.
  • Medium scale production typically ranges from 4,000 liters to 8,000 liters. Medium scale processes are typically either stacked in a vertical configuration or configured in a horizontal arrangement.
  • Small scale production is typically up to 2,000 liters. Small scale processes are typically configured in a horizontal arrangement.

Figure 6.1 provides a scale comparison, showing impact of production scale on the facility.

Figure 6.1: Bioreactor Comparison Diagram
[Diagram: comparison of facility footprint for bioreactors of different scales]

Figure 6.1: Bioreactor Comparison Diagram — comparing facility footprint impact across production scales

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — The Derivation Chain from Capacity Requirements to Facility Design

ISPE presents a complete design derivation logic:

Business objective (annual output) → BFD/PFD → equipment list → material balance → facility layout

Key insight: the degree of closure of the process must be decided at this stage, because it directly determines the cleanroom grades and number of rooms required later. The higher the degree of closure, the less controlled space is needed, and the simpler and cheaper the facility can be.

Scale Comparison

Two options for the same total capacity of 12,000 L:

Option A: 1 × 12,000 L bioreactor
  → Multi-story facility (vertical stack-up)
  → Requires strong structural support and heavy lifting equipment
  → Suited to large-scale commercial production

Option B: 6 × 2,000 L bioreactors
  → Horizontal layout (single or low-rise floors)
  → Can be combined with single-use (SU) consumables
  → More flexible, suited to multi-product CDMOs

Scale classification:
  Large scale: 10,000 - 30,000 L (vertical stack-up)
  Medium scale: 4,000 - 8,000 L (vertical or horizontal)
  Small scale: ≤2,000 L (horizontal layout)

Analogy — One Large Brewing Tank vs. Several Small Brewing Tanks

Imagine a brewery: one 12,000 L brewing tank needs a building with a high ceiling, special lifting equipment and a reinforced floor; six 2,000 L tanks can be laid out horizontally in an ordinary building, which is more flexible but costs more to manage.

Biopharmaceutical facilities face the same trade-off: a single large-scale unit vs. several small-scale parallel lines. The facility design approaches for the two are completely different.

Key Point — MAb Industry Trends

ISPE notes that the industry trend is toward ever larger MAb production scales, but at the same time increases in cell culture titer allow the same output to be achieved with smaller bioreactors. This means:

  • New facility designs must allow for flexibility after future process improvements
  • Reserving CNC (Controlled Not Classified) space offers more future flexibility than fixed cleanrooms
  • Single-use technology combined with small-scale bioreactors is the preferred strategy for a CDMO

Practice Questions

  1. Why do large-scale bioreactors (>10,000 L) tend to use a vertical stack-up design? In which process steps does gravity play a role?
  2. As a CDMO, would you favor a single large tank or several small tanks? Explain from the standpoint of commercial flexibility.

Original Text — Vertical Stack-up and Small-Scale Horizontal Design

Production processes that are vertically stacked can result in a facility with many processing levels. Three main processing levels:

  • The highest floor (L3) can contain weigh/dispense and media and buffer preparation.
  • The floor below (L2) can house the seed reactors and buffer hold.
  • The lowest production level (L1) can house the production bioreactors, harvest, purification, and bulk fill.

These three levels will generally sit on a utility level below L1 and have a HVAC penthouse above level L3, resulting in a five level building. Large scale processing typically consists of fixed stainless steel vessels with stainless steel distribution piping that is cleaned with CIP/SIP systems.

Figure 6.2🔍: Large Scale Vertical Stack-up Adjacency Diagram
[Diagram: functional distribution on each floor of a large-scale vertical stack-up facility]

Figure 6.2🔍: Large Scale Vertical Stack-up Adjacency Diagram

Other large scale stack-up diagrams are shown in Chapter 4, Figures 4.2 and 4.3.

Small scale bioreactors have smaller volume demands for liquids. Fixed stainless steel piping may be replaced by flexible hoses and tubing. The length of hoses and tubing between process equipment should be minimized. This can significantly change a layout, e.g., for buffer hold. A tube can be dropped from buffer hold to purification on a level below. However, where the hold volume is small, it may be preferable to place the hold vessel next to its use point either in the purification room or just outside the purification room. The tubing can be passed through the wall, allowing a considerably shorter tube to be used.

At smaller scale, stainless steel bioreactors, mix, and hold vessels can be replaced with disposable technology, typically referred to as biocontainers or "bags."

Figure 6.3🔍: Example Small Scale Horizontal Process Configuration Rendering
[Diagram: rendering of a small-scale horizontal process configuration, with a central corridor for buffer storage and two production lines on either side]

Figure 6.3🔍: Example Small Scale Horizontal Process Configuration Rendering

In this example, the media and buffer preparation areas are located on the same production level and at the end of the media and buffer hold area (or hall). The entire process is on a single production level. This facility may be described as 16,000 liter capacity in two production lines at 4 × 2 kL scale.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — The Five-Level Structure of a Vertical Stack-up Facility

The vertical stack-up logic of a large-scale biopharmaceutical facility follows the principle of gravity-assisted process flow:

  • Top level (L3): weighing/dispensing, media and buffer preparation → liquids flow downward
  • Middle level (L2): seed train, buffer hold
  • Production level (L1): bioreactors, harvest, purification, drug substance filling
  • Utility level (below grade): CIP/SIP, purified water, compressed air
  • Roof level (HVAC penthouse): air handling units

Although this five-level design makes full use of gravity, it is expensive to build and difficult to modify in the future.

Analogy — The Seed Train Is Like a Plant Nursery

The seed train is like a farm's seedling greenhouse: starting from a small amount of seed (the working cell bank), the culture volume is expanded step by step in a temperature-controlled environment and finally moved into the production greenhouse (the production bioreactor).

In facility design, the seed train is usually located on the floor above the production bioreactor or in an adjacent room, so that the inoculation route is shortest and the contamination risk is lowest.

Key Point — The Space Advantage of Single-Use (SU) Systems

The core advantage of single-use (SU) technology in a small-scale facility is that it greatly reduces the facility footprint:

  • No fixed stainless steel piping → lower piping spacing requirements
  • Buffer biocontainer bags can hang directly on storage racks → buffer hold room area is greatly reduced
  • The entire process can be compressed onto a single production floor

See the area comparison in Figure 6.4🔍 below.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is it recommended to keep hoses/tubing as short as possible in a small-scale facility? What are the GMP risks of overly long tubing?
  2. Are single-use (SU) bags also suitable in a vertical stack-up facility? Why?

Original Text — Single-Use Technology and In-Line Dilution

The use of disposables, in-line dilution process technologies, and other production scale and process technology improvements can have a significant impact on a facility layout.

Disposables have scale or bag size limitations (at time of publication), but work well for small scale processing. Disposables also can provide a significant footprint reduction over stainless steel. Figure 6.4🔍 shows an area comparison between:

  • Buffer hold vessels with a steel platform
  • Stainless steel vessels accessed by a moveable stair
  • Disposable bags in bins accessed by a moveable stair

In each example, there is a reduction in the building footprint.

Figure 6.4🔍: Stainless Steel and Disposables Room Area Comparison
[Diagram: room area comparison of stainless steel tanks vs. single-use bags]

Figure 6.4🔍: Stainless Steel and Disposables Room Area Comparison

Note: the disposable system shown in Figure 6.4🔍 is a 12 pack bin holder capable of holding 2,500 liter disposable bags for a total capacity of 30,000 liters.

In-line dilution for buffers is another technology which can reduce the building footprint. In-line dilution uses buffer concentrates that are diluted in-line (en route) to their point of use. This technology can significantly reduce the amount of storage required for buffers.

Combining in-line dilution and disposable technology can further reduce a building's footprint.

Figure 6.5🔍: Traditional Buffer Hold and In-Line Dilution Room Area Comparison
[Diagram: area comparison of traditional buffer hold vs. an in-line dilution system]

Figure 6.5🔍: Traditional Buffer Hold and In-Line Dilution Room Area Comparison

Facility designs should be adapted to incorporate processes and technologies used in a specified process.

The number of processing rooms and the application of segregation should be determined before the facility adjacency diagrams are finalized.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — In-Line Dilution

In-line dilution is a revolutionary buffer management strategy. Traditionally, chromatography purification requires large volumes of buffers at different concentrations, each needing a large tank and a separate room. In-line dilution instead:

  • Stores only concentrated buffer stock (concentrate)
  • Mixes it in the piping in real time with purified water in the required proportion
  • Delivers it directly to the chromatography column point of use

Result: buffer storage space can be reduced by 60-80%, greatly shrinking the facility footprint.

Footprint Reduction Estimate

Buffer storage options compared (downstream purification as an example):

Traditional stainless steel tank option:
  Typical number of buffer types: 8-12
  Each needs a separate tank + operating platform
  → Buffer storage area: ~500-800 m²

Single-use (SU bags) option:
  12-pack bin holder, 12 × 2,500 L = 30,000 L
  → Area reduction: about 40-60%

In-line dilution + SU combined:
  Only concentrated stock needs to be stored
  → Area reduction: up to 70-80%

Conclusion: technology selection directly determines the facility capital investment

Analogy — Beverage Concentrate vs. Cases of Ready-to-Drink Beverages

Traditional buffer storage is like filling a warehouse with cases of ready-mixed drinks; in-line dilution is like storing only fruit juice concentrate and adding water just before use. The former needs a huge warehouse, while the latter needs only a small shelf.

For a CDMO, in-line dilution not only saves space but also reduces batch-to-batch buffer variability, which helps reduce variation in purification steps.

Practical Application — Technology Selection Advice for CDMOs

For a clinical-stage (Phase 1-2) CDMO facility:

  • Recommended: SU bags + in-line dilution: low initial investment, small footprint, fast changeover
  • Avoid large-scale fixed stainless steel systems: early client demand is uncertain, and large fixed equipment offers poor flexibility
  • The facility design should leave room for future conversion to stainless steel (piping connection points, floor loading)

Practice Questions

  1. What are the GMP validation priorities for an in-line dilution system? How can the accuracy of the dilution ratio be ensured every time?
  2. What impact do the "scale limitations" of single-use systems have on large-scale commercial production?
6.3 Product Protection and Segregation Applications

Original Text

Sources of product contamination include:

  • Environmental contamination
  • Crossover contamination
  • Carryover contamination
  • Raw material contamination

For further information, see Chapter 4 of this Guide. Preventing product contamination may be achieved by segregating sources of product contamination from the product. An exposed product should have environmental protection, such as a classified cleanroom environment. Preventing crossover contamination between two exposed products is typically achieved by physical barriers. Cleaning methods should be sufficient to prevent carryover contamination. Preventing contaminated raw materials from entering the process can be assisted by proper material testing.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Point — The Dual Pressures in Biopharmaceuticals

Biopharmaceutical facility design faces two competing protection needs:

  • Product protection: preventing environmental microorganisms from contaminating the product → requires cleanrooms (positive pressure, filtered air)
  • Protection of personnel/environment (containment): preventing release of live biological agents → requires containment design (negative pressure, exhaust treatment)

The key lever that decides which takes priority is the process closure level. A closed process can solve both problems at the same time.

Practice Questions

  1. What is the essential difference between crossover and carryover? How do the facility design solutions for each differ?
  2. If a biopharmaceutical process is completely closed, are cleanrooms still needed? Explain your reasoning.

Core Concept — Countermeasure Matrix for the Four Sources of Contamination

Only by understanding these four sources of contamination can the right protection strategy be designed for each, rather than applying the strictest cleanroom grade to every situation.

6.3.1 Segregation Concepts

Original Text

Preventing product contamination by environmental and crossover sources of product contamination can have a significant impact on facility design. These sources of product contamination can be addressed using primary segregation and secondary segregation.

  • Primary segregation focuses on the facility design aspects required to protect an exposed product.
  • Secondary segregation focuses on non-critical facility aspects which result from the use of closed processing.

Both primary and secondary segregation consist of four basic segregation concepts:

  1. Physical (or spatial) segregation
  2. Environmental (including local environmental protection) segregation
  3. Chronological (temporal or time based) segregation
  4. Procedural (or SOP based) segregation

These segregation concepts apply to cGMP facility design, utility design, and process design.

6.3.1.1 Physical Segregation

Physical segregation involves a physical barrier which can prevent both environmental and crossover contamination. The space inside a closed vessel is physically segregated from the space outside the vessel.

In a closed system, non-exposed product is physically segregated from the room environment. Process closure is an application of physical segregation.

In open systems, exposed products require both a physical segregation and environmental protection from other open processes to prevent crossover contamination. Physical segregation for open processing is typically achieved by using separate rooms. This can result in dividing open process steps into a series of separate processing cleanrooms. These cleanrooms are surrounded by airlocks, and use the concept of nesting and facility flows that are designed and arranged to reduce the environmental challenge to the cleanroom.

Example facility applications of physical segregation include:

  • Open Processing: An open processing pre-viral reduction step and an open processing post viral reduction step requiring two separate rooms.
  • Closed Processing:
    • Closed processing pre-viral reduction and closed processing post-viral reduction steps located in the same room
    • Upstream multi-product and multi-host concurrent production in separate closed bioreactors located in the same room
    • Downstream multi-product concurrent production in separate closed equipment located in the same room
    • Closed upstream processing and closed downstream processing located in the same room
    • Closed processing in vessels, piping, bags, and tubes is an application of spatial segregation
    • A product transfer pipe and a separate waste pipe both located in the same pipe rack
    • A closed product vessel and a closed waste container following the same facility flow path and located in the same elevator

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — Primary vs. Secondary Segregation

Primary segregation: protection for exposed product. When the product is in direct contact with room air, it must be protected through physical segregation (separate rooms) and environmental segregation (cleanroom classification).

Secondary segregation: management of closed processes. Once the product is in closed equipment, the focus of segregation shifts to mix-up prevention and waste management.

Key insight: once closure technology is introduced, the need for primary segregation falls sharply and secondary segregation becomes the main concern.

Analogy — Segregation Is Like Graded Isolation Wards in a Hospital

A hospital uses different isolation levels for different infectious diseases:

  • General ward (CNC): ordinary patients, no special isolation needed (like a closed process)
  • Semi-isolation ward (Grade 8): protection needed but low infectivity (like some partially exposed processes)
  • Negative-pressure isolation ward (Grade 7/5): highly infectious patients (like open, exposed critical process steps)

The key point: not every patient needs negative-pressure isolation. Likewise, not every process step needs a Grade 5 cleanroom.

Key Point — Key Applications of Physical Segregation

The most important application of physical segregation is segregation of the steps before and after viral reduction:

  • Open process: the steps before and after viral reduction must be carried out in separate rooms, to prevent product that has been cleared of virus from being re-contaminated
  • Closed process: the steps before and after viral reduction can be carried out in the same room, because physical segregation is already achieved at the equipment level

This distinction is extremely important in the viral safety assessment and directly affects the conclusions of regulatory review.

Practice Questions

  1. "Process closure is an application of physical segregation." Explain what this statement means, using product transfer piping as an example.
  2. Why can pre- and post-viral-reduction materials use the same elevator in a closed process? Would regulators accept this design?

Original Text — Environmental, Temporal and Procedural Segregation

6.3.1.2 Environmental Segregation

Environmental segregation is used for open systems. It uses clean air to protect exposed product and product contact surfaces that cannot be functionally closed. A typical application is a cleanroom. The level of the room environment relates to the degree and the criticality of the product or surface exposure. An example cleanroom level is Grade 8, where the entire room is designed to meet the Grade 8 requirements. This is also referred to as the room background classification.

A local protected process (also referred to as local environmental protection) is a sub-set of environmental segregation. It is designed to provide a higher level of clean air protection for a specific purpose within the cleanroom background.

Example facility applications of environmental segregation include:

  • A cleanroom
  • A biosafety cabinet used as local protection in inoculum preparation
  • A local protection enclosure constructed around a fixed process step
  • A portable local protection enclosure moved as needed to a POU

6.3.1.3 Temporal Segregation

Temporal or time based segregation can be applied to both open and closed processing, and also can be a factor in maintaining control of materials. Time based segregation involves using the same physical area for more than one function, but where activities are separated by time, e.g., campaigned manufacturing. Two open processing production lines occupying the same room and operating concurrently will be at risk from crossover contamination. Separating the processing activities by time (i.e., campaigning) can mitigate the risk.

Closed processing can involve concurrent processing or campaigned processing of several products.

Example facility applications of temporal segregation include:

  • Campaigned production for either open or closed processing
  • An enclosed, but not closed waste container (e.g., trash in a tied garbage bag) following the same "facility flow" path as personnel and product and using the same elevator, but the waste travel is non-concurrent with the other flows. This could be scheduled for the third shift or another convenient time.
  • Bi-directional gowning scheduled for non-concurrent gowning and de-gowning. The two functions occupy the same space, but are separated by time.

6.3.1.4 Procedural Segregation

Procedural or SOP based segregation is typically applied to preventing mix-ups, but it also has open processing or exposed surface aspects. An example is manual equipment cleaning in a washroom with many components from different products being handled in the same area concurrently, but segregated by procedures. This would involve using the same area for different open functions (possibly handled concurrently), but using procedures to prevent equipment contamination.

Example facility applications of procedural segregation include:

  • Preventing mix-ups
  • Washing components and equipment, while exposed to the room environment
  • A closed process that briefly exposes product to the room environment that needs special SOPs to protect the product. Operator action may be needed when the process closure technology does not address all aspects of a functionally closed system.

Tutorial Commentary

Analogy — A Pressure Gradient Is Like an Air-Pressure Maze

The pressure cascade design in environmental segregation is like an air-pressure maze: air always flows from high pressure to low pressure. If the cleanroom is designed at the highest pressure, the corridor next, and the outside lowest, contaminants cannot flow back into the cleanroom.

Note, however, that biopharmaceutical facilities sometimes need negative-pressure containment to prevent the release of live biological agents; in that case the pressure direction is the opposite of an ordinary cleanroom. This dual requirement needs special attention during design.

Key Point — GMP Implications of Campaign Manufacturing

Campaigned manufacturing is the most important application of temporal segregation. When several products share a facility, the same room produces different products in different time periods, and between them the following are needed:

  • Thorough cleaning and cleaning validation
  • Environmental monitoring (EM) to confirm the level of cross-contamination
  • Documentation confirming segregation between preceding and following batches

A closed process makes it possible to produce multiple products simultaneously in the same room without campaigning, which is one of the biggest commercial advantages of closed systems.

Practical Application — Choosing a Segregation Strategy for a Multi-Product CDMO Facility

A CDMO usually takes on orders from several clients at the same time. Choosing the right segregation strategy is critical:

  • Open process: must use temporal segregation (campaigning) + cleaning validation; high cost, low flexibility
  • Closed process: can use physical segregation (at the equipment level) + simultaneous parallel production, greatly improving facility capacity utilization
  • Procedural segregation: widely used in support areas such as wash rooms and waste management; lowest cost but carries the greatest risk management responsibility

Practice Questions

  1. A gowning room used for both gowning and de-gowning: which type of segregation is this? What controls are required?
  2. When a closed process has a "brief exposure" point, what elements should the procedural segregation SOP include?
6.3.2 Application of Segregation Concepts

Original Text

Figure 6.6🔍 provides an example of how the application of closure technology can impact segregation concepts and result in different facility designs.

Figure 6.6🔍: Applications of Closure Technology and Segregation
[Diagram: Diagrams A/B/C showing the effect of closure technology on segregation requirements]

Figure 6.6🔍: Applications of Closure Technology and Segregation — Diagrams A, B, C

  • Diagram A: shows an open process fermenter in a cleanroom (indicated in white) with the support equipment in the adjacent Controlled Not Classified (CNC) area (indicated as grey).
  • Diagram B: process closure technology has been incorporated into the fermenter design. Closed processes can be in CNC space. The remaining non-closed function is sampling. The goal of this "alcove" approach is to reduce the amount of clean area to just the area needed for non-closed operations.
  • Diagram C: shows the incorporation of closed sampling and the entire unit operation is functionally closed. There is no need for a cleanroom and the room environment is reduced to CNC.

Table 6.1 provides a comparison of the three segregation concepts presented in Figure 6.6.

Review of the segregation concept:

  • Physical segregation: in Diagrams A and B physical segregation is on a room by room basis. In Diagram C, physical segregation is on a closed equipment basis.
  • Environmental segregation: in Diagrams A and B, environmental segregation employs cleanrooms to protect exposed product. In Diagram C, product is not exposed and a cleanroom is not required.
  • Time based segregation: in Diagrams A and B, multi-product production is on a campaigned basis for open processing. In Diagram C, multi-product and concurrent production can operate in the same processing room.
  • Procedural segregation: in Diagrams A and B, procedural segregation needs several SOPs for open processing; the most significant are for following cleanroom and gowning protocols. In Diagram C, procedural segregation does not need these SOPs.
  • In Diagrams A and B, maintaining the processing cleanroom requires a series of 'nested' cleanroom transitions and proper flows to reduce the environmental challenge. In Diagram C, the flows for protecting the cleanroom are not needed. Flows usually focus on avoiding mix-ups.

The benefit of the segregation shown in Diagram B, rather than that shown in Diagram A, is the reduction in the size of the cleanroom and the associated reduction in construction and operating costs. In Diagram B, the open sampling point could be protected by the use of local protection, e.g., a HEPA hood. This could allow multi-product concurrent production with non-concurrent sampling and could prevent cross-over contamination. The risk with open sampling is environmental contamination and false positives during testing.

The full benefit of the segregation shown in Diagram C is realized only when all aspects of the process are closed.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — The Evolutionary Logic of the Three Diagrams

Figure 6.6🔍 and Table 6.1 are the most important learning material in this chapter. They show how closure technology progressively "liberates" facility design:

  • Diagram A (fully open): the entire fermenter is in a cleanroom. All four segregation concepts are at their strictest: separate rooms, cleanroom classification, campaign production and a full gowning SOP.
  • Diagram B (partially closed): only the sampling point is open and the rest is closed. The cleanroom shrinks to enclose only the sampling area (alcove). This is the most common compromise in practice.
  • Diagram C (fully closed): even sampling is closed. The whole room drops to CNC and multiple products can be produced in parallel at the same time.

Degree of Closure → Facility Cost: A Quantitative Relationship

Diagram A (fully open) facility cost baseline: 100%

How closure technology reduces cost:
  Cleanroom area reduction  → construction cost -20~40%
  Fewer gowning rooms       → area -10~15%
  Simpler HVAC system       → equipment cost -15~25%
  Lower operating cost      → gowning consumables, environmental monitoring -30~40%

Diagram C (fully closed) facility cost:
  About 60~70% of Diagram A

Supported by the ISPE case studies:
  Case 1 (most open) vs Case 6 (most closed)
  → Case 1 construction and operating costs are about 40% higher

Analogy — The Alcove Design Is Like the Local Sterile Field in Surgery

The alcove design in Diagram B is like a surgical operating room: the whole operating room does not have to be maintained like a sterile room, but the local area above the operating table (the sterile surgical field) must maintain the highest level of sterile protection.

Likewise, the alcove approach provides local HEPA protection only for the points that are truly open (the sampling port), rather than making the whole fermentation room a cleanroom, which greatly reduces facility cost.

Key Point — What CNC (Controlled Not Classified) Means

CNC (Controlled Not Classified) is a space grade concept unique to biopharmaceutical facilities:

  • The space has basic environmental controls (temperature and humidity, positive/negative pressure, cleanliness management)
  • But it does not meet ISO 14644 classification standards (no particle count compliance certification)
  • Applicable to all spaces housing closed process equipment
  • By contrast: Grade A/B/C/D in conventional sterile pharmaceutical manufacturing are ISO-classified spaces

In biopharmaceuticals, CNC is the most common grade for process areas, which is quite different from conventional small-molecule sterile drug products (which make heavy use of Grade B/C).

Practice Questions

  1. In Table 6.1, what changes in the purpose of "Flows" from Diagram A to Diagram C? How does this affect corridor design?
  2. If the open sampling point in Diagram B were replaced with online PAT (Process Analytical Technology) monitoring, could it be upgraded to Diagram C?
6.3.3 Application of Physical Segregation Concepts

Original Text

Following technology improvements, most biopharmaceutical process equipment is able to provide a functionally closed system. These closed systems allow for CNC room environments. Closed equipment next to other closed equipment are physically segregated from each other. Closed equipment can be grouped into a single large processing room, which may be called a "ballroom" or a "dance floor." Where process steps are open, they will require a classified environment. These can be located off a ballroom with an airlock to the classified environments.

Through the process of adding closure technology to more production steps, the requirement of physical segregation can be reassigned from a room basis to an equipment basis. This transformation can provide a significant benefit for retrofitting an existing facility, as well as for planning a new facility.

Figures 6.7 and 6.8 are comparative layout examples of physical segregation on a room basis verses on an equipment basis. Figure 6.8🔍 assumes a facility retrofit where the supply and return corridors are still needed for other existing facility functions. If this is not the case, the exit PAL and MAL and the return corridor areas could be incorporated into the flexible processing area.

Figure 6.7🔍: Physical Segregation Reassignment (Traditional — Room-based)
[Diagram: traditional facility layout, with a separate room, PAL/MAL for each process step]

Figure 6.7🔍: Physical Segregation Reassignment — Traditional open processing with separate processing rooms

Figure 6.8🔍: Physical Segregation by Equipment (Ballroom concept)
[Diagram: closed-process facility layout, with partition walls removed to form a large flexible production space]

Figure 6.8🔍: Physical Segregation by Equipment — Ballroom concept after room consolidation

Figure 6.7🔍 is a traditional facility concept for open processing with separate processing rooms and dedicated entry Personnel Airlocks (PALs) and Material Airlocks (MALs) from a production supply corridor. A separate set of PALs and MALs exit the process room into the return corridor.

In Figure 6.7🔍, process room (a) is a fermentation room. De-classifying process room (a) will save some operating cost, but if process rooms (b) to (d) are cleanrooms and the supply and return corridors are also classified, accessing de-classified process room (a) would require de-gowning, then re-gowning to exit. This is unlikely to provide a tangible facility improvement.

Where process room (b) is a harvest room with closed equipment, the wall dividing process rooms (a) and (b) can be removed. In addition, the PALs (b) and MALs (b) for process room (b) can be demolished and converted into processing area. This new larger room increases both processing area and flexibility, but it is still connected to classified rooms via shared corridors.

If process rooms (c) and (d) are also converted to incorporate closed processing, the facility can fully adapt to a closed processing facility. Dividing walls can be demolished and the large processing room, as well as the supporting corridors can be de-classified. The reduction in gowning also can reduce the main locker/gowning room requirements. Figure 6.8🔍 shows the resulting processing area that is larger than the four previous processing areas combined.

When applied to an existing facility, de-classifying rooms could facilitate significant production or capacity expansions within an existing production core. This could eliminate the need for a facility expansion or the construction of a new facility to meet the capacity expansion needs.

When existing rooms are considered for de-classifying:

  • The existing production floor plan should be reviewed:
    • CNC should be added to the existing rooms
    • Existing dividing walls should be erased
    • The resultant combined area should be determined

Where existing equipment is not closed, CNC will not be feasible.

"The greatest benefit in the application of closed processing to a facility may be in the transformation from open processes segregated on a room basis to closed processes segregated on an equipment basis."

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — The Ballroom Concept

The ballroom (or dance floor) is a revolutionary concept in biopharmaceutical facility design:

  • All closed process equipment is gathered in one large open space
  • The entire space drops to CNC grade (no cleanroom certification needed)
  • Physical segregation between different pieces of equipment is achieved at the equipment level, not the room level
  • The few open operations (such as column packing) are carried out in a small cleanroom next to the ballroom, connected by an airlock

The biggest advantage of the ballroom: very high facility flexibility, with equipment that can be reconfigured to suit order demand.

Analogy — From Cubicle Offices to an Open-Plan Office

The traditional multi-room facility (Figure 6.7) is like a 1990s cubicle office: everyone is enclosed by partitions, communication is inefficient and space utilization is poor.

The ballroom concept (Figure 6.8) is like a modern open-plan office: with the partitions removed, the same area holds more workstations and collaboration is more flexible. The enclosure of each closed piece of equipment is the invisible partition around each "workstation".

Key Point — Strategic Path for Retrofitting Existing Facilities

ISPE clearly states that retrofitting for closure can free up capacity in existing facilities and avoid building new ones:

  1. Assess the closure of existing equipment: equipment that cannot be closed blocks downgrading to CNC
  2. Introduce closure technology step by step: start with the most frequently operated equipment (for example, introducing CIP/SIP to the harvest centrifuge)
  3. Remove partition walls in phases: start by merging two adjacent rooms and expand gradually
  4. Qualify the new facility configuration: repeat URS/DQ/OQ/PQ

Converting the area of removed PALs/MALs to production space is the "hidden dividend" for improving facility utilization.

Retrofit Benefit Calculation

Assume an existing facility has 4 separate process rooms:
  Area per room: 50 m²
  PAL/MAL area: 2×10 m² = 20 m² per room
  Total effective process area: 4 × 50 = 200 m²

After retrofitting for closure (Figure 6.8 concept):
  Removing 3 partition walls: frees about 3 × 5 m² = 15 m²
  Removing 3 sets of PAL/MAL: frees 3 × 20 = 60 m²
  Downgraded corridor can be integrated: +30 m²

New effective process area: 200 + 15 + 60 + 30 = 305 m²
Area increase: about +52% (no new building required)

Practice Questions

  1. In Figure 6.7, if only the process rooms (a) are downgraded to CNC while the corridor remains a cleanroom environment, why is this "unlikely to provide a tangible improvement"?
  2. What risks does the ballroom concept pose for simultaneous multi-product manufacturing in a CDMO? What controls are needed?
6.3.4 Facility Layout Overview — Six Case Studies

Original Text

Facility layouts have adapted as a result of process closure. This transition is presented in six example case studies with each facility option responding to the need to protect the product. The options presented are considered viable and each has benefits and disadvantages. The intent is to align product exposure with the appropriate facility design response.

Figure 6.9🔍: Room Division Case Studies
[Diagram: room division matrix for the six cases, with process steps on the vertical axis and Cases 1-6 on the horizontal axis]
Note: the diagram shows process rooms only; PALs and MALs are not shown

Figure 6.9🔍: Room Division Case Studies — from fully open (Case 1) to nearly fully closed (Case 6)

Figure 6.9🔍 lists typical biopharmaceutical process steps in the left column. The remaining six columns are the six case studies. The black boxed areas around functions are proposed rooms for physical segregations. The cases are arranged from open processing on the left to almost all functionally closed processing on the right.

Case Descriptions:

Case 1: This is a facility concept with all process steps significantly exposed to the room environment. Biotechnology facility designed as a series of Grade 7 rooms with Grade 5 local protection zones above the critical areas of exposed product, e.g., inoculum or cell culture, seed, and production using open cell stacks in lieu of closed bioreactors.

Case 2: Application of closure technology on vessels — technology applied to fermenters, bioreactors, and media and buffer hold tanks. These closures add a level of physical segregation — may not be fully closed systems. The room environments reduce one level to Grade 8, but not to CNC. Seed and production reactors, and media and buffer hold tanks combined into a single room. Remaining areas are Grade 7.

Case 3: Closure methods added to most process steps. This closure provides an increased level of product protection from the room environment, resulting in a shift to Grade 8 environments. Closure confidence may not be high, so individual rooms retained for physical segregation to prevent cross-over contamination. The facility benefits from reduced room environments — essentially a Grade 8 production facility. Inoculum preparation remains at Grade 7. Cell manipulations follow aseptic processing guidelines in local protection Grade 5 Biosafety Cabinets (BSCs) with Grade 5 requiring a Grade 7 room environment background. Bulk fill is similar, with pooling under a Grade 5 BSC requiring a Grade 7 room environment.

Case 4: Inoculum preparation redefined from aseptic "processing" to aseptic "techniques." Manipulations are under a Grade 5 "quality" BSC which does not require a Grade 7 room background environment. Inoculum preparation and bulk fill reduced to Grade 8. Seed and production bioreactors functionally closed and room is CNC. Harvest (centrifuges) becomes functionally closed and is CNC, but is kept in a separate room because it is manually cleaned in an exposed manner and because of acoustic concerns. Pre and post viral processing equipment are moved into the same room. Pre and post physical room segregation is mitigated by redefining the viral free step as the final UFDF step located in bulk fill. Purification becomes a single room and reduces to Grade 8. Media and buffer hold are closed and room class drops to CNC. Media and buffer preparations are open and Grade 8, but are combined into the same room with dedicated equipment and possible separate local protection zones.

Case 5: New harvest (centrifuge) equipment incorporates CIP/SIP technology and is now within acceptable acoustic (decibel) limits. Harvest joins the bioreactors in a production ballroom. Media and buffer preparation incorporate 0.2 µm filtration en route to hold vessels. This filtration step functionally closes the open preparation process to a closed state at hold. The preparation room environment drops to CNC. Following a risk assessment the downstream processes can follow the upstream example and combine into a single processing room at CNC. Column packing is not closed in this diagram. In this case example, column packing is a separate room with a classified environment accessed from the CNC ballroom.

Case 6: The upstream ballroom and the downstream ballroom are combined into a single process room. Media and buffer prep are combined with their hold vessels. Inoculum preparation is de-classed to CNC with monitoring, which can be designed to the European Grade D. Inoculum cell manipulations are not a closed process. They are in a local protection device (BSC) and the room environment needs to be assessed for the proper background level. A closed processing option for inoculum is a glove box which could be placed in the CNC ballroom adjacent to the seed reactors, but a glove box may be considered not user friendly. An option for column packing (or any other non-fully closed process) is in lieu of locating it in a separate cleanroom is to keep it on the CNC production floor with local protection devices protecting the area of concern.

"Through an independent cost analysis, the case 1 facility was approximately 40% more expensive to construct and operate than the case 6 facility."

Segregating the product from the room environment increases control over the product while reducing the facility size, complexity, and cost. The facility can be a non-critical aspect system of the product (for CNC areas) and the facility is removed from the product protection equation.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Point — Two Key Breakthroughs in Case 4

Breakthrough 1: Redefining inoculum preparation

Redefining inoculum preparation from "aseptic processing" to "aseptic technique" is a major conceptual shift:

  • "Aseptic processing" requires a BSC in a Grade 7 background environment
  • "Aseptic technique" with a "Quality BSC" does not require a Grade 7 background and can be performed in a Grade 8 room
  • This redefinition requires sound scientific justification and regulatory dialogue

Breakthrough 2: Pre- and post-virus steps in the same room

The point at which "virus removal is complete" is moved to the final UF/DF step, so that all purification steps can be carried out in the same room.

Key Point — The Centrifuge Issue in Case 5

In the earlier cases, the harvest centrifuge was always placed in its own room, for two reasons:

  1. Cleaning method: centrifuges needed manual cleaning with exposure (no CIP) and could not be in a CNC room
  2. Acoustic issue: noise from high-speed centrifuges exceeded acceptable levels (dB limits)

In Case 5, a new centrifuge with CIP/SIP technology also improved the noise problem, which allowed it to join the ballroom. This shows that advances in equipment technology are an important driver of facility design upgrades.

Analysis of the Sources of the 40% Cost Difference

The Case 1 vs Case 6 cost difference of about 40% comes from:

Construction cost differences:
  Greatly reduced cleanroom area     → -15~20%
  Smaller HVAC system                → -8~12%
  Fewer PALs/MALs                    → -3~5%

Annual operating cost differences:
  Gowning consumables (cleanroom garments etc.) → -30~40%
  Environmental monitoring (EM) frequency       → -40~60%
  Facility maintenance (filters etc.)           → -20~30%
  Energy consumption (HVAC)                     → -20~30%

Personnel efficiency gains:
  Less gowning time          → production time increases 10~15%
  Simpler material flow      → improved operating efficiency

Analogy — The Six Cases Are Like the Evolution of a Restaurant Kitchen

Imagine the evolution of a restaurant kitchen:

  • Case 1: a fully open kitchen where every dish requires the chef to wear special protective clothing, with glass partitions between every cooking station
  • Case 3-4: most ingredients are vacuum-packed, and local protection is needed only when opening the packaging and plating
  • Case 6: almost all ingredients stay sealed until the last moment, and the whole kitchen is just one well-ventilated large space, with the highest efficiency and the easiest hygiene management

Practice Questions

  1. In Case 4, if the regulator does not accept the argument that "inoculum preparation is aseptic technique rather than aseptic processing", what is the impact on facility design?
  2. In Case 6, inoculum preparation can still be designed to a European Grade D CNC level. Is this feasible under the regulatory environment in the Asia-Pacific region (Taiwan, Japan, Korea)?
  3. Why is "the facility can be a non-critical aspect system for products in CNC areas" an important regulatory strategy statement?
6.3.5 Flexible Facility Ballrooms

Original Text

Figure 6.10🔍 shows an example of a single large production ballroom. All processes in the ballroom are closed systems and the room environment is CNC. The production scale is 10,000 liters, but at 5 × 2,000 liters using disposable bioreactors.

Figure 6.10🔍: Production Ballroom Example
[Diagram: example of a 10,000 L production ballroom, 5 × 2,000 L single-use bioreactors, CNC environment]

Figure 6.10🔍: Production Ballroom Example — 10,000 L capacity using 5 × 2,000 L disposable bioreactors in a single CNC space

This example takes the case 6 example (see Section 6.3.4 of this Guide) further and combines the production room with the media and buffer preparation room. The supporting functions around this ballroom (not shown) are equipment cleaning and a Grade 8 cleanroom for column packing.

This example is a flexible facility concept and the equipment is closed. The concept is intended to work equally well with disposables and stainless steel equipment.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — The Complete Design Elements of a Ballroom

A complete ballroom facility includes:

  • Core ballroom (CNC): all closed process equipment (bioreactors, purification equipment, buffer systems)
  • Satellite cleanrooms (Grade 8): steps that cannot be closed, such as column packing, connected to the ballroom through an airlock
  • Equipment wash room: a separate area, which may be CNC or Grade 8 (depending on the use of the equipment after cleaning)
  • Gowning facilities (minimized): gowning requirements for CNC areas are far lower than for cleanrooms, saving space

Key Point — Single-Use and Stainless Steel Are Both Compatible

ISPE specifically emphasizes that the ballroom concept does not depend on a particular equipment material. Both single-use (SU) and stainless steel equipment can work under the ballroom concept, provided the equipment achieves a functionally closed state.

This is important information for a CDMO considering a transition from SU technology to large-scale stainless steel: the facility itself does not need to be rebuilt, only the closure of the new equipment needs to be ensured.

Analogy — The Ballroom Is Like a Modern Automated Factory

The production line of a modern car factory is a large open space, where robotic arms for different operations work in parallel in the same hall, achieving segregation through precise robot operation rather than walls.

The ballroom concept is exactly this: the enclosure of the closed equipment is the "precise boundary of the robotic arm", and the room is just a shell that provides basic environmental control (temperature and humidity, air changes), no longer the main tool of GMP segregation.

Practical Application — Commercial Considerations for a CDMO Choosing a Ballroom Design

For a CDMO planning a new facility or a retrofit:

  • Initial investment: ballroom construction cost is 20-40% lower than a multi-room design
  • Operational flexibility: the same space can be quickly reconfigured with equipment to meet different client needs
  • Expandability: only equipment needs to be added, with no new partition walls or cleanroom certification
  • Regulatory risk: requires a complete CCS (contamination control strategy) and robust SU management procedures
  • Regulatory documentation: the URS must clearly describe closure requirements, and the MAA/BLA must justify the CNC grade

Practice Questions
