  1. In a ballroom design, how should a brief opening caused by a failed seal on a bioreactor be handled? What provisions are needed in the SOP?
  2. If a 10,000 L ballroom (5 × 2,000 L SU bioreactors) is to be expanded to 20,000 L capacity, what design options are there? What is the facility impact of each?

Chapter Summary — Chapter 6A Key Takeaways

  • Derivation logic of facility design: from commercial capacity target → BFD/PFD → process closure decision → segregation strategy → facility layout, with a clear logical link at every step.
  • Effect of scale on the facility: large scale (>10,000 L) tends toward a five-level vertical stack-up design; small scale (≤2,000 L) uses single-use consumables arranged horizontally on a single production floor, which greatly reduces the facility area.
  • Four segregation concepts: physical, environmental, temporal and procedural segregation each have their own applications. The biggest design dividend of closed processing is moving physical segregation down from the "room level" to the "equipment level", so that the facility evolves from multiple cleanrooms to a single CNC ballroom.
  • Core message of the six cases: the construction and operating cost gap from Case 1 (fully open, Grade 7) to Case 6 (fully closed, CNC ballroom) is about 40%. The higher the degree of closure, the simpler, cheaper and more flexible the facility.
  • Importance of the CNC concept: CNC does not mean "no environmental control" but "controlled but not classified by ISO particle counts". For a fully closed process, CNC is a reasonable and regulator-accepted space grade.
  • Dual protection pressures: a biopharmaceutical facility faces two competing needs, "protecting the product (cleanroom design)" and "protecting personnel/environment (containment design)". Process closure is the best strategy for satisfying both at once.
  • In-line dilution + SU technology: in-line buffer dilution can reduce buffer storage space by 60-80%, and combined with single-use consumables can further compress the facility footprint, making it the preferred technology combination for a modern CDMO.
6B

Chapter 6B: cGMP Layout — Flows, Cleaning & Special Areas

p74-p85

Chapter 6B: cGMP Layout Approaches — Flows, Cleaning & Special Areas

Chapter 6, Part B: cGMP Facility Layout Strategy - Flows, Cleaning and Special Areas
ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p74 – p85

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the definition and purpose of logistical segregation: batch, product and virus-reduced operations segregation, and the difference between open and closed processing.
  • Master the three levels of facility and process flows: logical process presentation, avoiding mix-ups, and protecting the room environment; and compare when to use a dual-corridor versus a single-corridor facility design.
  • Understand the design principles for equipment and facility cleaning: CIP/SIP integration, equipment wash area layout (two-room vs. three-room), and surface finish requirements for different classes of environment.
  • Distinguish where vaccine and biological hazard containment conflicts with a standard GMP facility: BSL negative pressure vs. GMP positive pressure, and how to resolve this conflict in the design.
  • Identify the key functions of production support areas, and the advantages of modular design for facilities in emerging countries.
6.4 Logistical Segregation Considerations (Logistical Segregation)

Original Text

Logistical segregation is defined as the spatial or physical separation of systems or operations. The goal is to prevent mix-ups. Although material and personnel airlocks are designed to protect the classified environments of the bioprocessing suites during transitions from one space to an adjacent space, they also provide gates for the transition of materials and products through the process and facility.

Segregating batches and unit operations into individual silos also can result in segregation of the equipment, components, raw materials, and personnel involved in the execution of that unit operation. The protection provided by these barriers should be maintained or replaced for adequate logistical segregation, i.e., to prevent mix-ups. Technology to mitigate the risk of mix-ups includes:

  • RFID technology
  • Bar coding
  • Color coding

In addition, dedication of specific connecting devices could be used to prevent the possibility of mix-ups.

Where bioprocess unit operations are consolidated into a single multipurpose manufacturing suite (ballroom) (see Section 6.3.5 of this Guide), the risks of inadequate logistical product, batch, and intermediate or unit operation segregation should be evaluated. Methods for distinguishing between products, batches, or intermediates that have undergone a process of virus removal or inactivation, from those which have not, should be established.

The staging of soiled equipment should mitigate the risk of contamination of purified material from upstream processes. Examples where closed processing provides an opportunity for consolidation of operations and systems that have been traditionally segregated, include:

  • Batches
  • Products
  • Virus reduced operations
  • Cell containing from cell free operations
  • Open from closed operations

Logistical segregation should be considered for each operation.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Logistical Segregation: separating systems or operations by spatial or physical means. It has one core purpose: to prevent mix-ups. Unlike the protection provided by a GMP-classified environment, logistical segregation is about "the right thing in the right place".

A mix-up is an especially dangerous error because you have no idea it has happened. A wrong batch label, a mis-connected line, or virus-reduced intermediate stored alongside unreduced material can all lead to a product recall or patient harm.

Hierarchy of tools for preventing mix-ups:

  • Physical segregation (strongest): separate rooms, airlocks
  • RFID / barcode scanning (moderate): electronic tracking
  • Color coding (supporting): lines, connectors
  • Administrative controls (weakest): SOPs, training

Analogy

Think of the one-way passenger flow at an airport. Departing, arriving and transit passengers take different routes, not only for security screening but also to prevent "people mix-ups", so that the right people end up in the right place. Logistical segregation in a biopharmaceutical facility works like an airport flow planner, making sure every material, person and waste stream moves in its own "lane" and cannot take the wrong route.

A ballroom is like the open main terminal hall of an airport: the space is shared, but electronic ticketing (RFID/barcode) makes sure every passenger boards the correct flight.

Key Notes

Segregation of virus-reduced operations is an explicit regulatory expectation! Once a batch has passed a virus removal/inactivation step, it must be physically separated from untreated upstream material, because any cross-contamination would invalidate the downstream virus-free assurance.

Closed processing can remove the traditional need for segregation: when a system is fully sealed, batches and products can coexist in the same space, which is the basis of the ballroom concept. But as soon as an open operation is involved, all segregation requirements apply again.

Practice Questions

  1. In a ballroom-style biopharmaceutical facility, two batches of two different products are manufactured at the same time. In which situations is this acceptable? In which is it absolutely not?
  2. When RFID is used to track batches, what backup logistical segregation measures should apply if the RFID reader fails?
6.4.1-6.4.5 Segregation Sub-types (Details of Each Segregation Type)

Original Text

6.4.1 Segregation of Batches

Establishing closure or isolation from the environment also should eliminate the possibility of crossover from environmental sources. The risk for crossover contamination to occur of two neighboring closed systems is considered small, as it would require the breach of integrity of both systems, concurrently. There is a potential for crossover where two closed systems share elements of communication, such as CIP or common transfer headers.

If multiple batches of a same product are housed within the same room, it is likely that they also share ingredients and process equipment. Controls should be established to mitigate the risk of mix-ups of any shared components.

6.4.2 Segregation of Products

Manufacturing processes using only closed systems can potentially share real estate with other manufacturing process. When multiple products are housed in the same space, they may or may not share equipment or ingredients. If equipment and materials are shared, the risks are similar to those of a suite housing multiple batches of a single product. If multiple products are manufactured in a single suite, there is potential one product to impact on the other process. Different products can have different cleaning, sanitization and/or testing requirements that could promote errors, mix-ups, or crossover (e.g., from shared CIP systems) if not properly managed.

6.4.3 Segregation of Virus Reduced Operations

There is a regulatory agency expectation of segregation of process operations deemed "virus reduced." Manufacturers typically house bulk substance fill operations in an isolated suite accessed via an airlock that is independent of areas where upstream purification operations occur.

A risk assessment should be used to determine the appropriate (and logical) point of segregation if one is required. The risk assessment should evaluate possible means of "contaminating" the segregated environment by:

  • Personnel
  • HVAC
  • Process
  • Cleaning
  • Sanitization

For example, sharing the virus-reduced equipment, CIP, or glasswashers with processes or equipment upstream without effective autoclaving or SIP could result in the breach of the isolated "virus-reduced" suite. For this reason, single use systems are normally used for the lower volume "virus-reduced" applications.

6.4.4 Segregation of Cell (+) from Cell (-) Operations

6.4.5 Segregation of Open from Closed Operations

Segregation of upstream "cell (+)" and downstream "cell (-)" operations should be based on definition and logic. Additional processing, testing, and validation may be required to effectively verify that a downstream process is cell free. Flow and gowning of personnel, CIP strategy, HVAC zoning, and pressurization, etc., should be considered if the segregation of solutions and processes containing live cells is required.

Open bioprocesses should be segregated from closed bioprocesses to ensure that the integrity of the overall program is maintained. Open operations such as inoculum preparation or chromatography packing (if open) can be affected by the environment and by other processes operating in proximity of the open operations. Appropriate programs including classified environments, appropriate gowning of personnel, appropriate HVAC zoning and pressurization strategy, appropriate transitioning of materials, personnel and operations should be established to mitigate the risk of contamination.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Summary of the four segregation types:

  • Batch segregation: prevents mix-ups between different batches of the same product; the focus is control of shared components (CIP lines, transfer heads)
  • Product segregation: when different products share the same space, a shared CIP system is the biggest risk, because cleaning residue from one product could affect another
  • Virus-reduced segregation: an explicit regulatory expectation; entry must be through a separate route, separated from upstream; single-use systems are the best solution
  • Cell-containing vs. cell-free: downstream purified cell-free areas must be protected from contamination by upstream material containing live cells; this affects HVAC zoning, personnel flow and gowning strategy

Key Notes

A shared CIP system is a high-risk point! When two "closed" systems share a CIP line, a loss of integrity on one side (such as a leak) could carry contamination to the other side through the shared piping. This is why virus-reduced areas usually use single-use systems, which completely eliminate the risk of a shared cleaning system.

Segregation logic for open vs. closed processing: closed processing relies on system integrity to protect the product; open processing relies on the environment (classified cleanroom) and personnel controls. When the two are mixed, open operations must have their own controlled environment.

Practical Application

The question a CDMO hears most often from biopharmaceutical clients is: "Can my post-virus-inactivation product operate in the same facility as the pre-inactivation product?" The answer is yes, but only after a rigorous risk assessment, and the two must be separated into independent suites, each accessed through its own airlock. If the client's upstream process uses stainless steel CIP while the downstream virus-reduced process uses single-use systems, the two can usually coexist in the same building, but clear flow rules and SOPs for personnel/material transfer are needed.

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO plans to manufacture monoclonal antibodies (mAbs) for two clients at the same time in one facility. One product uses a stainless steel CIP system and the other uses a single-use system. What is the main risk of this arrangement, and how would you control it?
  2. When risk-assessing the segregation scheme for a "virus-reduced suite", which five potential contamination routes should you evaluate?
6.5 Facility and Process Flows (Facility and Process Flows)

Original Text

Facility and process flows are designated paths of travel for:

  • Personnel
  • Materials
  • Product
  • Equipment cleaning
  • Solid waste

The purpose of these flows can be categorized into three levels:

  1. Demonstrating a logical production process
  2. Avoiding mix-ups
  3. Protecting a room environment that is designed to protect an exposed product

6.5.1 Level 1: Demonstrating a Logical Production Process

Lean manufacturing principles may be applied to create an efficient facility design and a logical production process. Lean concepts can include risk assessments to improve quality, prevent production interruptions, eliminate failures, and increase production flexibility.

6.5.2 Level 2: Avoiding Mix-Ups

Avoiding mix-ups is a specific cGMPs regulatory requirement and the methodology for preventing mix-ups must be demonstrable. This may be achieved by administrative methods such as labeling raw materials, bar coding, or color coding hose connections, or by physical segregation such as placing the released materials for a production line in a separate room or in a segregated area.

6.5.3 Level 3: Protecting a Room Environment

For open processing, the design of a facility should create flows to reduce the environmental challenge to a cleanroom from adjacent less clean areas with the intent of reducing the likelihood of contaminating an exposed product. For closed processing, these Level 3 flows are not relevant to product protection.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three purposes of flow design (in increasing order of importance):

  • Level 1 - Logical process presentation: the facility layout must let an inspector (FDA investigator, client auditor) see the logical sequence of the process (upstream → downstream) at a glance. This is a basic requirement of lean manufacturing.
  • Level 2 - Avoiding mix-ups: an explicit cGMP requirement, and the method must be demonstrable. SOPs alone are not enough; they must be backed by a physical or electronic mechanism.
  • Level 3 - Environmental protection: needed only for open processing. Pressure differentials (positive pressure cascade), personnel flow and equipment flow are designed to reduce the risk of the environment contaminating exposed product. Because closed systems are sealed, closed processing does not need environment-protecting flows.

Analogy

Level 1 is like the flow design of a kitchen: ingredients come in, then prep, cooking, plating and serving, in a logical order, and "serving" and "garbage removal" must not share the same route. Level 2 is avoiding wrong orders: every dish must have a label traveling with it. Level 3 is the kitchen management of a high-end restaurant: exposed food must be kept in a hygienic environment, with no source of contamination nearby. If the food is in a sealed container (closed processing), Level 3 is not needed, because the container itself protects the food.

Key Notes

The transformative impact of closed processing: eliminating Level 3 flows is one of the major advantages of adopting closed processing (single-use systems, CIP/SIP). Traditionally, the dual-corridor design (clean supply + dirty return) was driven by the Level 3 requirement. Closed processing makes a single-corridor design possible, greatly reducing facility complexity and construction cost.

From the point of view of an FDA inspection, Level 2 (avoiding mix-ups) is a must-check item: you have to demonstrate a specific mix-up prevention mechanism, and cannot simply say "we have good SOPs".

Practice Questions

  1. Does a fully closed biopharmaceutical facility still have to be designed with Level 3 flows? Why?
  2. For a facility with open chromatography packing operations, which additional flow control measures should be added?
6.5.4 Multiple vs. Single Production Corridor (Dual-Corridor vs. Single-Corridor Design)

Original Text

6.5.4.1 Multiple Production Corridors

This facility layout is used to protect exposed product. Level 3 flows are usually divided into a two corridor system:

  1. A clean supply corridor
  2. A dirty return corridor

See Figure 6.7🔍 for the basic configuration. The supply corridor should include all "clean" flows, including personnel, raw materials, product, and clean equipment. The return corridor should include personnel exiting the process room, dirty or used equipment, and waste. The flow for personnel and dirty or used equipment are circular.

Personnel (potentially contaminated with residual product) exit the process room through the exit PAL, enter the return corridor, and proceed to a de-gown area to remove outer gowning layers. Personnel could continue to the main locker area to leave the facility or gown into clean over garments then enter the supply corridor and enter a processing room via the PAL.

Dirty equipment from the process rooms enters the return corridor and proceeds to equipment wash. It should be processed through the multiple cleaning rooms. When released, the equipment travels along the supply corridor to its processing room.

This facility layout also can provide a potential source for facility contamination, if misused, e.g., transporting non-contained dirty equipment or waste that contaminates the return corridor. This facility contamination could migrate to contaminate an open process.

6.5.4.2 Single Production Corridor

There are two main drivers for facilities designed with a single production corridor:

  1. The incorporation of closed processing with CIP/SIP systems
  2. The increasing use of disposables, which reduces the amount of items requiring cleaning

These systems reduce the quantity of equipment that needs to travel to an equipment wash room. A facility using stainless steel equipment and CIP/SIP throughout, along with disposables for inoculum, can reduce the portable equipment washing needs by more than 90% with very little solid waste. A facility using entirely disposables is similar, but with a higher quantity of solid waste.

Factors that can reduce the need for a return corridor include:

  1. Removing portable equipment washing from a facility
  2. Closed processing:
    • Eliminates the need for level 3 flows
    • One-way personnel flow is not needed because the process is closed and there is no residual contamination on garments.
    • Reduced number of individual processing rooms allows direct access to equipment wash from fewer and larger processing rooms.
  3. Proper containment of waste for clean transport in the production corridor
"Figure 6.11 shows a simplified concept of a single corridor facility centered on a one-way equipment wash flow... Dirty equipment from upstream processing and downstream processing enter equipment wash via MALs. Equipment can be pre-washed on a campaigned basis. The equipment enters a pass through parts washer and enters into the product free clean equipment room. Equipment can be sterilized via a single sided autoclave, released for use, and moved back to the processing room via the production corridor."

Where flow paths are shared, especially product and waste, a closed product vessel should be used to protect the product from environmental and cross-over contamination. Where a product vessel is not closed, the risk to the product from environmental contamination from the corridor environment is greater than that from an adjacent contained waste container. If there is physical contact with a contained waste container, the closed product vessel can be wiped down as it leaves for storage.

A final bulk product container may be stored in non-classified environments or be transported to a fill facility and the outside of the vessel can be exposed to several contaminants. When the vessel enters the fill finish facility for compounding, it should be adequately cleaned.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Core logic of the multiple production corridor design:

  • Clean supply corridor: clean personnel + raw materials + clean equipment enter the production rooms
  • Dirty return corridor: used personnel + dirty equipment + waste leave the production rooms
  • Personnel flow is a loop: dirty corridor → de-gowning → re-gowning → clean corridor

Conditions that trigger a single production corridor design:

  • CIP/SIP integration (stainless steel equipment cleaned in place, with no need to move it to an equipment wash room)
  • Heavy use of single-use systems: discarded after use, with no cleaning needed
  • The need to wash portable equipment falls by 90%+, and Level 3 flows are eliminated

Analogy

A dual-corridor facility is like the back-of-house flow of a five-star hotel: there is a "service route" for staff to enter guest rooms and a separate "back-of-house route" for removing dirty dishes and garbage. The two never cross, so guests never see anything dirty and hygiene standards are maintained.

A single-corridor facility is like a modern fast-food restaurant: because it uses disposable tableware (single-use systems), the cook does not need to walk to a dishwashing area and everything is done at the workstation (CIP/SIP). One corridor is enough, and it is more efficient too.

Design Decision Matrix

Corridor design selection guide:

Condition                            Recommended design
-----------------------------------------------------------
Open processing (exposed product)    Dual corridor (required)
High Level 3 flow requirement        Dual corridor

Mainly CIP/SIP + single-use systems  Single corridor (acceptable)
Fully closed processing              Single corridor (recommended)
Portable equipment washing <10%     Single corridor (recommended)

Reduction in portable equipment washing > 90%
= typical result of using CIP/SIP + disposables

Key Notes

The hidden risk of a single corridor: when the bulk product container and waste share the same corridor, you must make sure the product container is sealed (a closed vessel). The contamination risk from a sealed waste container is much smaller than the risk that an unsealed corridor environment poses to an open product container. The outer surface of the final bulk product container must be cleaned before it is transferred to the filling facility, a point that is often overlooked!

Practical Application

One of the most important decisions for a CDMO evaluating a new facility design is the corridor strategy. If the client pipeline is mainly small-molecule APIs (open processing), a dual corridor is almost a necessity. If the future pipeline is mainly antibody drugs (mAbs, using single-use bioreactors), a single-corridor design can save a great deal of construction cost (one fewer corridor = 20-30% less floor area) and simplify validation work. The corridor strategy should be set at the early facility planning stage, because once it is built it is very hard to change.

Practice Questions

  1. A brand-new biopharmaceutical CDMO facility plans to take on mAb and recombinant protein manufacturing, with heavy use of single-use bioreactors. The design team recommends a single corridor. List three technical arguments in support of this decision, and one risk that needs careful evaluation.
  2. In a single-corridor design, what controls should be applied to the final bulk product container before it enters the filling facility?
6.6 Equipment and Facility Cleaning and Finishes (Equipment and Facility Cleaning and Surface Finishes)

Original Text

Aspects of equipment cleaning are discussed in the ISPE Guide on Cleaning Process Development and Validation [51].

6.6.1 Facility Aspects of Equipment Cleaning

As CIP/SIP is incorporated into more equipment and with the increased use of disposables, the quantity of portable or mobile equipment accessing a common equipment wash room is decreasing. Some facilities may not need equipment wash areas.

Items that need cleaning should be identified and their cleaning protocols defined to determine the amount and type of cleaning equipment needed. The production schedule can be used to determine the quantity of each type of equipment. These factors can have a significant impact on cleaning room design.

Cleaning rooms with a large through-put of portable equipment tend toward a three room configuration. Lower through-put facilities tend toward a two room configuration.

For the three stage equipment wash layout, the functions per room can include:

  1. Decontamination and Washing: equipment disassembly, decontamination, manual pre-cleaning, feeding a pass through parts washer, and placing vessels in CIP pass through stations
  2. Equipment Assembly: cleaned (product free) equipment is collected from the parts washer and the CIP station, assembled and fed into an autoclave or an SIP pass through station
  3. Clean Equipment Staging: receiving final cleaned equipment for staging and QC release.

A lower through-put wash area using a two room approach combines stages 2 and 3 in one room, using a single sided autoclave.

cGMP equipment washing areas typically rely on pass through cleaning equipment that separates a dirty (product containing) side from the clean (product free) side. Galley style equipment wash areas can be used and may be appropriate for low through-put washing or for servicing dedicated rooms. The final facility layout test is the QC release testing.

There are two basic levels of facility cleaning:

  1. Good general housekeeping practices for non-critical aspect rooms (non-exposed product)
  2. cGMP level for critical aspect rooms where the room cleanliness can directly affect the product

For further information on the levels of finish as related to Grades 7, 8, Controlled Not Classified with monitoring (CNC+) and Controlled Not Classified (CNC) environments, see the ISPE Baseline Guide on Sterile Manufacturing Facilities [52]. The level of finishes and facility cleaning should correspond to the required level of product protection.

CNC room surroundings:

  • The room is a non-critical aspect to the product.
  • The engineering systems are designed to Good Engineering Practices (GEPs) with commissioning, but are not qualified.
  • Basic good housekeeping practices
  • Level of finish: can be industrial, floor finish to contain a spill
  • Image: can resemble chemical plants

Grade 8 room surroundings:

  • The room is a non-critical aspect to the product.
  • The engineering systems are designed to Good Engineering Practices (GEP) with commissioning and qualification.
  • Basic good housekeeping practices plus light cGMP sanitizers
  • Level of finish: easily cleaned, non-flush detailing
  • Image: Grade 8 cleanroom

Grade 7 room surroundings:

  • The room is a critical aspect to the product.
  • The engineering systems are designed to GEPs with commissioning and qualification.
  • Basic good housekeeping practices plus strong cGMP sanitizers
  • Level of finish: easily cleaned, flush detailing to minimize horizontal surfaces, cover corners
  • Image: Grade 7 cleanroom

Organizations may choose to add facility requirements that do not directly protect product or may add items from CPP environments to non-CPP environments. These facility requirements and items are discretionary. An example is providing the same level of finish for the entire facility independent of its relation to product protection. A Grade 8 level of finish could be used for Grade 8 and CNC spaces. This adds capital and operating cost, but simplifies the facility cleaning regimen. The higher level of finish does not assist in protecting the product, but the look of the facility may assist with the staff being in a production mind set.

Catastrophic equipment failures that release product into the room can be a housekeeping issue or a cGMP concern. An example of a housekeeping spill is a large multi-product concurrent production closed processing ballroom with a major product spill. The spilt product from line 1 cannot contaminate the adjacent closed equipment in line 2. The product in line 2 is not at risk and line 2 continues production without interruption. This is an example of a housekeeping spill.

Open processes are typically segregated into individual processing rooms. A spill in an open processing room is a significant event that interrupts the production and can become a facility contamination issue that could migrate to contaminate another open system. This is usually a cGMP concern.

Tutorial Commentary

Analogy

Think of a hospital sterile services department (CSSD): instruments come from the operating theater (dirty side), pass through a washer (pass-through equipment), exit on the other side into the sterilization area (clean side), and finally go to the sterile supplies store. The whole flow is one-way, with no backtracking. The equipment wash area of a biopharmaceutical facility follows exactly the same design logic.

The surface finish requirements for Grade 7 (flush details, coved corners) are like the walls of a hospital operating theater: no dead corners or crevices where bacteria can hide, and all surfaces can be wiped with strong disinfectants without being damaged.

Key Notes

The key judgment of "housekeeping spill vs. cGMP issue": this is a very important concept! In a closed-processing ballroom, even if equipment fails and causes a large spill, as long as the neighboring equipment is also sealed, the spilled material cannot contaminate other products. It is only a "housekeeping spill" and production can continue. In open processing, however, any spill is a serious cGMP event that can contaminate the whole room, and production must stop for investigation.

The business thinking behind a "Discretionary Upgrade": using the Grade 8 finish standard throughout the whole facility (including CNC areas) increases capital expenditure, but it can simplify cleaning validation work and help maintain a "production mindset" among staff. This is a business decision, not a regulatory requirement.

Practical Application

In CDMO facility design decisions, "how many rooms the equipment wash area needs" often depends on the scale of the future client pipeline. If the work is mainly small-batch (Phase I/II) clinical trial material, a two-room design is enough. If it is mainly large-scale commercial production, a three-room design greatly increases wash throughput and reduces production bottlenecks caused by equipment waiting to be cleaned. Design for the maximum design capacity 10 years from now, not for current capacity.

Practice Questions

  1. What are the key differences in surface finish requirements between a Grade 7 cleanroom and a CNC room? What is the scientific rationale behind these differences?
  2. In a facility, a pipe connection on a sealed 2000 L stainless steel bioreactor breaks and 1000 L of culture medium spills onto the ballroom floor. Is this a "housekeeping spill" or a "cGMP issue"? How do you decide?

Key Concepts

Equipment Wash Area Design:

The design principle is a one-way "dirty in, clean out" flow. Pass-through washing equipment (pass-through washer / autoclave) is a physical barrier that ensures the "dirty side" before washing and the "clean side" after washing never cross.

  • Three-room design: for high-throughput facilities. Dirty disassembly room → assembly room → clean staging room, with highly specialized division of work
  • Two-room design: for low-throughput facilities; assembly and staging are combined, and a single-sided autoclave is used

Facility grades and surface finishes compared:

6.7 Clinical Trial and Commercial Production (Clinical Trial and Commercial Production)

Original Text

The FDA Guidance for Industry for Phase 1 Investigational Drugs was issued in July 2008 [16]. It defined cGMP compliance as a step function with development and clinical phase 1 exempt from many cGMPs, while requiring full cGMP compliance for phase 2 clinical through commercial production. Clinical phase 2 and 3 production and commercial production have the same cGMP compliance requirements.

Producing phase 2 drugs in a commercial facility is a matter of scale alignment, rather than a regulatory segregation concern.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Phase-appropriate GMP compliance requirements:

  • Phase I (clinical): exempt from many cGMP requirements. The main goal is safety, and the facility can be research-type and flexible in design. A full cGMP documentation system is not required, but basic safety must be ensured.
  • Phase II and above: must fully comply with cGMP. The facility must pass GMP qualification and the documentation system must be complete.
  • The relationship between Phase II and commercial production: the cGMP compliance requirements are exactly the same! The difference is only in scale, not in regulatory requirements.

What "Scale Alignment" actually means: the main challenge of making a Phase II drug in a commercial production facility is that the process scale may be too small (small batches = low utilization = high cost), not regulatory compliance.

Analogy

A Phase I facility is like a test kitchen: chefs can explore recipes freely, without strict standardized procedures and a quality management system. From Phase II/III onward it is like a commercial kitchen: HACCP, SOPs, regular audits and strict cleaning procedures are required. The food safety requirements are the same for both; only the production volume differs. Making a small batch of an experimental dish in a commercial kitchen is a scale issue, not a regulatory issue.

Key Notes

Significance for CDMOs: if a CDMO facility is mainly for Phase II/III and commercial production, the facility design must meet full cGMP requirements from the start. You cannot "first build a Phase I facility and then upgrade it to a Phase II facility", because the cost and validation work of a later upgrade and rebuild is usually far greater than building a compliant facility directly.

Phase I exempts certain cGMP requirements, but this does not mean there are no quality requirements; there is just more flexibility, allowing more research-type judgment. The FDA standard when inspecting a Phase I facility is indeed more relaxed, but once the drug enters Phase II the facility must be upgraded.

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO client moves a Phase I clinical trial drug into Phase II. What changes must the CDMO make to the facility and the documentation system?
  2. Why does the ISPE Guide say that "performing Phase II production in a commercial production facility is a scale alignment issue, not a regulatory segregation issue"? What is the practical significance for facility design?
6.8 Vaccines and Biological Hazard Containment (Vaccines and Biological Hazard Containment)

Original Text

The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), the US National Institutes for Health (NIH) and individual national jurisdictions have clear facility requirements for levels of biosafety containment. All cGMPs apply to vaccine facilities. Biosafety requirements generally align with cGMP regulatory requirements. However, an example of a conflict is the requirement for biosafety BSL-1, BSL-2, and BSL-3 processing rooms to be at negative pressure in relation to the surrounding rooms, whereas non-BSL or GLSP facilities are typically at positive pressure. For further information, see Chapter 2 of this Guide.

Operator safety is considered very important in vaccine facilities; closed systems protect both the product and operators. The production ballroom concept applies equally to vaccine production with the exception that there is an additional equipment grouping — the "hot" BSL zone (area with, e.g., live virus or bacteria). Figure 6.13🔍 gives a comparison between a traditional GLSP facilities, GLSP production referred to as "Future Facility," and the final diagram is a "Future Facility" concept with the BSL containment overlay.

Whereas GLSP facilities tend to reduce to single rooms with the application of fully closed systems, vaccine facilities have an added BSL room segregation requirement.

Future/Flexible BSL Facility classification zones shown in Figure 6.13🔍:

  • Host Inoculum Preparation: GLSP, Grade 8 with local BSC
  • Host Cell Expansion: GLSP, CNC
  • Virus Inoculum Preparation: BSL, Grade 8 with local BSC
  • Virus Active Cell Processing: BSL, CNC
  • Inactivated Virus Processing: GLSP, CNC

There is an additional overlay that can be added to reduce production interruptions. Where the virus active cell processing room is very large and contains many process steps, the room could be further subdivided to contain catastrophic spills into smaller clean-up zones. The benefit of smaller rooms is the reduced facility clean up time.

Tutorial Commentary

Critical Design Conflict

BSL negative pressure vs. GMP positive pressure: the fundamental conflict in facility design!

Standard GMP aseptic processing: positive pressure, to prevent contamination from the external environment entering the clean area

BSL-1/2/3 biosafety requirements: negative pressure, to prevent hazardous biological agents (live viruses, bacteria) escaping into the external environment

A vaccine facility must satisfy both, which requires careful zoning: within the same facility, BSL areas maintain negative-pressure containment, while GLSP areas after virus inactivation can return to positive-pressure GMP conditions.

Analogy

A vaccine facility is like a hospital with negative-pressure isolation rooms: ordinary wards are at positive pressure (to keep outside bacteria from reaching immunocompromised patients), while infectious disease isolation rooms are at negative pressure (to keep highly infectious pathogens from spreading into the corridor). A hospital has both positive- and negative-pressure areas, achieved through precise pressure zoning. A vaccine facility is the same: the live virus area is the "isolation room (BSL negative pressure)", and the downstream area after virus inactivation returns to an "ordinary cleanroom (GLSP positive pressure)".

Key Notes

The design logic of "Hot BSL Zone" subdivision: in a virus-infected cell processing room, if the room is very large and contains many process steps, it can be subdivided further into smaller "Clean-Up Zones". This is not for GMP classification, but so that in the event of a catastrophic spill, the smaller area can be isolated and cleaned quickly, reducing downtime.

Virus inactivation is the key turning point: once the virus has been inactivated by chemical or physical means, the material moves from the BSL (negative pressure) area to the GLSP (positive pressure acceptable) area. The design of this turning point (routes, validation, test methods) is one of the core challenges of vaccine facility design.

Practical Application

During the COVID-19 pandemic, many biopharmaceutical facilities were temporarily converted to vaccine production. The main facility modification challenges included converting GMP cleanrooms originally designed for positive pressure into a BSL-2 negative-pressure design, which required a complete HVAC redesign (including exhaust filtration and pressure control logic). Staff also needed biosafety training, and some facilities had to install biohazardous waste handling systems. These modifications are often among the largest facility investments in vaccine production.

Practice Questions

  1. For a BSL-2 facility producing influenza vaccine, what are the main differences between its HVAC design and that of a standard mAb manufacturing facility? What special exhaust system designs are needed?
  2. In a vaccine process, how should facility pressures be configured before and after the virus inactivation step? How can the facility layout ensure that material before and after inactivation does not cross?
  3. Why does ISPE recommend further subdividing large live-virus processing rooms into "Clean-Up Zones"? What is the business driver behind this design decision?

Key Concepts

Biosafety Level (BSL) classification:

  • GLSP (Good Large-Scale Practice): for non-pathogenic recombinant microorganisms, equivalent to "BSL-0", and positive pressure can be used. Most standard biopharmaceuticals (mAbs, recombinant proteins) fall in this category.
  • BSL-1: non-pathogenic microorganisms (such as non-pathogenic E. coli); negative pressure is required.
  • BSL-2: microorganisms that can cause moderate human disease, such as influenza virus and adenovirus. Negative pressure + biological safety cabinet (BSC) required.
  • BSL-3: microorganisms that can cause serious human disease, such as Mycobacterium tuberculosis and certain rabies virus strains. Strict negative pressure + double airlocks + special protective equipment.

Area classification logic for vaccine processes (per Figure 6.13🔍):

6.9 Production Support Areas (Production Support Areas)

Original Text

Production is supported by many functions, including:

  • Warehousing
  • Facility engineering
  • In-Process QC
  • Quality
  • Administration
  • Environmental Health and Safety
  • Utilities

A production schedule can have direct links to these supporting areas, e.g., production pulling released materials from the warehouse, the production samples routed to QC on an hourly basis, or the scheduled equipment maintenance routing through the facility engineering shop.

Lean manufacturing concepts can be used to address operation of a production facility to help the production area and the facility operate as an integrated whole.

6.9.1 Emerging Countries

The use of modular design and construction should be considered for emerging countries. This allows for transfer of proven designs and the use of experienced trades in the construction of complex clean facilities.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Facility design considerations for Production Support Areas:

Support areas are often underestimated early in facility design, but they directly affect production efficiency. The location of each support function should be decided by how often it interacts with production:

  • Warehousing: should be close to the material entrance of the production area, with clearly separated "released vs. unreleased" material zones. The lean manufacturing concept of production "pulling" released material from the warehouse requires warehouse routes to connect directly with the production corridor.
  • In-process QC: production samples must reach the QC room every hour. The QC room should be located near the production area (but need not be inside the cleanroom) to minimize sample transfer time.
  • Facility engineering: equipment maintenance routes need to pass through the production corridor. The facility engineering shop should be located so that maintenance engineers can reach the production area quickly, without crossing the whole facility every time.
  • Utilities: distribution points for WFI, pure steam and compressed air should be as close to the point of use as possible, to reduce pipe length (lowering dead legs, cleaning needs and contamination risk).

Analogy

Production support areas are like the back-of-house systems of a hospital: the pharmacy (warehouse), central laboratory (QC), medical equipment maintenance department (facility engineering) and gas supply room (utilities) must all have efficient connecting routes to each ward (production room). If a hospital puts its pharmacy in the farthest corner of the building, nurses have to walk 10 minutes every time they collect medication, and overall efficiency drops sharply. The same logic applies in a biopharmaceutical facility.

Lean manufacturing is the hospital's "Just-In-Time" medication preparation system: drugs arrive exactly when needed, with no large stock held at the nurses' station.

Key Notes

The value of modular design for emerging markets: modular facility design is an important recent trend in biopharmaceutical facility construction. The main advantages include:

  • Can be pre-built and pre-tested in a factory, reducing on-site construction time (by 30-50%)
  • The design is proven: a new facility does not need to be designed from scratch and can directly adopt a template that has already passed FDA inspection
  • Construction know-how for complex cleanroom facilities can be exported from experienced countries (US, Europe) to emerging markets (India, Southeast Asia, Africa)
  • For a CDMO, modular design can be replicated quickly at different locations, enabling global capacity expansion

Lean Manufacturing Integration

Lean manufacturing guiding principles for facility design:

1. Value stream analysis:
   Raw material receipt → release → production → in-process QC →
   finished product → release → shipment

   Waiting time at each step = an optimization opportunity for facility design

2. Minimize distances to support areas:
   QC sample route: production room → QC laboratory <5 minutes
   Material replenishment route: warehouse → production room <same building

3. Eliminate waste (Muda):
   - Unnecessary movement (Transport)
   - Waiting for material release (Waiting)
   - Over-processing (Over-processing)

Practical Application

Modern biopharmaceutical CDMO facilities increasingly apply lean manufacturing principles. For example, a quality control (QC) laboratory designed as a "rapid analysis center" next to the production facility can complete in-process testing and release intermediates within 2-4 hours, whereas in a traditional design with the QC laboratory in a separate building, sample transfer and waiting for results can take 8-12 hours. This lean design directly shortens the overall batch cycle time and improves production efficiency.

Practice Questions

  1. At the facility design stage, how do you decide the best location for the QC laboratory? What factors should be considered?
  2. A new biopharmaceutical facility under construction in India is considering a modular design. List three main advantages of modular design and one potential challenge (compared with traditional on-site construction).
  3. If material release time in the warehouse is a persistent production bottleneck, which facility design strategies do lean manufacturing principles suggest to improve it?

Section Summary - Chapter 6B (Sections 6.4-6.9)

  • 6.4 Logistical Segregation: the core purpose is to prevent mix-ups. There are four main segregation types: batch, product, virus-reduced operations, and cell-containing vs. cell-free operations. Closed processing (CIP/SIP, single-use systems) can safely integrate operations that traditionally needed segregation, but shared components (CIP lines) are the biggest risk point.
  • 6.5 Facility and Process Flows: three purposes (logical presentation, avoiding mix-ups, environmental protection). A dual corridor (clean supply + dirty return) suits open processing; closed processing can use a single corridor, saving floor area and simplifying validation. Closed processing eliminates the need for Level 3 flows.
  • 6.6 Cleaning and Surface Finishes: equipment wash areas use pass-through equipment to achieve one-way dirty-to-clean flow (two-room vs. three-room design depends on throughput). CNC/Grade 8/Grade 7 each have corresponding surface finish standards and cleaning requirements, directly related to product protection. A spill in closed processing is usually only a housekeeping matter; a spill in open processing is a serious cGMP event.
  • 6.7 Clinical Trial vs. Commercial Production: Phase I is exempt from many cGMP requirements; Phase II and above must fully comply with cGMP. The cGMP requirements for Phase II and commercial production are the same; the difference is only in scale, not regulation.
  • 6.8 Vaccines and BSL Containment: the most critical design conflict is BSL (negative pressure) vs. GMP (positive pressure). A vaccine facility must resolve it through precise pressure zoning. Live virus areas are kept at negative pressure (BSL), and positive pressure is restored (GLSP) after virus inactivation. This is the most fundamental difference between vaccine facility design and a standard biopharmaceutical facility.
  • 6.9 Production Support Areas: support functions (warehouse, QC, facility engineering, utilities) should be located to minimize the distance of their interaction with production. Lean manufacturing principles (pull systems, waste elimination) guide facility design. Modular design is especially valuable for emerging markets: a proven design can be transferred quickly and facility construction time shortened.

Chapter 7: Architectural

p85-p97

ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p85 - p97

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the "inside-out" architectural design principle for biopharmaceutical facilities and the meaning of the three design stages (conceptual, basic, detailed)
  • Grasp the facility concepts: vertical/horizontal flow, modular (off-site) construction, and site flow management (personnel, materials, waste)
  • Learn the key considerations in site selection (masterplanning): climate, biosecurity, and the constraints of converting existing facilities (brownfield vs greenfield)
  • Understand how building envelope design and modular design affect the long-term return on a facility investment
  • Grasp the key points of structural design: floor loading, spans, seismic design, and their effect on future facility flexibility
  • Become familiar with the application of BIM (Building Information Modeling) in the design management of biopharmaceutical facilities
7.1 Introduction

Original Text

This chapter aims to provide a client, design manager, or project manager a set of guidance information to aid the initial design implementation process.

The focus of the chapter is on features of concern to biopharmaceutical and vaccines facilities. Information applicable to other pharmaceutical facilities is included for completeness of facility design.

Design Principles

Pharmaceutical facilities are usually designed from the inside out. The design should be based on user needs and requirements.

The life cycle and potential for future change of use of the overall facility site and buildings also should be considered in order to comply with sustainable construction expectations, particularly where an organization's product range includes more than biopharmaceuticals. In this case, the potential for sharing or re-use of facilities for oral solid dose or over-the-counter products should be considered, subject to compliance with GMP regulatory expectations and the definition of a "facility" from a single use perspective.

The knowledge learnt from initial design discovery and technical transfer should be combined. Project front end definition methodologies can help with rapid transfer of key project information.

Facility design has three phases:

  1. Conceptual
  2. Basic
  3. Detailed

Risk and safety assessment processes should be applied during each phase of the design to support the construction design and implementation process.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Inside-Out Design: The design of a biopharmaceutical facility starts by fixing the process requirements (equipment, flows, cleanliness classification) and then works outward to the building structure. This is the opposite of ordinary commercial buildings, where the shell is built first and the use is worked out afterwards.

The three design stages:

  • Conceptual: Determines the facility type, scale and overall layout direction. This is the most important stage, because decisions made after it are the most costly to change.
  • Basic: Determines specific dimensions, the structural system and the locations of major equipment.
  • Detailed: Completes all construction drawings and specifications.

Analogy

Inside-out design is like a tailor making a suit: first take the measurements (process requirements), then cut the cloth (architectural design). Doing it the other way round, building first and forcing the process in afterwards, is like buying an off-the-rack suit and finding the sleeves too short: altering it is troublesome and expensive.

Key Notes

A risk and safety assessment should be performed at every design stage. For a biopharmaceutical facility, biosafety classification requirements must already be considered at the conceptual stage, because they directly affect the ventilation system, effluent treatment and the building envelope. The later a problem is found, the more it costs to correct.

Practice Questions

  1. Why must the design of a biopharmaceutical facility be "inside-out"? What specific problems would arise if it were done "outside-in"?
  2. During technical transfer, which process information has the greatest impact on architectural design? Please give examples.
7.2 Facility Design

Original Text

Facility design deals with both the visual and functional aspects of biopharmaceutical facility operation.

In addition to process area, elements to be integrated within a facility design can include:

  • Administration and amenity facilities
  • Security facilities
  • Quality Assurance (QA)
  • Quality Control (QC) laboratories; including line In-Process Control (IPC) and microbiology laboratories for product and environmental testing
  • Warehousing, including cold chain operations
  • Downstream fill finish operations
  • Process and building services, including utilities feed housings

The appropriate integration of these elements together with an appreciation of any future needs in regard of adaptability, flexibility, and expandability should help to ensure the viability of a sustainable operation.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Full integration of facility design: A complete biopharmaceutical facility has more than a production area. It must also integrate administration, QA, QC laboratories, warehousing (including cold chain), downstream fill-finish, and utilities. The relative positions of these areas and the planning of the flows between them directly affect daily operating efficiency and GMP compliance.

Three key design attributes:

  • Adaptability: whether the facility can be adjusted to accommodate process changes
  • Flexibility: whether the facility can support multiple products or multiple processes
  • Expandability: whether the facility can increase capacity in the future

Key Notes

For a CDMO, flexibility is especially important, because a CDMO serves multiple clients and multiple products at the same time. The facility design must plan from the outset how product changeover will be achieved, rather than trying to fix it after the event. The QC laboratory should be located close to the production area to shorten the transfer time of IPC samples and improve production efficiency.

Practice Questions

  1. In CDMO facility design, "flexibility" and "efficiency" sometimes conflict. How would you balance them at the design level?
  2. What special considerations does cold chain storage require in architectural design?
7.2.1 Key Drivers for a Fully Functional Production Facility and Campus

Original Text

Operational and functional design drivers toward ensuring best flows and correct layout design include:

  • Functionally focused risk assessment and response:
    • Design risk assessment should be performed from the start of a project, starting with concept stage. The assessments should be reviewed and updated throughout the design and construction process.
  • Economy and efficiency of operation:
    • Simple work flow from site receipt to point of use:
      • Avoiding crossing transport/workflows
      • Where unavoidable, a provision of an SOP for ease of operation
    • Ease of access to location for facility and equipment maintenance
  • Flexibility, adaptability, and expandability:
    • Review of potential future operations. Acceptance of new methods or processes that may impact future operations:
      • Clear zones for storage, process, support, process services, building services, utilities, administration, amenity
      • Administration and amenity functions integrated with workflow and location
      • Visual integration and involvement of the administration function with the work process can be beneficial
    • Nice place to work: Bright, light, and visually sustaining; Safe; Open operation without barriers, where possible; Maintenance of visual links through use of clear materials allows capacity for visual checks by staff; Secure; Product risk awareness; Maintain staff and visitor parking outside the security perimeter

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three major design drivers:

1. Function-driven risk assessment: Design risk assessment starts at the concept stage and is continuously updated throughout design and construction. This is how ISPE expresses the Quality by Design (QbD) philosophy at the level of architectural design.

2. Operating economics and efficiency: The path of materials from receipt to point of use should be as simple and direct as possible, avoiding cross-flow. This is critical for both GMP compliance and daily operating efficiency.

3. Quality of the working environment: A bright, safe, open working environment raises staff morale and alertness, and also helps management carry out visual management.

Analogy

Avoiding cross-flow of work is like the one-way aisle layout of a supermarket: trolleys travel in one direction and the checkouts are near the exit, so customers do not collide in the aisles. The material, personnel and waste flows of a biopharmaceutical facility need similar one-way planning to reduce GMP risk.

Key Notes

The requirement to "keep administrative and visitor parking outside the secure perimeter" looks simple, but it goes to the heart of facility security design: unauthorized persons should not have access to the production area. A CDMO facility also needs special thought for client visit routes, which must let clients see production without breaching GMP flow rules.

Practice Questions

  1. How can "transparency (visual connection)" be balanced against segregation of clean areas in facility design?
  2. When a CDMO hosts client tours, how should the visitor route be planned to satisfy both GMP requirements and the client experience?
7.2.2 Facility Concepts

Original Text

The process design may demonstrate lean values that can be lost if they are not reflected in the design of the remainder of the facility. In this respect, both design consistency and a strong overall design concept are considered important.

Vertical, Stepped, Horizontal

Vertical or semi-vertical (stepped) flow is often necessary from a process concept viewpoint, rather than a horizontal flow. This may have a significant effect on facility design and integration with other facility activities, e.g., warehousing, integration of amenities and administration. This also may affect future facility flexibility.

Off-Site Construction

This may be referred to as modular, flat-pack, container, or skid based construction. The introduction of off-site construction techniques can provide an alternative to the traditional construction process (which may be referred to as a "stick-build").

Using these techniques, construction takes place in a controlled environment and is not dependent on variability weather at a construction site. Typical benefits of these techniques include:

  • Improved quality
  • Overall program (schedule) reduction, including reduced site time
  • Ease of future expansion
  • Less reliance on site/local resources with attendance weather conditions
  • Potential for cost reduction through repetition

Modular systems linked with off-site builds can provide better cost effectiveness through increased cleanliness, better fit, and a reduced program (schedule). The design team (particularly the architectural and structural disciplines) should focus on a modular approach from the beginning of the design.

Larger facilities can gain from a mixture of construction processes with, e.g.:

  • Modular process sets linked to flat pack building construction, and skid mounted and container based building services
  • Utilities linked to stick built or flat pack administration and amenities building construction

Geographical location should be considered when determining an appropriate construction technique.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Vertical vs horizontal flow:

Biopharmaceutical processes sometimes use gravity to transfer materials (for example, media prepared on an upper floor flows by gravity into bioreactors below), which creates a "vertical flow". Designers must reserve the space in building height and floor planning at an early stage. Horizontal flow, in which materials move across the same floor, is more common in small-molecule plants.

Off-site construction / modular construction: Modules are prefabricated in a factory and then transported to site for assembly. Compared with traditional on-site stick-build construction, this offers more consistent quality, a shorter schedule and reusability.

Analogy

A modular facility is like LEGO: each brick is made precisely in the factory and only has to be assembled on site. Traditional stick-build construction is like building a wall in concrete: you wait for it to cure, you are at the mercy of the weather, and once built it is hard to change. When a CDMO expands, a modular design lets it simply "add more bricks", saving a great deal of time and cost.

Key Notes

Biopharmaceutical facilities have a unique challenge: a large bioreactor can be several stories tall, so the building needs high ceilings (possibly 4-6 m clear height). This is a very different architectural requirement from a small-molecule plant. The design team must confirm the largest equipment dimensions early, otherwise late changes are extremely costly.

Practical Application

A CDMO in Taiwan plans to build a new biopharmaceutical facility with a limited budget and a tight schedule. Off-site construction offers these advantages: in Taiwan's changeable weather, factory prefabrication ensures consistent quality; modules can be qualified in advance; and if new clients are won in future, modules can be added quickly to expand capacity. The drawback is that early design decisions must be very firm, because once module dimensions are fixed they are hard to change.

Practice Questions

  1. What are the advantages and disadvantages of modular construction compared with stick-build, from a GMP validation perspective?
  2. If you were the engineering lead at a CDMO, in which situations would you choose a vertical flow design, and in which a horizontal flow design?
7.2.3 Flows and their Impact

Original Text

In-facility flows are of cGMP and regulatory concern. When in-facility flows are integrated with site flows, they contribute to the interdisciplinary understanding of a design layout, particularly at the masterplanning stage. The flow characteristics of a site and the impact of material, personnel, waste, and sample flows should be considered to help to ensure efficiency and economy of operation.

Space constrictions should be considered for smaller biopharmaceutical facilities and retrofits, particularly with closed or continuous processing. Internal site workflows should ensure safe and secure operation within the needs of GMP compliance and cleanliness requirements. For example, the master seed/cell bank should be positioned close to its use location, to avoid significant commercial, operational, or biosafety risks through unnecessary transport, security, and safety issues.

For closed processing, individual flows should be analyzed to ensure that each element of an operation follows an efficient and safe route without compromising the product. For example:

  • Personnel flows:
    • Street clothes
    • Facility gowning
    • Individual grade gowning paths
    • Warehouse clothing
    • Maintenance gowning
    • Location of specialist Personal Protection Equipment (PPE)
  • Material flows:
    • Master cell
    • Product flows: Upstream, In-process staging areas, Downstream, Fill/finish to store
    • Buffer and media flows including materials from store to location
    • Equipment maintenance flows
    • Clean-In-Place (CIP) considerations
    • Clean-out-of-place considerations

The impact of changes in flow formats should be understood in an architectural and structural site context and responded to accordingly.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

In-facility flow management: GMP regulations require strict control of the flow of personnel, materials, waste and samples to prevent cross-contamination. This is not only a matter of operating procedures but also of architectural design: corridors, door locations and gowning room arrangements must all support the correct flow patterns.

Main flow types (the six flows):

  • Personnel flow: different gowning levels need different gowning routes
  • Material flow: one-way flow from upstream to downstream
  • Buffer/media flow
  • Equipment maintenance flow
  • Waste flow
  • Sample flow

Key Notes

The requirement that the master seed/cell bank be located close to the point of use looks minor, but it involves major commercial and biosafety risk. The cell bank is the core asset of the whole production chain, and unnecessary handling increases the risk of contamination and loss. Placing the cell bank near the inoculation workstation at the design stage is an important design decision for reducing operational risk.

Analogy

In-facility flow management is like the clean, contaminated and semi-contaminated zoning of a hospital: a surgical nurse never walks into the operating theatre through the contaminated corridor. In the same way, operators in a biopharmaceutical facility need a clear gowning and changing procedure between areas of different cleanliness, and these procedures must be "enforced" by the spatial layout at the architectural design stage.

Practice Questions

  1. In a biopharmaceutical facility, which different gowning routes must be considered for personnel flow? Why can gowning for different grades not share the same route?
  2. How does the routing of CIP (clean-in-place) piping affect the overall material flow planning of the facility?
7.3 Placing the Facility on the Site

Original Text

A biopharmaceutical manufacturing facility may be constructed within an existing or new site. The facility should be able to operate efficiently within the type and location of the site.

7.3.1 Masterplanning

Masterplanning is the overall consideration of a site's or facility's current and future needs. It should be flexible in its response to varying product supply requirements. Masterplanning should reflect an operation's business planning aspirations and should oversee site and operational planning.

The need for defining a masterplan is dependent on the size of site operation (on-going or planned). A manufacturing facility dealing with all stages of pharmaceutical production should link the elements of the facility to each other in a logical sequence. For existing facilities, where a site has developed due to need and availability, the overall site operation should be reviewed to help maintain or improve cost effective production and management.

Bio Security and Safety Considerations

Local issues should be considered in the placement of a biopharmaceutical facility. Facilities may need significant security and safety protection due to local political situations. Risks around these concerns should be reviewed early in the design process to ensure that the issues are fully understood and appropriate protection, access, or facility placement and physical design/specification measures are implemented.

Facility Size and its Impact on Design and Concept

Existing sites and facilities may experience a size reduction through the use of continuous processes, particularly with large volume throughput. Significantly increased titers can result in reduced equipment sizing for the same throughput. Small high volume process modules may have a disproportionate effect on upstream and downstream operations with a consequent effect on site planning.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Masterplanning: Masterplanning is a comprehensive look at the present and future needs of the whole site. It is more than the layout of today's building; it is a "site development blueprint" that reserves space for future expansion, plans utility corridors, and considers the logical links between buildings.

Impact of continuous processing on facility size: The modern trend in biopharmaceuticals is a move from batch to continuous processing. Continuous processing can greatly reduce equipment size, which means a facility of the same capacity can be smaller. However, it may also cause a scale mismatch between upstream and downstream equipment, which must be considered at the masterplanning stage.

Key Notes

Site considerations for biosecurity: Biopharmaceutical facilities handle living biological materials (microorganisms, viruses and so on), so site selection must consider:
1. Surrounding sources of contamination (industrial emissions, agricultural dust)
2. The impact on the local environment of accidental release of biological material
3. Political stability (security of high-value biological materials)
These considerations barely arise in ordinary building design and are special challenges for biopharmaceutical facilities.

Analogy

Masterplanning is like city planning: a good city considers not only today's construction but also leaves room for future roads, metro lines and parks. A biopharmaceutical site is the same: when the first facility is built, the likely expansion locations for the next 10-20 years should already be planned. Otherwise, when expansion is needed and no land is available, the cost is very high.

Practice Questions

  1. How does the introduction of continuous processing affect the space planning of a biopharmaceutical facility? Analyze from both the upstream and downstream perspectives.
  2. Why must biopharmaceutical facility site selection give special consideration to biosecurity? Give two specific risk scenarios.
7.3.2 Facility Concepts: Large / Small / Greenfield / Brownfield

Original Text

Large Facility Concepts

The impact of climate on larger facility design means, e.g.:

  • An open campus format being more acceptable in mild temperate/mesothermal climate zones
  • An integrated or spine format being more suited to equatorial and cold climates where protection from the elements is necessary

A key consideration is the protection and ease of passage of goods or equipment in transit from one part of a facility to another. The selection of which format suits the operation is dependent on several factors, e.g., geographical location. Each format has advantages and disadvantages. For some locations or operations, a mixture of format may provide most flexibility in use, by providing expansion capacity, acceptance, and placement also will be a matter of compliance with local permitting criteria.

Small Facility Concepts

Smaller integrated biopharmaceutical facilities with multi-product or single product capability use less site area and are usually less affected by utilities issues that affect the large facilities.

Process Pods and Field Located Processes

The possibility of reduction in size of biopharmaceutical and vaccines facilities processes allows the capacity to contain processes in process pods. These process pods may be used both as 'plug and play' units within a facility or they could be positioned close to the drug need, e.g., as support to medical emergencies the needs of developing countries.

Greenfield

Layout opportunities depend on site size, access, and orientation. For further information, see Chapter 8 of this Guide.

Brownfield

For brownfield sites, pre-existing utilities, administration, warehousing, and engineering functions should be considered in association with the User Requirement Brief (URB) of a new project, e.g., to ensure the benefits of brownfield site integration. A brownfield site may be considered as a "retrofit" facility and introducing new ways of working may have a significant effect on other site operations. This can lead to further capital works with an overall operational life cycle gain.

Pre-existing Site Issues

Pre-existing site issues can significantly affect operation. Examples include:

  • Placing the primary HVAC intake of a biopharmaceutical facility close to the extract ventilation outlets of neighboring facilities and introducing significant environmental contamination risk
  • Locating a biopharmaceutical facility next to a conserved pond where clouds of small flying insects live and breed each season providing a continuing filtration issue
  • Locating a biopharmaceutical facility near cottonwood trees whose seeds cause significant filtration issues

Flood risk may be issue in some countries. This is a particular issue for biopharmaceutical facilities with potential risks to the local environment and public drainage system, as well as the potential risk of contamination of both process and product.

Retrofits

There may be unforeseen issues with a location. Pre-existing permitting, structural, or architectural issues should be considered prior to proceeding with a project. Local permitting should be checked as carrying out retrofit or conversion work may lead to the need to upgrade the entire facility to meet current environmental, accessibility, or other governmental requirements.

Pre-existing facilities: upgrade considerations:

  • Structural design: close columns indicates a high floor loading above or a facility that may not provide the required flexibility
  • Insulation costs to meet current regulations may be prohibitive as this may involve recladding the structure
  • Floor loadings may not relate to the use required: point or superimposed loads, such as individual leg loads of a large column or loadings of full bags of buffer, may prohibit use of a floor
  • Utilities and building services should be evaluated, as they may be oversized and not able to be "de-tuned" or undersized and unable to respond to the projected facility needs

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Greenfield vs Brownfield：

  • Greenfield: New construction on an empty site. It gives the greatest design freedom, but the highest initial investment, and there is no existing infrastructure to use.
  • Brownfield: Conversion based on an existing building or site. Existing facilities can be reused to reduce cost, but the design is constrained by the existing structure and there may be hidden problems (such as inadequate foundations or aging piping).

Process Pods: Small, self-contained process modules that can operate independently. They can be used within a facility as "plug-in" units, or deployed to remote areas (for example, for urgent medical needs in developing countries). They are an early form of decentralized manufacturing in biopharmaceuticals.

Analogy

A brownfield conversion is like renovating an old house: you save the cost of laying foundations, but you may find old pipes inside the walls, floors that cannot carry the load, or wiring that does not meet current codes. Sometimes the conversion costs more than new construction, which is why detailed due diligence must be done before conversion.

Key Notes

The location of the HVAC intake is critical in biopharmaceutical site selection! If the main HVAC intake is near the exhaust outlet of a neighboring facility, or near insect breeding sites (ponds) or trees (such as poplars shedding fluff), it will cause persistent filter clogging and seriously affect cleanroom classification and operating costs. These pre-existing site issues must be checked one by one during site evaluation, and must never be left until after construction.

Brownfield Assessment Checklist

Items to assess before conversion:
1. Structural column spacing
   → Columns too close together = high floor design loads = may be inadequate
   → Or: lack of flexibility to reconfigure equipment

2. Floor loading
   → Point loads from the feet of large chromatography columns
   → Area loads from large buffer bags

3. Assessment of existing utilities
   → Oversized → cannot be "turned down" to meet energy-saving needs
   → Undersized → cannot meet the needs of the new process

4. Insulation
   → Upgrading to current codes may require re-cladding the entire external wall
   → Cost may exceed that of new construction

5. Pre-existing permits
   → Conversion may trigger a requirement to upgrade fully to the latest building codes

Practice Questions

  1. Your company is considering acquiring an old pharmaceutical plant for biopharmaceutical conversion. Which five areas of "hidden risk" would you assess first?
  2. What potential applications do Process Pods have in a CDMO business model? Consider both customer service and capacity flexibility.
7.4 Envelope/Building Shell Design and Visual Design Influences

Original Text

The design of the building shell is significant in setting the tone of perception for a facility. The enclosure should perform a primary task of providing environmental shelter.

Local conditions should guide the approach to envelope design. This in turn affects the visual response. There can be an expectation of high visual quality for biopharmaceutical manufacturing facilities.

A facility is likely to be there for at least 30 years, and this should be considered when designing its shell. Issues to be addressed include:

  • Does the shell respond to the environmental conditions correctly?
    • Is it sensible to use flat roofs and parapets in high volume rain or snow areas?
    • Should items be integrated into the facade that provide short term value?
    • Will it wear well?
    • What is the location of intakes, extracts and extraneous pipework?
    • Is access for facility changeover correctly considered?
    • How easy and safe is it to maintain?
    • Is the opportunity for expansion considered through the provision of fallow or swing space or additional land?
