Facilities that look good and operate successfully over long time periods normally provide significant return on investment.

7.4.1 Modular Design

Consideration should be given to the integration of modules into a building shell or to the construction of fully modular facilities. Examples of limitations and opportunities provided by available construction technology, include:

  • Need to retain controlling dimensions: to obtain the advantages of repetition, a standard dimension set may apply that provides less dimensional flexibility than stick build
  • Different systems have different modular dimensions: where a modular system is to be used, the choice of system should be made early in the design process to ensure that the building is designed in accordance with the correct dimensions and any limitations are respected
  • How the module will be transported from the module fabrication shop to the final installation site. The method of transport should be considered, including:
    • Road limitations with overhead clearances, width and weight limits
    • Shipping sizes, including specialist hoist requirements
    • Airfreight limitations on size and weight
  • Allowance for module to module interconnection:
    • Space needed for process pipework connection
    • Lateral linking electrical and data trays, conduit, or ladders
    • Access space needed for maintenance of valves
  • Checking that future flexibility and adaptability are not compromised
  • Ensuring the risk of increased costs through initial factory tooling are balanced by site schedule/program and value gains

The decision to proceed with a modular design should be made at the front end of the design process, preferably at concept stage. Decisions on which process equipment is to be purchased, its design, the utility requirements of the equipment, and the process areas and method of installation, should be made early, as the facility design is significantly affected. The choice of modular contractors also should be made early to ensure their involvement from the commencement of the detail design process.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Building envelope / building shell: The external walls, roof and foundations, that is, the whole external structure separating the indoor and outdoor environments. For a biopharmaceutical facility, in addition to basic weather protection, the building envelope must also:

  • Support cleanroom pressure and temperature/humidity control
  • Minimize thermal bridges to reduce condensation
  • Position HVAC intakes and exhausts correctly
  • Allow for durability and maintainability over 30 years or more

Fallow/swing space: Undeveloped space deliberately left when the facility is first built, for future expansion. Although fallow space looks "wasteful" in the short term, it is a very worthwhile investment in the long term.

Key Notes

The decision on modular design must be made at the very start! Once detailed design begins, module dimensions are essentially fixed. If the decision to use modular construction is made only at the construction stage, not only is the cost advantage lost, but design changes may even cost more. In addition, the mode of transport of modules from the factory to the site (road, sea or air) must be planned in advance, because some mountain roads or ports in Taiwan may restrict oversized modules.

Analogy

Designing the building envelope is like choosing a car body: look not only at appearance but also at the local climate (rainy Taiwan means waterproofing performance), long-term maintenance cost (is it easy to repair), and whether it will still function well after 30 years. A beautiful but hard-to-maintain envelope will have a higher long-term TCO (total cost of ownership).

Practical Application

When building a biopharmaceutical facility in Taiwan, the envelope design needs particular attention to heavy rainfall from typhoons and the plum rain season (consider flat vs pitched roofs), the need for external wall insulation in high heat and humidity, and earthquakes (seismic design). These factors all affect the envelope design specification and increase cost at the outset, but they can greatly reduce maintenance expense over long-term operation.

Practice Questions

  1. What limitations may modular design meet at the transport stage? How do these limitations affect the maximum module size specification?
  2. What special requirements apply to module-to-module interconnection in biopharmaceutical facilities? Why is space for piping connections especially important?
7.5 Construction Issues

Original Text

Construction based decisions can be significant in both the implementation of a facility design, safety, and its subsequent use. Attributes that should be available to facilities include:

  • Flexibility, adaptability, and extendibility
  • Equipment access for replacement or maintenance

A biomanufacturing process may be installed only for five years of the building use. A facility represents significant investment and should be adaptable to another use without significant spend in order to meet its longer term objectives.

Examples of basic decisions that can be made that make a facility a better investment from the outset include:

  • The construction of the floor slab to take a variety of loadings should be considered. This will allow the potential for relocation of vessels without the need for consideration of specialist foundations for each location. This would provide only a small additional cost or even be cost equal to the specially provided condition if considered at commencement.
  • A transfer deck or suspended through floor vessel should be considered, as these affect loadings and provide vibration issues on an upper floor slab. This can cause the need for local or a special built-in support structures. Alternative options such that initial decisions do not affect future flexibility should be considered.
  • Provision of fallow space for utility expansion. Future utility needs for different products should be considered.
  • Provision for entry and exit paths for different equipment types. Installation of removable walls and floor sections for equipment movement should be considered.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

"The life of a biomanufacturing process is often shorter than the life of the facility": A biopharmaceutical process may use a facility for only 5 years, while the facility itself is designed for a life of 30 years or more. This means the facility must be designed from the start for multiple future uses, and not tailored only to the current process.

Forward-looking floor slab design: Designing the floor slab at the outset to carry a variety of loads adds a little initial cost, but saves the cost of special foundation work each time equipment is reconfigured later.

Removable walls / floor sections: Large equipment (such as bioreactors) may need to be brought in from outside the building when it is maintained or replaced in future. Designing the equipment access route in advance (including removable wall sections) can greatly reduce future refurbishment costs.

Key Notes

The most important insight of this section: spending a little more during construction saves a lot in future. For example, thickening the floor slab to support greater loads may cost 5% more at the outset, but could save millions in special foundation work over the next 10 years. This kind of forward-looking design thinking is an essential quality in a good biopharmaceutical facility engineer.

Analogy

Providing fallow space for utility expansion is like installing extra conduit and water pipe connections when building a house: you do not use them immediately, but when you later add air conditioning or a dishwasher you do not have to break open the walls and re-run the lines. In a biopharmaceutical facility, the cost of "one more pipe chase" is far lower than the cost of breaking through walls later.

Practice Questions

  1. Why do the "transfer deck" or the "suspended through-floor vessel" require special structural design? How do vibration problems affect production?
  2. If you were the engineering lead, how would you persuade the finance team to approve a budget request that spends more up front in exchange for future flexibility?
7.5.1 Impact of Structure on Design

Original Text

Additional Footings/Foundations

Inclusion of additional footings, foundation, and/or piling to support potential future expansion should be considered. This is a relatively small cost to allow flexibility.

Long Span/Short Span

Assuming a facility with more than one level, the risk to flexibility and adaptability of layout rests on structural loading and column location. Structural spans for commercial buildings have slowly been creeping up from a given 6 m × 6 m (20 ft × 20 ft) span 30 years ago to a normal 7.5 m × 7.5 m (25 ft × 25 ft) or even 9 m to 10 m (30 to 35 ft) spans in 2012. New secondary and biopharmaceutical facilities have been constructed with 30 m to 40 m (100 ft to 130 ft) spans to allow for flexibility in equipment layout and maximum future use flexibility. Safety codes should be met.

Dancefloor or Ballroom Concepts

The availability of smaller modular horizontal format biopharmaceutical equipment and more flexible set-ups allows the design of low building structures based on long spanning roofs. The structures are designed for reasonable service loads and capable of support of walk-on ceilings and/or high level service modules. This allows the opportunity for significant flexibility and adaptability with greater opportunity for expansion through extension or provision of swing space.

Construction Materials

These are generally dependent on geographical location and local availability and capability. Normally steel or concrete (either pre-cast or in situ) primary structures are used, although composite materials are becoming more available. Higher insulation requirements to meet energy targets have ensured the growth of modular insulated panel systems for both wall cladding (siding) and roofing. Workplace expectations and regulations are necessitating greater areas of glass wall both internally and externally.

Slab Thickening and Piling

Early decisions that affect future options become limiting factors for facility re-use or onward design. Careful consideration should be given to the laying of a piling mat and slab for very specific purposes. It may be preferable to design the slab for a general high loading initially and consider the area overall for a higher floor loading.

Example: A late design decision on volumes meant a late inclusion into a basement of an 18,000 L kill tank for a vaccines based facility. The new basement structure was designed for a general floor loading for mechanical plant, as there was a poor geotechnical condition and the kill tank was not highlighted to be an issue. Given a two week window, the design team was fortunate to design a special load spreading frame to fit the location, select a vessel that could fit the one sufficiently large access stairwell, pass between the column structure, and those items of plant that were in place. This provided a significant on-cost and will affect future flexibility.

Movement Issues

The inclusion of structural or constructional movement joints into clean buildings should be avoided.

Joint systems that provide a simple seal may be difficult to obtain; therefore, location of both primary structural joints, and screed or slab day joints, becomes critical for ease of maintenance of a cleanliness regime. Floor slabs at ground level should be designed to ensure either no joints or joints in discrete locations agreed with the design team and with a clear understanding of risk.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Effect of structural span on facility flexibility: The greater the distance between structural columns, the fewer columns obstruct the floor and the more flexible the equipment layout. Thirty years ago a commercial building span was 6 m × 6 m; today biopharmaceutical facilities can reach very large spans of 30-40 m. A long-span steel structure costs more initially, but it buys unlimited flexibility to reconfigure equipment.

Dancefloor / ballroom concept: A facility design with large spaces and long spans, like a big ballroom: there are no columns in the middle, equipment can be rearranged at any time, and the ceiling is high enough to allow maintenance routes. This is particularly suitable for single-story or low-rise biopharmaceutical manufacturing facilities.

Key Notes

Lessons from the 18,000 L kill tank case: This real case illustrates the cost of late design decisions. An 18,000 L kill tank was added late in the design, so the structural engineer had to design a special load-bearing frame within 2 weeks, and a tank also had to be found that would just pass through the stairwell and column positions. This could have been avoided entirely by confirming the process requirements early. The "hidden large equipment" of a biopharmaceutical facility (such as kill tanks and large buffer tanks) must be listed at the conceptual design stage.

Movement joints in cleanrooms: Ordinary buildings need movement joints to accommodate thermal expansion and contraction, but a movement joint in a cleanroom breaks the seal and becomes a potential contamination point for particles and microorganisms. Cleanrooms should therefore avoid structural movement joints as far as possible, or place them where they can be effectively sealed.

Structural Span Evolution

Evolution of structural spans in general buildings:
30 years ago (1990s):  6 m × 6 m  (20 ft × 20 ft)
Now (2010s):           7.5 m × 7.5 m  (25 ft × 25 ft)
                       9 m-10 m  (30-35 ft)
Advanced biopharmaceutical plants:  30 m-40 m  (100-130 ft)

Longer structural span → more flexible equipment layout → higher initial steel cost
But: future reconfiguration cost → greatly reduced

Height requirements for large bioreactors:
10,000 L bioreactor → about 4-6 m clear height
20,000 L bioreactor → may need 6-8 m clear height (including operating space above the equipment)
→ Floor height design must confirm the largest equipment size early!

Analogy

A "ballroom concept" facility is like a big warehouse-style hypermarket (such as Carrefour): no central columns, a high ceiling, and any area can be reconfigured. By contrast, a traditional short-span facility is like an old house: load-bearing columns everywhere and a layout that is very hard to change. Biopharmaceutical process equipment is constantly updated and replaced, and the ballroom concept lets the facility keep pace with process evolution.

Practice Questions

  1. Why do biopharmaceutical facilities need larger structural spans than ordinary commercial buildings? Analyze from the perspectives of equipment size and future flexibility.
  2. Why is the design of cleanroom floor joints so important? If joint locations are poorly designed, what GMP problems may result?
7.5.2 Seismic Considerations

Original Text

The design of biopharmaceutical facilities for seismic risk locations (e.g., west coast USA, Romania, North East India, south east China) should be carefully considered. For the US guidance, see the National Institute of Building Sciences Whole Building Design Guide [53].

The European Eurocode 8 [54] is likely to be adopted by Russia, Ukraine, and Belarus, along with the other nine structural Eurocodes. Kazakhstan is adopting the codes from 2015 in place of the previous reliance on Russian codes. For biopharmaceutical facilities, seismic design needs special consideration in terms of slab sizes and equipment connections. For example, design for earthquake code does not usually cover utilities considerations. Inclusion of additional bracing for the utility systems, as well as for the primary structure and securing of equipment may allow the opportunity for rapid restart for a biopharmaceutical facility after a seismic event.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Special features of seismic design for biopharmaceutical facilities: Seismic design of ordinary buildings considers only the building structure itself (it need only not collapse), but a biopharmaceutical facility must also consider:

  • Sloshing loads from large liquid tanks (buffer tanks, bioreactors) during an earthquake
  • Seismic anchoring of precision equipment (balances, mass flow meters)
  • Seismic support of utility piping (purified water, WFI, steam), which standard building codes usually do not cover
  • The ability to restart rapidly after an earthquake

Key Notes

Taiwan lies in a high-seismic-risk zone and is one of the most earthquake-prone regions in Asia-Pacific. Biopharmaceutical facilities in Taiwan must pay particular attention in design to the seismic support of utility piping (WFI, steam, nitrogen lines), the anchoring of large tanks, and establishing a standard operating procedure (SOP) for rapid restart after an earthquake. Once an earthquake halts production, the commercial loss far exceeds the cost of structural repair.

Practice Questions

  1. Why are standard seismic building codes "not enough" to protect a biopharmaceutical facility? What additional factors need to be considered?
  2. What is the commercial value of rapid restart capability after an earthquake for a CDMO? How can this be achieved at the design stage?
7.5.3-7.5.4 Modular Build & Structural Spans

Original Text

7.5.3 Modular Build

Process modules, whether as enclosed containers or skids, within a facility can be set on a deck or integrated into a frame. They are not usually self-supporting. Structural loading considerations and access to process modules, e.g., for replacement, should be considered.

7.5.4 Structural Spans

Longer spans and less floor interruption can provide greater flexibility.

Items of equipment which need special consideration for support on an upper level should be identified in order to maintain flexibility and adaptability. Items that require special consideration in terms of location and effect on current or future facility include:

  • Static loads (through floor buffer and/or media vessels)
  • Active loads (e.g., 2 tonne IBCs)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Static vs active loads:

  • Static loads: The weight of large fixed tanks (such as through-floor buffer tanks and media tanks). They need dedicated support points in the structure and may require special foundation treatment.
  • Active loads: Movable large intermediate bulk containers (IBCs), for example a 2-tonne IBC. When moved around the facility by forklift or crane, they create dynamic loads at different locations, and the floor design must allow for every area where these loads may occur.

Key Notes

Process modules are usually not self-supporting and must be placed on a support deck or in an integrated frame. This means that in a modular facility, the main building structure and the module structure must be designed together and not separately, otherwise a structural mismatch may occur.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is a 2-tonne IBC moving around the facility a greater challenge for floor design than a 2-tonne tank in a fixed position?
  2. How does the fact that a process module is "not self-supporting" affect the design of the main building?
7.6 Permitting, Code, and Insurance Issues

Original Text

Permitting varies between geographical locations. Planning and building codes should be understood early on in the construction process. Basic guidance includes:

  • International Building Code (IBC) is a US originated code that does not apply to all countries. A number of countries have stricter compliance requirements. This could affect building compartmentalization and escape compliancy for larger biopharmaceutical facilities, including the break-out requirements with highly contained deep plan facilities.
  • Insurers may have significantly higher and tighter expectations than many country codes. Depending on product value or facility throughput, insurers may apply Maximum Foreseeable Loss (MFL) requirements that could impact adversely on initial intended flexibility.
  • Fire and spillage risk issues: along with flood risk, the risk of fire, even in local facility areas, provides specific issues for biopharmaceutical facilities. Both code and insurer requirements may expect bunding to a facility either in specific areas, area by area, and/or at floor perimeters for sprinkler and/or fire water retention and analytical testing prior to safe release or disposal to ensure that there is no biological risk to public health via sewage, surface water, or ground water systems. This may introduce an undercroft or suitably sized basement to ensure the necessary volume is retained.

It is advisable to speak with the insurers and the permitting authorities from the outset of a project to ensure that code interpretations are in line with design expectations.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three key regulatory/insurance concepts:

1. IBC (International Building Code): A US building code that does not apply in every country, as each jurisdiction has its own building codes. Importantly, many countries' codes are stricter than the IBC.

2. Maximum Foreseeable Loss (MFL): The worst-case loss amount assessed by the insurer. MFL requirements may force the design to add extra fire/explosion compartments, which may conflict with the open, flexible space the designer wants.

3. Bunding: Low containment walls placed around equipment or areas to collect fire-fighting water and spilled liquids, preventing biological or chemical contaminants from entering the sewer. For a biopharmaceutical facility, fire-fighting water must undergo a biosafety assessment before discharge.

Key Notes

Insurance requirements are often stricter than regulations! This is an important reminder in the ISPE document. Architects usually ensure only compliance with building codes, but insurers (especially specialist insurers of high-value biopharmaceutical products) may impose stricter MFL requirements, including fire compartments, automatic sprinkler system specifications and fire-water collection systems. These requirements should be discussed with the insurer at the start of design, otherwise late changes are extremely costly.

Special handling of biological effluent: Unlike ordinary chemicals, fire-fighting water in a biopharmaceutical facility may contain biologically active material (such as live virus or recombinant protein) and must be analyzed and tested before discharge to confirm there is no biosafety risk. The physical space for this (basement or storage tanks) must be reserved in the architectural design.

Analogy

An insurer's MFL requirement is like a strict landlord: even if your design complies with building codes, the landlord may demand extra fire protection equipment before agreeing to lease. In a biopharmaceutical facility, the standard set by this "landlord" (the insurer) may be far stricter than government building codes, and must be built into planning at the outset, otherwise being forced into major changes midway causes great loss.

Practical Application

When building a new facility, a CDMO should invite the insurer and the government approval authorities to the design review at the conceptual design stage. This reveals differences between regulatory and insurance requirements early and allows a balance to be struck in the design. For example, an insurer may require a fire compartment every 300 square meters while the process design needs large open spaces. This conflict must be resolved through negotiation at the start, not after construction is complete.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the discharge of fire-fighting water from a biopharmaceutical facility need a special biosafety assessment? How does this differ from an ordinary chemical plant?
  2. How does bunding design affect architectural space planning? Why must an undercroft for underground storage tanks be considered in advance?
7.7 Facility Design and Design Management Methodologies and Tools

Original Text

Software developments provide design tools for the development and cross discipline integration of design from an early stage with a consequent capacity to design, record, and integrate projects from commencement. Visual development from instigation through 3D modeling of all aspects allows the consideration and review process to happen at significant speed allowing rapid option development and value judgment.

Subsequent construction and operation interfacing and sequencing review using real-time scheduling to post construction facility management using the fully integrated design model developed through construction for facilities management is possible. This is known as Building Information Modeling (BIM) and its attributes, referred as onward developments of 3D from 4D to 8D.

Biopharmaceutical and vaccines facilities can be extremely complex buildings, and may use up to 15 disciplines or more for design. This team needs to be integrated. BIM integrated with facility design management systems has evolved to make such programs significantly simpler to understand and implement.

Examples of integrated design tools include:

  • Integrated Project Delivery (IPD) [55]
  • ADePT design management tool [56]

7.7.1 BIM and Design Management

IPD was developed in the US as an integrated project management method. ADePT was developed initially in the UK by Loughborough University working with major contractors and design practices to provide an integrated design management system. Engineering companies may use similar integration methodologies for early project definition and front end design development.

BIM supports these systems through the integration of attributed 3D design software parametrics.

"Building information modeling may involve the introduction of new contracting methodologies as it allows for the adoption of a 'build before you build' opportunity with earlier review of design concepts and methodologies integrated with multi-discipline detail design interfaces."

For further information on the use and integration of the methodology, see the American Institute of Architects (AIA) [57].

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Building Information Modeling (BIM): BIM is a digital way of working centered on a 3D model that integrates all design, construction and operation and maintenance information. The dimensions expand "from 3D to 8D":

  • 3D: three-dimensional spatial model (length × width × height)
  • 4D: adds the time dimension (construction schedule simulation)
  • 5D: adds the cost dimension (cost management)
  • 6D: adds sustainable design (energy efficiency)
  • 7D: adds facility management (operation and maintenance information after completion)
  • 8D: adds safety management (construction safety simulation)

"Build Before You Build": BIM allows the whole facility to be "virtually built" on a computer before actual construction, revealing pipe clashes (clash detection) and construction sequence problems in advance and greatly reducing changes on site.

Analogy

BIM is like the "city-building simulator" in a video game: you build a virtual city on the computer first, check whether the roads flow, whether water and power are sufficient and whether the movement of people makes sense, and only then start real construction. A biopharmaceutical facility involves 15 or more design disciplines (architecture, structure, electrical, HVAC, piping, instrumentation and so on). Without BIM integration, conflicts between disciplines are almost inevitable, and the later they are found, the higher the cost.

Key Notes

The design complexity of a biopharmaceutical facility far exceeds that of an ordinary building, possibly involving 15 or more design disciplines. The core value of BIM is that all disciplines work in the same model, and when one discipline changes its design, the other affected disciplines can see the impact immediately. For example, when the HVAC engineer changes a duct route, the structural engineer can see at once whether it clashes with a structural beam, instead of finding out during construction that the ducting must be redone.

BIM Dimensions Reference

BIM dimension levels (From 3D to 8D):

3D → three-dimensional spatial model (geometry and position)
4D → + time (construction schedule planning and simulation)
5D → + cost (budget management and change estimation)
6D → + sustainability (energy analysis, green building assessment)
7D → + facility management (equipment asset management, maintenance records)
8D → + safety management (construction safety analysis)

Most critical BIM applications for biopharmaceutical facilities:
- Pipe clash detection: 3D
- Construction sequence verification: 4D
- Integration of the commissioning schedule: 4D
- Equipment maintenance data: 7D

Practical Application

Adopting BIM when a CDMO builds or expands a facility brings these specific benefits: eliminating pipe clashes before construction (clash detection) saves a large amount of rework cost; 4D construction simulation ensures the expansion sequence can be planned without disrupting existing production (especially important when expanding next to an operating facility, where construction dust and noise must be strictly controlled); and the 7D facility management model can be used directly for equipment maintenance management after handover, improving facility management efficiency.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is BIM clash detection particularly important in biopharmaceutical facility design? Give two pipe conflict scenarios specific to biopharmaceutical facilities.
  2. A biopharmaceutical facility may need 15 or more design disciplines. What are the coordination challenges among these disciplines, and how does BIM help solve them?

Chapter Summary

  • Inside-Out Design: Biopharmaceutical facility design starts from the process requirements, fixing equipment, flows and cleanliness classification first and then working out the building structure. This is the most fundamental difference from ordinary building design.
  • Three major design drivers: function-driven risk assessment, operating economics and efficiency, and flexibility/adaptability/expandability. The three must be continuously balanced throughout the design cycle.
  • Flow management: The design of the six flows (personnel, materials from upstream to downstream, waste and samples) is not only a matter of operating procedures but must also be "enforced" through the spatial layout of the building.
  • Greenfield vs Brownfield: A brownfield conversion looks cheaper, but hidden structural, utility and code-upgrade problems may push the total cost above that of new construction, so thorough due diligence is needed up front.
  • Timing of the modular design decision: The decision to use modular construction must be made at the very start of design (the conceptual stage), and the contractor should be selected to take part in detailed design, in order to truly gain the cost and schedule advantages.
  • Unique structural challenges of biopharmaceutical facilities: the high space requirement of large bioreactors (4-8 m clear height), concentrated loads from large tanks, very large structural spans (30-40 m), and no structural movement joints in cleanrooms. These design considerations do not exist in small-molecule plants.
  • Forward-looking design during construction: strengthened floor slabs, reserved utility space and removable wall panels. The small extra spend up front is the most worthwhile investment over the 30-year life of the facility.
  • Dual regulatory and insurance requirements: insurers' MFL requirements are often stricter than government building codes, and biological effluent needs special collection and assessment facilities. Both should be confirmed with the relevant parties at the start of design.
  • BIM is an essential tool for modern complex facility design: coordination of 15 or more design disciplines, virtual construction verification ("build before you build"), and integration of information across the whole life cycle from construction to operation and maintenance are all made possible by BIM.
8

Chapter 8: Mechanical

p97-p111

ISPE Baseline Guide Vol. 6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p97 - p111

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the core role of mechanical, electrical, plumbing and fire protection (MEP) systems in biopharmaceutical facility design, and how the risk assessment framework is applied
  • Master how to assess the risk factors in HVAC (heating, ventilation and air conditioning) design, including biosafety level (BSL), kinetic energy, biocontainment and biosafety, and distinguish how each factor affects the airflow strategy
  • Become familiar with the ISO 14644-1 cleanroom classification standard, the equivalence between ISPE / EU GMP / FDA / PIC/S classification grades, and the design range for air changes per hour (ACH)
  • Recognize the fire protection and electrical considerations specific to biopharmaceutical plants, including flammable solvent handling, clean agent suppression systems, and the critical need for uninterruptible power supply (UPS) and emergency standby power in cell culture processes
8.1 Introduction

Original Text

Mechanical, Electrical, Plumbing, and Fire Protection (MEP) systems should be designed to meet local codes and regulations and to satisfy both good engineering practice and business needs.

The utilities needed for biopharmaceutical facilities are common to many manufacturing facilities, and include:

  • Lighting
  • Power
  • Cooling water
  • Process water
  • Fire protection
  • Compressed air
  • Other gases

The design of HVAC and other supporting systems specific to biopharmaceutical facilities relies on the evaluation of factors pertaining to the risk of product contamination or adulteration by the system or the ambient environment. In addition, the design should consider the sometimes conflicting risks to worker or public health from the product or process.

The factors to be evaluated may be divided into two groups:

  1. Those risk factors that add to the risk of product contamination
  2. Those protection factors that provide layers of protection against contamination

The combination of risk and protection factors determines the design of the facility. Example risk and protection factors include:

  • Product Risk Factors:
    • Kinetic energy of the process (ability to produce aerosols)
    • Biocontainment (related to pathogenic or hazardous nature of organisms or genetic material)
    • Biosafety (related to biocontainment with regard to operator exposure)
    • Hazardous and flammable material handling
    • Product potency
    • Material sensitivity to molecular contaminants
    • Material ability to support microbial growth
  • Business Risk Factors:
    • Resource conservation and sustainability
    • Product value
    • Product medical criticality
  • Protection Factors:
    • Process closure
    • Process containment (use of micro environments)
    • Downstream processing (virus reduction and removal of other impurities and contaminants)
    • Pre and post-viral separation
    • Gowning
    • Classification
    • General arrangement and physical construction

A risk management approach is usually applied to these factors and may be formal, informal (or both). Risk mitigation approaches should be concomitant with the level of identified risk and the acceptable level of operator exposure. The risk to product quality should be assessed, appropriate mitigations designed, and the risks tracked. These tasks may be performed by the MEP engineering team.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

MEP (Mechanical, Electrical, Plumbing and Fire Protection): These four subsystems are collectively called MEP and form the backbone of facility construction. In a biopharmaceutical plant, MEP is not just infrastructure that "makes the building work"; it directly affects product quality, operator safety and the integrity of biocontainment.

HVAC (Heating, Ventilation, Air Conditioning): Of all the MEP systems, HVAC is the most critical for biopharmaceuticals because it affects product microbial contamination risk, operator biosafety exposure, and biocontainment of the external environment all at once.

The two-sided framework of risk factors vs protection factors: The design philosophy of ISPE Vol.6 is to first identify the risk sources of each process step, then layer on the corresponding protection measures, and finally decide the room classification and HVAC requirements. This is fully consistent with the ICH Q9 quality risk management framework.

Analogy

Designing the HVAC for a biopharmaceutical plant is like designing the infection control system of a hospital: it must keep external pathogens out of the operating room (protecting the product), keep germs in the hospital from spreading into the corridors (biocontainment), and also prevent medical staff from catching patients' infectious diseases (biosafety). The three goals sometimes conflict, which calls for an integrated risk assessment.

Key Notes

Key differences between biopharmaceutical and conventional pharmaceutical manufacturing: The main goal of conventional pharmaceutical HVAC design is "protecting the product from environmental contamination". Biopharmaceuticals add two unique challenges: biocontainment, which prevents live organisms from escaping and harming the public, and biosafety, which prevents operators from being infected by pathogens in the process. As a result, HVAC design must satisfy three goals that sometimes contradict one another.

Practice Questions

  1. Why might the same process space need both "positive pressure" (to protect the product) and "negative pressure" (for biocontainment)? Under what circumstances do these two needs exist at the same time, and how should they be resolved?
  2. List three design strategies that are "protection factors" and explain how each one reduces contamination risk.
8.2 Mechanical Systems and Environmental Controls

Original Text

Each step in the manufacture of a biopharmaceutical drug substance should be evaluated for risk and protection factors to determine the type of mechanical systems and environmental controls required to assure both a safe product and a safe work environment.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The key logic of this section is the step-by-step assessment. Risk profiles differ greatly between process steps: the main risk in cell culture (upstream) is external contamination getting in, while the main risk in the virus inactivation step (downstream) is the release of live virus. Different areas of the same facility may therefore need very different HVAC strategies.

8.2.1 Applying Risk Tools to Classification and HVAC Design

Original Text

HVAC design can impact basic parameters of the work environment and emissions to the outside, including:

  • Temperature
  • Pressure
  • Moisture content
  • Particulate content
  • Molecular (contaminant) content

The capabilities of an HVAC system can impact product contamination risk factors. Risk factors also can impact the need for the HVAC system to enhance or provide environmental parameters to support product quality.

An assessment of the impact of the environment on the process is required in order to understand the environmental control requirements of each space within a facility appropriately. During this risk assessment, product risk factors specific to each stage of a process should be considered and the effect on HVAC design should be identified.

8.2.1.1 Kinetic Energy

Process steps which add significant energy to a culture or product present an increased risk of generating aerosols and cross-contaminating other products or increasing operator exposure. Examples of processes and equipment that, if breached, may allow product to become airborne include positive pressure and pumped transfers (for chromatography, filtration, or movement of material), centrifugation, and pressurized reactor vessels. Typical mitigations in areas of high aerosol risk include increasing air exchange rate to dilute aerosols in the space and enhanced filtration to remove aerosols from the airstream.

8.2.1.2 Biocontainment (Related to Hazardous or Pathogenic Nature of Organisms or Genetic Material)

Biocontainment requires the prevention of the escape of airborne organisms from a space to protect public health. When assessing the biocontainment risk, the probability of the material becoming airborne should be considered. Liquids or solids will need added energy (e.g., by pumping) to allow the material to become airborne.

The need for biocontainment can drive risk mitigation approaches such as:

  • Primary process containment or closure
  • Secondary containment isolation or facilities
  • Treatment of the exhaust air from a space (e.g., scrubbing, chemical or thermal deactivation, or filtration)
  • Negative pressurization of spaces to prevent accidental emissions
  • Terminal filtration of supply air to prevent emission through the supply ductwork in the case of fan failure
  • Dedicated air handling equipment

Filtration is a frequently used treatment for exhaust air with single and double HEPA filters used to help provide assurance of containment. A single properly installed HEPA filter can provide a greater than 4 log reduction in airborne pathogens. The need for biocontainment also may drive redundancy in HVAC systems to assure that pressure reversals and flow stoppages do not occur.

Biocontainment should not drive decisions relating to the use of recirculation or once-through ventilation.

8.2.1.3 Biosafety (Similar to Biocontainment — With Regard to Operator Exposure)

Biosafety requires the containment of airborne organisms within a space. The need for biosafety can drive risk mitigation approaches such as:

  • Secondary containment isolation or facilities
  • Treatment of the recirculated air from a space (e.g., filtration)
  • Negative pressurization of spaces to prevent accidental emissions
  • Terminal filtration of supply air to prevent emission through supply ductwork in the case of fan failure
  • Dedicated air handling equipment
  • Once-through ventilation

Filtration is a frequently used treatment for recirculated air with single and double HEPA filters used to help provide assurance of reduction of airborne pathogens. A single properly installed HEPA filter can provide a greater than 4 log reduction in airborne pathogens. Where isolators are used, the need for biocontainment also may drive redundancy in HVAC system, to assure that primary containment is preserved.

When assessing this risk of potential airborne contaminants, the probability of the material becoming airborne should be considered. Liquids or solids will need energy added (e.g., by pumping) to allow the material to become airborne.

The quantitative reduction in potential airborne contaminants provided by filtration should be evaluated as part of the risk assessment. At high levels of biosafety (e.g., BSL-3 and 4) once-through ventilation is frequently used. Although HEPA filters are effective against microorganisms, increasing levels of biosafety discourages the use of recirculation, favoring once-through ventilation.

8.2.1.4 Hazardous and Flammable Material Handling

Hazardous or flammable material handling requires dilution of the hazard within a space and containment of airborne hazards to prevent fugitive emissions from escaping a space. Hazardous or flammable material handling can drive risk mitigation approaches such as:

  • Primary process containment closure
  • Treatment of the exhaust air from a space (e.g., scrubbing, adsorption, thermal oxidation, or filtration)
  • Negative pressurization of spaces to prevent accidental emissions
  • Redundancy to assure pressurization in all conditions
  • Lower Explosive Limit (LEL) detection
  • Deflagration containment and venting

Fume scrubbing or carbon adsorption may be used to treat exhaust air. Hazardous or flammable material handling also may drive redundancy in HVAC systems to assure that dilution of contaminants and pressurization are not lost.

The use of hazardous or flammable materials usually discourages the use of recirculation, favoring once-through ventilation. When assessing this risk, the actual probability of the material becoming airborne should be considered. Flammable liquids and some other hazardous materials may have a high vapor pressure, emitting vapors without added energy; some liquids or solids will need to have energy added (e.g., by pumping) to allow the material to become airborne.

8.2.1.5 Product Potency

The handling of potent products has the same impact as hazardous material handling; however, the actual concentration of the potent product at the particular stage of the process should be considered. The potency of biological products usually increases as they move downstream through purification. The potency of an API is usually reduced during formulation, as it is mixed with excipients.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Kinetic energy and aerosol risk: Any operation that applies mechanical energy to culture fluid or product (centrifugation, pressurized transfer, agitation and so on) can atomize droplets into aerosols. Aerosols are more dangerous than droplets because they can stay suspended and drift for a long time, travelling across a room or even into other spaces. The core of HVAC design for high-kinetic-energy areas is to increase air changes per hour (ACH) to dilute the concentration and to strengthen exhaust filtration.

The subtle difference between biocontainment and biosafety:

  • Biocontainment: Prevents viable organisms or genetic material from escaping from the facility into the external environment, protecting public safety. The emphasis is on "outward containment" and usually requires negative pressure.
  • Biosafety: Prevents viable organisms from exposing operators inside the facility, protecting employee safety. The emphasis is on "inward containment" and likewise requires negative pressure.
  • Both point to a negative pressure design, but the boundary being controlled and the objective are different.

Analogy

Double HEPA exhaust filtration = the pharmaceutical version of nuclear plant exhaust filtration: To prevent radioactive particles from escaping, nuclear power plants install multiple filters in their exhaust ducts. The exhaust systems of biopharmaceutical plants at BSL-2+ and above likewise install single or double HEPA filters so that air containing viable microorganisms is thoroughly cleaned before it is discharged. A single HEPA filter can provide >4 log (99.99%) pathogen removal, which is a very strong protective barrier.

Single-pass (once-through) air: Like a hospital operating room, the air comes in, is used once and is exhausted, never recirculated. This is standard practice for high-level biosafety areas (BSL-3, 4). The price is very high energy consumption, because every cubic meter of air has to be heated or cooled again.

Key Notes

The importance of LEL (lower explosive limit) detection: Downstream purification in biopharmaceuticals commonly uses large amounts of ethanol and isopropanol (IPA) as eluents for chromatography columns. Vapors of these organic solvents form explosive mixtures within certain concentration ranges. LEL detectors are mandatory safety equipment; once a reading above 10-20% LEL is detected, an alarm and emergency exhaust procedures must be triggered.

Potency increases along the process: The active ingredient of a biopharmaceutical is concentrated during downstream purification, so potency increases. This means downstream process spaces carry a higher potential hazard from airborne exposure: even a small amount of aerosol can cause serious operator exposure, so stricter containment measures are needed.

Practice Questions

  1. A centrifuge is regarded as "high kinetic energy equipment" in a biopharmaceutical plant. If a centrifuge seal leaks during use, list three possible HVAC mitigation measures.
  2. Why should "biocontainment not drive the decision between recirculated and single-pass ventilation", while "biosafety can drive single-pass ventilation"? What is the fundamental difference between the two?
8.2.1.6 – 8.2.1.12 Pre/Post-Viral Separation, Process Closure & Classification

Original Text

8.2.1.6 Pre- and Post-Viral Separation

Processes normally use several process steps to reduce the concentration of organisms or DNA/RNA and "virus-like particles" from a culture. In the case of vaccines, the pathogens may not be completely removed, but an acceptable level of inactivation may be achieved by a similar series of steps. The continuous nature of these processes may make "pre-viral" and "post-viral" steps difficult to determine precisely; however, a point in the process where virus reduction is adequate to justify separation should be identified.

Air systems that serve upstream open processing areas (e.g., bioreactor and harvest) are usually separated from downstream processes (e.g., purification) at the point at which an acceptable reduction is achieved. Air systems are usually separated with the intent of reducing the chance of contamination of the final product; however, this may not be scientifically supportable.

Using closed processing during pre- and post-viral separation can minimize the need to use HVAC systems for separation of these process stages.

The ability of the product or organisms to become airborne and the ability of treatment steps to remove viruses or virus-like particles should be considered.

8.2.1.7 Product Sensitivity to Molecular Contaminants

Open processes may be susceptible to ambient contamination by particulate or molecular contaminants, such as copper or zinc, which can denature products. This may impact HVAC material and filtration selections.

8.2.1.8 Product Ability to Support Microbial Growth

The greater the ability of the product or culture to sustain microbial growth, the greater the risk and associated drive toward classified space or process closure. A frequently used mitigation, required to protect product, is supply filtration with single HEPA filtration providing a very high level of protection.

8.2.1.9 Process Closure

The more layers of closure (separation from the room environment) a process has, the less impact the room environment normally has on product quality. This can drive a reduction in the:

  • Classification of spaces
  • Required contamination and cross contamination control
  • Need for exhaust of any hazardous gases, vapors, or dusts
  • Once-thru air supply

Process closure also can provide primary containment to address issues of biocontainment and biosafety.

8.2.1.10 Downstream Processing

Bioprocesses typically move from a clean or sterile media to an aseptic culture to a harvested solution that is contaminated with cellular material and other effluvia. These processes isolate desirable proteins and discard undesirable proteins and organisms from a culture. Downstream stages of processing, which are closer to the final API state are typically more sensitive to contamination than are early stages, see Figure 8.1.

[Figure 8.1🔍: Sensitivity of Contamination to Processing Stage — referenced in source, image not available]

8.2.1.11 Gowning

The level of gowning can have a significant impact on room contamination and particulate/microbial performance. Personnel can be significant contributors to space contamination, due to the shedding of both particulate and viable organisms.

The higher the level of gowning, the lower the demand for diluting airflow to offset the particulate and bioburden from operators. This becomes increasingly important as the space classification increases. For further information on gowning and its relationship to space classification, see the ISPE Good Practice Guide: HVAC [58].

8.2.1.12 Classification

Manufacturing environments should be controlled in a manner appropriate to the process and product being handled. Where the risk of product quality being impacted by the ambient environment is unacceptable; the environment is typically identified as "high risk" or "critical" and an enhanced level of environmental control is usually required.

Classification of critical environments in biotech and vaccine facilities according to airborne particulate concentration, as outlined in ISO 14644-1 and ISO 14644-2, is intended to reduce this risk to product quality. While there is no direct correlation between total particulate count and microbiological purity, it is generally accepted that reductions in airborne particulate should result in reductions in airborne colony forming units.

Regulations further enhance these particulate requirements by setting limits for the expected and tested airborne particle count and airborne microbial counts in sterile product facilities. Although there is no specific requirement for formal classification of spaces for non-sterile product according to ISO 14644-1, the latest draft of the EudraLex Annex 2 suggests that the principles of space classification outlined in Annex 1 (for sterile product) should be applied to biological product production as appropriate. This is also consistent with historical and risk-based practices which have typically employed classified spaces for both sterile and non-sterile therapeutic protein and vaccine manufacture.

Process closure (see Chapter 3 of this Guide) also can be a significant driver of environmental classification. Generally speaking, the fewer layers of closure a process has, the greater the need to perform it in a classified space. The relationship between process risk of contamination and layers of protection (e.g., from process closure) requires a flexible, multi-dimensional, approach to classification of spaces. For further information, see Appendix 1.

Historical approaches to area classification were usually more prescriptive.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Pre/post-viral separation point: In a biopharmaceutical process there is a key "viral separation point". Before this point, the process fluid contains live virus or virus-like particles (from the cell culture); after it, the virus inactivation/removal steps have reduced the virus concentration to an acceptable level. HVAC design needs to identify this separation point and use different air-handling systems before and after it, to prevent virus-containing upstream air from contaminating the already "clean" downstream product.

The leverage effect of process closure: This is one of the most important design principles in this chapter. Each step up in the degree of process closure (for example, from an open process to closed piping) allows the environmental classification requirement to be lowered accordingly. This means lower HVAC complexity, less cleanroom area, and significant savings in energy and construction cost. Biopharmaceutical plants widely adopt single-use (SU) bioreactors and closed transfer systems, partly to take advantage of this leverage.

The ISO 14644-1 classification system: It defines ISO Class 1 to 9 by particle concentration per cubic meter of air; the smaller the number, the cleaner. On the regulatory side, EU GMP uses Grade A/B/C/D, the PIC/S system is similar, the FDA uses ISO 5/6/7/8, and ISPE proposes a unified bridging system of Grade 5-8 + CNC+ / CNC / UC.

Analogy

The viral separation point = a special version of a sewage treatment plant: A municipal sewage treatment plant must kill germs before discharge; the virus inactivation step in a biopharmaceutical plant is the "disinfection station" of the process. The air-handling system upstream of this "disinfection station" must be completely isolated from the one downstream, just as a sewage pipe must never be connected to a drinking water pipe.

The trade-off between gowning level and air change rate: Just as a chef wearing a hat and gloves greatly reduces the risk of contaminating food, operators in more complete cleanroom garments release significantly fewer particles and microorganisms into the environment, so the HVAC system does not need as high an air change rate (ACH) to dilute contamination. The strict gowning procedures in higher-grade areas are exactly this engineering trade-off of "trading gowning for a lower ACH".

Key Notes

EudraLex Annex 2 classification requirements for non-sterile biological products: Traditionally, the ISO 14644-1 classification system was aimed mainly at sterile products. However, the latest draft of Annex 2 states that even the manufacture of non-sterile biological products should follow the classification principles of Annex 1 (sterile products). This has a far-reaching impact on vaccine and recombinant protein manufacturers and means a higher compliance investment.

Downstream processing is more sensitive to contamination: One might intuitively think that "the closer to the end, the cleaner it gets", but the logic shown in Figure 8.1🔍 is this: the closer the product gets to its final API state, the higher its purity, but the less buffering capacity it has, because once contaminated there is no later removal step that can remedy it. Downstream areas (purification, formulation) no longer contain live virus, yet they are actually more sensitive to environmental contamination.

Practical Application

A CDMO is designing a new biopharmaceutical plant (producing a therapeutic mAb) and is evaluating whether to adopt a fully closed single-use (SU) bioreactor system (compared with a conventional open stainless steel bioreactor):

  • Benefit of increased process closure: An SU system nearly eliminates the points of contact between manual handling and room air, so the culture area (upstream) can be reduced from ISO 7 (Grade B) to ISO 8 (Grade C), greatly reducing HVAC design complexity and energy use
  • HVAC cost savings: ISO 7 requires 30-60 ACH, while ISO 8 needs only 15-20 ACH. Halving the air change rate means AHU capacity, duct size and energy consumption can all be reduced significantly
  • Recommendation: Adopting an SU system combined with closed transfer allows a classification downgrade, which is the mainstream strategy in modern biopharmaceutical plant design

Practice Questions

  1. If the cell culture (bioreactor) area of an mAb plant uses a fully closed SU bioreactor, to what grade can its environmental classification be lowered from ISO 7? Under which principle of this section?
  2. Why is "the downstream purification area more sensitive to contamination than the upstream culture area"? Give one reason from the process logic and one from the regulatory perspective.
8.2.2 Classification Standards, Tables & Typical HVAC Parameters

Original Text

The ISO standard 14644-1 and 14644-2 is designed to support a number of industries classifying spaces according to the concentration of airborne particulate in a number of size categories. Only some of these size categories are of interest in the biopharmaceutical and vaccine facilities.

The ISO standard only describes a system for categorizing or classifying cleanrooms by particle size and concentration (count per cubic meter). It addresses multiple particle size ranges and operational states, but it does not specify which particle sizes are of importance or in which state they should be measured.

Table 8.1: ISO 14644-1 Air Quality Classes (particles per cubic meter by size)

Notes: Values in parenthesis are US Customary Class per USP and defunct FS 209. Yellow highlighted values are recognized by all regulatory authorities. Green highlighted values are applied for products sold outside the US.

Regulations and regulatory guidance documents normally outline the particle sizes of concern and the operational state in which they are to be measured. In addition, these regulations establish action limits for airborne viable organisms.

Table 8.2 outlines the relationship between ISO classifications and several regulatory systems of classification, including an ISPE system which is intended to bridge other regulatory systems and designations.

Table 8.2: Environmental Control Requirements

Notes: Values may be averages. EU and PIC/S require measurement of particles at 0.5 µm and 5 µm; the US requires 0.5 µm. Samples from Grade 5 areas should normally show no viable organisms. Recovery from the "In Operation" to the "At Rest" state should be verified to occur within 15 to 20 minutes for ISPE grades 6, 7, and 8. "At Rest" figures support Recovery and Static Room Classification testing — maintenance of these levels during idle periods is not intended.

The level of space classification drives both airflows and filtration directly. All other factors being equal, cleaner spaces (lower number classification) require greater airflow and higher grades of filtration.

Classifications of CNC, CNC+ (and/or ISO 9) can usually be achieved with high efficiency non-HEPA filtration (e.g., MERV 14/15) filters. These filters can be mounted in the Air Handling Unit (AHU) in lieu of a terminal location.

Grade 8 (ISO 8 in operation) classification also can be achieved using high efficiency non-HEPA filters; however, both regulation and industry practice suggest that HEPAs should be employed for all sterile operations, starting at ISO 8.

The greater airflow in cleaner spaces may be expressed in terms of the ventilation rate (or Air Change (AC) rate). While this measure has been dropped by IEST and others, the USP cites air change rates from 20 AC/hr for ISO-8 to 100 AC/hr for ISO-5. This approach is also embodied in the FDA 2004 Sterile Guide, which contains a suggestion that 20 AC/hr should be adequate for ISO-8 and suggests that cleaner spaces should have higher ventilation rates.

However, considering only the ventilation rate neglects the correction for the mixing factor (K). The mixing factor is a measure of room design performance. In addition, considering only the air change rate in the design of cleanrooms fails to account for the following fundamental factors:

  • Contaminant Sources:
    • Occupants (people): Population, Gowning, Activity level
    • Process: Equipment, Materials
    • Contamination Ingress: Through facility fabric (cracks in walls, ceilings, etc.), Through doorways
  • Configuration:
    • Architecture
    • HVAC system layout
    • Room distribution
  • Engineering Controls and Mitigations:
    • Local exhaust ventilation at generation points
    • Containment of generated particles
    • Room performance (ventilation effectiveness)
    • Pressurization/direction of airflow
    • Supply filtration
    • Airlocking

When using the ventilation rate principal to set air volumes (flowrates) in classified spaces, airflow should be increased as the classification becomes more stringent. However, the air volume required should be adjusted to accommodate the factors above. Sophisticated cleanroom airflow calculators and Computational Fluid Dynamic (CFD) modeling can help designers to achieve effective and efficient designs that relate directly to the process design and architectural configuration.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Classification system cross-reference (memory shortcut):

  • Grade 5 / ISO 5 / EU Grade A: the highest grade, aseptic filling area, <1 cfu/m³, static unidirectional airflow
  • Grade 7 / ISO 7 / EU Grade B: high-grade background, supports Grade A operations
  • Grade 8 / ISO 8 / EU Grade C: general aseptic support area
  • CNC+ / EU Grade D: Controlled Not Classified Plus
  • CNC / UC: general manufacturing or storage areas

Reference values for air changes per hour (ACH): These numbers are design starting points, not fixed requirements, and must be adjusted based on contamination source analysis:

  • ISO 5 (Grade A): usually unidirectional airflow (UDAF), defined by air velocity (0.45 m/s) rather than ACH
  • ISO 7（Grade B background）：30–60 ACH
  • ISO 8 (Grade C): 15-20 ACH (non-sterile); a higher rate is recommended for HEPA spaces
  • CNC+：6–15 ACH
  • CNC：6–10 ACH

Formula and Calculation

Air change rate calculation (ACH):
ACH = Q_supply (m³/hr) / V_room (m³)

Mixing factor (K factor) correction:
Effective ACH = actual ACH / K
K is usually between 1.0 (perfect mixing) and 10 (very poor mixing)
Design targets usually assume K = 2~4

CFD (computational fluid dynamics) can help confirm the actual K value

Example: a 100 m³ ISO 7 space
Target ACH = 45 (mid-range value)
Q_supply = 45 × 100 = 4,500 m³/hr = 1.25 m³/s
If K = 2, the required actual ACH = 90,
Q_supply = 9,000 m³/hr
→ Using CFD to optimize airflow distribution is the key to saving energy

Key Notes

The limitation of "relying on ACH alone": ACH is a rough design tool that ignores three key factors: (1) the strength and distribution of contamination sources; (2) airflow distribution efficiency (K factor); (3) the direction of supply air and the pressure cascade. A room with a lower ACH but good airflow design may actually perform better than one with a high ACH but many dead zones. Modern design increasingly relies on CFD simulation for verification.

The two-state test of "At Rest" vs "In Operation": EU GMP requires cleanrooms to meet different particle limits in two states. The "At Rest" limit is stricter (a lower particle count) because there are no operators or equipment generating contamination; "In Operation" allows a higher limit, but regulators pay more attention to this state. The recovery test (recovering from the "In Operation" state to the "At Rest" limit within 15-20 minutes) is an important verification metric for HVAC performance.

Practice Questions

  1. An ISO 7 (Grade 7) cleanroom in the "In Operation" state measures 400,000 particles/m³ at 0.5 µm. According to Table 8.2, does this value exceed the limit? By how many times?
  2. Why is CFD simulation increasingly important for HVAC design in modern biopharmaceutical plants? List two design parameters that can be optimized using CFD.
8.2.1.13–8.2.1.16 Sustainability, Product Value & Physical Construction | 8.2.2 Typical HVAC Parameters

Original Text

8.2.1.13 Resource Conservation and Sustainability Targets

As much as 60% of the energy consumed by an average pharmaceutical facility is dedicated to the HVAC systems. Optimizing the selected critical parameters and acceptance criteria for HVAC can provide a significant reduction in both cost and greenhouse gas emissions.

The design of highly efficient systems, employing a few core principles, can lead to further reductions and achievement of sustainability goals. The core principles for energy reduction include:

  • Classification of a space should be based on real risk. Over-classifying spaces should be avoided.
  • Over-filtering should be avoided. A single HEPA filter can provide a greater than 4 log reduction in airborne contaminants.
  • Where operations are not continuous, an idle mode should be considered to reduce airflow (and possibly relax temperature requirements) whenever an area is idle or when no open operations are present.
  • Consideration of advanced control techniques such as discharge air dewpoint control and adaptive static pressure reset.
  • Consideration of use of recirculated air wherever practical.
  • Consideration of heat recovery from exhaust streams.
  • Consideration of use of once-through air systems for heat rejection during winter months.
  • Consideration of utilizing local recirculation systems separate from primary pressurization air to allow recirculation to be shut down during idle periods.

8.2.1.14 Product Value

As product value increases, business drivers may push design toward a higher level of space classification to minimize the risk of a lost batch due to environmental contamination or due to an inconclusive investigation into potential contamination.

8.2.1.15 Product Medical Criticality

An increased medical need for a product may push design toward a higher level of space classification to minimize the risk of a lost batch due to environmental contamination or due to an inconclusive investigation into potential contamination.

8.2.1.16 General Arrangement and Physical Construction

The physical arrangement of spaces and details of construction can have a significant impact on HVAC system design. The adjacency of spaces with different classifications and biosafety needs can drive pressurization schemes and outside air usage. Similarly, the use of tight space construction can minimize the use of outside air for pressurization while maintaining good environmental control. Typical elements of concern include:

  • Doors and door seals
  • Walls
  • Ceilings

8.2.2 Other Typical HVAC Design Parameters

Tables 8.3 and 8.4 offer typical practice for selected design parameters; they are not intended to be definitive nor to provide minimum acceptable practice:

8.2.2.1 Room Conditions

Table 8.3: Typical Room Condition Ranges

Note: Conceptual air change rates are for the purpose of estimating only and are derived from previously published documents. Actual airflows should be calculated during the design process.

8.2.2.2 Biosafety

Table 8.4: Recommended Design Conditions for Biosafety Levels

Gaseous decontamination can be applied to most biopharmaceutical and all vaccine processes. For gaseous decontamination, exposed HVAC ductwork should be compatible with the decontamination means. The HVAC system also should be arranged to allow isolation of gaseous decontamination spaces or recirculation of the decontaminating gas in order to assure good distribution.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

What it means that HVAC accounts for 60% of energy consumption: Pharmaceutical plants are energy-intensive facilities, and HVAC is the single largest energy consumer within them. This translates directly into large operating costs (OpEx) and carbon emissions. Over the life cycle of a facility, the cumulative energy waste caused by over-classification can far exceed the construction cost savings.

The concept of "idle mode": When no open operations are under way in a cleanroom (for example during shift changes or holidays), the air change rate can be temporarily reduced (usually to 20-50% of full speed) and raised back to full speed before production resumes, with a recovery test confirming that the specification is met. This technique is called "setback mode" or "idle mode" and is one of the core strategies of modern energy-saving HVAC design.

The progressive relationship between biosafety level (BSL) and HVAC requirements: From BSL-1 to BSL-4, requirements for pressure, exhaust filtration, recirculation and airlocks all increase:

  • BSL-1/GLSP: positive pressure, recirculation allowed, no special filtration
  • BSL-2: negative pressure, recirculation allowed, airlock "recommended"
  • BSL-2+: negative pressure, HEPA filtration (optional), partial recirculation, airlock "recommended"
  • BSL-3: strong negative pressure, mandatory HEPA, 100% fresh air (no recirculation), multiple airlocks
  • BSL-4: fully enclosed negative pressure, double HEPA, 100% fresh air, multiple mandatory airlocks

Analogy

"Do not over-classify" = a restaurant does not need an operating-room-grade sterile kitchen: If a chef's ingredients do not need to be handled under sterile conditions, insisting on an ISO 5 clean kitchen only wastes electricity. In the same way, if a biopharmaceutical process step uses fully closed equipment, classifying its room as ISO 7 rather than ISO 8 only spends extra money on air changes. This is the real meaning of ISPE's emphasis on "classification based on true risk".

HVAC idle mode = an office air conditioner that automatically turns down after work: Modern smart buildings automatically lower the air conditioning setpoint after working hours to save energy overnight. The logic of cleanroom idle mode is the same: reduce the air change rate when no one is working to save a great deal of electricity, as long as the room is "restored to specification before work starts".

Key Notes

Most biopharmaceutical plants operate at GLSP or BSL-1/2: Although BSL-3 and BSL-4 are often discussed in academic literature and regulatory guidance, the great majority of commercial biopharmaceutical (mAb, vaccine) manufacturing plants operate under GLSP (good large scale practice) or BSL-1/BSL-2 conditions. BSL-2+ applies to processes involving conditionally pathogenic elements, and BSL-3/4 is very rare in commercial-scale manufacturing. Understanding Table 8.4 helps when discussing the suitability of a facility with regulators while designing a new plant.

The importance of temperature control for cell culture: The temperature control range for Grade 7/8 in Table 8.3 (±3–5°C) looks loose, but note that this is the temperature control of the room. The cell culture temperature inside a bioreactor (usually 37°C ± 0.5°C) is controlled by the equipment's own jacket, not by environmental control. The HVAC ambient temperature mainly affects operator comfort and equipment heat dissipation.

Practical Application

Energy-saving design case for a CDMO facility: A new biopharmaceutical plant built by a Taiwan CDMO produces 200 mAb batches a year, each with a 14-day process cycle. The plant HVAC design can adopt the following combination of energy-saving measures:

  • Idle mode: During weekend shutdowns (2 days a week), reduce the air change rate in ISO 7 areas to 30%, saving roughly 20-25% of HVAC energy per year
  • Heat recovery: An exhaust heat exchanger recovers energy from the exhaust air to preheat the supply air, reducing heating and cooling loads by 15-20% under Taiwan's climate conditions
  • Precise classification (avoid over-classification): Designing the fully closed SU bioreactor area as ISO 8 rather than ISO 7 saves roughly 35% of air change energy per year

Practice Questions

  1. According to Table 8.4, what are the differences between BSL-2 and BSL-2+ in exhaust filtration and airlock requirements? In an actual design, how do these differences show up in facility construction cost?
  2. A new plant plans to produce an HPV vaccine (using an E. coli expression system with a non-pathogenic strain). According to this section, which biosafety level (BSL) should it be designed for, and why?
8.3 Fire Protection

Original Text

Attributes of fire protection systems specific to biopharmaceutical and vaccine facilities include:

  • Use of cleanable sprinkler heads that are compatible with cleaning agents. Sealing of concealed heads should be considered for reducing risk of accidental release and increasing the capacity for cleaning. These heads can also minimize air leakage.
  • Use of pre-action systems to minimize the risk of accidental release in cleanrooms and biosafety areas.
  • Collection of sprinkler discharge may be required in manufacturing and biosafety areas. The collected discharge may require abatement prior to discharge.
  • Dry chemical extinguishing systems are typically only needed where volatile organic solvents are used.
  • Clean agent extinguishing systems (gaseous suppression) may also be used in high risk/high value areas, to assure prevention of contamination of product by the fire extinguishing agent:
    • Clean agent extinguishing systems normally require HVAC isolation, room seal testing. Any means of egress and notifications should be considered. Appropriately qualified personnel should be consulted when employing these systems.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Pre-action fire protection system: A conventional wet-pipe sprinkler system discharges water as soon as the sprinkler head melts. A pre-action system needs two triggering conditions before water is released (usually smoke detection plus a heat-activated fusible link), which greatly reduces the chance of accidental discharge. In a cleanroom or biosafety area, accidental water discharge would cause: (1) batch loss (water contamination); (2) plant downtime for cleaning (recovery validation takes several days); (3) a biosafety incident (if liquid containing viable organisms is sprayed and dispersed). Pre-action is therefore the standard choice in biopharmaceutical plants.

Clean agent fire suppression systems: These use gaseous chemicals (such as FM-200 and Novec 1230) to extinguish fires, leaving no residue and not contaminating the product. They are suitable for high-value equipment rooms (computer rooms, precision analytical instrument areas, high-value API storage areas).

Biosafety hazard of sprinkler discharge: If a fire sprinkler system is activated in an area at BSL-2 or above, the water sprayed will mix with liquids containing viable microorganisms. The resulting wastewater must be handled as biological waste and inactivated before discharge. This is the meaning of "abatement prior to discharge".

Analogy

A pre-action fire protection system = a safe with two-factor authentication: An ordinary sprinkler head is like a lock that opens when the temperature is reached; a pre-action system is like a safe that needs both a code and a key. Two conditions must be met at the same time before it activates, which greatly reduces the chance of false triggering. In biopharmaceutical production where a single batch can be worth millions of dollars, the cost of avoiding accidental discharge is well justified.

Limitations of dry chemical powder systems: Dry powder is the cheapest way to fight a fire, but powder residue would contaminate cleanrooms of ISO 7/8 and above and require a very long time to clean and recover. It is therefore used only in storage or handling areas with large quantities of organic solvents (such as ethanol tanks), not inside production cleanrooms.

Key Notes

The widespread use of ethanol and IPA in biopharmaceutical downstream processing: Protein A chromatography uses aqueous buffers, but steps such as reversed-phase chromatography (RP-HPLC), virus inactivation (low pH with solvent) and 70% IPA disinfection of equipment all use large amounts of flammable organic solvents. The downstream purification suite (DSP area) must undergo an explosion protection assessment (ATEX / hazardous area classification), including LEL detectors, explosion-proof electrical equipment and, where necessary, a dry powder or clean agent fire suppression system.

Practice Questions

  1. A fire sprinkler system is activated (falsely triggered) in a BSL-2 bioreactor room (about 200 m²) and a large amount of water sprays into the room. List the three emergency actions that must be taken immediately and explain the reasons.
  2. A clean agent system requires "HVAC isolation" when it is used. Why? What would happen if the HVAC were not isolated?
8.4 Electrical

Original Text

Attributes of electrical systems specific to biopharmaceutical and vaccine facilities include:

  • Cleanroom lighting that is fully sealed from the room side may be required for manufacturing areas. Top access should be considered to minimize the risk of contamination of these rooms.
  • Cleanroom phones and intercoms that are fully sealed from the room side are recommended for manufacturing areas.
  • It may be beneficial to seal all conduits and penetrations from the room side in manufacturing areas.
  • Within biosafety level 2+, 3, and 4 areas conduits and penetrations from the room side in manufacturing areas should be sealed.
  • Biopharmaceutical processes are typically wet and the use of NEMA 3R, 4, and 4X closures may be beneficial within manufacturing areas.
  • Within biosafety areas, NEMA 4X wiring devices and liquid tight conduit are recommended.
  • Backup power should be considered for all biological product facilities.
