Loss of power to bioreactors and processing equipment for more than a brief period can be catastrophic and result in the loss of batches. This is generally a business issue, rather than a GMP issue, but in cases of medical necessity the loss can be more than financial.
  • When evaluating the need for backup power, the need for HVAC during periods of power loss to assure environmental control should be considered. Qualification of the period that the facility is capable of maintaining conditions without active HVAC systems and a study of the sequencing of the restart of all systems upon the application of emergency power are recommended.
  • The transition back from emergency power to normal power also should be evaluated. Standard transfer switches make an "open" transition from normal power to emergency power and back, requiring equipment to restart in both cases. It may be advisable to make the transition back to normal power a manual transition (rather than automatic) in order to minimize impact. Similarly, "closed" transition equipment, which is capable of transferring back to normal power without a loss of current for more than a few milliseconds, can be considered to minimize this impact. Paralleling equipment can be considered, which would allow a facility to transition to backup power and back to normal power without loss of current, in a planned outage, offers the greatest flexibility, but at the greatest cost.
  • Uninterruptable power supplies (UPS) should be provided for critical control systems and equipment. The loss of data from process equipment can result in lost batches. While it is advisable to put environmental monitoring systems on uninterruptable power; it is not usual to put environmental equipment on these systems as the inrush current needed to start motors presents a challenge to these systems. In addition, environmental control (HVAC) systems can usually withstand a short shut-down period.
  • Wiring means and methods within biopharmaceutical facilities vary, but the electrical services within production areas should be liquid tight and capable of withstanding the cleaning products normally used in these environments.
  • Coated rigid conduit, stainless steel conduit and PVC conduit can be used, along with "liquid tight" flexible conduits. NEMA 4 and 4X enclosures may be used in wet areas. NEMA 12 may be used for powder handling or other process areas. NEMA 3R is not generally recommended because of its incapability of withstanding water from all directions.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three levels of backup power systems:

  • UPS (Uninterruptible Power Supply): Uses batteries to maintain power for a few minutes up to 30 minutes, designed to prevent short outages from causing control system data loss and equipment malfunction. A UPS cannot drive large motors (such as HVAC fans) and is used only for controllers, instruments and data recording systems.
  • Emergency generator: A diesel generator starts within about 10-30 seconds after a power failure and can drive large equipment (HVAC, chillers, agitators). The gap between the power loss and the generator taking over needs to be bridged by a UPS.
  • Paralleling equipment: The highest-level solution, which allows a switchover from normal power to standby power during planned power work (such as annual electrical maintenance) without interrupting the supply. It has the highest cost but is essential for CDMOs running 24/7 uninterrupted production.

NEMA enclosure ratings (protection standards for electrical equipment):

  • NEMA 3R: rainproof, limited water resistance, cannot withstand water sprayed from all directions, not recommended for wet process areas in biopharmaceutical plants
  • NEMA 4: protects against water spray (from any direction), suitable for general manufacturing areas
  • NEMA 4X: protects against water spray plus corrosion (stainless steel), mandatory at BSL-2+ and above, able to withstand strong cleaning agents
  • NEMA 12: dust-tight, suitable for powder handling areas (such as API weighing rooms)

Analogy

A power failure during cell culture = "suffocation" for the cells: Animal cell cultures (such as CHO cells producing mAbs) grow in a tightly controlled environment at 37°C and 5% CO₂ and have a very high metabolic rate. Once a power failure stops agitation, disables pH control and interrupts the gas supply, the cells begin to die within tens of minutes and the batch cannot be saved. A 2000 L bioreactor mAb batch can be worth more than USD 5 million, so the investment in a UPS and emergency generator is negligible by comparison.

"Open" vs "Closed" transition = the difference in shifting gears on a bicycle: An "open transition" is like a bicycle that must briefly stop pedaling to shift gears, so equipment has to restart; a "closed transition" is like a seamless shifting system where power is not interrupted. During cell culture, even a few seconds of power interruption can cause the control system to restart, leaving gaps in batch data records and triggering a deviation investigation.

Key Notes

Restart sequencing after a power failure is critical: After emergency power is restored, systems must be restarted in the correct order, usually: UPS systems first (already supplying power), then HVAC start-up (to establish differential pressure and environmental control), then process equipment (once the environment is confirmed to be within limits), and finally control system confirmation of data integrity. If the sequence is wrong (for example, starting a bioreactor before HVAC is restored), it may violate GMP requirements for aseptic manufacturing and lead to the batch being rejected.

The environmental monitoring system (EMS) needs a UPS: The EMS (including online particle counters, temperature and humidity sensors and differential pressure gauges) should be connected to a UPS so that environmental data recording is not interrupted during a power failure; this is a GMP requirement for batch record integrity. However, environmental "control" equipment (HVAC fans and so on) has too high a starting current to suit a UPS and is powered by the emergency generator.

Formula and Calculation

Backup power planning evaluation framework:

Critical load classification:
Level 1 (UPS required):
  - Process control systems (DCS/PLC)
  - Environmental monitoring system (EMS)
  - Life safety systems (emergency lighting, evacuation systems)
  - Critical data recording equipment

Level 2 (generator takes over within 10-30 seconds):
  - HVAC systems (environmental control)
  - Bioreactor agitation/control
  - Freezer/refrigeration systems (-80°C ultra-low temperature, 2-8°C)
  - Wastewater treatment/biological inactivation systems

Level 3 (short interruption acceptable):
  - General lighting
  - Office equipment
  - Non-critical manufacturing equipment

UPS capacity estimation (simplified):
UPS capacity (kVA) = total critical load (kW) / power factor (≈0.9) × safety factor (1.25)
Runtime = battery capacity (kWh) / actual load (kW)

Practical Application

CDMO power failure risk assessment scenario: A CDMO's mAb plant (3 × 2000 L bioreactors, each batch worth USD 3M) plans to evaluate its investment in backup power:

  • UPS system ($50K investment): Protects the control systems and prevents a power failure from causing gaps in batch data records and deviation reports; protects EMS data continuity and maintains GMP compliance
  • Emergency generator ($300K investment): Protects all Level 2 equipment and prevents batch loss from outages longer than 30 seconds; the potential loss per outage event (3 batches × $3M = $9M) is far higher than the equipment investment
  • Closed transition paralleling ($800K additional investment): Eliminates production interruptions during planned electrical maintenance; for a large CDMO producing 50+ batches a year, the investment can be recovered within 2 years
  • Conclusion: For a high-value biopharmaceutical CDMO, UPS + generator is the minimum investment, and closed transition is the option with the best long-term benefit

Practice Questions

  1. A CHO cell culture batch (14 days, currently on day 10) suffers a 45-second power outage. Assume the UPS kept the control system running but the HVAC stopped. Questions: (a) Must the batch necessarily be scrapped? (b) What steps should be taken to assess the usability of the batch?
  2. Why is NEMA 3R not recommended for wet process areas in biopharmaceutical plants, while NEMA 4X is mandatory? Analyze this design decision from the perspective of cleaning validation.
  3. Sealing of conduits and penetrations in biopharmaceutical plant electrical systems is mandatory at BSL-2+ and above but only recommended at BSL-1. What is the essential difference between these two cases in terms of biocontainment?

Chapter Summary

  • MEP design philosophy: Mechanical system design in a biopharmaceutical plant must satisfy three goals at the same time: protecting product quality (product contamination control), protecting employee safety (biosafety) and protecting public health (biocontainment). The three sometimes conflict, so a multi-dimensional integrated design is needed through systematic risk assessment (combining risk factors and protection factors)
  • Core HVAC design principles: Room classification should be based on true risk (no over-classification); process closure is the most effective design lever for lowering classification requirements; ACH is a design starting point rather than a requirement and must be corrected using CFD simulation and the mixing factor (K); the BSL level determines exhaust filtration (HEPA requirements) and the airflow strategy (recirculation vs single-pass)
  • Classification system cross-reference: The ISPE Grade 5/6/7/8 + CNC+/CNC/UC system bridges the different regulatory frameworks of ISO 14644-1, EU GMP (A/B/C/D) and the FDA (ISO 5-8); Grade 7 corresponds to ISO 7 / EU Grade B, 30-60 ACH, HEPA filtration; Grade 8 corresponds to ISO 8 / EU Grade C, 15-60 ACH
  • Special fire protection considerations: Pre-action fire protection is the standard for cleanrooms and biosafety areas; sprinkler discharge in areas containing viable microorganisms must undergo biological inactivation (abatement); the use of ethanol/IPA in downstream purification areas requires LEL detection and explosion-proof design; clean agent gas suppression systems can be chosen for high-value areas
  • Electrical and backup power: A cell culture batch cannot withstand a power failure (power failure = batch loss). The UPS protects control systems and EMS data recording, the emergency generator protects HVAC and process equipment, and closed transition switching equipment eliminates the impact of planned outages. Electrical equipment in all BSL-2+ areas must use NEMA 4X corrosion-resistant, waterproof enclosures, and conduits must be sealed liquid-tight
  • Sustainability considerations: HVAC accounts for 60% of total energy consumption in a pharmaceutical plant. "Precise classification, idle mode energy saving, heat recovery and avoiding excessive filtration" can significantly reduce energy consumption and carbon emissions while maintaining GMP compliance
9+A1

Chapter 9+A1: Sustainability & Appendix 1

p111-p121

Chapter 9 & Appendix 1: Sustainability + Supporting Information

Chapter 9 Sustainability + Appendix 1 Supporting Information (Technical Reference on Environmental Classification)
ISPE Baseline Guide Vol. 6 (2nd Ed.): Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing Facilities | p111 – p121

Learning Objectives for This Chapter

  • Understand the seven core strategies of sustainable design in biopharmaceutical facilities, and how closed processing reduces both energy consumption and the required environmental grade.
  • Master the precise definitions of the mechanical terms in Appendix 1, including the differences between CNC / CNC+ / Grade 5/7/8, and HVAC parameters such as MERV, air change rate, and ventilation effectiveness.
  • Learn the two-step methodology of "Risk-Based Environmental Classification" in Section 10.2: first assess the exposure risk, then convert it by the level of protection to arrive at the minimum required environmental grade.
  • Apply the FMEA methodology to carry out a six-step environmental risk assessment, translate the written method into facility design decisions, and understand how this approach can save a CDMO millions in construction and operating costs.
Chapter 9 - Sustainability

Original Text

9.1 Sustainability in the Context of Biopharmaceutical Facility Design

Although the processing chemicals used as buffers can be a challenge to the environment and the risk of non-deactivated viruses should be given careful consideration in regard to disposal, biopharmaceutical drug substances can be considered sustainable, as they are cultured in a growth medium which promotes their growth through the use of enhanced natural means.

Sustainable facility design involves:

  • Designing layouts that are spatially efficient.
  • Designing layouts that consider passive solar design in the placing and orientation of individual blocks of the facility on the site to gain maximum advantage/ecological benefit of the climatic location.
  • Insulating the facility to ensure that expenditure on energy is kept to a minimum.
  • The minimization of heat loss and heat gain through an efficient building envelope should be balanced against the need for cooling load reduction considerations in pharmaceutical facilities.
  • Correct site orientation and sun screening to minimize heat gain for cooling and to maximize heat gain for heating.
  • Commissioning and monitoring the facility for ongoing energy savings.
  • Ensuring that the facility operation and maintenance is efficient by design.
  • Providing renewable energy sources as a strategy including sources that recycle or return energy to the community.
  • Using process heat sources rather than exhausting them.
  • Minimizing water consumption and considering grey water recycling.
  • Working in conjunction with the process to provide all of the above to the benefit of the stakeholders, company, personnel, and public at large.

Each of the above activities may be considered as a sustainable design basis or criterion when placed in the context of building designs that consider:

  • Adaptability and flexibility in use
  • The potential for re-use, given full life cycle/design life assessment from implementation to demolition

Closed processing can provide opportunities for a significant reduction in:

  • Energy use and consumption (by reductions in Grade)
  • Once through air requirements
  • Facility response
  • Facility and utility size

The use of modular continuous processing lines and the potential both for closed processing and the greater use of disposables could allow facility size reduction and adaptability and flexibility in the use of a facility. This is likely to provide both an environmental benefit and an operational cost benefit through reduced local taxes and reduced facility energy usage.

Simpler manufacturing formats for retrofitting available commercial industrial buildings may provide a cost benefit, when compared to new bespoke facility designs expected of open, batch type processing.

An ISPE Good Practice Guide on Sustainability [67] is in development and is intended to provide overall guidance on sustainability and related topic areas.

Sustainable facility design can provide significant contributions to sustainability in the following areas:

  • Application of green chemistry
  • Reduction in carbon and/or total greenhouse gas emissions (metric tons CO2 equivalent), downward trend in annual figures
  • Process intensification, reduced waste, improved yield
  • Waste minimization (e.g., reduced water usage), reduced total waste, downward trend in annual figures
  • Energy reduction (in GJ), downward trend in annual figures
  • Reduction in solvent usage (tons), reduction VOC emissions (tons), downward trend in annual figures
  • Reduction in waste water COD (mg/l)
  • Areas where policies have been instituted
  • Verification of a system, e.g., ISO 14001 [68]
  • Certification using an agreed environmental assessment method/system to meet a specified target
  • Performing a life cycle assessment using an agreed assessment methodology

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Definition of Sustainability

Sustainable Facility Design: a facility that meets current manufacturing needs while minimizing its long-term negative impact on the environment. For a biopharmaceutical plant this is not optional; under regulatory pressure, carbon taxes, and ESG reporting requirements it is a must-answer question.

Importantly, the raw materials of biopharmaceuticals (biological culture media) are inherently closer to nature than chemical synthesis, so the process has greater sustainability potential, provided that the facility design keeps pace.

Key indicators:

  • Carbon emissions (tonnes CO₂ equivalent)
  • Energy consumption (GJ / year)
  • Wastewater COD (mg/L)
  • Solvent usage (tonnes / year)

Analogy - A Sustainable Plant = Energy-Saving Renovation of a Hospital

Imagine a hospital pursuing energy savings without compromising patient safety: it cannot switch off the air conditioning in the operating room, but it can optimize corridor lighting, recover waste heat to warm hot water, and use natural daylight in office areas.

The logic of sustainable design in a biopharmaceutical plant is exactly the same: sterility assurance areas (Grade A/B) are never compromised, but the other support areas offer plenty of room to save energy. Closed processing allows the core area to be downgraded (Grade C → CNC+), which is like shrinking the "operating room" so that the remaining space can be designed to ordinary energy-efficient building standards.

Key Point - Closed Processing Is the Strongest Sustainability Lever

The most critical insight of this section is: Closed Processing is the technical prerequisite for lowering the environmental grade, and a lower environmental grade translates directly into major savings in energy and construction cost.

The annual energy consumption per square meter of a Grade A/B cleanroom can be 50–100 times that of an ordinary office (mainly due to the very high HVAC air change rates). If closed processing allows an operation to be lowered to Grade C or CNC+, the energy consumption of that area is cut by tens of times, while the facility footprint and operating and maintenance costs are also reduced.

This is also the core value of the risk-based classification methodology in Section 10.2.

Practical Application - ESG Competitiveness of a CDMO

When selecting a CDMO partner, large pharmaceutical customers (Big Pharma) increasingly treat ESG scores as one of their procurement criteria. A CDMO with the following sustainable facility features has a clear advantage when bidding:

  • ISO 14001 environmental management certification
  • Life cycle assessment (LCA) report
  • Modular / flexibly adjustable plant design (supporting multiple customers and fast changeover)
  • A high proportion of closed processes (indirect evidence of strong contamination control capability combined with low energy consumption)

Framework of Sustainable Design Indicators

Energy intensity = total annual energy consumption (GJ) / output (kg API)

Water consumption indicator = annual water usage (m³) / number of batches

Waste intensity = total annual waste (kg) / output (kg API)

Carbon emissions = direct emissions (Scope 1)
       + indirect electricity emissions (Scope 2)
       + supply chain emissions (Scope 3)

These indicators should be tracked year over year and show a "downward trend" to meet the continual improvement requirement of ISO 14001.

Practice Questions

  1. Why can "Closed Processing" reduce both the required environmental grade and energy consumption? What is the mechanism linking the two?
  2. How does Modular Continuous Processing achieve both sustainability and commercial flexibility?
  3. Why are "non-deactivated viruses" in biopharmaceutical plant wastewater a sustainability challenge? How should they be handled?
Appendix 1 - Supporting Information (Technical Reference Material)

Original Text

10.1 Mechanical Information

10.1.1 Aide Mémoire 07121104 Classifications

Grade F: a classified space that satisfies the German Aide Mémoire 07121104 [69] airborne viable microorganisms < 500 cfu/m³.

Grade E: a classified space that satisfies the German Aide Mémoire 07121104 [69] airborne viable microorganisms < 250 cfu/m³.

10.1.2 PIC/S and EMA Application of ISO 14644

Grade D: a classified space that satisfies EMA and PIC/S requirements to meet ISO-8 measured via airborne 0.5 μm and 5.0 μm particulate in the "at-rest" state only and airborne viable microorganisms < 200 cfu/m³.

Grade C: a classified space that satisfies EMA and PIC/S requirements to meet ISO-8 measured via airborne 0.5 μm and 5.0 μm particulate in the "in-operation" state and meet ISO-7 measured via airborne 0.5 μm and 5.0 μm particulate in the "at-rest" state (with a transition between states of 15 to 20 minutes) and airborne viable microorganisms < 100 cfu/m³.

Grade B: a classified space that satisfies EMA and PIC/S requirements to meet ISO-7 measured via airborne 0.5 μm and 5.0 μm particulate in the "in-operation" state and meet ISO-5 measured via airborne 0.5 μm and 5.0 μm particulate in the "at-rest" state (with a transition between states of 15 to 20 minutes) and airborne viable microorganisms < 10 cfu/m³.

Grade A: a classified space that satisfies EMA and PIC/S requirements to meet ISO-5 measured via airborne 0.5 μm particulate and ISO-4.8 measured via airborne 5.0 μm particulate in the "in-operation" and "at-rest" states and airborne viable microorganisms < 1 cfu/m³. These spaces are normally unidirectional flow with an air velocity of 0.20 to 0.45 m/s.

10.1.3 Revised ISPE Grades

Grade 8: a classified space that satisfies US FDA requirements for ISO-8 measured via airborne 0.5 μm particulate in the "in-operation" state, as well as EMA and PIC/S requirements to meet ISO-8 measured via airborne 0.5 μm and 5.0 μm particulate in the "in-operation" state and meet ISO-7 measured via airborne 0.5 μm and 5.0 μm particulate in the "at-rest" state (with a transition between states of 15 to 20 minutes) and airborne viable microorganisms < 100 cfu/m³.

Grade 7: a classified space that satisfies US FDA requirements for ISO-7 measured via airborne 0.5 μm particulate in the "in-operation" state, as well as EMA and PIC/S requirements to meet ISO-7 measured via airborne 0.5 μm and 5.0 μm particulate in the "in-operation" state and meet ISO-5 measured via airborne 0.5 μm and 5.0 μm particulate in the "at-rest" state (with a transition between states of 15 to 20 minutes) and airborne viable microorganisms < 10 cfu/m³.

Grade 5: a classified space that satisfies US FDA requirements for ISO-5 measured via airborne 0.5 μm particulate in the "in-operation" state, as well as EMA and PIC/S requirements to meet ISO-5 measured via airborne 0.5 μm particulate and ISO-4.8 measured via airborne 5.0 μm particulate in the "in-operation" and "at-rest" states, and airborne viable microorganisms < 1 cfu/m³. These spaces are normally unidirectional flow with an air velocity of 0.20 to 0.45 m/s.

Controlled Not Classified (CNC): areas where HVAC systems are specifically designed to reduce airborne contaminants below the level of the ambient environment and both temperature and Relative Humidity (RH) are controlled more tightly than in the ambient environment. Claims for environmental control in these areas are related to the design of the system and installation qualification is common. No claim is made or qualified for the specific control of particulate. Typical systems will have heating, cooling and filtration meeting MERV 13 or better. These areas are sometimes referred to as "pharmaceutical" or "clean" areas within pharmaceutical facilities.

Controlled Not Classified with Local Monitoring (CNC+): areas where HVAC systems are specifically designed to reduce airborne contaminants below the level and both temperature and RH are controlled more tightly than in the ambient environment. Claims for environmental control in these areas are related to both the design of the system and system performance, installation qualification and operational qualification is common. These areas are typically qualified to meet ISO 8 requirements at rest only to control temperature and humidity within a specified band and are monitored for viable particulate during operation to provide background information for investigations and to assure the adequacy of layers of closure. These areas are generally aligned with PIC/S designation "Grade D" and airborne viable microorganisms < 200 cfu/m³.

Temperature Controlled: areas where HVAC systems are specifically designed to control both temperature and (where applicable) Relative Humidity (RH) more tightly than in the ambient environment. Temperature and RH are typically qualified in these areas and temperature mapping is expected. This designation is typically found in warehouse spaces, cold rooms, logistics.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Complete Cross-Reference of the Environmental Classification System

This section is the technical glossary of the whole Guide. Of particular importance is that ISPE consolidates the grade definitions of the three major regulatory systems (FDA / EMA / PIC/S). The full cross-reference follows:

Key Point - The Critical Difference Between CNC and CNC+

CNC and CNC+ are innovative grade concepts specific to this Guide, and are the two grades most commonly used by CDMOs when designing closed-process areas:

  • CNC (Controlled Not Classified): only temperature and humidity control plus MERV 13 filtration, with no particle count requirement. Suitable for fully enclosed operating areas with no product exposure.
  • CNC+ (with local monitoring): adds ISO 8 at-rest verification plus in-operation microbial monitoring. Although not a "formal classification", it has OQ records and continuous monitoring, serving as the last line of background assurance in a closed process.

From a cost perspective: the Grade 8 air change rate is about 20–30 ACH, CNC+ can be as low as 15–20 ACH, and CNC as low as 6–10 ACH. In a large facility, the difference per ACH can mean an annual electricity cost gap of more than NT$1 million.

Analogy - Environmental Grades = Choosing the Grade of Protective Mask

It is like choosing a protective mask: surgical mask (Grade D/CNC+) → N95 (Grade C/Grade 8) → PAPR (Grade B/Grade 7) → full-body positive-pressure suit (Grade A/Grade 5).

The point is not "the more expensive the better" but to choose the protection level that best matches the hazard (exposure risk). This is exactly the core logic of the risk-based classification in Section 10.2. If your operation is itself "closed" (equivalent to having no droplet transmission), then not even a surgical mask is needed. That is the meaning of the CNC grade.

Practice Questions

  1. To which EU GMP grade does Grade 7 (ISPE) correspond? What are its ISO classes at rest and in operation?
  2. How are CNC+ and Grade D (PIC/S) related? Why does CNC+ still require OQ and microbial monitoring even though it is "not formally classified"?
  3. Why is the airflow velocity requirement for Grade A "unidirectional flow 0.20–0.45 m/s"? How does this fundamentally differ from turbulent grades in its contamination control mechanism?

Original Text - Mechanical Terms (continued)

Gaseous Decontamination: one of a number of technologies that disseminate a biocidal gas to produce a minimum 3 log (and as much as 6 log) reduction in viable organisms in the environment. These technologies diffuse at the molecular level and can pass through a HEPA filter.

Fogging Decontamination: one of a number of technologies that disseminate a biocidal agent as very small particle size to produce a minimum 3 log (and as much as 6 log) reduction in viable organisms in the environment. These technologies cannot pass through a HEPA filter efficiently (greater than 20% loss).

Containment: equipment, systems or construction intended to prevent the escape of products, materials or organisms.

Biological Containment: an organized system of equipment, and construction intended to prevent the escape of organisms. Biological containment is usually identified according to the biological safety designation of the organism(s) being contained.

Containment Boundary: the imaginary line in the construction of systems or facilities on which the various components of a containment barrier are aligned. Spaces within this boundary are normally considered as contaminated with the contained material or organism.

Containment Barrier: construction that is impervious or highly resistant to the passage of micro-organisms. Solid materials make up the majority of containment barriers, but HEPA and ULPA filters are also considered containment barriers.

Global Recirculation: re-use (recirculation) of conditioned air leaving a room to serve any room of similar type or function.

Local Recirculation: re-use (recirculation) of conditioned air leaving a room only to serve that room or suite of rooms with similar biological and contamination risks.

N+1 Redundancy: a system where the failure of any single major component will not result in a reduction in capacity below the design requirement.

XX% N+1 Redundancy: a system where the failure of any single major component will result in XX% of the design required capacity remaining.

Decontamination Capable HVAC: an HVAC system that is arranged to recirculate gaseous decontamination agent for effective decontamination of a space and the air system(s) serving it.

Decontamination Compatible HVAC: an HVAC system where exposed components are resistant to gaseous decontamination agents.

Decontamination Capable Exhaust: an exhaust system that is arranged to allow recirculation of gaseous decontamination agent for effective decontamination of filter and fans as well as exhaust of decontamination agent from a served space.

Pre and Post-Viral Separation (Viral+ and Viral−): the separation of pre-viral and post-viral product or upstream (bioreaction and harvest) from downstream (purification) is not precise. Most processes utilize multiple process steps to reduce the concentration of organisms or DNA/RNA from the culture, creating the final product. In the case of vaccines, the viral contaminants may never be removed. The convention is to separate air systems that serve these two open processing areas to reduce the chance of contamination of the final product; however, this may not be scientifically supportable. Similar to earlier evaluations, the ability of the product or organisms to become airborne should be considered. This separation is affected by process closure, the achievement of closure obviates the need for HVAC separation.

Multiple Efficiency Reporting Value (MERV): the ASHRAE Standard 52.2 [70] method of testing filter efficiency by challenging filters with particles of multiple sizes and integrating the efficiency into a single number rating. The higher the MERV number the more efficient the filter. MERV 14/15 is approximately equivalent to a 95% efficient filter.

Air Change Rate (Ventilation Rate): the volume of air supplied to a room (per hour) divided by the volume of the room. This is a purely numerical evaluation used as a rule of thumb in cleanroom design.

Particle Generation Rate: the number of particles of a target size released into a room (per hour) by processes, people, or in the supply air.

Dilution Ventilation: Reduction in airborne contamination via mixing of clean incoming air with contaminated air within the room and removal of an equivalent amount to exhaust or recirculation via treatment (e.g. filtration).

Displacement Ventilation: reduction in airborne contamination via "plug flow" of clean incoming air forcing contaminated air within the room to exhaust or recirculation via treatment (e.g., filtration).

Ventilation Efficiency (Effectiveness): the ability of an HVAC system to dilute contaminants when compared to ideal dilution. A ventilation effectiveness of 1.0 indicates that a room is able to dilute contaminants as effectively as is indicated by an ideal dilution calculation. Displacement ventilation may have an effectiveness as high as 3.0 to 4.0.

Recovery: a test defined in ISO 14644-3 [63] intended to show a cleanroom's ability to "cleanup" from an operational particulate specification to a lower (cleaner) classification.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Gaseous Decontamination vs Fogging

Gaseous Decontamination (e.g. VHP / H₂O₂ gas): diffuses at the molecular level and can pass through HEPA filters, so the entire supply air system (including ducts and the filter bodies) can be decontaminated. This is the mainstream technology for BSL-2/3 biological facilities or biopharmaceutical plants with a risk of viral exposure.

Fogging (e.g. hydrogen peroxide fogging): the particles are larger and cannot effectively pass through HEPA filters (loss >20%), so it can decontaminate only the room itself and cannot cover the HVAC system.

When to choose: if both the room and the HVAC must be decontaminated (such as an overall re-clean after isolator validation), a gaseous technology must be chosen.

Core Concept - Principle of Viral+ / Viral- Zoning

Biopharmaceutical production is divided into two zones, "before viral clearance (Viral+)" and "after viral clearance (Viral-)":

  • Viral+ zone: upstream processing (cell culture, harvest), spaces containing active virus and cells
  • Viral- zone: after downstream purification, where in theory the virus has been removed or inactivated

The traditional practice is to completely separate the HVAC systems of the two zones to prevent Viral+ air from contaminating the final product. The key insight, however, is that if the process itself is closed, this HVAC separation has no scientific necessity, because virus inside a closed system does not become an airborne contaminant. This again underlines the fundamental influence of "process closure" on facility design.

Calculation of Key HVAC Design Parameters

Air change rate ACH = supply airflow (m³/h) ÷ room volume (m³)

Ventilation effectiveness comparison:
  Dilution ventilation (turbulent) effectiveness ≈ 1.0
  Displacement ventilation (unidirectional) effectiveness ≈ 3.0–4.0

Implication:
  To achieve the same cleanliness,
  displacement ventilation needs only 1/3 the air changes of dilution ventilation
  → major energy saving potential

MERV equivalence:
  MERV 13 ≈ 75–90% efficiency (≥1μm)
  MERV 14 ≈ 90–95% efficiency
  HEPA   ≈ 99.97% efficiency (≥0.3μm)

Key Point - What N+1 Redundancy Actually Means

N+1 Redundancy is a basic safety principle of GMP facility design: if any one major component fails, the system still maintains 100% of its total capacity.

XX% N+1 redundancy is a more practical version: for example, "50% N+1 redundancy" means that after any one component fails the system still maintains 50% capacity. This may be acceptable for non-sterile manufacturing areas, but Grade A/B spaces generally require 100% N+1.

Commercial considerations for a CDMO design: N+1 redundancy increases the initial construction cost, but it prevents batch failures caused by equipment failure (a single biopharmaceutical batch can be worth tens of millions of NT dollars). An ROI model should be used to evaluate the optimal redundancy strategy.

Practice Questions

  1. What HVAC design specification does a BSL-2 biological facility that uses VHP (vaporized hydrogen peroxide) decontamination require? (The difference between Decontamination Capable and Compatible)
  2. If a cell culture operation uses a sealed single-use bioreactor (fully closed), is a Viral+/Viral- HVAC separation still required? Explain your reasoning.
  3. Why is the ventilation effectiveness of "Displacement Ventilation" as high as 3.0–4.0? How is this reflected in a Grade A unidirectional flow design?
10.2 Methods of Environmental Classification through Risk Assessment

Original Text

10.2 Methods of Environmental Classification through Risk Assessment

In a risk-based approach to area classification, increasing exposure risk (based on process sensitivity, bio-safety, or the presence of downstream mitigations) should be considered in light of established layers of protection, in order to determine the appropriate space classification for an operation. There are numerous methods to do this analysis. Tables 10.1 and 10.2 are an example of one possible approach to risk assessment.

In stage 1, the risk of contamination is determined based on the stage of the process and the inherent risk to contamination of the process. The risk may derive from a lack of isolation from the ambient environment, high energy in adjacent processes (high risk of aerosol contaminants), the presence of growth media in the process, or the inherent fragility of the organism or protein. The product of these two factors yields an exposure risk ranking (given here on a 1 to 5 scale of Low, Med-Low, Medium, Med-High, and High).

In the second step, this exposure risk is adjusted by evaluating the layers of protection available to shield the process from contamination. The more layers of protection available to prevent contamination the less critical the ambient environment is to product quality. The risk of failure of the protection should be included in this evaluation; for example, a silicone tube has a higher risk of failure than a stainless steel tube; therefore, the layers of protection it provides would be less.

This example equates the levels of protection, approximately, with log reduction of contaminants provided by that layer. This concept is leveraged from sterile processing where log reductions in contamination are the standard for rating process closure. This concept is also well aligned with the environmental classifications, where each succeeding lower classification number is one order of magnitude cleaner than its predecessor. This example equates the five levels of risk with five classifications (Grade 5, Grade 7, Grade 8, CNC+, and CNC) as discussed in the following section.

Notes:
  1. A fully hermetic system should provide > 4 log reduction in contaminants or 4 layers of protection.
  2. An open micro-environment can typically provide a > 1 to 2 log reduction in contaminants, or 1 to 2 layers of protection.
  3. A closed micro-environment can provide a > 3 log reduction in contaminants, or 3 layers of protection.
  4. The log reductions proposed herein are derived from sterile and containment equipment performance.
  5. The risk of failure should be included in the evaluation (e.g., stainless steel piping would rank as greater protection than a plastic tube).

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Logical Framework of Risk-Based Classification

This is one of the most practical tools in the Guide. The traditional method is to apply the regulatory grades by rote; the risk-based method instead requires:

Final required environmental grade = f (exposure risk, number of protection layers)

Core logic:
• Higher exposure risk → a stricter environmental grade is needed
• More protection layers → the environmental grade requirement can be lowered
• The two offset each other, giving the minimum necessary grade

This approach gives facility designers a scientific basis to argue that "although my process is a high-risk step, it has 4 layers of protection, so only a CNC environment is needed", which is a strong argument in regulatory communication.

Analogy - Risk Classification = Disaster-Resistance Rating

Imagine a structural engineer assessing how strong the seismic specification of a building must be: seismic risk level (exposure risk) × structural protection measures (number of protection layers) = required building code level.

Taipei (high seismic zone) + purely timber structure (0 layers of protection) → the strictest code is required (Grade 5 equivalent).
Taipei (high seismic zone) + steel frame + base isolation pads + dampers (4 layers of protection) → the standard code is sufficient (CNC equivalent).
Offshore Yilan (low seismic zone) + any structure → a relaxed code (CNC).

Key insight: a dangerous location does not necessarily require the most expensive design, because protective measures can "hedge" the risk.

Key Point - How to Read Table 10.2

Table 10.2 is a "look-up tool":

  1. First determine the exposure risk level from Table 10.1 (High / Med-High / Medium / Med-Low / Low)
  2. Then count how many layers of protection there are (0 = fully open; 4 = fully sealed system)
  3. Look up the table to obtain the minimum required environmental grade

Key conclusion: even for "High" exposure risk (such as open bioreactor feeding), with 4 layers of protection the environmental grade can be as low as CNC, not Grade 5 as intuition would suggest. This is the key lever for saving construction cost.

Conversely, a "Low" risk operation with zero protection (such as a fully exposed purification operation) needs only CNC, which shows that the step itself does not require a high-grade environment.

Log-Equivalence Calculation of Protection Layers

Protection equivalence (approximate):
  0 layers → fully exposed (0 log reduction)
  1-2 layers  → open microenvironment (RABS, etc.)
             1–2 log contamination reduction
  3 layers    → closed microenvironment (isolator, etc.)
             >3 log contamination reduction
  4 layers    → fully sealed system
             >4 log contamination reduction

Effect of material reliability (Note 5):
  Stainless steel piping > silicone tubing
  (lower failure rate of stainless steel → higher "credit" for the protection layer)

Spacing between environmental grades = 1 order of magnitude:
  Grade 5 (ISO 5) → Grade 7 (ISO 7) about 100× difference
  Grade 7 (ISO 7) → Grade 8 (ISO 8) about 10× difference
  → each step up in grade ≈ 1 log cleaner

Practical Application - Calculating the Benefit of Classification for a CDMO

Take a 500 m² purification operating area as an example:

  • Traditional conservative approach (Grade C / Grade 8): HVAC air change rate 20–30 ACH, construction cost +30% higher, annual electricity cost NT$2 million higher
  • Closed process + risk assessment (CNC+): air change rate 15 ACH, lower construction cost, annual electricity savings of NT$2 million or more
  • Fully sealed system (CNC): air change rate 6–10 ACH, maximum savings

Over a 15-year plant life cycle, correct risk-based classification can save more than NT$30 million in energy and construction costs while remaining fully compliant with regulations. This document (Appendix 1, Section 10.2) is the technical justification document you use to explain to customers or regulators why a lower grade was selected.

Practice Questions

  1. A "Final Purification" step uses a sealed stainless steel piping system (3 layers of protection). According to Table 10.2, which environmental grade should be assigned? Walk through the logic step by step.
  2. Why does stainless steel piping provide a higher protection "credit" than silicone tubing? How can this difference be quantified in a risk assessment?
  3. If a CDMO wants to downgrade an operating area from Grade 8 to CNC+, which elements must its risk assessment report argue? (Hint: work backwards from Tables 10.1 and 10.2)

Original Text

10.2.1 Other Approaches to Risk Assessment

FMEA is one of the most popular methods of risk assessment and prioritization. Most companies have a standard approach (evaluating the probability, impact and detectability of an environmental control failure) that is fully applicable to this type of assessment. Given the consistency of the factors that must be considered, as discussed in the preceding, an approach with a fixed taxonomy can be used to promote a consistent evaluation of risk and resultant environmental controls. The following is an example of one such approach and method.

Figure 10.1🔍: Example Environmental Risk Assessment Figure
[Risk assessment tool figure referenced in source — shows a multi-factor FMEA framework for environmental risk evaluation]

The above tool is used in a multi-step process when performing an environmental risk assessment to determine area biosafety and cleanliness classifications:

Step 1:

Look at every room or function in the facility to consider the risk associated with the activities in that room.

Step 2:

Look at every activity and sub activity (e.g., charging, processing, discharging) in the room.

Step 3:

Look at every activity and sub-activity and assign a risk associated with its sensitivity to the room environment. Consider the level of energy, the level of openness, the sensitivity of the process itself (presence of growth media, hand manipulation, etc.)

Look for the presence of organisms requiring biosafety.

Examine both the impact of contamination as well as the probability of contamination at that step.

Step 4:

Look at the layers of protection against the above sensitivity to the room environment.

Step 5:

Look at both the impact of contamination at the stage in the process and the presence of downstream processing that can further mitigate the environmental risk.

Step 6:

Take the product of the preceding factors and determine a level of environmental control required. Gowning may be a factor included in this determination.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Application of FMEA to Environmental Classification

FMEA (Failure Mode and Effects Analysis) is a core tool of Quality Risk Management (QRM) and is recommended by ICH Q9. When applied to environmental classification, the three scoring dimensions are:

  • Severity: if environmental control fails, how large is the impact on product quality?
  • Probability: how likely is it that environmental control fails?
  • Detectability: if it occurs, can it be detected in time and action taken?

Multiplying the three gives the RPN (Risk Priority Number). A high RPN requires stricter environmental control; a low RPN allows the grade requirement to be lowered.

Analogy - The Six Steps Are Like a "Safety Review Checklist"

The six steps are like an airline pilot's pre-flight checklist: each step has a clear purpose and must be carried out in sequence without skipping:

  • Steps 1–2: break the "flight plan" down to every detail (every operation in every room)
  • Step 3: assess the "hazard" of each detail (energy level, degree of openness, presence of growth medium)
  • Steps 4–5: take stock of the "safety equipment" (number of protection layers, downstream removal steps)
  • Step 6: from the above, determine "how many cabin crew this flight needs" (the required environmental grade)

The key point: every room and every operating step must be assessed independently, because a single large room may contain both an operation that needs Grade 8 and one that needs only CNC.

Key Point - The Importance of "Downstream Mitigation" in Step 5

The "downstream mitigation" in Step 5 is an often overlooked but highly valuable factor:

If a potentially contaminated operation has several removal steps downstream (for example: Protein A purification + viral filtration + UF/DF), then even if a small amount of environmental contamination is introduced at this step, the subsequent process can remove it, so the environmental requirement can be lowered.

Conversely, if a step is the "last barrier" (such as final formulation and filling) with no filtration or removal downstream, the environmental requirement must be the strictest.

This is exactly why API steps (before final filling) are labeled as "High" exposure risk in Table 10.1.

Summary of the Six-Step Decision Framework

Step 1: List all rooms / functional areas
         ↓
Step 2: Break down each operation within each room
         (sub-activities such as filling, charging, discharging)
         ↓
Step 3: Assess the exposure sensitivity of each operation
         · Energy level (risk of aerosol generation)
         · Degree of openness
         · Presence of growth medium
         · Biosafety level requirements
         · Contamination impact × contamination probability
         ↓
Step 4: Count the protection layers
         (sealed containers, isolators, closed piping, etc.)
         ↓
Step 5: Assess downstream mitigation measures
         (can subsequent purification steps remove the contamination?)
         ↓
Step 6: Look up Table 10.2
         → determine the minimum required environmental grade
         (+ consider personnel gowning requirements)

Practical Application - How to Use This Report in Regulatory Communication

In an FDA Pre-approval Inspection (PAI) or an EMA GMP inspection, if the inspector questions the environmental grade chosen for an area, this six-step FMEA documentation is your technical line of defense:

  • Document the exposure risk score of each operation (with rationale)
  • Document the calculation of protection layers (including the material reliability analysis)
  • Document the list of downstream mitigation measures
  • The logic chain of the final grade selection (from Table 10.1 → Table 10.2 → selected grade)

Once this document is completed and approved at the design stage, it becomes the "Scientific Justification" for your facility design and can be cited in all subsequent regulatory communication.

Practice Questions

  1. Use the six-step framework to assess the following scenario:
    A closed Protein A column purification step (stainless steel piping throughout, no manual open operations, with viral filtration and UF/DF downstream). What environmental grade do you recommend?
  2. Why is "Gowning" listed as a consideration in Step 6? What role does it play in the protection logic (protecting the product, protecting the operator, or both)?
  3. If the same room contains two operations, one needing Grade 8 and the other needing only CNC, what grade should be set for the whole room? Could the problem be solved by zoning?

Section Summary

  • The core lever of sustainable design is Closed Processing: closed processing allows the environmental grade to be lowered, and a lower grade translates directly into tens of times lower energy consumption and substantially lower construction cost, while also improving ESG performance.
  • ISPE consolidates the grade definitions of the three major regulatory systems (FDA / EMA / PIC/S): Grade 5 = Grade A, Grade 7 = Grade B, Grade 8 = Grade C, CNC+ ≈ Grade D. Mastering this cross-reference avoids confusion in cross-jurisdiction communication.
  • CNC and CNC+ are the environmental grades most commonly used for closed biopharmaceutical processes: they are not "second-class" unclassified spaces but design-controlled spaces supported by complete IQ/OQ documentation, with a major cost advantage in closed-process design.
  • The two-step logic of risk-based classification: Step 1 determines the exposure risk level from the process stage and contamination sensitivity; Step 2 converts the number of protection layers (0–4) into the minimum required environmental grade, which is read from Table 10.2.
  • The six-step FMEA is a technical line of defense in regulatory communication: complete six-step documentation gives scientific justification to design choices below the traditional conservative grade, and serves as strong supporting documentation in FDA PAI and EMA inspections.
  • Downstream Mitigation is an important argument for lowering the grade: if an operation is followed by several purification and removal steps, the environmental requirement can reasonably be lowered; conversely, the environmental requirement for a "last barrier" operation must be the strictest.

  • Use the quick-reference summary table as an everyday GMP terminology lookup tool.

Trainer's Analysis — Why the Core Regulatory Documents Matter

ICH Q7/Q7A — GMP for APIs

This guideline sets out GMP requirements for active pharmaceutical ingredients (APIs) and is the common baseline followed by manufacturers worldwide. Although biopharmaceutical APIs are mostly large-molecule proteins, the guideline establishes core concepts such as production environment control, equipment qualification and batch records, which underpin later biotech-specific guidelines such as ICH Q11.

ICH Q11 — Development and Manufacture of Drug Substances

Q11 extends Q7 and addresses the development of biotechnological/biological drug substances, including cell culture, fermentation and purification. It expects manufacturers to establish a Design Space and explain the scientific rationale for process development, which is closely linked to the facility design logic of ISPE Vol.6.

21 CFR Part 211 — FDA GMP for Finished Pharmaceuticals

21 CFR Part 211 is the FDA's core regulation for finished pharmaceuticals, covering facility design, equipment, personnel, production and process controls, quality control and more. A biopharmaceutical plant that intends to sell product in the US must ensure that all facility designs and operating procedures meet the minimum requirements of Part 211. Key point: Part 211 is the regulatory floor, whereas ISPE guidance is best practice; for compliance purposes, the regulation always takes precedence.

ICH Q9 — Quality Risk Management (QRM)

Q9 establishes a general risk management framework for the pharmaceutical industry, including tools such as FMEA (Failure Mode and Effects Analysis) and HACCP (Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Points). The facility design decisions throughout ISPE Vol.6 (closed systems, environmental classification, HVAC design and so on) are all grounded in QRM; without understanding Q9, it is impossible to understand why some spaces require stricter environmental control.

Analogy: The Hierarchy of Regulatory Documents

Think of these documents as a system of building codes: ICH Q7/Q11 are the "general building code" (applicable to all buildings), 21 CFR Part 211 is the "mandatory fire safety code" (must be followed), ICH Q9 is the "risk assessment toolbook", and ISPE Vol.6 is the "best-practice guide to designing a high-end laboratory building". The latter tells you how to build it best, but the former tell you the minimum you must achieve.

Practice Questions

  1. What roles do ICH Q8(R2) (Pharmaceutical Development) and ICH Q9 (Quality Risk Management) each play in facility design decisions?
  2. If a biopharmaceutical plant applies for approval from both the FDA (US) and the EMA (EU), which EU document must it comply with in addition to 21 CFR Part 211?
