Trainer's Analysis — ISPE, EU GMP, WHO and Biosafety Documents

ISPE Vol.5 (C&Q) — The Language Foundation of Qualification

All the system classifications in ISPE Vol.6 relating to qualification (such as Direct Impact / No Impact, later evolved into QRM verification) originate from Vol.5. So when Vol.6 describes a closed system as not a Critical Aspect, the underlying decision logic is the framework of Vol.5 C&Q. CDMO engineers must read the two together.

EU GMP Annex 2 — EU-Specific Requirements for Manufacture of Biological Products

EU GMP Annex 2 specifically governs the manufacture of biological products (including cell culture, fermentation, blood products, monoclonal antibodies and so on) and is a prerequisite for access to the EU market. The draft version of this annex, at the time this book was published, had already introduced the concepts of "degree of process closure" and "environmental control strategy", which directly influenced the facility design recommendations of ISPE Vol.6.

EU GMP Annex 1 — Standards for Sterile Products

Annex 1 is the "bible" of sterile manufacturing facilities worldwide. It defines the four environmental grades (Grade A/B/C/D) and the environmental monitoring standards for airborne particles, settle plates, contact plates and so on. The environmental classification chapter of ISPE Vol.6 corresponds closely to Annex 1, and the two must be consulted together.

NIH/CDC Biosafety Guidelines — The Legal Basis for BSL Levels

The NIH Guidelines and the jointly published CDC/NIH publication Biosafety in Microbiological and Biomedical Laboratories (BMBL) are the authoritative documents that define Biosafety Levels BSL 1-4. The containment strategy and facility design in ISPE Vol.6 rely on these guidelines to determine the hazard level of the microorganisms and hence the physical containment measures required. If microorganisms of BSL-2 or above are handled, the facility design must meet the corresponding containment requirements; this is not just GMP but a legal biosafety obligation.

ISO 14644-1 — Cleanroom Air Classification Standard

ISO 14644-1 defines ISO Class 1-9 through scientific particle counting and is the common language of environmental monitoring in US, European and Japanese pharmaceutical plants. The FDA uses ISO 5/7/8 as equivalents of its traditional Class 100/10,000/100,000; EU GMP uses Grade A/B/C/D but likewise refers to ISO test methods. The environmental chapter of ISPE Vol.6 is based on ISO 14644 and bridges the two systems.

Practical Application — CDMO Dual-Market Filing Scenario

A Taiwanese CDMO manufactures biologics for both US customers (FDA-regulated) and EU customers (EMA-regulated). It must comply with all of the following: 21 CFR Part 211 (FDA), EU GMP Annex 1 & 2 (EMA), ISO 14644-1 (environmental classification), ICH Q9/Q10 (quality system) and NIH/CDC biosafety guidelines (BSL assessment). The reference list in this appendix is the starting point for building a multi-market compliance matrix.

Complete Reference List (Selected — p121-p127)

Original Text — Complete Reference List

Trainer's Analysis — Overview of the Document System

Six Major Families of Source Documents

  • ICH (International Council for Harmonisation of Technical Requirements for Pharmaceuticals for Human Use): Q7/Q8/Q9/Q10/Q11 form the backbone of the quality system, recognized jointly by the three regions (US, EU, Japan).
  • FDA regulations: 21 CFR Part 210/211 is the mandatory regulatory floor for the US market.
  • EU GMP EudraLex Vol.4: Annex 1 (sterile) and Annex 2 (biological) are the key to access to the EU market.
  • ISPE Baseline Guide series: best practice for facility design; Vol.3 (Sterile), Vol.5 (C&Q) and Vol.7 (Risk-Based) are closely linked to this volume.
  • ISO 14644 series: technical cleanroom standards that bridge the environmental test methods of the FDA and EU GMP.
  • NIH/CDC biosafety guidelines: the scientific basis for determining BSL levels and physical containment requirements.

Key Reminder — Be Aware of Document Versions

This book (ISPE Vol.6, 2nd Ed.) was published in 2013. Some of the documents it cites have been updated since publication:

  • EU GMP Annex 1 was fully revised in 2022, introducing the new Contamination Control Strategy (CCS) framework.
  • The EU GMP Annex 2 draft has since been formally issued.
  • ISPE Baseline Vol.5 C&Q had its second edition published in 2019.

When consulting this guide, always check whether newer versions of the cited documents exist.

Appendix 3 — Glossary
12.1 Acronyms and Abbreviations

Original Text — Acronyms & Abbreviations

Trainer's Analysis — Key Abbreviations Explained

BSL (Biosafety Level) — The Starting Point of Containment Design

BSL 1-4 classify microorganisms by the hazard they pose to humans and are the key input for determining the containment level of a facility:

  • BSL-1: low hazard, standard microbiological practices (general laboratory)
  • BSL-2: moderate hazard (most biopharmaceutical cell lines, such as CHO and E. coli); requires negative pressure, an autoclave and a BSC
  • BSL-3: serious hazard, potentially lethal but treatable (e.g. Mycobacterium tuberculosis); requires double-door entry and dedicated exhaust
  • BSL-4: extremely high hazard with no treatment (e.g. Ebola virus); requires positive-pressure suits or isolator-type facilities

Most biopharmaceutical plants operate at BSL-1/2; understanding the BSL levels is a prerequisite for the containment chapter of facility design.

CNC / CNC+ — Environmental Classifications Specific to Biopharmaceuticals

CNC (Controlled Not Classified) is an environment type specific to ISPE Vol.6, sitting between ordinary spaces (UC) and ISO-classified cleanrooms:

  • HVAC control (temperature, humidity, air change rate) is in place, but the formal classification standards of ISO 14644 are not met
  • Suitable for closed-system operations (such as closed piping and closed bioreactors), because the process itself provides an adequate contamination barrier
  • CNC+ additionally installs local monitoring equipment (such as particle counters) to improve visibility of environmental data

CHO (Chinese Hamster Ovary cell line)

CHO is the most widely used mammalian expression system in the biopharmaceutical industry; about 70% of therapeutic proteins (including most MAbs) are produced in CHO cells. Understanding the characteristics of CHO cells (sensitivity to pH, dissolved oxygen and temperature) helps in understanding bioreactor design requirements.

MAb (Monoclonal Antibody) — The Flagship Biopharmaceutical Product

MAbs (monoclonal antibodies) are currently the highest-selling category of biopharmaceutical products worldwide (including oncology and autoimmune disease therapies). The MAb production flow (cell culture → harvest → Protein A purification → virus removal filtration → formulation) is the main application scenario for the facility design in this ISPE Vol.6.

SIP vs CIP — Two Concepts That Must Be Distinguished

CIP (Clean-In-Place): uses cleaning solutions (NaOH, acids, etc.) to remove soil and residues without dismantling the equipment. The goal is bioburden reduction.

SIP (Steam-In-Place): uses saturated steam (typically 121°C / 15 minutes) to achieve sterilization (sterile = zero bioburden) without dismantling the equipment. The goal is to destroy all viable microorganisms.

Sequence: CIP first, then SIP. It is like washing dishes (CIP) before sending them for autoclave sterilization (SIP).

Analogy: MCB vs WCB

Think of the MCB (Master Cell Bank) as the "original master tape" of the cells and the WCB (Working Cell Bank) as the "copy". Each production batch uses one vial from the WCB, while the MCB is always kept as a backup. If the WCB becomes contaminated, it can be re-derived from the MCB. The storage conditions and characterization of the MCB are among the most important asset-protection measures in a biopharmaceutical plant.

12.2 Definitions — Part 1: A through C

Original Text — Definitions: A–C

Adventitious Agents

Adventitious agents are microorganisms that have been unintentionally introduced into the manufacturing process of a biological product. Adventitious agents include bacteria, fungi, mycoplasmas, rickettsia, protozoa, parasites, Transmissible Spongiform Encephalopathy (TSE) agents, and viruses.

Airborne Total Particulate

A measurement of airborne particles in a specified size range (typically determined by mass mean diameter) as recorded by a Discrete Particle Counter (DPC). Since the vast majority of particle counters are incapable of distinguishing between viable and non-viable particulate, this measurement is erroneously called "non-viable".

Air Change Rate / Ventilation Rate

The volume of air supplied to a room (per hour) divided by the volume of the room. This is a purely numerical evaluation used as a rule of thumb in cleanroom design.

Aseptic Operations

Processes that are devoid of measurable (detectable) bioburden. Aseptic operations generally require sterilization of the environment, equipment and process solutions to achieve the sterile state prior to use.

Aseptic Transfer

Material transfer where the risk of contamination from the environment has been mitigated.

Axenic State

A culture that includes the intended living organism but is entirely free of all other contaminating organisms.

Bioburden-Free

A condition where bioburden is not detectable. Bioreactors used in cell culture or fermentation operations should be sterile prior to use for consistent and reproducible performance. Biosafety Cabinets (BSCs), RABs and isolators should provide an environment free of bioburden for consistent and reproducible aseptic operations. Sterile product manufacturing testing and qualification guidelines are by necessity more stringent and prescriptive than that of active pharmaceutical ingredient manufacturing and therefore are not required.

Bioprocessing

Biologics manufacturing. Includes all operations used in the manufacture of biologics including upstream processes such as inoculum preparation, cell culture/fermentation processes, harvest and clarification processes such as sedimentation and filtration processes, downstream processing such as chromatography and filtration processes.

Briefly Exposed

Open processes containing process and/or product components that are rendered closed by means of an appropriate closing process. Examples of briefly exposed processes include open buffer or media preparations where the solution is "briefly" exposed to the environment prior to closing by sterile filtration and/or thermal sanitization. Briefly exposed operations may be performed in Controlled Not Classified (CNC) or low bioburden environments if measures used to close the process are appropriate to mitigate risk of contamination from the environment.

Carryover

Contaminants detected in process streams arising from insufficient removal of contaminating components from previous manufacturing steps or batches. Maximum allowable carryover residues should be estimated and meet defined criteria. Carryover is typically a result of insufficient cleaning, sanitization, rinsing and/or conditioning.

Trainer's Analysis — Key Terms A to C

Adventitious Agents — The Number One Risk in Biopharmaceuticals

Adventitious agents (especially viruses, including TSE prions) are a core safety challenge for biopharmaceuticals, because:

  • Raw materials (animal-derived serum, starting cells) may carry latent viruses
  • TSE prions are exceptionally resistant to sterilization (ordinary 121°C steam sterilization cannot fully destroy them)
  • ICH Q5A specifically governs the viral safety testing requirements for biological products

The facility design must include "virus removal/inactivation steps" (such as low-pH inactivation and nanofiltration) and provide virus segregation areas.

Airborne Total Particulate — The Myth of "Non-Viable" Particles

This definition specifically reminds us that a particle counter cannot distinguish viable (microbe-bearing) from non-viable (sterile) particles, so the commonly used "non-viable particle count" is really a "total particle count". Monitoring of viable microorganisms requires separate methods (such as settle plates and contact plates). This is a fundamental limitation that must be understood when designing an environmental monitoring program.

Axenic State — How It Differs from Sterile

Axenic emphasizes "only the target organism, with no contaminating organisms" and is often used to describe the ideal state of a cell culture or fermentation process. How it differs from Sterile:

  • Sterile: completely free of any living microorganisms, including the target organism
  • Axenic: only the target organism is present, with no foreign microbes, but the target organism itself is alive

After inoculation, the ideal state of a fermenter is Axenic (pure culture), not Sterile.

Bioburden-Free — Not the Same as Sterile

The technical definition of Bioburden-Free is "bioburden cannot be detected with the available test methods". This means:

  • It is a concept of a detection limit, not an absolute zero
  • It describes the state that environments (inside a BSC or RABS) should achieve
  • It is a more conservative, "functional" statement, in contrast to the stricter "Sterile"

This is also why ISPE Vol.6 does not require every bioprocess space to reach sterile grade, but instead designs the environment according to how open the process is.

Analogy: Briefly Exposed Is Like a Pot with the Lid Off

"Briefly exposed" is like lifting the lid for a moment to add an ingredient while simmering soup, then putting it straight back (sterile filtration or heat sterilization "closes" the process). The key points: the exposure time must be short, and the subsequent closing step must be adequately validated. If the exposure is too long, or the sterilization step is insufficient to destroy potential contamination, the environmental grade needs to be upgraded.

Carryover — The Core Risk in Multiproduct Facilities

For a CDMO producing several biological products, carryover is the most important risk of cross-batch contamination. ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.7 (Risk-MaPP) provides a methodology for assessing the maximum allowable carryover (MACO). A well-designed CIP procedure, appropriate rinse validation and residue testing are the keys to controlling carryover.

12.2 Definitions — Part 2: Closed / Open System & Process Closure

Original Text — Closed System, Open Process & Related Terms

Classified Space

Areas where HVAC systems are specifically designed to reduce airborne contaminants below a specified level as defined in ISO 14644-1 (tested per ISO 14644-2 and ISO 14644-3) and both temperature and Relative Humidity (RH) are controlled more tightly than in the ambient environment. These areas must be performance verified / qualified. These areas may be tested to meet ISO requirements for airborne 0.5 µm particulate and viable organisms in the "in-operation" state to meet US FDA requirements or they may be tested to meet ISO requirements for both airborne 0.5 µm and 5.0 µm particulate as well as viable organisms in both the "in-operation" state as well as the "at-rest" state to meet EMA and PIC/S requirements. Where EMA and PIC/S requirements are to be satisfied the transition between these two states should take place in 15 to 20 minutes.

Closed System

A process system that is designed and operated such that the product is never exposed to the surrounding environment. Additions to and draws from closed systems must be performed in a completely closed fashion. Sterile filters may be used to provide effective barriers from contaminants in the environment. A system is closed (or isolated from the environment) when the risk of contamination to the product or process cannot be mitigated by housing the operation in a bioburden-free or particulate-free environment. In Quality Risk Management (QRM) Verification, the environment does not represent a Critical Aspect of a closed process. A closed process is one that has been validated to show that there are sufficient layers of protection to mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment. The detection of a contaminant from the environment in an otherwise closed system indicates a breach of the closed system and therefore constitutes a system failure. Examples of closed systems include sterile single use bags supplied with integrated aseptic connection devices.

Functionally Closed

Process systems that may be opened but are "rendered closed" by a cleaning, sanitization and/or sterilization process that is appropriate or consistent with the process requirements. These systems shall remain closed during production within the system. A functionally closed process must be validated to show that sufficient layers of protection have been implemented to mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment. Transfers into or from these systems must also be validated as closed. Examples include process vessels that may be cleaned in place and steamed in place between uses.

Open Process

A process that is not closed and therefore requires appropriate environmental controls to mitigate the risk of contamination from the environment. Under QRM verification, the environment within the RABs and/or BSCs represents a critical aspect of the open process. Open sterile and aseptic operations must be performed in an environment where the probability of contamination is acceptably low. Open bioburden-controlled processing may be performed in a Grade 7 or 8 environment as appropriate for the unit operation.

Critical Control Point — CCP

A step where control is required to prevent or eliminate a product safety or quality hazard or reduce it to an acceptable level.

Critical Process Parameter — CPP

A measurable input (input material attribute or operating parameter) or output (process state variable or output material attribute) of a process step that must be controlled to achieve the desired product quality and process consistency.

Critical Quality Attribute — CQA

Physical, chemical, biological or microbiological properties or characteristics that need to be controlled (directly or indirectly) to ensure product quality (ISPE PQLI Guide). Physical, chemical, biological or microbiological properties or characteristics that should be within an appropriate limit, range, or distribution to ensure the desired product quality (ICH Q8(R2)).

Cross-Contamination / Crossover

Contamination of the product or processing area by components or contaminants found in a neighboring product or processing area. Cross contamination typically occurs between open processes. Recirculating HVAC can be a source of cross contamination if improperly designed.

Trainer's Analysis — Closed/Open Systems and CQA/CPP

Three Key Criteria for a Closed System

The ISPE Vol.6 definition of a "closed system" is stricter than intuition suggests; all of the following must be met:

  1. Closed by design: the product is never exposed to the surrounding environment
  2. Closed in operation: all entries and exits (sampling, additions, discharge) are performed in a closed manner
  3. Closed by validation: the validated system provides an adequate protective layer

When all three conditions are met, the environment is "not" a Critical Aspect of the process; that is, the environmental grade of the space can be lowered (e.g. CNC instead of ISO 7/8).

Consequences of a Closed System Breach

"If contaminants from the environment are detected in a system that was intended to be closed, this constitutes a system failure." This definition shows how serious a closed-system breach is: it is not just a deviation but the collapse of the design assumption of the entire closed system, and it requires a thorough root cause investigation and design review.

Analogy: Closed vs Open Systems Is Like a Thermos vs an Open Cup

A closed system is like a thermos: no matter how dirty the surroundings, the drink inside is unaffected (as long as the lid is properly sealed and the flask has been cleaned and sterilized). An open system is like an open cup: once placed in an unclean environment, it is inevitably exposed to contamination risk. Open-system operations must therefore be performed in a cleanroom (like placing the cup in an extremely clean dust-free room).

CQA vs CPP — The Core Language of QbD in Biopharmaceuticals

These two concepts are at the heart of the ICH Q8/Q10 quality system and are also the driving force behind ISPE Vol.6 facility design:

  • CQA (Critical Quality Attribute): defines "what I need to protect", for example the glycoform distribution, biological activity and purity of a protein. CQAs relate directly to patient safety.
  • CPP (Critical Process Parameter): defines "what I need to control", for example pH, dissolved oxygen and temperature in cell culture. CPPs are the means of controlling CQAs.

Facility design logic: CQA → identify the CPPs that affect the CQA → design facilities and systems that can effectively monitor and control the CPPs.

Practice Questions

  1. Is a stainless steel bioreactor equipped with CIP/SIP a Closed, Functionally Closed or Open system? What environmental grade does the space it sits in require?
  2. What CQAs might a monoclonal antibody have? What are the corresponding facility design requirements?
  3. Why is a "closed system breach" considered more serious than an ordinary deviation?

Comparison of Environmental Requirements for the Three Degrees of Closure

12.2 Definitions — Part 3: Environmental Monitoring, Classification & Remaining Terms

Original Text — Environmental & Remaining Definitions

ISO 14644 — Classification

This standard classifies spaces following a decimal system according to the concentration of particulate in a series of size ranges, specifically: 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 1.0, and 5.0 µm. An ISO class designation indicates that particulate within the space are within limits as defined by the formula Cn = 10N × (0.1/D)2.08 where Cn is the particle count, N is the ISO class, and D is the particle mean diameter in µm.

ISO 14644-1 Air Quality Classes Table

Note: 1 cubic meter = 35.3 cubic feet. FDA measures only 0.5 µm (in-operation). EMA/PIC/S measures both 0.5 µm and 5.0 µm (at-rest and in-operation).

FDA Application of ISO Classifications
  • ISO 5: 3,520 particles/m³ (0.5 µm), in-operation. Normally with unidirectional flow (0.20–0.45 m/s)
  • ISO 6: 35,200 particles/m³, in-operation
  • ISO 7: 352,000 particles/m³, in-operation
  • ISO 8: 3,520,000 particles/m³, in-operation
  • ISO 9: 35,200,000 particles/m³ (not in FDA guidance but found in FDA-regulated facilities)
Local Protected Environments

Local protected environments may be used for open processes. Examples include Biosafety Cabinets (BSCs), Unidirectional Down Flow Hoods (UDFHs), isolators and Restricted Access Barriers (RABs). When used, it is critical that the local environment be protected from unexpected breach. Only a bioburden-free environment should be used for open aseptic operations.

Low Bioburden (operation)

Process where a limited and controlled level of measurable bioburden is acceptable under specific conditions. (e.g. WFI, chromatography operations).

MERV (Multiple Efficiency Reporting Value)

The ASHRAE Standard 52.2 method of testing filter efficiency by challenging filters with particles of multiple sizes and integrating the efficiency into a single number rating. The higher the MERV number the more efficient the filter. MERV 14/15 is approximately equivalent to a 95% (ASHRAE) efficient filter.

Risk Assessment

Systematic process of organizing information to support risk decisions. This includes identification of risk(s) and evaluation of the nature, severity and likelihood of those risk(s).

Sanitization

Operation that reduces the level of bioburden.

Sterile / Sterilization

Sterile: Devoid of life. Zero bioburden. Free of bioburden.

Sterilization: Form of sanitization that results in a bioburden-free (sterile) condition. Methods include thermal sterilization (121°C/15 minutes or higher), sterile filtration (0.2 µm or better), chemical sterilization (e.g., VHP, chlorine dioxide, ozone) or irradiation (e.g. gamma, UV).

Uncontrolled — UC

Areas where the HVAC systems may be present, but no claim is made or qualified for the specific control of particulate, temperature or humidity. These areas are sometimes referred to as "general" or "comfort controlled" areas within pharmaceutical facilities such as office and technical space.

Ventilation Efficiency (Effectiveness)

The ability of an HVAC system to dilute contaminants when compared to ideal dilution. A Ventilation Effectiveness of 1.0 indicates ideal dilution. Displacement ventilation may have an effectiveness as high as 3.0 to 4.0.

Trainer's Analysis — Environmental Monitoring and Classification in Depth

ISO Classification Formula

C_n = 10^N × (0.1 / D)^2.08

Where:
  C_n = maximum permitted particle concentration (particles/m³)
  N   = ISO class number (1-9)
  D   = mean particle diameter (µm)

Example: ISO Class 5, 0.5 µm particles:
  C_n = 10^5 × (0.1/0.5)^2.08
      = 100,000 × (0.2)^2.08
      ≈ 3,520 particles/m³  ✓ (consistent with the table)

15-20 Minute Recovery Time — A Requirement Specific to the EMA

EU GMP requires that environmental recovery from the "in-operation" to the "at-rest" state be completed within 15-20 minutes, which can be verified by the "recovery test" of ISO 14644-3. This requirement directly affects the HVAC design air change rate (ACH) and air velocity. A Taiwanese CDMO applying for the EU market must include this test data in its qualification report.

Principles for Selecting a Sterilization Method

Different sterilization methods suit different scenarios:

  • Heat sterilization (SIP, 121°C/15min): the most reliable; used for stainless steel equipment and piping; the F₀ value must be validated
  • Sterile filtration (0.2µm): used for heat-labile solutions (culture media, protein solutions)
  • VHP (vaporized hydrogen peroxide): used for environmental surfaces and isolator decontamination; sporicidal efficacy must be validated (6-log D-value)
  • UV radiation: used for air and surfaces; poor penetration, so limited to exposed surfaces
  • Gamma radiation: used for single-use items (such as SU bags); cannot be used on site

Analogy: MERV Is Like the Filtration Rating of a Face Mask

The MERV value is like the N rating of a mask (N95, N99): the higher the number, the greater the filtration efficiency. MERV 14/15 ≈ 95% filtration efficiency, roughly equivalent to a typical cleanroom bag filter; HEPA is 99.97% or higher (beyond the upper limit of the MERV scale). Choosing a MERV grade requires a balance between filtration performance and system pressure drop (energy cost).

Practice Questions

  1. What ISO class should be achieved inside an isolator equipped with a VHP decontamination system? What method is used to validate it?
  2. What are the two main differences in environmental monitoring between FDA-regulated and EMA-regulated facilities?
  3. Can sterile filtration with a 0.2µm filter remove viruses? Why is an additional virus removal step needed?

ISO Classification vs EU GMP Grades — Mapping the Dual Systems

Global biopharmaceutical plants must understand both languages of environmental classification:

FDA vs EMA differences: the FDA requires testing only "in-operation" for 0.5µm particles; the EMA/PIC/S additionally requires "at-rest" data and 5µm particle data, and the transition between the two states should be completed within 15-20 minutes.

Quick-Reference Summary Table — The 15 Most Important Terms

The following 15 terms are the most frequently used and practically influential core concepts in ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.6; it is recommended to memorize their abbreviations and one-line definitions.

Section Summary

  • The 70 references in Appendix 2 form a complete international regulatory ecosystem: ICH Q7/Q9/Q10/Q11 establish the foundation of the quality system, 21 CFR Part 211 and EU GMP Annex 1/2 set the market access requirements, the ISO 14644 series provides the technical standards for environmental classification, and the NIH/CDC biosafety guidelines determine the containment level.
  • The closed system is the most important design concept in Vol.6: a system validated as closed does not need a high-grade cleanroom (CNC is sufficient), but a closure breach is treated as a "system failure" and requires root cause analysis.
  • CQAs (Critical Quality Attributes) and CPPs (Critical Process Parameters) are the drivers of facility design; the essence of facility design is "to establish a physical environment that effectively monitors and protects the CPPs, thereby ensuring the CQAs".
  • BSL (Biosafety Level) is the starting point of the facility containment strategy; this is a legal biosafety obligation beyond GMP, and both must be complied with at the same time.
  • The dual-track differences in ISO classification (FDA: in-operation 0.5µm only; EMA: at-rest + in-operation, 0.5µm + 5µm, 15-20 minute recovery time) require particular attention when qualifying facilities for multiple markets.
  • The 15 core terms (CQA, CPP, BSL, CNC, SIP, CIP, Closed System, Axenic State, Bioburden-Free, RABS, QRM, VHP, WFI, MCB/WCB, Classified Space) are the common language for communicating the design of a biopharmaceutical plant to any regulator.
